# Seneca, Moral Letters to Lucilius: a selection

*A new English translation from the Latin of the sections the Stoic Constitution quotes (seneca-v1)*

Made 2026-11-23. Every section of Seneca’s Moral Letters that the Stoic Constitution quotes: 304 sections from 94 letters. Each was translated from the Latin alone by one model, checked against the Latin by a second, and each point was settled in writing by a third reading. None has been read by the owner or checked by a person who reads Latin.

How it was made:
- A first translator (Claude Fable 5.2) worked from the Latin alone, with a short glossary that keeps shared Stoic terms in the same English as the project’s Marcus, and wrote notes where the Latin is open.
- A second reader saw only the Latin and the English and listed every place where the English says more, less or other than the Latin.
- A third reading settled each point in writing (Accepted, Partly accepted or Declined, with the reason from the Latin) and gave the final English.
- No reader saw any published translation of Seneca. Gummere’s 1917–25 English is kept privately and used only to measure similarity (as the 1634 English is for Marcus); none of its words are published.
- Letter titles are the project’s own, written from each letter’s Latin.

What it cannot promise:
- The Latin is an unattributed older text, not a modern critical edition; doubtful readings are noted where a reader saw them.
- All three readings were made by language models; no person who reads Latin has checked the English.
- Only the quoted sections are translated, so a section may begin in the middle of an argument; its notes say so where it matters.

Source text: The Latin Library, Seneca, Epistulae morales ad Lucilium (https://www.thelatinlibrary.com/sen.html), an older public-domain text. The Latin is not included in this file.

Sections are numbered by letter and section as in the standard editions. Only the quoted sections are translated, so a section may begin in the middle of an argument. The letter titles are the project’s own.

Licence: Dedicated to the public domain (CC0 1.0 Universal, https://creativecommons.org/publicdomain/zero/1.0/). You may copy, change, publish and sell it without asking and without naming anyone. Words of the Greek or Latin quoted in the notes come from the source texts named below.

On the web: https://ilyadays.vercel.app/apps/stoic-constitution.html

## Letter 1: On valuing and saving your time

### 1.2

Who will you give me who sets any price on time, who puts a value on a day, who understands that he is dying every day? For we are deceived in this, that we look ahead to death: a great part of it has already gone by; whatever of our lifetime lies behind us, death holds. So do, my Lucilius, what you write you are doing: take hold of every hour. In this way it will come about that you depend less on tomorrow, if you lay hands on today.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quem mihi dabis*: Literally “whom will you give”, an idiom for “name a man”. The English keeps the verb “give” and the form of a question. Other reading: Name me a man who sets any price on time
- *se cotidie mori*: The present infinitive can be heard as a process (“is dying”) or as a repeated act (“dies”). The English takes the process, since the next sentence says part of death is already behind. Other reading: that he dies every day
- *quod mortem prospicimus*: The verb means to see ahead, in front of one. The point is that death is placed only in the future. Other reading: that we see death as lying ahead of us
- *magna pars eius iam praeterit*: The pronoun refers to death, not to life. The verb as spelled can be a present (“is going by”) or a contracted perfect (“has gone by”); the English takes the perfect, because the next clause speaks of what already lies behind. Some editions may print the perfect in full (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: a great part of it is already going by
- *omnes horas complectere*: An imperative of the verb “to embrace, to clasp”. “Take hold of” is used because the sentence ends with a hand laid on today. Other reading: embrace all your hours
- *ex crastino pendeas*: Literally “hang from tomorrow”, the ordinary Latin way of saying “depend on”. The English gives the plain sense, since “hang on tomorrow” would read as waiting. Other reading: that you hang less on tomorrow
- *hodierno manum inieceris*: To lay a hand on something is an act in Roman law by which one claims a thing or a person as one’s own. “Lay hands on” is the English phrase for seizing and keeps the hand; the Latin has a single hand. Other reading: if you have claimed today as your own

</details>

## Letter 4: On overcoming the fear of death

### 4.5

Practise this every day, so that you can leave life with an even mind: life, which many clasp and hold on to in the way that people being swept away by rushing water clasp thorns and jagged edges. Most people are tossed about, wretched, between the fear of death and the torments of life, and they do not want to live, they do not know how to die.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Hoc cotidie meditare, ut possis*: The verb covers both thinking a thing over and rehearsing it as an exercise. “This” may point back to the thought of the section before or forward to the clause that follows; the English leaves “this” open and takes the clause as purpose. Other reading: Rehearse this every day: to be able to leave life with an even mind
- *aequo animo*: A set phrase, literally “with a level mind”, meaning without distress or resentment. “Mind” is kept for animus as the glossary prefers, and “calm” is left for tranquillitas. Other reading: calmly, without complaint
- *aqua torrente*: The second word can be a participle (“rushing water”) or part of a noun phrase (“a torrent of water”). The sense is much the same. Other reading: by a torrent
- *spinas et aspera*: The second word is a bare neuter plural, “rough things”, whatever is harsh to grasp; “jagged edges” narrows it a little. The Latin has no second verb, and “clasp” is repeated to make the English run. Other reading: thorns and rough things
- *Plerique*: In classical Latin “most people”; in writers of Seneca’s time it often means only “very many”. The English keeps the stronger sense. Other reading: A great many people
- *miseri fluctuantur*: The verb is from the word for a wave and carries on the water image: they are tossed like something afloat. The adjective “wretched” describes the people, not the manner. Other reading: drift miserably back and forth
- *mori nesciunt*: With an infinitive this verb means “do not know how to”. The two clauses stand side by side in the Latin with no “and” or “but” between them. Other reading: they are unwilling to live and unable to die

</details>

## Letter 5: On living philosophy without showing off

### 5.2

Rough dress, an uncut head of hair, a rather neglected beard, hatred declared on silver, a bed laid on the ground, and whatever else seeks attention by a perverse route: avoid them. The very name of philosophy, even if it is handled with restraint, draws enough ill will: what if we begin to withdraw ourselves from the customs of people? Inside, let everything be different; let our outward face fit in with the people.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *evita*: The section as given starts in the middle of a sentence: the list is the object of this one imperative at its end, and it continues the warning in the section before against doing conspicuous things. The English puts the list first and the command last, as the Latin does. Other reading: Avoid rough dress, an uncut head of hair (and so on)
- *asperum cultum*: The noun covers dress, grooming and style of living generally. It is narrowed to dress because hair, beard and bed follow separately. Other reading: a harsh style of living
- *neglegentiorem barbam*: The comparative can mean “rather careless” or “too careless”. Other reading: a beard too carelessly kept
- *indictum argento odium*: The participle is the word used for declaring war, and “declared on” is meant to keep that sound. “Silver” may be silver tableware or money; the next section speaks of silver plate, and “silver” is kept for the same word in both sections. Other reading: open war declared on silverware
- *ambitionem perversa via sequitur*: The noun here is not political ambition but the courting of notice and admiration. The road is “turned the wrong way”: display is sought through seeming to reject display. Other reading: chases after show by a road turned back to front
- *invidiosum*: The word means attracting ill will or resentment, not “invidious” in the modern English sense. Other reading: is resented enough
- *hominum consuetudini*: The noun can mean custom and habits, or company and dealings with people. The English takes custom, because the list before it is of habits of dress and living and the next sentence speaks of the outward face. Other reading: what if we begin to withdraw ourselves from the company of people
- *consuetudini coeperimus excerpere*: The verb is “to pick out, to remove”, with the speakers themselves as its object. The tense is future perfect, literally “shall have begun”, given here as a plain present. Other reading: what if we shall have begun to pick ourselves out of the common habit of mankind
- *frons populo nostra conveniat*: The noun is literally the forehead or brow, and then the front or outward face of a thing; it is set against “inside”. “The people” could also be “the crowd” or “the public”. Other reading: let our front agree with the crowd

</details>

### 5.3

Let the toga not gleam, nor be dirty either. Let us not own silver with engraved work of solid gold sunk into it, but let us not think it a sign of frugality to have gone without gold and silver. Let us make it our aim to follow a better life than the crowd, not a contrary one: otherwise we drive from us and turn away the people we want to see reformed. We also bring it about that they want to imitate nothing of ours, for fear that everything has to be imitated.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ne sordeat quidem*: The construction is “not … either”. The verb covers both being dirty and being shabby or mean-looking. Other reading: but let it not be shabby either
- *in quod solidi auri caelatura descenderit*: Literally “into which the engraving of solid gold has gone down”: silver plate with ornament in solid gold sunk or inlaid into it. The exact technique is not certain; “sunk into it” keeps the downward movement. Other reading: silver inlaid with reliefs of solid gold
- *auro argentoque caruisse*: A perfect infinitive, “to have gone without”. It may be only a general perfect with the force of “to go without”. Other reading: to do without gold and silver
- *frugalitatis indicium*: The first noun is wider than thrift: it is sober, plain living. “Frugality” is used here and in Ep. 5.5 so that the two sections match. Other reading: a sign of plain living
- *vulgus*: The common mass of people, with a slighting tone. “The crowd” is used here, and “the people” is kept for populus in the section before. Other reading: than the common run of people
- *fugamus a nobis et avertimus*: The first verb is “to put to flight”, not “to flee”. The second is “to turn away”, which could mean turning them from the speakers or turning them from the better life. Other reading: we frighten them off and put them against us
- *nihil imitari velint nostri*: The last word can be a genitive going with “nothing”, as translated, or a nominative plural that names the philosophers’ own people as the subject. The genitive is taken because the subject is the people to be reformed. Other reading: that our own people want to imitate nothing
- *dum timent*: Literally “while they fear”, with a shade of cause. “For fear that” gives that shade without a bare “because”. Other reading: while they fear that everything has to be imitated

</details>

### 5.5

Just as it is a mark of luxury to long for refined things, so it is a mark of madness to run from things that are in ordinary use and can be had at no great price. Philosophy demands frugality, not punishment; and there can be a frugality that is not unkempt. This is the mean I favour: let life be balanced between good conduct and the conduct of people in general; let everyone look up to our life, but let them find it recognisable.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *luxuriae est*: A genitive of characteristic: “it is the mark of”. The noun is self-indulgent, extravagant living; “luxury” is used here and again in Ep. 7.7. Other reading: is a mark of self-indulgence
- *delicatas res*: The adjective means choice, refined, pampering. The same word describes the self-indulgent housemate of Ep. 7.7. Other reading: dainty and costly things
- *non magno parabiles*: Literally “obtainable at no great”, with “price” understood. Other reading: cheap to get
- *non poenam*: The noun is a penalty or punishment. The section before spoke of torturing one’s own body, so self-punishment is meant, but the Latin says only “punishment”. Other reading: not self-punishment
- *non incompta frugalitas*: The adjective is used first of hair that is not dressed, then of anything left unadorned. The English keeps the Latin’s double negative. Other reading: frugality need not be slovenly
- *Hic mihi modus placet*: The noun can be a measure, a limit, a mean, or simply a manner. “Mean” is used because what follows is a balance between two things. Other reading: This is the way I think best
- *temperetur vita inter bonos mores et publicos*: The verb means to mix in due proportion. The noun, used once for both adjectives, covers ways, habits and morals: the life is to be blended between the ways of a good man and the ways of people at large. No possessive is expressed with “life”. Other reading: let our life be a blend of good morals and common morals
- *suspiciant omnes vitam nostram sed agnoscant*: The first verb is literally “look up at”, so admire. The second is to recognise something as known or as one’s own: people should find the life familiar, not alien. Other reading: let everyone admire our life, but let them recognise it

</details>

### 5.9

Wild animals run from the dangers they see, and when they have escaped they are free from care: we are tormented both by what is coming and by what is past. Many of our good things do us harm: for memory brings back the torment of fear, and foresight takes it ahead of time. No one is wretched from present things alone. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *cum effugere*: The second word is a short form of the perfect, “they have escaped”, not an infinitive. The Latin has no “and” before it; one is added to join the clauses. Other reading: once they have got away
- *securae sunt*: “Free from care” matches the glossary’s rendering of securitas. The word describes a state of mind, not safety. Other reading: they are untroubled
- *et venturo torquemur et praeterito*: The verb is from the same root as the noun “torment” in the next sentence, so “tormented” and “torment” keep the link. Both nouns are neuter singulars, “what is to come” and “what is past”. Other reading: we are tortured by the future and by the past alike
- *Multa bona nostra*: Bona is the plural of bonum, the glossary’s “the good”. “Good things” is used because “goods” alone reads as possessions; what is meant is the advantages of being human, memory and foresight, and the section before calls foresight the greatest good of the human condition. Other reading: Many of our blessings
- *providentia anticipat*: The noun is human foresight, not divine providence. The verb is “to take beforehand”: foresight takes the torment of fear before its time. Other reading: foresight anticipates it
- *nemo tantum praesentibus miser est*: The second word here means “only”. The ablative gives the cause and is taken as neuter (“present things”); “present troubles” could be understood, less probably. Other reading: no one is made wretched only by what is present

</details>

## Letter 6: On sharing wisdom between friends

### 6.5

So I will send you the books themselves, and so that you do not spend much effort chasing here and there after what will be of use, I will put marks in them, so that you can go straight to the very things I approve of and admire. Yet both the living voice and a shared life will do you more good than a set discourse. You ought to come to the thing itself, on the spot, first because people trust their eyes more than their ears, and then because the road is long by way of precepts, short and effective by way of examples.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ipsos tibi libros*: “The books themselves”, as against a report or extracts from them. The section does not say which books. Other reading: the actual books
- *dum passim profutura sectaris*: Literally “while you chase here and there after things that will be of use”. The clause is folded into “chasing”. Other reading: while you hunt everywhere for the useful parts
- *imponam notas*: The noun is most likely marks or signs set against passages. It could also mean written notes or comments. Other reading: I will add notes
- *viva vox*: A set phrase, “the living voice”, for teaching heard in person as against reading. It is kept literally. Other reading: the spoken word
- *convictus*: Living with someone: daily company, especially sharing a table. “A shared life” is used, which keeps the echo of “living” that this word has with “living voice” in the Latin. Other reading: daily companionship
- *quam oratio proderit*: The noun is speech or a composed discourse. Set against the living voice, and following the sending of books, it seems to mean discourse on the page, but the Latin does not say “written”; “set” marks it as composed. Other reading: than the written word
- *in rem praesentem venias oportet*: A legal idiom: to go to the very spot where the thing in dispute is, so as to judge it at first hand. The English keeps both “the thing” and “the spot”; seeing is left to the next clause, as in the Latin. Other reading: you must come and see for yourself on the spot
- *per praecepta*: Precepts are rules or instructions given in words, set against examples, which are patterns seen in a person. “Precepts” and “examples” are kept as a fixed pair. Other reading: by way of instructions

</details>

## Letter 7: On avoiding the crowd

### 7.7

A single example of luxury or of greed does a great deal of harm: a self-indulgent housemate little by little weakens and softens, a rich neighbour stirs up craving, a spiteful companion rubs his own rust off on a man however spotless and straightforward. What do you think happens to a character on which the attack has been made by the people as a body?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *luxuriae aut avaritiae*: “Luxury” is used as in Ep. 5.5, and “greed” for the love of money. Other reading: of extravagance or of avarice
- *convictor delicatus*: The noun is someone who lives with you day to day and shares your table. The adjective is “pampered, self-indulgent”, the same word as the “refined things” of Ep. 5.5. Other reading: a pampered companion at table
- *enervat*: Literally “takes out the sinews”. The two verbs have no object in the Latin, and none is supplied. Other reading: unstrings and softens us
- *cupiditatem irritat*: The noun is the general word for desire, here the wish to have what the rich neighbour has, which answers the greed named at the start. “Craving” is used so that “greed” stays for avaritia, a different word. Other reading: stirs up greed
- *malignus comes*: The adjective can be “ill-natured, spiteful” or “grudging, mean”. The English takes the first. Other reading: a mean-spirited companion
- *quamvis candido et simplici*: The first adjective is “shining white”, and of character “frank, clean”. “Spotless” is chosen so that the image of rust on a clean surface holds; “a man” is supplied. Other reading: on even the most honest and straightforward person
- *rubiginem suam affricuit*: The noun is rust, and by extension blight or ill will. The verb is a perfect stating a general truth among present tenses, given here as a present. Other reading: has rubbed his own rust onto him
- *his moribus*: The noun is character or morals. “These” is vague: it may mean such a character as was just described, or the characters of people like Seneca and Lucilius; the English reads “a character”. Other reading: to those morals of ours
- *in quos publice factus est impetus*: The adverb can mean “by the people as a body” or “in public, openly”. The English takes the first, which sets the whole people against the single example at the start; “attack” picks up the “attack of the vices” in the section before. Other reading: on which the attack has been made openly

</details>

### 7.8

You are bound either to imitate or to hate. But both are to be avoided: do not become like the bad because they are many, and do not become an enemy to many because they are unlike you. Withdraw into yourself as far as you can; keep company with those who will make you better, and let in those whom you in your turn can make better. This works both ways, and people learn while they teach.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *aut imiteris aut oderis*: Neither verb has an object in the Latin; the crowd of the section before is understood. The English leaves the object out as the Latin does. Other reading: You must either copy them or hate them
- *neve similis malis fias, quia multi sunt*: The “because” clause gives the reason one might be tempted, not Seneca’s reason for the warning. English leaves this as open as the Latin does. Other reading: do not grow like the bad just because there are many of them
- *neve inimicus multis*: The Latin plays on “many” twice: the bad are many, and many are unlike. “To many” could also be heard as “to the many”, the same crowd; “you” after “unlike” is supplied. Other reading: nor an enemy to the many because they are different
- *Recede in te ipse*: The last word is nominative and stresses the subject, “you yourself”. English cannot easily carry it beside “into yourself”, so it is not separately rendered. Other reading: Withdraw, you yourself, into yourself
- *cum his versare*: The verb form is an imperative of the deponent verb “to move among, to spend time with”, not an infinitive. Other reading: spend your time with those
- *illos admitte*: The verb is used of letting visitors into one’s house or presence, and “let in” keeps that. Other reading: welcome those
- *quos tu potes facere meliores*: The pronoun “you” is expressed in the Latin, which it need not be, and sets this clause against the one before. “In your turn” carries that stress. Other reading: those whom you can make better
- *Mutuo ista fiunt*: Literally “these things are done mutually”: the making better goes both ways. Other reading: These things are done in both directions
- *homines dum docent discunt*: This has the ring of a proverb and is often cited as one. No earlier source for it is known here (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: men learn by teaching

</details>

### 7.11

That man too put it well, whoever he was (for it is disputed who said it): when he was asked what was the aim of such great care over an art that would reach very few, he said, “A few are enough for me, one is enough, none is enough.” Epicurus put this third one excellently, when he was writing to one of the partners in his studies: “I write these things,” he said, “not for many, but for you; for we are a large enough theatre, each for the other.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Bene et ille*: The Latin has no verb: “well, that man too”. “Put it” is supplied. “Too” looks back to the saying of Democritus quoted in the section before. Other reading: Well said, too, by that man
- *ambigitur enim de auctore*: The Latin sets this aside between dashes; the English uses brackets. The noun is the author or originator of the saying. Other reading: for the author is uncertain
- *quo tanta diligentia artis spectaret ad paucissimos perventurae*: Literally “where such great care over an art that would reach very few was looking”. The noun can be an art, a craft or a skill; the future participle goes with “art”, not with “care”. Other reading: what was the point of taking such pains over a craft that only a handful would ever come to know
- *satis est nullus*: Literally “no one is enough”. The paradox is in the Latin and is meant. Other reading: not even one is enough
- *Egregie hoc tertium Epicurus*: Again there is no verb: “excellently, this third one, Epicurus”; “put” is supplied, as with “well” above. It is the third of the three sayings promised in the section before. It survives as a fragment of Epicurus, known perhaps chiefly from this passage (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: This third saying, an excellent one, is Epicurus’s
- *consortibus studiorum suorum*: The noun is someone who shares a lot or a property with you, so a partner. The studies are his philosophical pursuits. Other reading: one of his fellow students
- *ego non multis, sed tibi*: No verb is expressed; “write” is supplied from the context of a letter. “These things” keeps the plural of the Latin, and “not for many” could also be “not for the many”. Other reading: these things are meant not for the many, but for you
- *satis enim magnum alter alteri theatrum sumus*: The noun is a theatre, and so the audience that fills one. The image is kept as “theatre”. Other reading: for each of us is audience enough for the other

</details>

### 7.12

These things, my Lucilius, are to be stored away in your mind, so that you may despise the pleasure that comes from the approval of the greater number. Many praise you: have you any reason to be pleased with yourself, if you are the kind of man whom many understand? Let your good things face inward. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *condenda in animum sunt*: The verb is used of laying up stores. “These things” are the three sayings just quoted in the letter. Other reading: must be laid up in your heart
- *ex plurium assensione*: The first word is a comparative, “of more people”: the greater number, the majority, or simply a good many. “The greater number” is kept so that it stays distinct from the “many” that follows, a different Latin word. The noun is assent or approval, including applause. Other reading: from the applause of the many
- *ecquid habes cur placeas tibi*: The opening word marks an open question, “have you anything”, with a hint that the answer is no. The phrase “to please oneself” means to be satisfied with oneself. Other reading: what reason have you to be satisfied with yourself
- *si is es quem intellegant multi*: The subjunctive describes a type: “the kind of man whom many understand”. Being understood by many is treated as a bad sign. Other reading: if you are a man the many can understand
- *introrsus bona tua spectent*: “Your good things” is taken as the subject of the verb “face, look”, as a house is said to face a direction: they should be turned inward, not toward an audience. The words could also mean “let them look at your good things within”, with people as the subject. The input has a stray space before the question mark and a small letter after it; a new sentence is read here. Other reading: let them look at your good things on the inside

</details>

## Letter 9: On self-sufficiency and why the wise want friends

### 9.13

“The wise man is content with himself.” Most people, my dear Lucilius, interpret this wrongly: they push the wise man back on every side and force him inside his own skin. But a distinction must be drawn about what that saying promises and how far. The wise man is content with himself for living happily, not for living, since for living he has use for many things, and for living happily he has use only for a mind that is sound and held high and looks down on fortune.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Se contentus est sapiens*: The Stoic claim that the wise man is enough for himself. “Contentus” means both satisfied and contained within. “Content with himself” is kept so that the same words can return in the next two sections; it means that he is enough for himself, not that he is pleased with himself. Other reading: The wise man is self-sufficient.
- *mi Lucili*: A plain affectionate address. The English uses the ordinary form of address in a letter of today, which adds “dear”. Other reading: my Lucilius
- *sapientem undique submovent*: “Submovere” is to make someone move back, as a crowd is cleared out of the way, and “undique” is from or on every side. The English has him pressed back from every direction, which the image of the skin then completes. The words can also be read as removing him from everything around him. Other reading: they cut the wise man off from everything
- *quid et quatenus vox ista promittat*: “Vox” is a saying or phrase, and “promittat” is what it holds out or undertakes. “Promises” is kept. Other reading: what that phrase claims, and within what limits
- *ad hoc enim multis illi rebus opus est, ad illud tantum*: “Hoc” points to the nearer term (simply living) and “illud” to the further one (living happily). The English names each instead of writing “the latter” and “the former”, and gives “enim” as “since”. Other reading: for the latter he has use for many things, for the former only for
- *multis illi rebus opus est*: “Opus est” is “has use for” here and in 9.14 and 9.15, because 9.14 sets “opus est” against “egere” and ties it to knowing how to use things. “Needs” would be more natural in this sentence alone, but in 9.14 it would run into “nothing is necessary to the wise man”. Other reading: he needs many things
- *animo sano et erecto et despiciente fortunam*: “Erecto” is raised up, unbowed, and can also mean alert; “held high” is used because “upright” would be heard as honest. “Despiciente” is literally looking down on. Fortune has no capital, since nothing here makes it a person. Other reading: a mind that is healthy, alert and scornful of fortune

</details>

### 9.14

I want to point out to you Chrysippus’s distinction as well. He says that the wise man is in want of nothing, and yet has use for many things: “on the other hand there is nothing the fool has use for, since he does not know how to use anything, but he is in want of everything.” The wise man has use for hands and eyes and many things necessary for daily use; he is in want of nothing. For to be in want is a matter of necessity; nothing is necessary to the wise man.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Chrysippi quoque distinctionem*: Chrysippus was the third head of the Stoic school, in the third century BC (from memory, unchecked). “Quoque” adds his distinction to the one drawn in 9.13. Other reading: a distinction of Chrysippus too
- *sapientem nulla re egere, et tamen multis illi rebus opus esse*: The whole section rests on two Latin expressions for need: “egere” (to be in want, to feel a lack) and “opus est” (there is use or occasion for). “Is in want of” is kept for the first and “has use for” for the second throughout 9.13 to 9.15. English “need” would fit either and so blur the distinction. Other reading: the wise man lacks nothing, and yet needs many things
- *stulto nulla re opus est*: Literally, for the fool there is use for nothing. The English turns it as “there is nothing the fool has use for” so that it is not heard as the idiom “has no use for”, which today means scorns. Other reading: the fool needs nothing
- *nulla enim re uti scit*: This parenthesis explains why the fool has no “opus”: he cannot use anything. “Opus est”, “uti” here and “usum” below are different Latin words that all become “use” in the English; the link is in the thought and is kept on purpose. The Latin sets the words off as a parenthesis; the English uses commas. Other reading: since he knows how to make use of nothing
- *sed omnibus eget*: The quotation marks are the editor’s. It is open whether the words of Chrysippus end here or whether the next sentence about hands and eyes is still his; the English follows the marks as given. Other reading: but he lacks everything
- *multis ad cotidianum usum necessariis*: Seneca calls these things “necessary” for daily use and then says nothing is necessary to the wise man. The tension is in the Latin and is not softened. Other reading: many things required for everyday use
- *egere enim necessitatis est, nihil necesse sapienti est*: The genitive says that being in want belongs to, or is a mark of, necessity, which can also mean compulsion. “Necessity” and “necessary” are kept so that the echo stays. Other reading: for to be in want is to be under compulsion, and nothing compels the wise man

</details>

### 9.15

So, although he is content with himself, he has use for friends. He desires to have as many of them as possible, not in order to live happily, for he will live happily even without friends. The highest good does not look for equipment from outside; it is cultivated at home, it comes wholly from itself. It begins to be subject to fortune if it looks for any part of itself outside the house.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *amicis illi opus est*: The same “opus est” as in 9.13 and 9.14, so the same English. “Has use for friends” sounds more calculating in English than the Latin does; the next clause corrects it. Other reading: he needs friends
- *hos cupit habere quam plurimos*: “Cupit” is a strong word for wishing. “Desires” is used, not “wants”, so that it does not collide with “is in want of” (egere) in the section before. Other reading: he wants to have as many of them as he can
- *extrinsecus instrumenta non quaerit*: “Instrumenta” are tools, gear or means. The English has “equipment”. Other reading: does not seek tools from outside
- *domi colitur*: “Colitur” can mean is tended or cultivated, or is worshipped, as a household god is worshipped at home. The English takes the first and keeps “at home” as the image. Other reading: it is worshipped at home
- *ex se totum est*: Either it derives entirely from itself or it is a whole made of itself. The English takes the first, which answers “from outside” just before. Other reading: it is complete in itself
- *si quam partem sui foris quaerit*: “Foris” is outside the house and answers “domi”, at home. “Outside the house” keeps the image; “out of doors” would be heard today as in the open air. Other reading: if it looks for any part of itself outside

</details>

### 9.22

“What then?” you say. “If that man who is rich to his shame calls himself happy, and that one who is master of many but slave of more, will he become happy by what he himself thinks?” What matters is not what he says but what he thinks, and not what he thinks on one day but what he thinks constantly. But there is no reason for you to fear that so great a thing may come to someone unworthy: what is his own pleases no one but the wise man; all folly suffers from disgust with itself. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ille turpiter dives et ille multorum dominus sed plurium servus*: The repeated “ille” points to two familiar types. “Turpiter dives” may mean rich by shameful means or rich in a shameful way; “rich to his shame” leaves both open and cannot be heard as merely very rich. “Multorum” and “plurium” can be people or things (slaves owned, or masters and passions served); “many” and “more” are left as open as the Latin. Other reading: the man whose wealth was basely won, and the man who owns many slaves but is a slave to more masters
- *beatus sua sententia fiet*: “Sententia” is an opinion held or delivered, a vote or a verdict, and “sentiat” in the reply is from the same root. The English gives both with “think” so that the reply can be heard to answer the question. Other reading: will he become happy by his own verdict?
- *Non quid dicat sed quid sentiat refert*: “Sentire” covers both to feel and to think or hold a view. “Thinks” is used all three times: the line of comedy quoted just before this section is about what a man thinks himself to be, and “feels” would suggest a passing mood. Other reading: What matters is not what he says but what he feels
- *res tanta*: The great thing is being happy, carried over from the line of comedy quoted just before this section. Other reading: so great a prize
- *nisi sapienti sua non placent*: “Sua” is neuter plural: his own things, which may be his possessions, his condition or his lot. The English keeps it as wide as the Latin. Other reading: no one but the wise man is satisfied with what he has
- *omnis stultitia laborat fastidio sui*: “Laborat” is to be sick with something. “Fastidium” ranges from squeamish distaste to weariness. “Sui” is its object: disgust at itself. Other reading: all folly is sick with weariness of itself

</details>

## Letter 10: On being alone with yourself

### 10.5

But, to send this letter with some small gift as is my habit, what I found in Athenodorus is true: “Know that you are released from all desires at the point when you have come to ask god for nothing except what you can ask for openly.” For as things are, how great is the madness of people! They whisper the most shameful prayers to the gods; if anyone brings his ear close, they will fall silent, and what they do not want a human being to know they tell to god. See, then, whether this might not be a wholesome precept to give: live with people just as if god saw, speak with god just as if people heard. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *cum aliquo munusculo*: A diminutive: a little present. In these early letters Seneca usually closes with a saying borrowed from another writer, and that is the gift. Other reading: with some little present
- *apud Athenodorum*: A Stoic philosopher. More than one Athenodorus of Tarsus is known, and which is meant is not clear (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: in the writings of Athenodorus
- *verum est quod apud Athenodorum inveni*: The English keeps the literal statement that what was found in Athenodorus is true. It could be smoothed into an introduction of the saying. Other reading: here is a true saying that I found in Athenodorus
- *omnibus cupiditatibus solutum*: “Solutum” is loosed or untied, as from bonds. “Cupiditates” are desires or cravings. Other reading: set free from all cravings
- *nihil deum roges*: The singular “deum” can be god or a god. The passage moves between the singular and the plural “dis”; each is kept as it stands, with “god” in lower case as the glossary has it. Other reading: you ask a god for nothing
- *turpissima vota dis insusurrant*: “Vota” are vows or wishes addressed to a god, so prayers in the sense of requests. “Turpissima” is most shameful or most foul. Other reading: they whisper the foulest wishes into the gods’ ears
- *quod scire hominem nolunt deo narrant*: “Homo” is a human being as against a god. The English has “human being” here and “people” for the plural, not “man” and “men”. Other reading: what they do not want a man to know they tell to a god
- *Vide ergo ne hoc praecipi salubriter possit*: “Vide ne” with the subjunctive can mean “take care that it does not” or, as a polite way of proposing something, “consider whether it does not”. The English takes the second, since Seneca goes on to give the precept. Other reading: So take care that this cannot be laid down as a healthy rule
- *sic vive cum hominibus tamquam deus videat*: “Sic” answers “tamquam” in both halves of the precept: in such a way, as if. English “as if” does most of that work, and “just” is added to carry the repeated “sic”. “Videat” and “audiant” are plain seeing and hearing, so “saw” and “heard”. Other reading: live with people in such a way as if god were watching, speak with god in such a way as if people were listening

</details>

## Letter 11: On blushing and choosing a role model

### 11.8

Now the letter calls for its close. Take one, and a useful and wholesome one at that, which I want you to fix in your mind: “We must hold some good man dear and keep him always before our eyes, so that we live just as if he were looking on and do everything as if he saw it.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *clausulam*: A term from rhetoric for the close of a sentence or speech. Here it is the closing saying Seneca habitually adds to a letter. Other reading: its closing sentence
- *Accipe, et quidem utilem ac salutarem*: “Accipe” is “take” or “receive”, with “clausulam” understood, so the English supplies “one”. “Salutarem” is giving health or saving; the English has “wholesome”. Other reading: Here it is, and a useful and salutary one too
- *affigere animo*: Literally to fasten or nail to the mind. Other reading: to fasten to your mind
- *aliquis vir bonus nobis diligendus est*: The saying is that of Epicurus, as the next section says. “Diligere” is to esteem and love; its root sense is to pick out, and 11.10 goes on to say “choose” (elige). Some editors may print “eligendus” here (from memory, unchecked); the English translates the word as given, with “hold dear”. Other reading: We must choose some good man
- *tamquam illo spectante vivamus et omnia tamquam illo vidente faciamus*: Two different verbs, “spectare” (to watch, look on) and “videre” (to see), are kept distinct. “Just” carries the “sic” that stands before the first “tamquam”, as in the similar precept at Ep. 10.5. Other reading: so that we live as if he were watching and do everything as if he were looking at us

</details>

### 11.9

This, my dear Lucilius, is Epicurus’s precept. He gave us a guardian and a minder, and not without reason: a great part of wrongdoing is removed if a witness stands beside those who are about to do wrong. Let the mind have someone to revere, by whose authority it may make even its privacy purer. How fortunate is the man who corrects not only when he is present but even when he is thought of! How fortunate is the man who can revere someone in such a way that he composes and orders himself even at the memory of him! One who can revere someone in this way will soon be someone to be revered.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Epicurus praecepit*: Literally Epicurus prescribed this, or gave this as a precept. The verb is turned into a noun to match “precept” in Ep. 10.5. Other reading: This is what Epicurus taught
- *mi Lucili*: A plain affectionate address, given in the ordinary English form, which adds “dear”, as at Ep. 9.13. Other reading: my Lucilius
- *custodem nobis et paedagogum dedit*: A “paedagogus” was the slave who escorted a boy to school and watched over his behaviour, not a teacher. “Tutor” would mislead, so the English has “minder”, the plain word for someone who looks after a child. Other reading: he gave us a guardian and a tutor
- *magna pars peccatorum tollitur, si peccaturis testis assistit*: The same word appears twice (“peccatorum”, “peccaturis”), so “wrongdoing” and “do wrong” are kept. “Sins” would bring in a Christian tone. Other reading: a great share of sins is done away with if a witness stands by those about to sin
- *quem vereatur*: “Vereri” is respect tinged with awe or fear, which is why a witness deters. The word comes four times and ends in “verendus”, so one English word, “revere”, is kept throughout. Other reading: someone to stand in awe of
- *etiam secretum suum sanctius faciat*: “Secretum” is a place or state of being alone and unobserved, or the hidden inner part. “Sanctius” is purer, more blameless, or more holy and inviolable; the English takes the moral sense. Other reading: it may make even its solitude more holy
- *O felicem illum*: “Felix” is lucky or blessed by fortune. “Fortunate” keeps “happy” for “beatus”, as the glossary has it. Other reading: O happy the man
- *sed etiam cogitatus emendat*: “Emendat” (corrects, makes better) has no object in the Latin, and the English leaves it without one. Those corrected are the people who have him before them or think of him. Other reading: but even when he is only in our thoughts makes us better
- *ad memoriam quoque eius se componat atque ordinet*: “Ad memoriam” may be “at the memory of him” or “by the standard of his memory”, as “ad regulam” is used in 11.10. The English takes the first. Other reading: that he shapes and orders himself even by the memory of him

</details>

### 11.10

So choose Cato. If he seems to you too stiff, choose Laelius, a man of a more relaxed mind. Choose the man whose life and speech and very face, which carries his mind in plain view, have pleased you. Show him to yourself always, either as guardian or as example. Someone is needed, I say, against whom our character may measure itself: you will not straighten what is crooked except against a ruler. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Elige itaque Catonem*: Seneca does not say which Cato. It may be the younger Cato, the Stoic who died at Utica and whom Seneca often holds up, or the elder Cato the Censor, who is nearer in time to Laelius (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: So choose a Cato
- *nimis rigidus*: “Rigidus” is stiff, unbending or stern. The physical word is kept because it is set against “relaxed” (remissioris). Other reading: too stern
- *remissioris animi virum Laelium*: Gaius Laelius, the friend of Scipio Aemilianus, known as “the Wise” (from memory, unchecked). “Animus” here is disposition or temper; the English keeps the glossary’s “mind”, so that the word is the same as in the next sentence. Other reading: Laelius, a man of gentler temper
- *ipse animum ante se ferens vultus*: Literally the face itself carrying the mind in front of it. “Ante se ferre” is an idiom for showing openly; “carries his mind in plain view” keeps the carrying and gives the sense. Other reading: the face itself, which shows his mind openly
- *Opus est, inquam, aliquo*: The same “opus est” that is “has use for” in Ep. 9.13 to 9.15. There is no contrast with “egere” here, so the English has plain “is needed”, and it keeps the impersonal form of the Latin. “Inquam” marks a point being repeated. Other reading: There is use, I repeat, for someone
- *ad quem mores nostri se ipsi exigant*: “Exigere ad” is to test or measure against a standard. “Mores” is character, habits or conduct. “Se ipsi” stresses that the character does the measuring itself. Other reading: by whom our habits may themselves be tested
- *nisi ad regulam prava non corriges*: A play on words. “Regula” is a straight edge and also a rule. “Prava” is crooked and also morally wrong. “Corrigere” is to straighten and also to correct; the English keeps the carpenter’s image. Other reading: you will not correct what is wrong except by a rule

</details>

## Letter 12: On old age and living each day fully

### 12.4

I owe this to my country place near the city: my old age showed itself to me wherever I turned. Let us embrace it and love it; it is full of pleasure, if you know how to use it. Fruit is most welcome when it is slipping away; the beauty of boyhood is greatest at its close; those given over to wine are delighted by the last drink, the one that sinks them, the one that puts the finishing touch to drunkenness;

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *suburbano meo*: A “suburbanum” is an estate or house in the country close to Rome. “Suburban” would mislead today. Other reading: my suburban villa
- *quocumque adverteram*: A pluperfect with no object expressed (eyes or attention is understood). The English uses the simple past. Other reading: wherever I had turned my eyes
- *si illa scias uti*: A general “you” in the subjunctive (if one knows). The second person is kept as the Latin has it. Other reading: if one knows how to use it
- *Gratissima sunt poma cum fugiunt*: Literally “when they are fleeing”: the fruit is nearly over for the year, or is just going over. “Slipping away” keeps the image of flight without speaking of supply. Other reading: Fruit is most welcome when it is fleeing
- *pueritiae maximus in exitu decor est*: “Decor” is grace, charm or good looks. “In exitu” is as boyhood goes out. Other reading: boyhood has its greatest charm as it ends
- *illa quae mergit*: “Mergit” is plunges under or drowns. The object (the drinkers) is understood. Other reading: the one that drowns them
- *ebrietati summam manum imponit*: An idiom from the workshop: to put the last hand to a work is to finish it off. Other reading: that lays the last hand on drunkenness
- *imponit;*: The section as given ends on a semicolon. The sentence runs on into section 5, which is not in the input, so the English ends on a semicolon too and is not rounded off. Other reading: the same words closed with a full stop

</details>

### 12.8

So every day must be ordered as if it were bringing up the rear of the column, and were completing and filling out life. Pacuvius, who made Syria his own by right of possession, whenever he had made the offerings for the dead to himself, with wine and those funeral feasts, used to be carried from dinner to his bedroom in such a way that, amid the applause of his kept men, this was sung to music: “Life has been lived, life has been lived.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *tamquam cogat agmen*: A military idiom: “agmen cogere” is to close the marching column, to be the rearguard. The day is pictured as the last in the line of days. Other reading: as if it closed the series
- *consummet atque expleat vitam*: “Consummare” is to add up to a total and so to finish. “Explere” is to fill up to the full measure. Other reading: and rounded off and made up the full sum of life
- *qui Syriam usu suam fecit*: A legal joke. “Usus” is the continued possession by which a thing becomes one’s property, so “by right of possession”. Pacuvius is thought to have run Syria for years under Tiberius as deputy for a governor kept at Rome (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: who made Syria his own by long use
- *cum vino et illis funebribus epulis sibi parentaverat*: “Cum” with the pluperfect indicative marks a repeated act, hence “whenever”. “Parentare” is to make the yearly offerings to one’s dead; “sibi” says he made them to himself, as if already dead. “Illis” may mean the customary funeral feasts or those notorious feasts of his. Other reading: each time he had sacrificed to his own ghost with wine and the usual funeral banquet
- *inter plausus exoletorum*: “Exoleti” were male slaves past boyhood kept for sex. “Kept men” is the nearest plain English, though it does not say that they were slaves. Other reading: amid the clapping of his male prostitutes
- *ad symphoniam*: “Symphonia” is a band of musicians or their playing: to an accompaniment. Other reading: to the accompaniment of a band
- *bebiotai, bebiotai*: The chant is Greek, written in Latin letters in this copy, and the English translates it. It is an impersonal perfect passive, literally “it has been lived”, that is, the life is over. “Life has been lived” keeps the impersonal form and the verb “live”, so that the link to “vixi” in the next section is plain. Other reading: He has lived, he has lived.

</details>

### 12.9

There was no day on which he did not carry himself out for burial. What he used to do from a bad conscience let us do from a good one, and as we go to sleep let us say, glad and cheerful: “I have lived, and I have finished the course that Fortune had given.” If god adds a tomorrow, let us receive it gladly. The happiest man, and the untroubled owner of himself, is the one who waits for tomorrow without anxiety; whoever has said “I have lived” rises every day to a gain.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nullo non se die extulit*: “Efferre” is the usual verb for carrying a body out to burial. The double negative “nullo non die” means on every single day. “Se efferre” can also mean to exalt oneself, which may be a second sense heard underneath. Other reading: Every single day he held his own funeral.
- *ex mala conscientia*: “Conscientia” is awareness of what one has done, so conscience. The pair bad and good is kept as in the Latin. Other reading: out of a guilty awareness of himself
- *vixi et quem dederat cursum fortuna peregi*: A line of Vergil: the words of Dido before her death, Aeneid 4.653 (from memory, unchecked). This copy does not mark it as a quotation; the English puts it in quotation marks as the words to be said. Fortune has a capital because she is spoken of here as a giver. Other reading: I have lived, and I have run the course that fortune had assigned
- *Crastinum si adiecerit deus*: “Deus” is singular: god or a god. “Adiecerit” is throws in or adds, as an extra. Other reading: If a god throws in tomorrow as well
- *securus sui possessor*: “Sui” is taken with “possessor” (owner of himself) and “securus” as describing that owner: untroubled, secure. The glossary’s “free from care” is not used here because it cannot stand before “owner” without becoming unclear. “Securus sui” could also be read together, as unworried about himself. Other reading: and is untroubled about himself, his own master
- *cotidie ad lucrum surgit*: “Cotidie” (every day) can go with “rises” or with “has said”; the English takes it with “rises”. “Lucrum” is a term from accounts: each further day is entered as gain over and above what was counted on. Other reading: whoever has said “I have lived” every day gets up to pure gain

</details>

## Letter 13: On needless fears about the future

### 13.2

An athlete who has never been beaten black and blue cannot bring great courage to the contest. The one who has seen his own blood, whose teeth have rattled under a fist, the one who has been tripped and has taken his opponent on the whole of his body, who, thrown down, has not thrown away his spirit, who has got up more defiant every time he has fallen: he goes down to the fight with great hope.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *magnos spiritus*: Literally “great breaths”, that is, high mettle or confidence. “Great” is used here and again for magna spe at the end so that the echo can be heard, and “courage” keeps this word apart from animum, which is “spirit” later in the sentence. Other reading: high spirits; great confidence
- *suggillatus*: The word means bruised by blows, with a second sense of being insulted or jeered at. The English takes the physical sense, which the rest of the sentence supports. Other reading: who has never been bruised; who has never been jeered at
- *dentes crepuere sub pugno*: The verb is a sound word: to rattle, crack or clatter. “Rattled” leaves open whether the teeth broke. Other reading: whose teeth have cracked under a fist
- *subplantatus ad versarium toto tulit corpore*: The copy splits one word in two: the second and third words here are read as a single word meaning “opponent”. The phrase is literally “bore his opponent with whole body”. The English takes the body as the athlete’s own, flat on the ground under the other man; it can also be the opponent’s, coming down with all his weight. Other reading: who has been tripped and has taken his opponent’s full weight on his body
- *nec proiecit animum proiectus*: A play on one verb: proiectus is thrown to the ground and proiecit is threw away, gave up. “Thrown down” and “thrown away” keep the echo. Animum is “spirit” here, as the glossary allows where the sense is courage. Other reading: who, flung to the ground, did not give up his spirit
- *descendit ad pugnam*: The verb form can be present or perfect; the English takes it as a general present. “Goes down” is the set phrase for entering the arena or the ring, and the literal verb is kept. Other reading: he has gone down to the fight; he enters the fight

</details>

### 13.5

So some things torment us more than they ought, some torment us before they ought, some torment us when they ought not to torment us at all; we either increase our pain, or anticipate it, or invent it. The first of these, since the matter is in dispute and we have a case formally before the court, should be put off for the present. What I call slight you will insist is very grave; I know that some people laugh under the lash and others groan at a blow of the fist. Later we will see whether those things are strong by their own strength or by our weakness.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *torquent*: The word is the one for torture on the rack. It comes three times, and “torment” is used each time so that the threefold division can be followed. Other reading: torture us
- *aut augemus dolorem aut praecipimus aut fingimus*: The three verbs answer the three kinds just listed. Praecipimus is literally “take beforehand”, here “anticipate”. Dolorem is “pain” by the glossary, though here it covers distress of mind as well as of body. Other reading: we either enlarge our distress, or take it in advance, or make it up
- *Primum illud*: “That first thing” is the first of the three kinds: the things that torment more than they ought. “The first of these” makes the reference plain. Other reading: That first point
- *litem contestatam habemus*: A term of Roman court procedure: the stage at which both parties have formally stated the issue before a judge. Seneca jokes that he and Lucilius are parties to a pending lawsuit on this question. The English gives the sense in plain words. Other reading: we have a suit in which issue has been joined
- *leve dixero tu gravissimum*: Literally light and heavy, in the sense of slight and serious; “slight” and “grave” are used because English does not call a suffering light or heavy in this way. The first verb is a future perfect, and the superlative can be “very grave” or “gravest”. Other reading: What I call light you will maintain is the heaviest of all
- *inter flagella*: Literally “among whips”, that is, while being flogged. “Under the lash” is the English phrase for it. Other reading: some people laugh among the whips
- *sub colapho*: A blow with the fist or a box on the ear, a lesser thing than the whips just mentioned. Other reading: at a punch; at a slap
- *suis viribus valeant an imbecillitate nostra*: The Latin sets being strong by one’s own forces against being strong by another’s weakness. The paradox of the second is kept. Other reading: whether those things owe their power to their own strength or to our weakness

</details>

### 13.6

Do this for me: whenever people stand around you trying to persuade you that you are wretched, think not about what you hear but about what you feel, and take counsel with your own power to endure, and be the one to question yourself, you who know your own affairs best: “Why is it that these people are lamenting over me? Why is it that they are alarmed, that they even fear infection from me, as if misfortune could jump across? Is there any evil in that, or is that thing ill spoken of rather than evil?” Be the one to question yourself: “Am I perhaps in torment and grieving without cause, and making an evil of what is not one?”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Illud praesta mihi*: The verb means to make good, guarantee or perform something for someone. The English takes the plain “do this for me”. Other reading: Guarantee me this
- *miserum*: The word covers both wretched and pitiable. The English takes “wretched”. Other reading: that you are miserable; that you are to be pitied
- *quid sentias*: The verb can mean what you feel (by sense) or what you think (your own view). Set against “what you hear”, it is taken as “feel”. Other reading: but about what you yourself think
- *cum patientia tua deliberes*: Literally “deliberate with your own patientia”. The noun can be the power to endure or the fact of undergoing something, so the sense is either to consult your own power to bear the thing or to consult what you are in fact going through. The English takes the first. Other reading: consult what you are yourself going through
- *te ipse interroges*: The pronoun ipse stresses that the questioner is to be Lucilius and not the bystanders. The same words come again in the command at the end, and “be the one to question yourself” is used both times. Other reading: and ask yourself
- *contagium quoque mei*: Literally contagion from the speaker’s own person: he is treated as a source of infection by touch, which prepares the image of misfortune jumping across. Other reading: that they even fear catching it from me
- *magis infamis est quam mala*: Infamis is “of ill repute”. “Evil” is kept for mala and for the word for evil just before and after, so that the one word runs through the passage. Other reading: is that thing more notorious than bad?
- *crucior*: The form is passive, of one who is tortured, and can also be used of one who tortures himself. “In torment” leaves both open, as the Latin does. The verb is not the one rendered “torment” in Ep. 13.5. Other reading: Am I perhaps tormenting myself and grieving without cause
- *quod non est malum facio*: Literally to make evil what is not. It can mean turning something into an evil or treating it as one; “making” is kept. Other reading: and treating as an evil what is not one

</details>

### 13.8

“But it is to come.” First look carefully at whether there are sure proofs of an evil to come. For most of the time we are troubled by suspicions, and we are made fools of by rumour, the thing that so often finishes a war, but finishes individuals far more. That is how it is, my Lucilius: we are quick to side with opinion. We do not refute the things that lead us into fear, nor do we search them, but we panic, and turn and run like those whom dust raised by a herd in flight has driven from their camp, or whom some tale spread about with no source has terrified.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *At enim futurum est*: An imagined objection from Lucilius. No subject is stated: “it” is the trouble he fears, which the next sentence names as an evil to come. The words can be a claim that it will happen or a reminder that it lies ahead; “it is to come” allows both. Other reading: “But it is going to happen.”
- *certa argumenta*: The noun covers proofs, evidence and signs. The English takes “sure proofs”. Other reading: reliable signs; definite evidence
- *conficere bellum solet fama, multo autem magis singulos conficit*: A play on one verb used twice: to finish (settle, decide) a war, and to finish off (wear out, destroy) a person. “Finishes” is used both times. “Far more” is a matter of degree in the Latin, not of frequency. Other reading: rumour, which often decides a war but much more surely destroys individuals
- *cito accedimus opinioni*: The verb is to go over to a side, and so to assent. “Opinion” follows the glossary: it is what is supposed or believed as against the fact, and it could be a person’s own unexamined belief or what people are saying. Other reading: we are quick to agree with what is commonly believed
- *non coarguimus illa quae nos in metum adducunt nec excutimus*: The first verb is a court word, to prove false or convict. The second is literally to shake out, as a garment is shaken out to see what is hidden in it, and so to search or examine; “search” gives the sense. Other reading: we do not show up as false the things that bring us into fear, nor shake them out
- *vertimus terga*: Literally to turn the back, the soldier’s phrase for running away. In English that phrase suggests ignoring something, so “turn and run” is used. Other reading: and turn our backs
- *pulvis motus fuga pecorum exuit castris*: The last two words are a soldier’s phrase, literally “stripped of the camp”: troops are made to abandon their camp and lose it. The picture is of soldiers who take a dust cloud raised by fleeing animals for an enemy. Pecorum is any herd or flock, cattle or sheep. Other reading: whom dust raised by cattle in flight has stripped of their camp
- *sine auctore*: Literally “without an author”: a story no one will answer for. “Source” is used here and for the same noun in Ep. 13.13. Other reading: with no one to vouch for it

</details>

### 13.13

So weigh hope and fear, and whenever everything is uncertain, favour yourself: believe what you prefer. If fear has more votes, lean rather to the other side all the same, and stop disturbing yourself, and keep turning this over in your mind: that the greater part of mortals, though they have nothing evil upon them and nothing evil that is certain to come, seethe and run this way and that. For no one resists himself once he has begun to be pushed, and no one brings his fear down to the truth; no one says “The source is worthless, worthless: he either made it up or believed it.” We give ourselves to the breeze to be carried; we take fright at doubtful things as though they were certain; we do not keep things in proportion: a slight unease at once turns into fear.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *examina*: The verb comes from the tongue of a balance, so “weigh” keeps the image. It can also mean simply “examine”. Other reading: So examine hope and fear
- *plures habebit sententias*: The image is of a jury or a senate voting: fear “will have more votes”. The noun can also mean opinions or considerations. The Latin verb is future; the English has a present after “if”. Other reading: If fear has more arguments on its side
- *in hanc partem*: The Latin says “to this side” and does not name it: it is the side just recommended, hope and what one would prefer to believe. Straight after fear’s votes, “this side” would read in English as fear’s, so the English has “the other side”. Other reading: lean rather to this side all the same
- *cum illi nec sit quicquam mali nec pro certo futurum sit*: The pronoun is singular, agreeing with “the greater part”; the English has a plural. The second half is literally “nor is going to be for certain”, and it can mean either that nothing evil is certain to come or that it is certain none will come. The English takes the first. Other reading: though nothing evil has befallen them and it is certain none will
- *aestuare ac discurrere*: The first verb is to seethe or boil, used of water and of an anxious mind; here it is agitation, not anger. The second is to run in different directions. Both images are kept. Other reading: are in a fever and rush about
- *redigit ad verum*: The verb can be “bring back to” or “bring down to, reduce to”. The English takes the second, which suits the talk of proportion at the end of the section. Other reading: and no one brings his fear back to the truth
- *vanus [est]*: The square brackets are the editor’s mark for a word he thinks should be deleted, and the second “is” is left out. The adjective is literally “empty”, and so idle or untrustworthy. “Source” is the same noun so rendered in Ep. 13.8. Other reading: “The man who reports it is empty, empty”
- *aut finxit aut credidit*: The second verb is bare: “he believed”. The sense is that he either invented the story or took it on trust from someone else. Other reading: either he invented it or he was taken in by it
- *Damus nos aurae ferendos*: The noun is a light wind, and is also used of a breath of rumour or popular favour. The plain image is kept. Other reading: We let ourselves be carried along by every breath of rumour
- *modum rerum*: Literally “the measure of things”: the true scale of each thing. “Keep things in proportion” is the English phrase for it. Other reading: we do not keep the measure of things
- *statim in timorem venit scrupulus*: The last word is literally a small sharp stone, such as one in a shoe, and so a nagging unease. The English “scruple” now means a moral qualm, so it is avoided, and the image of the stone is given up. Literally the unease “comes into fear”. Other reading: a pebble of doubt at once becomes a fear

</details>

### 13.14

I am ashamed to be talking with you like this, and to be reviving you with such mild remedies. Let someone else say “Perhaps it will not come.” As for you, say: “And what if it does come? We will see which of the two wins. Perhaps it is coming on my side, and that death will bring honour to my life.” Hemlock made Socrates great. Wrest from Cato the sword that championed freedom: you will have taken away a great part of his glory.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ibi*: This word, “there”, has nothing clear to point to in the opening sentence, and editors have doubted it (from memory, unchecked). The English leaves it untranslated and does not guess at a sense. Other reading: I am ashamed, at that point, to be talking with you like this
- *focilare*: The verb is to revive someone by warmth, to nurse back to life. “Reviving” is used; the word can carry a note of coddling. Other reading: to be nursing you with such gentle remedies
- *tu dic*: A command to Lucilius, set against what someone else may say. “As for you” is added so that “say” reads as a command and not as a statement. Other reading: You say:
- *uter vincat*: “Which of the two”: the speaker and the thing that is coming. Other reading: We will see which of us wins
- *pro me venit*: The verb form can be present (“comes”) or perfect (“has come”), and the phrase can be “on my side” or “for my benefit”. The English takes the present and “on my side”, which suits the contest in the clause before. Other reading: perhaps it has come for my benefit
- *mors ista vitam honestabit*: Only here does the passage say that the feared thing is death. The verb is from the family of the word the glossary renders “what is honourable”; “bring honour to” is used. “My” is supplied: the Latin says only “life”. Other reading: that death will make life honourable
- *Cicuta magnum Socratem fecit*: Socrates was put to death at Athens by being made to drink hemlock. Other reading: It was the hemlock that made Socrates great
- *gladium assertorem libertatis*: The middle word is a legal one: the person who formally lays claim to someone as free in court. “Championed” gives that sense of acting for another. Cato the Younger killed himself with his sword rather than submit to Caesar; the Latin says only “of freedom”, which can be his own or that of Rome. Other reading: the sword that made good his claim to freedom

</details>

## Letter 14: On caring for the body and staying safe

### 14.1

I admit that it is planted in us to hold our own body dear; I admit that we hold it in our guardianship. I do not deny that it should be indulged; I deny that we should be its slaves. For the man who is a slave to his body, who fears too much for it, who measures everything by it, will be a slave to many.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *caritatem*: The noun is “dearness”: affection, holding something precious. “Hold dear” is used so that the link with the word “dear” in the next section of the letter (Ep. 14.2) can be heard. Other reading: a fondness for our own body is planted in us
- *gerere tutelam*: A legal phrase: to act as guardian, as of a ward who cannot look after himself. The image is kept. Other reading: that we are charged with looking after it
- *indulgendum*: The verb is to be kind to, to make allowances for, to humour. “Indulged” is meant without the modern sense of spoiling. Other reading: that we should be kind to it
- *serviendum nego*: The verb is the one for being a slave, and it comes three times in the sentence; “slave” is used each time. The Latin is impersonal, and the English gives it the first person plural of the first sentence. Other reading: I deny that it should be served
- *multis enim serviet*: “Many” can be masculine (many masters, many people) or neuter (many things). The English leaves it as open as the Latin. Other reading: will be a slave to many masters; will be a slave to many things
- *ad illud omnia refert*: Literally “refers everything to it”: makes the body the standard by which everything is judged. Other reading: who refers everything to it

</details>

### 14.2

This is how we ought to conduct ourselves: not as though we ought to live for the sake of the body, but as though we could not live without the body. Too great a love of it unsettles us with fears, loads us with anxieties, exposes us to insults. What is honourable is cheap to the man to whom his body is too dear. Let it be cared for with the greatest diligence, but in such a way that, when reason demands it, when dignity demands it, when good faith demands it, it must be thrown into the flames.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Sic gerere nos debemus*: Literally “to carry ourselves thus”. “This is how” gives the “thus”, which the two “as though” clauses then explain. Other reading: We ought to conduct ourselves not as though
- *tamquam non possimus sine corpore*: The verb “live” is understood from the clause before and is supplied. Other reading: but as though we could not do without the body
- *contumeliis obicit*: The noun covers insults and humiliating treatment. The verb is to throw in the way of, to expose to. Other reading: lays us open to humiliations
- *honestum ei vile est cui corpus nimis carum est*: A play on price: vile is cheap and carum is dear, both of cost and of affection. English “cheap” and “dear” keep both senses, and “dear” answers “hold dear” in Ep. 14.1. Honestum is “what is honourable”, by the glossary. Other reading: the man who values his body too highly sets little value on what is honourable
- *cum dignitas, cum fides*: The first noun is standing or self-respect; the second is good faith, loyalty or one’s pledged word. The English takes “dignity” and “good faith”. The verb “demands” is given once in the Latin (in the future tense) and is repeated. Other reading: when self-respect, when loyalty demands it
- *mittendum in ignes sit*: Literally “it is to be sent into fires”, in the plural. The image is of giving the body up to burning, and it is kept as an image. Other reading: it must be sent into the fire

</details>

### 14.16

Finally, in everything the wise man looks to the intention, not the outcome. The beginnings are in our power; on the result Fortune passes judgment, and I do not allow her a verdict on me. “But she will bring some harassment, some adversity.” A robber does not condemn when he kills.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *consilium rerum omnium*: Literally “the plan of all things”. The first noun is plan, considered intention or deliberate purpose; “intention” is used, and “of all things” is given as “in everything”. Other reading: the wise man looks to the plan of every action
- *initia*: “Beginnings”: how a thing is set about, as against how it turns out. Other reading: the first steps are in our power
- *de eventu fortuna iudicat*: Fortune sits as a judge here, so she has a capital and “she”, as the glossary allows where she is personified. “Passes judgment” begins the court image that “verdict” and “condemn” continue. Other reading: the result is decided by fortune
- *cui de me sententiam non do*: A court phrase, literally a refusal to give her a verdict about the speaker. It is taken to mean that he does not allow Fortune the right to pronounce on him. The construction is unusual and could be read otherwise. Other reading: to whom I do not allow a vote on my case
- *afferet*: The verb has no stated subject. The English takes it to be Fortune, from the clause before, and the reply about the robber suits that; it could also be the course of action being recommended. Other reading: “But it will bring some harassment, some adversity.”
- *Non damnat latro cum occidit*: The first verb is the court word for finding guilty. The point, left unstated, is that a blow from Fortune is violence and not a verdict on the man. The bare image is kept. Other reading: A robber does not pass sentence on a man when he kills him

</details>

## Letter 16: On philosophy as the guide of life

### 16.1

I know that this is clear to you, Lucilius: that no one can live happily, or even tolerably, without the study of wisdom, and that the happy life is brought about by wisdom made complete, but a tolerable life even by wisdom begun. But this, which is clear, must be made firm and fixed deeper by daily practice: there is more work in keeping to what you have resolved than in resolving on what is honourable. One must persevere and add strength by constant study, until what is a good intention becomes a good mind.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Liquere*: A word from the courts, used when a case is clear enough to decide. It returns in the next sentence and “clear” is used both times. Other reading: I know you are satisfied of this
- *sapientiae studio*: Literally “zeal for wisdom” or “pursuit of wisdom”, a Latin way of saying philosophy. The same noun returns at the end of the section, so “study” is used in both places, though the Latin word is wider than study from books. Other reading: without the pursuit of wisdom; without philosophy
- *perfecta sapientia*: “Completed” or “perfected” wisdom, set against wisdom merely begun. “Made complete” keeps the pair of finished and started. Other reading: by perfect wisdom
- *cotidiana meditatione*: The noun is practice, rehearsal or exercise as much as reflection. The English takes “practice”. Other reading: by daily reflection
- *ut proposita custodias quam ut honesta proponas*: A play on one verb: proposita are things resolved on, and proponas is to resolve on. “Resolved” and “resolving” keep it. Honesta is plural, “honourable things”, here “what is honourable”. Other reading: in keeping your resolutions than in making honourable ones
- *assiduo studio*: The same noun as in the first sentence; here it could equally be effort or application. “Study” is kept. The verbs are impersonal, hence “one must”. Other reading: by constant effort
- *donec bona mens sit quod bona voluntas est*: The word for mind here is mens, not the animus of the glossary; bona mens is a set phrase for a sound, good state of mind. The other noun is will, wish or intention; “intention” is used because “a good will” can be misread. The Latin has the plain verb “is” in both halves, and “becomes” brings out the change after “until”. Other reading: until what is a good will is a good mind

</details>

### 16.5

Whichever of these is the case, Lucilius, or even if all of them are, one must do philosophy. Whether the fates bind us by an inexorable law, or god, the arbiter of the universe, has arranged everything, or chance drives and tosses human affairs about without order, philosophy ought to protect us. It will urge us to obey god willingly, and Fortune defiantly; it will teach you to follow god and bear chance.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quidquid est ex his*: “These” are the three possibilities raised by an objector in the section before: fate, a ruling god, or chance. Other reading: Whatever is true among these
- *vel si omnia haec sunt*: Literally “or if all these are”. It is taken as “even if all of them hold together”. Other reading: or if all these things exist
- *philosophandum est*: An impersonal form, “there must be philosophising”. It is kept impersonal with “one must”. Other reading: we must be philosophers
- *inexorabili lege fata constringunt*: The adjective is literally “not to be moved by entreaty”; the English uses the same word, “inexorable”. The noun is plural, “the fates”, and the plural is kept. Other reading: whether the fates bind us by a law that no prayer can move
- *arbiter deus universi*: The first noun is a judge or umpire with full discretion, and so a ruler or master; “arbiter” is kept. “God” is lower case and has no article, here and in the next sentence, so that one being is meant throughout; Latin has no articles, and “a god” is equally possible. Other reading: or a god who rules the universe has arranged everything
