- *ut fortunae contumaciter*: The verb “obey” has to be carried over from the clause before, giving the paradox of obeying Fortune defiantly: giving way to what she does without giving in. The adverb is stubbornly or unyieldingly. Fortune has a capital because she is obeyed like a master, as in Ep. 14.16 where she judges. Other reading: and to submit to fortune without yielding
- *ut deum sequaris, feras casum*: The Latin moves from the first person plural in the clause before to “you” (singular) here, and the change is kept. Other reading: it will teach you to follow god and put up with chance

</details>

### 16.6

But now is not the time to cross over into this discussion: what is ours to decide if providence is in command, or if the chain of the fates drags us along bound, or if the sudden and the unforeseen are the masters. I return now to this: to warn you and urge you not to let the impulse of your mind slip away and grow cold. Keep hold of it and set it firm, so that what is an impulse becomes a settled state of mind.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quid sit iuris nostri*: A legal phrase, literally “what is of our right”: what falls under a person’s own authority. “Ours to decide” gives the sense. Other reading: what lies within our own right
- *fatorum series illigatos trahit*: Literally “the series of the fates drags (us) tied on”. The first noun is a row or linked sequence; “chain” suits the dragging. “Us” is supplied. Other reading: if the sequence of the fates pulls us along in bonds
- *repentina ac subita*: Two words of nearly the same meaning for what comes suddenly, standing for chance. Two different English words are used so that neither is dropped. Other reading: if sudden and unexpected events hold sway
- *impetum animi tui*: The first noun is a rush, drive or onset; “impulse” is used, and “mind” for the second noun by the glossary, though “spirit” would also suit. Both words return in the last sentence with the same English. Other reading: the ardour of your spirit
- *delabi et refrigescere*: Literally “slip down and cool off”. Both images are kept. Other reading: to sink and go cold
- *Contine illum et constitue*: The first verb is to hold together, hold in or keep hold of; the second is to set up, make stand, settle. It is open whether the first means restraining the impulse or keeping it from escaping; the English takes the second. Other reading: Hold it in check and settle it
- *habitus animi*: A technical term (the Latin for the Greek hexis): a lasting condition of the mind, as against a passing movement. The English “habit” would suggest mere custom, so “settled state” is used. Other reading: a habit of the mind

</details>

## Letter 17: On not postponing philosophy for money

### 17.5

If you want to have time for your mind, you must either be poor or be like a poor man. Wholesome study cannot be carried on without a concern for frugality; and frugality is voluntary poverty. So away with those excuses: “I do not yet have as much as is enough; if I reach that sum, then I will give myself wholly to philosophy.” And yet nothing should be acquired before this thing that you put off and acquire after everything else; one must begin with this. “I want,” you say, “to acquire something to live on.” At the same time learn to acquire as well: if something forbids you to live well, it does not forbid you to die well.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *vacare animo*: The noun can be dative (“be free for the mind”, have leisure to attend to it) or ablative (“be free in mind”). The English takes the dative. Other reading: If you want to be free in mind
- *aut pauper sis oportet*: The Latin word for poor means of small means, not destitute; the next section sets it against a stronger word. “Poor” and “poverty” are kept. Other reading: you must either be a man of small means
- *Non potest studium salutare fieri*: This construes two ways: “wholesome study cannot be carried on”, with the adjective attached to the noun, or “study cannot become wholesome”, with the adjective as predicate. The English takes the first, since the adjective stands beside the noun and the pair names philosophy as the study that heals. The adjective is giving health or saving. Other reading: Study cannot do you good without a concern for frugality
- *parandum*: One verb runs through the rest of the section in four forms: to get, acquire or provide. “Acquire” is used each time so that the reply to the objector can be followed. What is to be acquired first is philosophy. Other reading: nothing should be got ready before this
- *unde vivam*: Literally something to live from: the means of living. Other reading: the means to live
- *Simul et parare disce*: The sentence is very compressed and not certain. Literally “at the same time also learn to acquire”, and the verb has no object: it may be a living, got alongside philosophy, or philosophy itself, of which the same verb has just been used, or the art of acquiring, which philosophy teaches. The English supplies no object. Other reading: Learn to get that too, at the same time; or: Learn while you acquire
- *si quid te vetat bene vivere, bene mori non vetat*: “Live well” can mean live comfortably or live rightly, and the first sense seems to be turned against the objector’s wish for a living. “Live well” and “die well” leave the play open. Other reading: if anything prevents you from living in comfort, it does not prevent you from dying well

</details>

### 17.6

There is no reason for poverty to call us back from philosophy, or even destitution. For those who hurry towards this must bear even hunger. Some have borne it in sieges, and what other reward was there for that endurance than not falling into the conqueror’s power? How much greater is this that is promised: unending freedom, and fear of no one, neither man nor god. Should one not come to these things even hungry?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ne egestas quidem*: A stronger word than the one for poverty: actual want, lacking the necessities. The English takes “destitution”. Other reading: not even real want
- *ad hoc properantibus*: “This” is neuter and unnamed: the goal just spoken of, philosophy and what it promises. Other reading: those who are hastening to this goal
- *in arbitrium non cadere victoris*: The noun is the will or discretion of someone who may do with a person as he pleases. The English takes “power”. Other reading: not to be at the victor’s mercy
- *perpetua libertas*: The adjective is unbroken or continuous, and so lasting. The English takes “unending”. Other reading: lasting freedom; unbroken freedom
- *nullius nec hominis nec dei timor*: Literally “fear of no one, neither of man nor of god”. The word for no one can also be neuter, “of nothing”. It is taken of persons, as the two nouns suggest. Other reading: fear of nothing, neither of man nor of god
- *Ecquid vel esurienti ad ista veniendum est*: The opening word marks an urgent question and carries no negative. The English adds “not” to show that the answer expected is yes, because a bare “must one” would read in English as a protest. The verb is impersonal and the participle is literally “to one who is hungry”. Other reading: Must one, then, come to these things even hungry?

</details>

## Letter 18: On practising poverty during the holidays

### 18.5

But I am so much in favour of testing the firmness of your mind that, following the precept of great men, I shall give a precept to you as well: set some days in between on which, content with the scantiest and cheapest food and with coarse and rough clothing, you say to yourself, “Is this what was feared?”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *adeo mihi placet*: Placet can express liking or a settled decision. The English takes it as approval, which is the weaker of the two senses. Other reading: so resolved on testing
- *e praecepto magnorum virorum tibi quoque praecipiam*: The noun and the verb share a root, so “precept” is used twice. Quoque goes with tibi: what the great men laid down for others is now laid down for Lucilius as well. The great men are not named in this section; Epicurus is named later in the letter (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: following the teaching of great men, to instruct you as well
- *interponas aliquot dies*: Interponere is to put something in between other things: the days are set into ordinary life. Other reading: set aside a number of days
- *minimo ac vilissimo cibo*: Minimo is least in amount; vilissimo is cheapest or commonest. Other reading: the smallest amount of the commonest food
- *dura atque horrida veste*: Durus of cloth is stiff or coarse; horrida is rough, bristly, shaggy to the touch. Other reading: stiff and shaggy clothing
- *hoc est quod timebatur?*: The verb is an impersonal passive in the imperfect: no one is named as the one who feared. The English keeps it impersonal. Other reading: Is this what you were afraid of?

</details>

### 18.7

There is no reason now for you to think that I mean dinners in the style of Timon and poor men’s rooms and whatever else there is by which luxury plays, out of boredom with riches: let that pallet be a real one, and the rough cloak, and the bread hard and coarse. Bear this for three or four days, sometimes for more days, so that it is not play but a test: then, believe me, Lucilius, you will leap for joy, filled for two coppers, and you will understand that for freedom from care there is no need of Fortune; for this, which is enough for necessity, she will give even when angry.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Timoneas cenas*: An adjective from Timon, the Athenian known for hating mankind and living apart. It seems to name a fashionable meal of mock austerity (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: Timon’s dinners
- *pauperum cellas*: Said to be small rooms in rich houses fitted out like a poor man’s room, for playing at poverty (from memory, unchecked). Cella is any small plain room. Other reading: paupers’ cells
- *luxuria divitiarum taedio ludit*: Taedio is taken as a cause (“out of boredom”) with divitiarum depending on it. Divitiarum could instead go with luxuria. Ludit is echoed by lusus below, so the English has “plays” and “play”. Other reading: the luxury of riches plays out of boredom
- *grabattus ille verus sit et sagum*: A grabattus is a low, cheap bed; a sagum is a coarse woollen cloak worn by soldiers and by poor men. “Rough” is used for the cloak because “coarse” serves for the bread in the same sentence. Other reading: let the camp bed be a real one, and the soldier’s cloak
- *panis durus ac sordidus*: Sordidus is dirty, mean, low. Of bread it is taken as the dark loaf of the lowest grade, not as literally soiled. Other reading: bread that is hard and dirty
- *triduo et quatriduo*: Literally “for three days and for four days”. The English gives the sense, stretches of three or four days, with “or”. Other reading: for three days and for four days
- *dipondio satur*: A dupondius was a small coin worth two asses. The ablative is price or means. Satur is a bare adjective, “full”, so no “at being” is supplied. Other reading: fed full on two pennies’ worth
- *ad securitatem non opus esse fortuna*: Fortune is personified here, since she is “angry” in the next clause, so the capital is used. Securitas is “freedom from care” as in the glossary. Other reading: that peace of mind does not need good fortune
- *hoc enim quod necessitati sat est*: Hoc can point back to the meal just described (“this, which is enough”) or simply lead into the relative clause (“that which is enough”). The English takes the first. “Necessity” is kept for necessitas because “need” already renders opus esse in the clause before. Other reading: for what is enough for need

</details>

### 18.15

This passion flares up against all persons; it is born of love as much as of hatred, no less in serious matters than in play and jokes; and what matters is not how great the cause it is born from but what kind of mind it reaches. So with fire: it does not matter how great it is but what it falls on; for solid things do not take even the greatest fire, while things that are dry and catch fire easily nurse even a spark all the way to a blaze. So it is, my Lucilius: the outcome of enormous anger is madness, and therefore anger is to be avoided not for the sake of moderation but for the sake of sanity. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *hic exardescit affectus*: “This passion” is anger, the subject of the saying of Epicurus quoted just before. “Passion” for affectus follows the glossary. Other reading: this emotion blazes up
- *In omnes personas*: Persona is a person in a given role or standing, so the phrase may mean people of every kind. The English keeps the plain “all persons”. Other reading: against persons of every kind
- *quo incidat*: Quo is “to what place”: what the fire lands on. Other reading: where it falls
- *etiam maximum solida non receperunt*: The perfect states a general truth from experience, so the English uses the present. Solida is neuter plural, “solid things”, such as sound timber or stone; it is kept general. Other reading: solid timbers have been known to resist even the largest fire
- *arida et corripi facilia*: Corripi is “to be seized”. The Latin does not say by what, and the English supplies “fire” from the sentence. Other reading: things that are dry and easy to seize
- *scintillam quoque fovent usque in incendium*: Fovere is to keep warm, to cherish. “Nurse” keeps the image. Other reading: feed even a spark until it becomes a conflagration
- *ingentis irae exitus furor est*: Furor is raving madness; the saying of Epicurus just before uses a different word for madness. Exitus is the way out, the end or outcome. Other reading: the end of huge anger is frenzy
- *non moderationis causa sed sanitatis*: Sanitas is both health and soundness of mind. “Sanity” is chosen because it answers “madness”. Moderatio is keeping within measure. Other reading: not for the sake of self restraint but for the sake of health

</details>

## Letter 20: On matching deeds to words

### 20.2

Those who declaim and angle for the approval of a ring of listeners have one purpose, those who hold the ears of young men and men of leisure with varied or rapid discussion have another: philosophy teaches doing, not saying, and it demands this, that each person live by his own law, that his life not be at odds with his speech, nor his life itself at odds with itself; let all actions be of one colour. This is both the greatest duty of wisdom and its greatest sign: that deeds agree with words, that the man himself be everywhere consistent with himself and one and the same. “Who will achieve this?” Few, but some all the same. For it is difficult; and I am not saying that the wise man will always go at one pace, but that he will go by one road.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Aliud propositum est*: “One purpose, another” can mean that the two groups differ from each other, or more loosely that both differ from philosophy, which follows after the colon. The English keeps the literal shape and adds no link. Other reading: Declaimers have a different aim, and so do those who hold the ears of the young: philosophy, though, teaches doing
- *assensionem coronae captantibus*: Corona is the ring of bystanders around a speaker. Captare is to hunt or fish for. Other reading: fishing for the applause of the crowd standing round
- *iuvenum et otiosorum*: Otiosi are people with leisure, with time on their hands. The word can carry blame but need not, so the English has “men of leisure”. Other reading: of young men and idlers
- *disputatione varia aut volubili*: Varia is changing or wide ranging. Volubilis is rolling, rapid, and can be heard as praise (“fluent”) or as blame (“glib”); “rapid” leaves that open. Other reading: with wide ranging or glib argument
- *ad legem suam quisque vivat*: Suam with quisque normally means “each his own”. The reflexive could instead point back to philosophy, the subject of “demands”. The English takes the first. Other reading: that each person live by its law (philosophy’s law)
- *unus sit omnium actio[dissentio]num color [sit]*: The square brackets are an editor’s deletions carried into the copy; the English reads “unus sit omnium actionum color”. As given the words stand after a semicolon with no “that”, so they are translated as a fresh wish, though they could be one more thing that philosophy demands. The wording of this sentence is disturbed in the tradition and other editions print it differently (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: that all his actions be of one colour
- *officium sapientiae et indicium*: The two nouns nearly rhyme in Latin. Officium is duty, task or function; indicium is a sign or proof. Other reading: the greatest task of wisdom and the greatest proof of it
- *par sibi idemque*: Par sibi is literally “equal to himself”, a match for himself, and idem is “the same”. The English gives the sense in current words. Other reading: equal to himself and the same
- *Est enim difficile [hoc]*: The bracketed word is marked for deletion and is left out. “For” explains why only a few achieve it. Other reading: For this is difficult
- *uno semper iturum gradu, sed una via*: Gradus is a step or pace. The contrast is between the same speed and the same road. “Will go” is repeated in the second half for English grammar. Other reading: will always walk with the same step, but along the same road

</details>

### 20.9

Begrudge it if you like: even now Epicurus will gladly pay on my behalf. “Your talk, believe me, will seem grander on a pallet and in rags; for those things will not only be said but proved.” I at least hear in a different way what our Demetrius says when I have seen him naked, lying on something far less than straw bedding: he is not a teacher of the truth but a witness to it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Invideas licet*: A concession: “though you may grudge it”. Invidere is to envy or begrudge. What Lucilius might grudge is not stated; perhaps that the payment again comes from Epicurus. Other reading: You may be envious
- *pro me dependet*: Dependere is to pay down what is owed, and the form is future. It continues the joke of these letters that the saying attached to each one is a payment due. Other reading: will settle the account for me
- *etiam nunc*: Literally “even now” or “still”. With the future verb it can also be narrowed to “this time too”: Epicurus will pay once more. Other reading: this time too
- *in grabatto videbitur et in panno*: The quoted words are given as those of Epicurus; their place in his remains is not identified here (from memory, unchecked). Grabattus is a low, cheap bed; pannus is a rag or patch of cloth. Other reading: will seem grander on a camp bed and in a rag
- *probabuntur*: Probare is to prove or test, and also to win approval. The section before asks for words to be proved by deeds, with the same verb, so “proved” is kept. Other reading: but will be believed
- *cum illum vidi nudum*: Cum with the perfect indicative beside a present main verb says what holds whenever the thing has happened; it could also point to one occasion. Nudus can be naked or merely without an outer garment, and the English chooses “naked”. Demetrius is the Cynic philosopher, a friend of Seneca (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: ever since seeing him without a cloak
- *quanto minus quam [in] stramentis incubantem*: The edition brackets “in” for deletion. The phrase is compressed and does not construe strictly: literally “lying on, by how much less than, straw bedding”. The English takes it to say that his bed was far poorer than straw; with “in” kept it could mean that he was not even lying on straw. Other reading: and, what is more, not even lying on straw
- *non praeceptor veri sed testis est*: Praeceptor is one who gives precepts, a teacher; testis is a witness in court. Other reading: he does not teach the truth, he gives evidence for it

</details>

## Letter 21: On lasting fame through philosophy

### 21.5

A fathomless depth of time will come over us; few talents will lift their heads out and, though bound to go off some day into the same silence, will hold out against oblivion and will long assert their freedom. What Epicurus was able to promise his friend, this I promise you, Lucilius: I shall have favour with those who come after; I can bring out with me names that will last. Our Vergil promised everlasting memory to two men, and he is making it good: “Fortunate pair! If my songs have any power, no day shall ever take you out of time that remembers, so long as the house of Aeneas dwells beside the immovable rock of the Capitol and the Roman father holds the empire.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Profunda super nos altitudo temporis veniet*: Altitudo is depth as well as height, and profunda, from a different root, deepens it. The image is deep water closing over everyone, with a few heads rising above it. Other reading: The deep flood of time will roll over us
- *in idem quandoque silentium abitura*: Quandoque is “at some time, some day”. The future participle concedes that even these will go in the end. Other reading: though sooner or later they will pass into the same silence
- *se diu vindicabunt*: Vindicare is a legal word: to claim something as one’s own, or to claim a person into freedom. Here the talents claim themselves back from oblivion, and “assert their freedom” gives that sense. Other reading: will long keep their claim to themselves
- *amico suo*: The friend is Idomeneus, named in the sentences just before this section. Other reading: his own friend
- *habebo apud posteros gratiam*: Gratia is favour, credit or influence with someone. Other reading: shall have credit with posterity
- *mecum duratura nomina educere*: Educere is to lead out, here out of the depth of time. The names are those taken along by being written to. Other reading: carry out with me names that will endure
- *fortunati ambo! si quid mea carmina possunt*: Vergil, Aeneid 9, on Nisus and Euryalus (from memory, unchecked). The Latin as given has no quotation marks; they are supplied because the colon introduces the words of Vergil. The verse is given as prose. Other reading: Happy both! If my poems can do anything
- *memori vos eximet aevo*: Literally “from the mindful age”: time that keeps a memory. Other reading: shall ever erase you from the memory of the ages
- *imperiumque pater Romanus habebit*: Who “the Roman father” is has been disputed: Jupiter of the Capitol, the ruler, or the Roman head of a house (from memory, unchecked). It is kept literal. Imperium is command, rule or empire, and “the empire” is the widest of these. Other reading: and the father of Rome holds command

</details>

## Letter 22: On withdrawing from a busy career

### 22.15

“No one,” he says, “goes out of life otherwise than as he was born.” That is false: we die worse than we are born. That fault is ours, not nature’s. She ought to remonstrate with us and say: “What is this? I gave birth to you without desires, without fears, without superstition, without treachery and the other plagues: go out such as you came in.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquit*: “He” is Epicurus, whose saying is quoted in other words just before this section. Seneca restates it here and then contradicts it. Other reading: it is said
- *aliter quam quomodo natus est*: Literally “otherwise than in the way he was born”. Other reading: in any other state than the one he was born in
- *exit e vita*: “Goes out” is used so that it is heard again in “go out” at the end, where the Latin repeats the verb and sets it against “came in”. Other reading: leaves life
- *Nostrum istud, non naturae vitium est*: The glossary gives “vice” for vitium. Here the point is whose defect it is that people die worse than they were born, so “fault” is used. Other reading: That vice is ours, not nature’s
- *nobiscum queri*: Queri cum aliquo is to bring a complaint to someone about his conduct, to remonstrate with him. It does not mean to complain alongside him. Other reading: to complain to us
- *genui*: Gignere is used of a father begetting and of a mother bearing. Nature is feminine here, so the English has “gave birth”. Other reading: begot you
- *quales intrastis exite*: A plural command to everyone. Literally “of what kind you entered, go out”. Other reading: leave as you were when you entered

</details>

## Letter 23: On finding true joy within

### 23.3

Do this before all else, my Lucilius: learn to feel joy. Do you now think that I am taking many pleasures away from you, I who clear away the things of chance, who think that hopes, the sweetest of delights, are to be avoided? No, on the contrary: I do not want gladness ever to be lacking to you. I want it to be born for you at home: it is born if only it comes about within yourself. The other kinds of cheerfulness do not fill the breast; they smooth the brow, they are slight, unless perhaps you judge that a man who laughs is feeling joy: the mind ought to be lively and confident and raised up above all things.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *disce gaudere*: Gaudere is the verb of gaudium, “joy”, which the Stoics set apart from pleasure (from memory, unchecked). “Joy” is kept for gaudium and gaudere, “pleasure” for voluptas, “gladness” for laetitia and “cheerfulness” for hilaritas. Other reading: learn how to rejoice
- *fortuita summoveo*: Fortuita are things that come by chance. Summovere is to move something out of the way. Other reading: pushing aside the gifts of chance
- *spes, dulcissima oblectamenta*: Oblectamenta are things that delight or divert. Other reading: hopes, the sweetest of amusements
- *Volo illam tibi domi nasci*: “At home” is the image of something grown in one’s own house, not brought in from outside. The image is kept. Other reading: to be grown at home for you
- *nascitur si modo intra te ipsum fit*: The Latin has no “there”: “at home” is understood from the clause before. Fit is “comes about” or “is made”. Some editions may read a different verb here (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: it is born there, provided it is produced inside you yourself
- *frontem remittunt, leves sunt*: Remittere is to relax or unbend. Leves is light, slight, shallow, and can also be fickle. Other reading: they unknit the brow, they are lightweight
- *alacer et fidens et supra omnia erectus*: Alacer is brisk, lively, in good spirits. Erectus is upright or lifted. Other reading: eager and confident and standing upright above everything

</details>

### 23.4

Believe me, true joy is a stern thing. Or do you think that anyone with a relaxed and, as those pampered people say, “cheery little” face despises death, opens his house to poverty, holds pleasures under the bridle, rehearses the endurance of pains? The man who turns these things over within himself is in great joy, but a joy with little charm. It is this joy that I want you to be in possession of: it will never fail, once you have found where it is to be got from.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *res severa*: Severus is stern, grave, serious. Other reading: a serious matter
- *soluto vultu*: Solutus is loosened, relaxed, unbent. Other reading: with a carefree look
- *ut isti delicati loquuntur, hilariculo*: Hilariculus is a diminutive of the word for cheerful, quoted as a pet word of a pampered set. It is very rare and the text may be an editor’s correction (from memory, unchecked). Delicati are the pampered or self indulgent. Other reading: and, as those spoilt people put it, a “blithe little” one
- *voluptates tenere sub freno*: Frenum is the bridle or bit of a horse. Other reading: keeps pleasures on a tight rein
- *meditari dolorum patientiam*: Meditari is to think something over, or to practise it as an exercise. The English takes the second. “Pains” is plural in the Latin. Other reading: thinks over the bearing of pain
- *Haec qui apud se versat*: Literally “turns these things over with himself”. “These things” are the four acts just listed. Other reading: Whoever keeps turning these thoughts over in his mind
- *parum blando*: Parum is “too little”, often a mild way of saying “not”. Blandus is coaxing, caressing, charming. Other reading: but a joy that is not at all soft
- *unde petatur*: Literally “from where it is to be sought”: the place it is fetched from. Other reading: where it is to be sought

</details>

### 23.6

Do, I beg you, dearest Lucilius, the one thing that can make a person happy: scatter and trample those things that shine on the outside, that are promised to you by someone else or from something else; look to the true good and take your joy from what is your own. But what is this “from what is your own”? From yourself, and from the best part of you. As for the little body too, even though nothing can be done without it, believe it to be a necessary thing rather than a great one; it supplies empty pleasures, short ones, ones to be regretted and, unless they are tempered with great moderation, bound to pass into their opposite. This is what I say: pleasure is on a steep slope; it tilts towards pain unless it has kept within bounds; but to keep within bounds is difficult in a thing that you have believed to be good: greed for the true good is safe.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *praestare felicem*: The Latin names no person: “make (someone) happy”. “A person” is supplied; “you” is as possible, since the command is to Lucilius. Felix is lucky or fortunate as well as happy; the glossary keeps “happy” for beatus, and it is used here too for want of a better plain word. Other reading: that can make you fortunate
- *extrinsecus splendent*: Extrinsecus can be “on the outside”, an outward shine, or “from without”, a borrowed light. The English takes the first. Other reading: that shine with a light from outside
- *ab alio vel ex alio*: “By someone else” is the one who promises; “from something else” is the source the thing is to come from. Alio in the second place could also be a person. Other reading: by another or from another
- *de tuo gaude*: “De tuo” is a money idiom: from your own property, at your own cost. That sense is kept, since the next sentence asks what “your own” is. Other reading: rejoice in what is yours
- *Corpusculum quoque*: A diminutive of “body”, said slightingly. “Too” is hard to place: perhaps the body also counts as one’s own, but only as a necessity. It is left as it stands. Other reading: This poor body as well
- *nisi magna moderatione, temperentur*: The comma in the digitised text is a slip; the words are read together. Moderatio shares a root with modus, the “bounds” of the next sentence. Other reading: unless they are kept in check by great restraint
- *In praecipiti voluptas , ad dolorem vergit*: The capital and the spacing are slips in the copy. “In praecipiti” is on a sheer drop or steep slope; “slope” is chosen because the next verb is “tilts”. It is taken as two clauses with “is” supplied; it could be one. Other reading: Pleasure, on the brink, tilts towards pain
- *nisi modum tenuit*: Modus is measure or limit. The verb is perfect: “unless it has kept”. Other reading: unless it has kept to measure
- *quod bonu esse credideris*: “bonu” is a slip for bonum. The “you” is general, and the perfect subjunctive is “have come to believe”. Other reading: in anything one has come to think good
- *veri boni aviditas tuta est*: Aviditas is greed or keen appetite. The strong word is kept, since the point is that here even greed does no harm. Other reading: eagerness for the true good is without danger

</details>

### 23.7

Do you ask what that is, or where it comes up from? I will tell you: from a good conscience, from honourable plans, from right actions, from contempt for the things of chance, from the peaceful and unbroken course of a life that keeps to one road. For those who leap from one set of purposes to another, or do not even leap but are carried across by some accident, how can they have anything certain or lasting, when they are suspended and wandering?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quod sit istud*: “That” points back to what the letter has just named, most likely the true good at the end of the section before, possibly the joy that goes with it. The answer that follows replies to “where from” and not to “what”. Other reading: what this thing is
- *unde subeat*: Subire is to come up from below or to steal in. Other reading: where it arises from
- *ex bona conscientia*: Conscientia is knowledge shared with oneself. It is wider than the modern “conscience” but includes it. Other reading: from a good awareness of oneself
- *ex honestis consiliis*: Consilia are plans, intentions or decisions. “Honourable” follows the glossary’s word for honestum. Other reading: from honourable intentions
- *ex contemptu fortuitorum*: The same word as in 23.3, so the same English: “the things of chance”. Casu below is a different word and is given as “accident”. Other reading: from scorn of what chance brings
- *ex placido vitae et continuo tenore unam prementis viam*: Placidus is quiet, peaceful; “calm” is avoided because the glossary keeps it for tranquillitas. Prementis agrees with vitae: a life that treads, or keeps to, one road. Other reading: from the quiet, unbroken tenor of a life that treads a single path
- *casu quodam transmittuntur*: The verb is passive: they are sent or carried across, and do not move themselves. Other reading: are thrown across by a kind of chance
- *suspensi et vagi*: Suspensus is hanging, and so in suspense, unsettled. The image is kept with “suspended”, and “when they are” is added for English grammar. Other reading: unsettled and drifting

</details>

### 23.11

There is no reason for you to think that these are few: they are nearly everyone. Some, indeed, begin at the time when they must stop. If you judge this a wonder, I will add something for you to wonder at more: some have stopped living before they began. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *paucos.esse hos*: The full stop is a slip in the copy; the words are read together. “These” are the people of the section before, who are only now beginning to live. Other reading: that such people are few
- *propemodum omnes sunt*: Literally “they are almost all (people)”. Other reading: it is almost all of mankind
- *tunc incipiunt cum desinendum est*: “Begin” has no object; “to live” is understood from the context. The second verb is impersonal, “when there must be a stopping”; the English gives it the subject “they”. Other reading: begin to live only when it is time to leave off
- *adiciam quod magis admireris*: The verb shares a root with mirum in the clause before, so the English has “a wonder” there and “wonder at” here. Other reading: something that will surprise you more
- *ante vivere desierunt quam inciperent*: The subjunctive in the “before” clause may carry “before they could begin”. The plainer form is kept. Other reading: have left off living before they could begin

</details>

## Letter 24: On facing feared troubles and death

### 24.12

So, free from care, listen to your enemy’s threats; and although your own conscience gives you confidence, still, because many things outside the case carry weight, hope for what is fairest and also prepare yourself for what is most unfair. But above all remember this, to take the uproar away from things and to see what is in each thing: you will find that there is nothing terrible in those things except the fear itself.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Securus*: The adjective belonging to securitas, which the glossary renders as freedom from care. That phrase is kept so the link shows, and it stands first, as in the Latin. Other reading: Listen to your enemy’s threats without anxiety
- *inimici*: A personal enemy. The words about the case that follow suggest an opponent at law, but the Latin word is the general one and is kept general. Other reading: your opponent’s threats
- *conscientia tibi tua fiduciam faciat*: Conscientia is knowledge shared with oneself, here the knowledge of being in the right. English “conscience” is a little narrower. Other reading: although your knowledge of your own innocence gives you confidence
- *multa extra causam valent*: Legal language: causa is the case being tried, and things outside it can still sway the result. The legal word is kept. Other reading: many things that have nothing to do with the merits have power
- *quod aequissimum est*: Aequus can mean just, fair or favourable, and iniquissimum is its opposite. “Fairest” and “most unfair” keep the two words as plainly opposite as they are in the Latin, and suit a verdict. Other reading: hope for the most favourable outcome and prepare yourself for the most unfavourable
- *demere rebus tumultum*: Tumultus is noise, commotion, alarm. The verb demere returns in the next section for taking off a mask, so a plain “take away” is used. Other reading: to strip things of their commotion
- *in istis*: Istis is the pronoun that points at what belongs to the person addressed: here the threats Lucilius is facing. “Those things” keeps the pointing without suggesting his possessions. Other reading: in those things of yours
- *nisi ipsum timorem*: Ipsum can point to the fear of these particular things or to fear in general. The English takes the first. Other reading: except fear itself

</details>

### 24.13

What you see happen to children happens to us too, who are slightly bigger children: the people they love, the people they are used to, the people they play with, if they see them wearing masks, they take fright. The mask must be taken off not only people but things, and their own face given back to them.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quod vides accidere pueris*: Pueri can be boys or children in general. The general sense is taken. Other reading: What you see happen to boys
- *maiusculis pueris*: Maiusculus is a diminutive of the comparative: somewhat bigger. The phrase stands in apposition to the pronoun before it. Other reading: us too, boys who are only a little older
- *illi quos amant, quibus assueverunt, cum quibus ludunt*: The Latin starts with “they” (the children) and then piles up three relative clauses before the verb. That loose order is kept. Other reading: they take fright if they see in masks the very people they love, are used to and play with
- *personatos*: Wearing a persona, the mask of the stage. Other reading: disguised
- *persona demenda est et reddenda facies sua*: Sua refers back to the people and things: their own face. The Latin has no word for “each”. Demenda repeats the verb of the section before, where the uproar is taken away from things. Other reading: the mask must be removed and their true appearance restored

</details>

### 24.14

Why do you show me swords and fires and a crowd of executioners roaring around you? Take away that parade you hide behind and frighten fools with: you are death, which a slave of mine recently scorned, which a slave girl scorned. And you in turn, why do you lay out whips and racks before me with great display? Why the separate devices fitted to separate joints to wrench them out, and a thousand other instruments for torturing a man piece by piece? Put away those things that stun us; order the groans to fall silent, and the cries, and the harshness of voices forced out while the body is being torn. You are pain, after all, which that man with gout scorns, which that man with a bad stomach endures in the very midst of his luxuries, which a girl endures to the end in childbirth. You are light if I can bear you; you are short if I cannot bear you.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *turbam carnificum circa te frementem*: Carnifex is the public executioner, who also carried out torture. With swords and fires, in a scene that turns out to be death’s, “executioners” is used. Fremere is to roar, growl or mutter. Other reading: a mob of torturers snarling around you
- *Tolle istam pompam*: Pompa is a ceremonial procession, so the image is of death arriving with a train of attendants. Other reading: Take away that pageantry
- *nuper servus meus*: This may point to a real recent death in Seneca’s household or be a general example; the Latin does not say. The possessive can be read as pointing to one particular slave or to any slave of the household, and the English takes the second. Other reading: which my own slave scorned not long ago
- *contempsit*: Contemnere is to think little of, to hold cheap. “Scorn” is used here, for contemnit below and for contemnunt in 24.25, so that the repetition shows; “despise” is avoided because it now suggests loathing. Other reading: made light of
- *Quid tu rursus*: The address turns from death to a second figure, named only later as pain. The English does not name it early. Other reading: And you, pain, why do you in your turn
- *eculeos*: Literally “little horses”, the name of a torture frame on which the body was stretched. Other reading: the wooden horse
- *excarnificandi particulatim hominis*: Excarnificare is built on carnifex, the word behind “executioners” in the first sentence: to do the executioner’s work on someone to the end, to torture or tear to pieces. English cannot keep the echo without calling death’s attendants torturers, so the echo is recorded here. Other reading: for butchering a man piece by piece
- *vocum inter lacerationem elisarum acerbitatem*: Elidere is to squeeze or crush out, so the voices may be forced out of the victim or choked off; the English takes the first. The Latin says only “amid the tearing”, and “the body” is supplied so that the English says what is torn. Acerbitas could also be bitterness or shrillness. Other reading: the shrillness of voices choked off amid the tearing
- *nempe dolor es*: Nempe marks something as obvious: of course, to be sure. Other reading: Why, you are only pain
- *stomachicus ille in ipsis delicis*: Delicis stands for deliciis: luxuries, pleasures, indulgences in general, which for this man may well be those of the table. “His” is supplied. Ille here and with the gouty man points to a familiar type, not one person. Other reading: which the dyspeptic puts up with in the very midst of his delicacies
- *Levis es si ferre possum; brevis es si ferre non possum*: The Latin rhymes levis and brevis. The thought that pain is either light or short is Epicurean (from memory, unchecked). The second half implies that unbearable pain ends the sufferer, but the Latin leaves that unsaid and so does the English. Other reading: You are slight if I can bear you; you are brief if I cannot

</details>

### 24.15

Turn over in your mind these things, which you have often heard and often said; but whether you have truly heard them, whether you have truly said them, prove in action. For this charge that is commonly thrown at us is a most shameful one: that we handle the words of philosophy, not its deeds. What? Have you learned now for the first time that death hangs over you, now exile, now pain? You were born to these things. Whatever can happen, let us think of it as going to happen.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Haec in animo voluta*: Voluta is the imperative of volutare (turn over and over), though it looks like a participle. It is taken as the command, which the following proba supports. Other reading: These things, turned over in the mind
- *effectu proba*: Effectus is the carrying out as well as the outcome. With the contrast of words and deeds that follows, the carrying out is taken. Other reading: prove by the result
- *hoc enim turpissimum est quod nobis obici solet*: The superlative can mean “a most shameful thing” or “the most shameful of the things thrown”. The English takes the first. The Latin does not say who is charged: philosophers in general, or the Stoics. Other reading: For this is the most shameful of the charges commonly thrown at us
- *verba nos philosophiae, non opera tractare*: Tractare is to handle or deal in; “handle” is used here and again in 24.24. Opera are deeds, set against words. Other reading: that we deal in the words of philosophy, not its works
- *scisti*: A shortened form of scivisti: have come to know. Other reading: Did you find out now for the first time
- *in haec natus es*: In with the accusative: born into these things, or born for them. Other reading: You were born for this
- *quasi futurum cogitemus*: The Latin moves from the second person singular to a first person plural exhortation. The shift is kept. Other reading: let us think of whatever can happen as something that will happen

</details>

### 24.17

I will become poor: I will be among the greater number. I will become an exile: I will think myself born in the place I am sent to. I will be bound: what of it? Am I unbound now? Nature has bound me fast to this heavy weight of my body. I will die: what you are saying is this, I will cease to be able to be ill, I will cease to be able to be bound, I will cease to be able to die.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Pauper fiam*: Fiam and moriar can be future or subjunctive (a supposition). Alligabor is plainly future, so all four are put as futures. Other reading: Suppose I become poor
- *inter plures ero*: Plures is a comparative: the more numerous, the majority. The poor outnumber the rich. Other reading: I will have more company
- *Alligabor*: To be tied up, that is, chained or imprisoned. “Bound” is kept so that solutus (unbound) and adstrinxit (bound fast) answer it in English. Other reading: I will be put in chains
- *quid enim?*: A short idiom of dismissal. Other reading: Well, what then?
- *ad hoc me natura grave corporis mei pondus adstrinxit*: Ad hoc is taken with pondus: to this heavy weight. Ad hoc could also stand alone, with the weight in apposition. Other reading: Nature has tied me to this: the heavy weight of my body
- *hoc dicis*: “You” is whoever is making the threat, an imagined speaker; the Latin does not say whether Lucilius is meant. The sense is “what your words amount to is this”. Other reading: You mean this:
- *desinam mori posse*: A deliberate paradox, kept literally. Other reading: I will no longer be liable to death

</details>

### 24.18

I am not so silly as to go through the Epicurean refrain at this point and say that the fears of the underworld are empty, that Ixion is not turned on a wheel, that no rock is pushed uphill by the shoulders of Sisyphus, that no one’s entrails can both grow back and be torn at every day. No one is so much a child as to fear Cerberus and the darkness and the ghostly look of those whose bare bones hang together. Death either consumes us or strips us bare. For those who are let out, better things remain, with the burden taken off; for those who are consumed, nothing remains: good things and bad alike have been removed.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ineptus*: Silly, out of place. “Foolish” is avoided because the glossary keeps “fool” for stultus. Other reading: I am not so foolish
- *Epicuream cantilenam*: Cantilena is a song sung over and over, a stale tune. The Epicureans argued at length that the tales of punishment after death are false. Other reading: the old Epicurean song
- *inferorum metus*: Inferi are those below: the dead and their world. Other reading: fears of the dead below
- *nec Ixionem rota volvi nec saxum umeris Sisyphi trudi in adversum*: Two sinners of myth: Ixion bound to a turning wheel, Sisyphus pushing a rock up a slope for ever. In adversum is “against the slope”. Other reading: nor a stone shoved up the hill on the shoulders of Sisyphus
- *nec ullius viscera et renasci posse cotidie et carpi*: No name is given. The allusion is probably to Tityus, whose liver is eaten by vultures in the underworld (from memory, unchecked). Viscera is entrails or flesh; carpi is to be plucked or torn at bit by bit, and “every day” is taken with both verbs. Other reading: nor can anyone’s liver grow again every day and be pecked at
- *Cerberum*: The many-headed dog that guards the entrance to the underworld. Other reading: the hound of the underworld
- *larvalem habitum nudis ossibus cohaerentium*: Larvae are hostile ghosts, pictured as skeletons. Literally: the larva-like appearance of those holding together with bare bones. Habitus could also be dress or bearing. Other reading: the spectral shape of skeletons
- *aut consumit aut exuit*: Exuere is to strip off clothing: death either uses a person up entirely or takes the body off like a garment. The English adds “bare” to complete the clothing image; the release it implies comes in the next clause. Other reading: Death either destroys us or sets us free
- *emissis meliora restant onere detracto*: Emissis is a participle in the dative, “for those sent out or released”, as from a prison. The burden is the body. Other reading: if we are released, better things await us once the load is lifted
- *bona pariter malaque summota sunt*: Plural: good things and bad things. The glossary’s “the good” is for the singular bonum; the plural “goods” would read as merchandise, so “good things” is used. Other reading: goods and evils alike have been removed

</details>

### 24.20

We die every day; for every day some part of life is taken away, and even at the time when we are growing, life is growing less. We have lost infancy, then childhood, then youth. Right up to yesterday, whatever time has passed has perished; this very day that we are living we share with death. Just as it is not the last drop that empties a water clock but whatever has run out before, so the last hour, in which we cease to be, does not alone make death but alone completes it. That is when we come to it, but we have been coming for a long time.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *cum crescimus vita decrescit*: The Latin plays on crescere (grow) and decrescere (grow less). The English keeps “grow” in both halves so the play can be heard. Other reading: even while we grow, life is shrinking
- *pueritiam*: Pueritia covers childhood in general as well as boyhood. With a general subject, “childhood” is used. Other reading: then boyhood
- *deinde adulescentiam*: Roman adulescentia runs later than English “adolescence”, to about thirty. Other reading: then young manhood
- *trans;t*: A slip in the digitised text. It is read as transit or transiit, “has passed”. Other reading: whatever time passes
- *quidquid trans;t temporis perit*: Perit can be present (“perishes”) or a shortened perfect (“has perished”). With “right up to yesterday” the perfect is taken. Other reading: whatever time goes by, up to yesterday, is lost
- *cum morte dividimus*: Dividere cum is to share out with someone: death takes its part of today. Other reading: we divide with death
- *clepsydram non extremum stilicidium exhaurit*: A clepsydra is a clock that measures time by dripping water. Stilicidium (for stillicidium) is a drip. Other reading: it is not the final trickle that drains the water clock
- *non sola mortem facit sed sola consummat*: Consummare is to bring to a total, to finish off. The repeated sola (alone) is kept. Other reading: is not the only one that causes death, but the only one that finishes it
- *tunc ad illam pervenimus, sed diu venimus*: Pervenire (to come all the way) is built on venire (to come), and the English keeps “come” for both. Both forms can be present or perfect; they are taken as presents, and the present with diu is what English puts as “have been coming”. Other reading: we reached it then, but we were a long time on the way

</details>

### 24.22

I see what you are looking for: you are asking what I have packed into this letter, what spirited saying of someone’s, what useful precept. Something will be sent from this very subject, the one that has been in hand. Epicurus rebukes those who long for death no less than those who fear it, and says: “It is ridiculous to run to death out of weariness of life, when it is by your way of life that you have made it necessary to run to death.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Video quo spectes*: Literally, where Lucilius is looking, that is, what he has his eye on. Other reading: I see where you are looking
- *infulserim*: From infulcire, to stuff or cram in. It refers to Seneca’s habit of enclosing a saying in each letter. Other reading: what I have stuffed into this letter
- *dictum alicuius animosum*: Animosus is full of spirit, courageous. Other reading: what brave saying of someone’s
- *quae in manibus fuit*: Idiom: literally “which has been in the hands”, the matter being handled. The Latin names no handler, and the English keeps it so. Other reading: the subject we have had in our hands
- *mittetur aliquid*: A passive with no sender named. It is kept so. Other reading: I will send you something
- *taedio vitae*: Taedium is weariness shading into disgust. Other reading: out of disgust with life
- *cum genere vitae ut currendum ad mortem esset effeceris*: A saying of Epicurus (a known fragment, from memory, unchecked). Cum can be “when” or “although”; genus vitae is kind or manner of life. The saying turns on “run” being repeated, so it is repeated. Other reading: although it is your manner of living that has brought you to the point of having to run to death

</details>

### 24.24

Whichever of these you handle, you will strengthen your mind to endure either death or life; for we need to be warned and strengthened in both directions, both not to love life too much and not to hate it too much. Even when reason advises making an end of oneself, the leap is not to be taken rashly or at a run.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quidquid horum tractaveris*: “These” are the sayings just quoted in the section before. Tractare is to handle, work at, think over; “handle” is used, as in 24.15. Other reading: Whichever of these you ponder
- *vel ad mortis vel ad vitae patientiam*: Patientia is the bearing or endurance of something. The noun is turned into a verb. Other reading: for the endurance either of death or of life
- *[at]*: The square brackets are taken as the editor’s sign that the word should be deleted, which is the usual convention (from memory, unchecked). “But” does not fit beside enim (“for”), so the English follows the deletion. Other reading: but in both directions we need to be warned and strengthened
- *monendi ac firmandi*: Firmandi echoes confirmabis earlier in the sentence, so “strengthen” is used for both. Monere is to warn or advise. Other reading: we need to be advised and steadied
- *ratio suadet finire se*: Literally “reason advises to end oneself”. By strict grammar se would point back to reason, but the sense is the person; the Latin names no person and no “life”, so the English has “making an end of oneself”. Ratio is “reason”, as in the glossary. Other reading: Even when reason urges us to put an end to our life
- *non temere nec cum procursu capiendus est impetus*: Procursus is a running forward, a run before a jump. Impetum capere can be to take a spring or gather momentum, which fits the run, or to form an impulse, impetus being also the Stoic word for impulse. The English keeps the bodily image with “leap”. Other reading: the impulse is not to be formed rashly or in a rush

</details>

### 24.25

A brave and wise man ought not to flee from life but to go out of it. And above all, that passion too should be avoided which has taken hold of many: the lust for dying. For there is, my Lucilius, as towards other things, so also towards dying, an unconsidered leaning of the mind, which often seizes men who are noble and of the keenest character, and often the spiritless and the downcast. The former scorn life; the latter find it a burden.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Vir fortis ac sapiens*: Sapiens is here an adjective beside fortis. It is the same word the glossary renders “the wise man”. Other reading: The brave man and the wise man
- *non fugere debet e vita sed exire*: The contrast is between running away and walking out. Exire is the plain word for leaving. Other reading: should not run away from life but take his leave of it
- *ille quoque vitetur affectus*: Affectus is the Stoic term for a passion, a disordered movement of the mind. “Passion” is used, as in the glossary. Other reading: that feeling too must be avoided
- *libido moriendi*: Libido is craving or lust, a word with a bad sense. Other reading: a craving to die
- *inconsulta animi inclinatio*: Inconsulta: without deliberation. Inclinatio: a leaning or bent. Other reading: an unreflecting bent of the mind
- *generosos atque acerrimae indolis viros*: Generosus can mean of noble birth or of noble spirit. Acerrima indoles is a very keen, energetic nature. The two are joined by “and” in the Latin and are taken as describing character. Other reading: men of high birth and the fiercest temperament
- *ignavos iacentesque*: Ignavus is idle, spiritless or cowardly; set against men of keen character, “spiritless” is taken. Iacentes is literally “lying down”, said of those who are sunk and without energy; “downcast” keeps something of the image. Other reading: the cowardly and the prostrate
- *contemnunt*: Contemnere is to think little of, to hold cheap. “Scorn” matches 24.14. Other reading: The former make light of life
- *hi gravantur*: Gravari is to feel something as a burden; the form could also be a plain passive. Other reading: the latter are weighed down by it

</details>

### 24.26

Some people are overcome by having had their fill of doing and seeing the same things, and by not hatred of life but distaste for it. We slide into this with philosophy itself pushing us, while we say: “How long the same things? Of course I will wake, I will sleep, I will be hungry, I will be cold, I will be hot. Nothing has an end, but all things are linked in a circle; they flee and they follow. Night presses on day, day on night; summer ends in autumn, winter bears down on autumn, and is checked by spring. All things pass in such a way that they come back. I do nothing new, I see nothing new: at some point there comes nausea at this too.” There are many who judge living to be not bitter but superfluous. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *eadem faciendi videndique satietas*: Satietas is fullness, having had enough. “Having had their fill” keeps the food image, which continues in fastidium and nausia. Other reading: A weariness of doing and seeing the same things comes over some people
- *vitae non odium sed fastidium*: Fastidium is the distaste of someone who is overfed, a squeamishness. Other reading: not hatred of life but disgust with it
- *ipsa impellente philosophia*: An ablative absolute. Philosophy itself gives the push, by teaching that everything returns. Other reading: driven on by philosophy itself
- *quousque eadem?*: The Latin has no verb. The English is kept as bare. Other reading: How long must it be the same things?
- *nempe ex pergiscar dormiam, esuriam, algebo aestuabo*: Ex pergiscar is expergiscar split in two. The verbs go in opposed pairs (wake and sleep, be cold and be hot), but esuriam (be hungry) has no partner in the Latin as given; editors supply a word here (from memory, unchecked). The English translates what stands. Other reading: Of course I will wake and sleep, eat and be hungry, be cold and be hot
- *in orbem nexa sunt omnia*: Nexa: tied or woven together. In orbem: into a ring. Other reading: all things are bound into a ring
- *autumno hiemps instat*: Premit just before is “presses on”; instare is to stand close upon, to threaten. The two verbs are given different English, as Seneca varies them. Other reading: winter follows close on autumn
- *quae vere compescitur*: Vere is here the ablative of ver, “by spring”, not the adverb “truly”. Other reading: which is held in by spring
- *fit aliquando et huius rei nausia*: Nausia is seasickness, nausea. Aliquando can be “at some point” or “sometimes”; the first is taken, in answer to “how long?”. “This” is the endless return. Other reading: sometimes there comes nausea at this too
- *Multi sunt qui non acerbum iudicent vivere sed supervacuum*: Acerbus is bitter or harsh. Supervacuum is superfluous, not needed. “Judge” is kept for iudicent, as in the glossary. Other reading: There are many who think living not painful but pointless

</details>

## Letter 25: On reforming friends and imagining a watcher

### 25.5

“Do everything,” he says, “as though Epicurus were watching.” It is without doubt useful to have set a guard over oneself and to have someone you look to, someone you judge to be present at your thoughts. This is indeed far grander, to live as though under the eyes of some good man who is always present; but I am content even with this, that you do whatever you do as though someone were watching. Solitude persuades us to every evil.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquit*: “He” is Epicurus, named at the end of the section before. The saying is a fragment of Epicurus addressed to a pupil (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: “Act in everything,” says Epicurus, “as if Epicurus were looking on.”
- *custodem sibi imposuisse*: Custos is a guard, keeper or watcher. The verb custodi in the next section picks it up, so “guard” is used in both places. Other reading: to have appointed a guardian over oneself
- *habere quem respicias*: Respicere is to look back at, and so to look to or have regard for. The Latin moves here from the impersonal to “you”; the shift is kept. Other reading: to have someone you look back to
- *quem interesse cogitationibus tuis iudices*: Interesse is to be present at, or to take part in. Other reading: someone you regard as a witness of your thoughts
- *Hoc quidem longe magnificentius est*: This first “this” looks forward to living under a good man’s eyes. The second “this” (what Seneca is content with) is the lesser thing: acting as though anyone at all were watching. Other reading: It is certainly far nobler
- *alicuius boni viri ac semper praesentis*: Literally “of some good man, and one always present”. Other reading: of some good man who is always at your side
- *omnia nobis mala solitudo persuadet*: Persuadere with the thing in the accusative: to talk someone into something. Other reading: solitude urges every kind of evil on us

</details>

### 25.6

When you have made so much progress that you have respect even for yourself, you may dismiss your minder. Meanwhile guard yourself by the authority of certain men: let it be the famous Cato, or Scipio, or Laelius, or another at whose coming even depraved men would suppress their vices, while you are making yourself the man in whose company you would not dare to do wrong. When you have made yourself that, and have begun to have some standing in your own eyes, I will begin to allow you what the same Epicurus advises: “Withdraw into yourself most of all at the time when you are forced to be in a crowd.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *tui reverentia*: Reverentia is respect mixed with awe or shame before someone. Other reading: a sense of shame before yourself
- *licebit dimittas paedagogum*: The paedagogus was the slave who went about with a boy and watched his behaviour. He was a minder, not a teacher, and the image is of a child who has outgrown him. Other reading: you may send away your tutor
- *aliquorum te auctoritate custodi*: Custodi picks up custodem in the section before, so “guard”. Aliquorum is plural: some people. Other reading: keep yourself under the authority of certain men
- *aut Cato ille sit aut Scipio aut Laelius*: Ille marks Cato as the well-known one, hence “the famous Cato”. The Latin does not say which Cato (the Elder or the Younger) or which Scipio. Laelius is presumably Gaius Laelius, the friend of the younger Scipio (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: let it be that Cato, or Scipio, or Laelius
- *cuius interventu perditi quoque homines vitia supprimerent*: Interventus is a coming in upon the scene. Perditi are men morally lost, not men who have lost their money. The verb is “would suppress”, not “did suppress”. Other reading: at whose appearance even abandoned men would hold their vices in
- *dum te efficis eum cum quo peccare non audeas*: Dum with the present indicative is “while”. Cum quo is “with whom”, that is, with yourself as the witness. Peccare is to do wrong. Other reading: until you make yourself someone before whom you would not dare to go wrong
- *Cum hoc effeceris*: Effeceris repeats efficis from the clause before, so “make yourself” is repeated. Hoc is a plain “this”: the thing just described. Other reading: When you have achieved this
- *aliqua coeperit apud te tui esse dignatio*: Dignatio is esteem, the standing one has with someone. Apud te is “with you, in your judgment”. The English makes “you” the subject where the Latin has the esteem. Other reading: and some esteem for yourself has begun to exist with you
- *tunc praecipue in te ipse secede cum esse cogeris in turba*: A saying of Epicurus (a known fragment, from memory, unchecked). Secedere is to go apart, to withdraw. Other reading: Retire into yourself above all when you are compelled to be in a crowd

</details>

### 25.7

You need to become unlike the many, until it is safe for you to withdraw to yourself. Look around at people one by one: there is no one for whom it is not better to be with anyone at all than with himself. “Withdraw into yourself most of all at the time when you are forced to be in a crowd”: if you are a good man, if you are untroubled, if you are self-controlled. Otherwise you must withdraw from yourself into the crowd: there, with yourself, you are closer to a bad man. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Dissimilem te fieri multis oportet*: Multis can be “the many” (the crowd) or simply “many people”. The first is taken. Other reading: You ought to become unlike most people
- *[non]*: The brackets are taken as the editor’s sign to delete non, which is the usual convention (from memory, unchecked). The English follows the deletion. With non kept, the clause says how long Lucilius must go on becoming unlike the many. Other reading: as long as it is not safe for you to withdraw to yourself
- *dum tibi tutum*: With non deleted, dum with the subjunctive can be “until” or “provided that”. “Until” is taken, since the section before makes withdrawal something to be allowed later. Other reading: provided that it is safe for you to withdraw to yourself
- *nemo est cui non satius sit cum quolibet esse quam secum*: Satius is “better, preferable”. Cum quolibet is “with anyone you please”. Other reading: there is no one who would not be better off with anybody at all than with himself
- *si bonus vir , si quietus, si temperans*: The Latin as given has no verb here and does not construe as it stands; the stray space before the comma suggests that a word such as es has dropped out in copying. “You are” is supplied three times. Other reading: if you will be a good man, untroubled, self-controlled
- *quietus*: At rest, untroubled. “Quiet” would suggest not talking, and the glossary keeps “calm” for tranquillitas, so “untroubled” is used. Other reading: if you are calm
- *in turbam tibi a te recedendum est*: This reverses the saying: away from yourself, into the crowd. The Latin has recedere here and secedere in the saying; “withdraw” is used for both so the reversal is plain. Other reading: you must retreat from yourself into the crowd
- *istic malo viro propius es*: Istic is “there where you are”, that is, with yourself, not in the crowd. The bad man is Lucilius himself. Propius is a comparative with nothing stated to compare against, and “closer” is left hanging in the same way. Other reading: there you are too close to a bad man

</details>

## Letter 26: On old age and death as the test

### 26.2

Still, I thank myself in your presence: I do not feel the injury of age in my mind, though I feel it in my body. Only the vices and what waits on the vices have grown old: the mind is vigorous and is glad that it has not much to do with the body; it has laid down a great part of its burden. It exults and disputes with me about old age: it says that this is its flowering. Let us believe it: let it make use of its own good.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Gratias tamen mihi apud te ago*: The idiom is “to give thanks to someone before a third party”, here to himself with Lucilius as the audience. It is kept literal; “tamen” answers the section before, where he counts himself among the worn out. Other reading: Still, I congratulate myself, and I tell you so
- *aetatis iniuriam*: “Iniuria” is a wrong done by someone as well as the damage it leaves. “Injury” keeps both, where “harm” kept only the damage. Other reading: the harm of age
- *vitiorum ministeria*: “Ministeria” are services, or the staff who perform them. They are taken as the bodily parts and powers that serve the vices, and “what waits on” keeps the image of service without suggesting people. The Latin has no possessive with “vitia”, and the English has none. Other reading: the servants of my vices
- *viget animus*: “Animus” is “mind” throughout, as the glossary prefers. It is set against the body here, but not as what survives death, so “soul” is not used. Other reading: the spirit is vigorous
- *mihi facit controversiam de senectute*: A legal idiom: to raise a dispute or bring a contested case against someone. The mind contests Seneca’s claim that this is old age. Other reading: it takes me to court over old age
- *hunc ait esse florem suum*: “Hunc” is masculine by attraction to “florem” and points to old age, or to this present time of life. “Flos”, flower, is the Latin image for the prime of life; “flowering” keeps the image in a form English uses of a time of life. Other reading: it says that this is its prime
- *bono suo utatur*: “Uti” is to use and also to enjoy; the English keeps the first sense. “Bonum” is “good” as in the glossary, and the subjunctive is a permission. Other reading: let it enjoy its own good

</details>

### 26.5

“What we have shown so far, whether in deeds or in words,” I say, “is nothing. Those are slight and deceptive guarantees of what the mind is, and wrapped in much flattering ornament. As to what progress I have made, I shall take death’s word. So it is not timidly that I am readying myself for that day on which, with the tricks and the paint set aside, I shall pass judgment on myself: whether I say brave things or mean them, whether all the defiant words I have flung at fortune were a pretence and a piece of acting.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquam*: This is Seneca addressing himself, as the section before announces. The quotation opens here and does not close until the end of 26.6, so the English of this section ends with the quotation still open. Other reading: I say to myself
- *exhibuimus*: First person plural, though the rest of the speech is in the singular. The plural is kept as the Latin has it; it may be the plural of a speaker talking of himself. Other reading: What I have shown so far
- *pignora animi*: “Pignus” is a pledge or security handed over as proof, and it goes with “crediturus” (to give credit, to believe) in the next clause. “Guarantees of what the mind is” spells out the bare genitive. “Animus” could lean towards “courage” here, but “mind” is kept to match “robur animi” in 26.6. Other reading: pledges of my courage
- *multisque involuta lenociniis*: “Lenocinium” is literally a pimp’s trade, hence adornment that makes a thing look better than it is. “Flattering ornament” gives the sense and loses the sordid colour. Other reading: and wrapped up in much pandering finery
- *quid profecerim morti crediturus sum*: “Credere” with the dative is read as “believe”: he will believe death on the question of his progress. It could also be “entrust”: he will leave that question to death to settle. Other reading: what progress I have made, I am going to leave to death to decide
- *Non timide itaque componor*: “Componor” is passive with a middle sense, to compose or prepare oneself. “Non timide” is an understatement and is kept as “not timidly”, since “timidissimis” returns in 26.6. Other reading: So I am composing myself without fear
- *remotis strophis ac fucis*: “Stropha” (a Greek word) is a twist or dodge; “fucus” is red dye or face paint, hence disguise. Both are kept as images. “Set aside” renders the same verb as at the start of 26.6. Other reading: with all dodges and face paint removed
- *utrum loquar fortia an sentiam*: “Sentire” is both to feel and to think or mean. Set against mere speaking, it is rendered “mean”. Other reading: whether I speak brave things or feel them
- *simulatio fuerit et mimus*: A “mimus” is the Roman mime, a low stage piece with spoken parts, or an actor in one. “A piece of acting” keeps the stage; “farce” and “mime” both mislead in English of today. Other reading: were a pretence and a farce
- *contra fortunam iactavi verborum contumacium*: “Iactare” is to throw about and also to boast; the English keeps the throwing. “Fortune” has no capital, though as the opponent defied it is close to being personified. Other reading: all the stubborn words I have boastfully thrown out against Fortune

</details>

### 26.6

Set aside the estimate people make: it is always uncertain and is split both ways. Set aside the studies you have pursued all your life: death is going to pronounce on you. This is what I say: debates, literary conversations, words collected from the teachings of the wise, and learned talk do not show the true strength of the mind; for even the most timid have bold speech. What you have done will be seen at the moment when you breathe your last. I accept the terms; I do not shrink from the judgment.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Remove existimationem hominum*: The second person is Seneca still addressing himself: this section continues the speech opened in 26.5 and closes it at the end. “Existimatio hominum” is the estimate people form of a man, his reputation; “opinion” is avoided because the glossary keeps it for “opinio”. Other reading: Set aside what people think of you
- *in partem utramque dividitur*: Literally “is divided into each of two sides”, as of a vote or a split jury. Other reading: it is divided between the two sides
- *mors de te pronuntiatura est*: “Pronuntiare” is the word for a judge delivering a verdict. “Pronounce on you” keeps the courtroom sense that “iudicium” picks up at the end. Other reading: death is going to deliver the verdict on you
- *litterata colloquia*: “Litteratus” is lettered, educated. “Literary” is the nearest word of today, though the talk need not be about books. Other reading: cultivated conversations
- *ex praeceptis sapientium verba collecta*: “Sapientium” is plural, so “the wise” and not the glossary’s singular “the wise man”. “Verba” is “words”, as twice in 26.5, so that the contrast of words with real strength can be followed across both sections. Other reading: sayings gathered from the precepts of wise men
- *verum robur animi*: “Robur” is oak, hence hard strength. “Animus” is kept as “mind”, though “spirit” or “courage” would also fit the context. Other reading: the real toughness of one’s spirit
- *est enim oratio etiam timidissimis audax*: Dative of possession: even the most timid have bold speech. “Oratio” is speech in general, not a formal oration. Other reading: for speech is bold even in the greatest cowards
- *Quid egeris tunc apparebit cum animam ages*: A play on “agere”: “quid egeris” is “what you have done” or “achieved”, and “animam agere” is the idiom for being at the last breath, literally “driving out the soul”. English cannot keep one verb for both, so the sense is translated. Other reading: What you have accomplished will appear when you are giving up your soul
- *Accipio condicionem, non reformido iudicium*: “Condicio” is the terms of an agreement or of a trial. “Iudicium” is “judgment” as in the glossary, and here also the court hearing itself. Other reading: I accept the condition; I do not dread the trial

</details>

## Letter 28: On travel as no cure for the mind

### 28.1

Do you think this has happened to you alone, and do you wonder at it as something new, that by so long a journey and so great a variety of places you have not shaken off the sadness and heaviness of your mind? It is your mind you must change, not your sky. Though you have crossed the vast sea, though, as our Vergil says, “lands and cities fall away,” your vices will follow you wherever you arrive.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *tristitiam gravitatemque mentis*: “Mens” here and “animus” in the next sentence are both rendered “mind”: the Latin has two words, but no difference seems to be meant. “Gravitas” is heaviness, and “gravia” in 28.6 is used of oppressive places. Other reading: the gloom and weight on your spirits
- *tot locorum varietatibus*: “Varietas” is variety. It is kept apart from “change”, which is saved for “mutare” in the next sentence and “mutatio” in 28.4. Other reading: so many changes of place
- *Animum debes mutare, non caelum*: “Mutare” is to change or exchange, and “caelum” is sky, hence climate or region. In English “change your mind” usually means to alter an opinion, which is not meant: the sense is a different mind, not a different sky. Other reading: You need a different mind, not a different climate
- *Licet vastum traieceris mare*: Perfect subjunctive after “licet”: the crossing is already done when the vices follow, so “though you have crossed”. Other reading: Though you may cross the vast sea
- *terraeque urbesque recedant*: From Vergil, Aeneid 3.72, where the verb is “recedunt” (from memory, unchecked); Seneca fits it to his own sentence with a subjunctive. The Latin marks it only by naming Vergil; the quotation marks are added in the English. Other reading: though lands and cities drop back out of sight
- *sequentur te quocumque perveneris vitia*: The Latin has no “your”; it is supplied, since the vices that follow him are his own. “Vitia” is “vices” as in the glossary, though “faults” would also fit sadness and heaviness of mind. Other reading: faults will follow you wherever you get to

</details>

### 28.2

To someone who made this same complaint Socrates said: “Why do you wonder that your journeys do you no good, when it is yourself you carry around? The same cause that drove you out is pressing on you.” What help can the newness of lands be? What help is getting to know cities or places? That tossing about of yours comes to nothing. Do you ask why that flight of yours does not help you? You flee with yourself. The burden of the mind must be laid down: until then no place will please you.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Socrates ait*: Seneca gives the remark as a saying of Socrates; an earlier source for it could not be named (from memory, unchecked). It is translated as quoted speech, as the Latin marks it. Other reading: Socrates replied
- *cum te circumferas*: Literally “when you carry yourself around”: he takes on every journey the self he is trying to leave behind. “Cum” could be “when”, “since” or “although”; “when” is used. Other reading: seeing that you take yourself about with you
- *premit te eadem causa quae expulit*: “Premit” is presses, weighs on or pursues; “pressing on” leaves both the weight and the pursuit open. “Expulit” is “drove out”, that is, from home; the Latin has no “you” with it, and it is supplied. Other reading: the same cause that drove you away is at your heels
