- *in irritum cedit ista iactatio*: “Iactatio” is a tossing about, used of ships at sea and of the sick in bed; the letter goes on to use the same word of shifting cargo. The physical image is kept, and “of yours” gives the force of “ista”, which points at the person addressed. Other reading: all that restless moving about is wasted
- *tecum fugis*: Two words in the Latin: “you flee with yourself”, that is, in your own company. It is kept as short as English allows. Other reading: you take yourself along when you run away
- *Onus animi*: The genitive can mean the burden that lies on the mind or the burden that the mind is. The plain “of” leaves it as open as the Latin. Other reading: the load on your mind
- *non ante tibi ullus placebit locus*: Literally “not before will any place please you”; “before” looks back to laying the burden down. Other reading: no place will please you before that

</details>

### 28.4

But once you have removed that ill, every change of place will become pleasant. Though you are driven out to the farthest lands, though you are set down in whatever corner of barbarian country, that dwelling, whatever it is like, will be hospitable to you. Who you are when you come matters more than where you come to, and for that reason we ought not to sign our mind over to any place. One must live with this conviction: “I was not born for one corner; this whole world is my country.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *istuc exemeris malum*: “Malum” is any ill; here it is the weight sitting on him in the section before, where he is called a sick man, so “ill” and not the moral “evil”. “Eximere” is to take out or remove, as a doctor does. Other reading: once you have taken that evil out
- *in ultimas expellaris terras licebit*: “Licebit” is the conceding “licet” of 28.1 in the future tense, so “though”, as there. It governs “colloceris” as well. Other reading: You may be driven out to the farthest lands
- *in quolibet barbariae angulo colloceris*: “Barbaria” is the world that is neither Greek nor Roman; “quolibet” is “any you like”. Other reading: though you are settled in any corner of the uncivilised world you please
- *hospitalis tibi illa qualiscumque sedes erit*: “Sedes” is a seat, hence a place to stay or live. “Dwelling” is used so that “place” can be kept for “locus”, which runs through the section. Other reading: that seat, such as it is, will welcome you
- *Magis quis veneris quam quo interest*: Very compressed: literally “it matters more who you have come than where to”. It is expanded just enough to be English. Other reading: It matters more as who you arrive than where you arrive
- *nulli loco addicere debemus animum*: “Addicere” is a legal word: to adjudge or sign over property, or a debtor to his creditor as a bondsman. “Sign over” keeps the legal sense; the Latin says literally “to no place ought we to sign over the mind”. Other reading: we ought not to bind our mind in bondage to any place
- *Cum hac persuasione vivendum est*: “Persuasio” is a settled conviction; “opinion” is avoided, since the glossary keeps it for “opinio”. The gerundive is impersonal, so “one must”. Other reading: We must live in this belief
- *patria mea totus hic mundus est*: “Patria” is the native country; “mundus” is the world or the whole universe. The thought is the Cynic and Stoic one of being a citizen of the world; Seneca gives it here as a motto without naming a source. Other reading: this whole universe is my fatherland

</details>

### 28.6

Can anything be as turbulent as the forum? Even there one may live quietly, if it should be necessary. But if one might place oneself, I would flee far from even the sight and the neighbourhood of the forum; for just as oppressive places test even the strongest health, so for a good mind too, one that is not yet perfected and is recovering, there are some places that are not wholesome enough.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Num quid tam turbidum fieri potest quam forum?*: “Num” expects the answer no, as the English question does. The forum is the public square of Rome, the place of the law courts, business and politics. Other reading: Surely nothing can be as turbulent as the forum?
- *si liceat disponere se*: Literally “if it were allowed to dispose oneself”, that is, to station oneself by choice. “Liceat” repeats “licet” from the sentence before, so “may” is answered by “might”. The Latin is impersonal and then switches to the first person; the switch is kept. Other reading: if one were free to arrange one’s own life
- *fugiam*: The form is either future indicative or present subjunctive. After “si liceat” it is read as subjunctive. Other reading: I shall flee
- *loca gravia*: “Gravis” is heavy, and of a place or its air oppressive and unhealthy. “Oppressive” keeps it apart from “valetudinem” (health) and “salubria” (wholesome), and nearer to the heaviness of mind in 28.1. Other reading: unhealthy places
- *temptant*: “Temptare” is to put to the test and, of illness, to attack. “Test” is used. Other reading: attack even the strongest health
- *bonae quoque menti necdum adhuc perfectae et convalescenti*: “Bona mens” is a good or sound mind; “good” is kept, because this mind is said to be only now regaining its health. “Necdum adhuc” is a doubled “not yet” and goes with “perfectae” alone; “convalescenti” keeps the medical picture of a patient regaining strength. Other reading: for a sound mind too, as yet unfinished and gaining strength
- *sunt aliqua parum salubria*: “Aliqua” is neuter plural and can pick up “loca” (“some places”) or stand alone (“some things”). The English takes it with “loca”, since the sentence gives the reason for fleeing a place. “Parum” is “too little”, “not enough”. Other reading: some things are far from wholesome

</details>

## Letter 29: On giving advice only to the willing

### 29.1

You ask about our Marcellinus and want to know how he is doing. He seldom comes to us, for no other reason than that he is afraid to hear the truth; and that is a danger he is now clear of, for it should be spoken to no one unless he is going to hear it. That is why, about Diogenes and no less about the other Cynics who spoke freely to all alike and admonished whoever came their way, it is commonly doubted whether they ought to have done this. For what if someone were to rebuke the deaf, or those who are mute by nature or by illness?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Marcellino nostro*: “Noster” marks him as a shared acquaintance, as it marks Vergil as a shared author in 28.1. The same English is used in both places. Other reading: our friend Marcellinus
- *quid agat*: The everyday idiom for “how he is” or “what he is up to”; with what follows it also asks how he is getting on morally. Other reading: what he is doing
- *Raro ad nos venit*: “Nos” may be a true plural (Seneca and those around him) or the plural an author uses for himself. The plural is kept. Other reading: He seldom comes to me
- *a quo periculo iam abest*: Literally “from which danger he is now away”. With the next clause it is taken that Seneca has stopped telling him the truth, since he will not listen; it could also mean only that he is safe by staying away. Other reading: though he is by now far from that risk
- *nulli enim nisi audituro dicendum est*: “Audituro” is “one who is going to hear”, with the sense of listening. “Hear” is kept to match “afraid to hear the truth” before it and the deaf after it; the “it” (the truth) is supplied. Other reading: for one should speak only to a man who will listen
- *de Diogene*: Diogenes of Sinope, the best known of the Cynic philosophers, famous for accosting people in the street with blunt reproofs. Other reading: about Diogenes the Cynic
- *libertate promiscua*: “Libertas” here is freedom of speech, frankness; “promiscua” is used on all alike, without choosing. The English turns the noun into “spoke freely” so that the kind of freedom is plain. Other reading: who used an indiscriminate freedom
- *dubitari solet*: “Solet” is “it is usual”, so “commonly”. The Latin is impersonal and the English keeps it so. Other reading: people often question
- *obvios monuerunt*: “Obvii” are those met in the road; “monere” is to advise, warn or admonish. Other reading: gave advice to anyone they met
- *natura morbove mutos*: The mute are added beside the deaf, presumably because those mute from birth are usually deaf as well; the Latin does not say so. “Obiurget” is stronger than “monuerunt”, so “rebuke”. Other reading: or people dumb from birth or from disease

</details>

### 29.3

This, my Lucilius, I do not think a great man should do: his authority is watered down and does not have enough weight with those whom it could set right if it were less cheapened. An archer ought not to hit now and then, but to miss now and then; that is not an art which comes to its result by chance. Wisdom is an art: let it aim at what is sure, let it choose those who will make progress, let it draw back from those of whom it has despaired, yet let it not abandon them quickly, and in despair itself let it try remedies of last resort.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Hoc, mi Lucili*: “This” is the practice defended in the section before: admonishing many people at random in the hope that some of it works. “Mi” is “my”; an English letter would say “my dear”, which adds a word. Other reading: That, my dear Lucilius
- *diluitur eius auctoritas*: “Diluere” is to dilute, as wine with water; the image is kept. Other reading: his authority is weakened
- *minus obsolefacta*: “Obsolefacta” is made common, worn by overuse, like a coin or a garment. It agrees with “auctoritas”: authority that had been made less common. Other reading: if it had not grown so stale
- *non aliquando ferire debet, sed aliquando deerrare*: The point lies in the repeated “aliquando” (“sometimes”, “now and then”): hitting should be the rule and missing the exception. The repetition is kept, and “now and then” is used because “ought not to hit sometimes” can be misread as “ought sometimes not to hit”. Other reading: ought not to hit the mark only now and then, but to miss it only now and then
- *Sapientia ars est*: “Ars” is a skill or craft with a method, as against luck. “Art” is used to match the “arts” of 29.11 and 29.12. Other reading: Wisdom is a skill
- *certum petat*: “Certum” is something definite or sure: a fixed mark for the archer, or a result that can be counted on. The English takes “what is sure”, against “by chance” just before, and lets “aim” carry the archer on. Other reading: let it aim at a definite mark
- *eligat profecturos*: The participle could come from “proficere” (to make progress, to benefit) or from “proficisci” (to set out). The sense requires the first. Other reading: let it pick out those who will benefit
- *ab iis quos desperavit recedat, non tamen cito relinquat*: Wisdom is to draw back from the hopeless, yet not abandon them quickly; “recedat” and “relinquat” are kept apart as “draw back” and “abandon”. The “them” is supplied. Other reading: let it withdraw from those it has given up on, but not be quick to desert them
- *in ipsa desperatione extrema remedia temptet*: “Extrema” can go with “remedia” (the last and most drastic remedies a doctor has) or with “desperatione” (“in utmost despair itself”). It is taken with “remedia” and rendered “of last resort”. Other reading: and even in utter despair let it try remedies

</details>

### 29.11

“Who said this?” you ask, as if you did not know whom I order to pay. Epicurus. But all of them, from every house, will shout this same thing to you together: Peripatetics, Academics, Stoics, Cynics. For who can please the people if virtue pleases him? The favour of the people is sought by bad arts. You must make yourself like those people: they will not approve of you unless they recognise you. But what you seem to yourself matters far more than what you seem to others; the love of base people cannot be won except by base means.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quis hoc?*: The Latin has no verb: “Who this?”; “said” is supplied. “This” is the saying quoted in the section before, that he never wished to please the people. In the Latin as given a stray character stands before “Quis” where an opening quotation mark belongs, a slip of the digitised copy. Other reading: “Whose is this?”
- *tamquam nescias cui imperem*: “Imperare” with a dative is to give orders to someone and also to levy on him, to demand payment or supplies. The section before is all in the language of debt and instalments, so the levy sense is taken; the one made to pay is Epicurus. Other reading: as if you did not know whom I give my orders to
- *ex omni domo*: “House” is the household of a philosophical school; it is kept as an image, since the list of schools follows at once. “Conclamabunt” is to shout all together. Other reading: from every school
- *Quis enim placere populo potest cui placet virtus?*: The sentence turns on “placere” twice, so “please” is used both times, and “the people” for “populus” to match the next sentence. Other reading: For who that likes virtue can be liked by the public?
- *malis artibus*: “Artes” are arts, skills or practices; “bad arts” is the Latin phrase for dishonest methods. “Arts” is kept because 29.3 calls wisdom an art and 29.12 sets philosophy above all “arts”. Other reading: by dishonest means
- *Similem te illis facias oportet*: “Illis” is plural by sense after the singular “populus”: the people whose favour is sought, not the philosophers just listed. The English writes “those people” so that this cannot be mistaken. Other reading: You must make yourself like them
- *non probabunt nisi agnoverint*: Neither verb has an object in the Latin; English needs them, and “you” is supplied twice. “Agnoscere” is also to acknowledge as one of one’s own, which is the point: they must find their own likeness in him. Other reading: they will not approve of you unless they recognise themselves in you
- *nisi turpi ratione*: “Ratio” here is a way or method, not “reason”; the glossary allows this. “Turpis” is base or shameful, and it is repeated in “turpium”, so “base” is used twice. Other reading: except in a shameful way
- *amor turpium*: “Turpium” is taken as persons: the love that base people give. Grammatically it could be neuter, “love of base things”, but that does not fit “conciliari” (to win over). Other reading: the affection of shameful people

</details>

### 29.12

What, then, will that philosophy deliver, which is praised and is to be preferred to all arts and all things? This, of course: that you would rather please yourself than the people, that you assess judgments and do not count them, that you live without fear of gods and men, that you either overcome evils or end them. But if I see you celebrated by the approving voices of the crowd, if when you come in there is a din of shouting and applause, the honours of a pantomime dancer, if throughout the whole city women and children praise you, why should I not pity you, since I know what road leads to that favour? Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *praestabit*: “Praestare” is to deliver, provide or make good on what was promised. Other reading: What, then, will that philosophy do for you
- *illa laudata*: “Illa” points to philosophy as something well known and much spoken of; “laudata” is a bare “praised”. Other reading: that much praised philosophy
- *omnibus praeferenda artibus rebusque*: “Artibus” echoes the “bad arts” of 29.11, so “arts” is kept. “Rebus” is simply “things”; the second “all” is repeated in English for clarity. Other reading: to be ranked above every skill and every possession
- *scilicet*: “Scilicet” is “of course”, “namely”, sometimes with irony. It is taken here as a plain answer to his own question. Other reading: Why, this
- *ut aestimes iudicia, non numeres*: “Aestimare” is to assess the worth of a thing; “numerare” is to count. What matters is how good the judgments passed on a man are, not how many there are. Other reading: that you weigh judgments and do not count them
- *ut aut vincas mala aut finias*: “Finias” (“end them”) probably looks to ending troubles by ending life, a thought common in these letters, but the Latin says only “end”. The English is left as open as the Latin. Other reading: that you either conquer your ills or put an end to them
- *secundis vocibus vulgi*: “Secundus” is favourable, like a following wind. “Vulgus” is the crowd or common mass, a more contemptuous word than “populus” above, so “people” is not used. Other reading: by the cheers of the mob
- *pantomimica ornamenta*: In apposition to “shouting and applause”: these are the distinctions that belong to a pantomime, the solo stage dancer who was the popular star of Seneca’s Rome. “Ornamenta” are trappings or marks of honour. Other reading: the trappings of a stage dancer
- *feminae puerique*: “Pueri” can be boys or children in general. The pair is taken as the usual Latin way of naming all who are not grown men, so “women and children”; they stand for those whose praise carried least weight in Roman eyes. Other reading: women and boys
- *cum sciam*: “Cum” with the subjunctive gives the reason here, so “since”. Other reading: when I know

</details>

## Letter 30: On an old man's calm before death

### 30.4

This is a great thing, Lucilius, and one that has to be learned over a long time: to go away with an even mind when that hour which cannot be avoided draws near. Other kinds of death are mixed with hope: an illness comes to an end, a fire is put out, a falling building has set down the people it seemed about to crush; the sea has thrown out unharmed those it had swallowed, with the same force with which it was sucking them down; a soldier has drawn his sword back from the very neck of the man who was about to die. The man whom old age is leading to death has nothing to hope for; against this alone no one can step in. By no kind of death do people die more gently, but by none more slowly either.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *diu discenda*: The Latin form carries obligation: the thing must be learned, and over a long time. The English says both. Other reading: one that takes a long time to learn
- *aequo animo abire*: Aequo animo is literally “with a level or even mind”. “Mind” is kept for animus as the glossary prefers, and “even” leaves “calm” free for tranquillitas. Abire is the plain verb for leaving, used here of dying, and the image is kept. Other reading: to depart calmly; to leave without complaint
- *spei mita sunt*: Mita is not a Latin word. It is read as a copying slip for mixta (“mixed”), which the sense requires. Other reading: have hope mingled with them
- *ruina quos videbatur oppressura deposuit*: Ruina is the collapse of a building, or the fallen mass itself. Deposuit is “has set down”, as one sets down a load, so the picture is of the wreck lowering people gently instead of crushing them. Other reading: a collapse has let down safely those it seemed about to bury
- *eadem vi qua sorbebat*: Sorbere is to suck in or gulp down. The phrase is taken with the throwing out: the same force that was drawing them in threw them out. Other reading: as violently as it had been swallowing them
- *re vocavit*: A word split in two in the copy, read as revocavit, literally “has called back”. With a sword as its object the English says “drawn back”. The soldier is an executioner who stops at the last moment. Other reading: has called his sword back
- *huic uni intercedi non potest*: Intercedere is the public-law word for lodging a veto, and the form here is impersonal. The English takes “this” as the thing a veto would block, the death that old age brings. It can also be the old man, on whose behalf no one can step in. Other reading: for him alone no one can step in
- *sed nec diutius*: Diutius is “for a longer time”. It is taken of the dying itself being long drawn out. Other reading: but in none do they take longer over it either

</details>

### 30.10

You may count Bassus among these, a man who did not want us to be deceived. He says that a man who fears death is as much a fool as one who fears old age; for just as old age follows youth, so death follows old age. Whoever does not want to die did not want to live; for life was given with death as its proviso; it is towards death that one goes. To fear it is what a madman does, for this reason: what is certain is waited for, what is doubtful is feared.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Inter hos*: “These” are the people of the section before: those who have stood close to death, seen it coming and received it. The pronoun is left as it is. Other reading: Among such witnesses
- *qui nos decipi noluit*: Noluit can be a plain “did not want” or a stronger “refused to let”. The plain sense is kept. Other reading: who refused to let us be deceived
- *adulescentiam*: Roman adulescentia runs from the mid teens to about thirty, so it is wider than English “adolescence”. The English says “youth”. Other reading: young manhood
- *cum exceptione mortis*: Exceptio is a legal term: a reserving clause or proviso attached to a grant. The literal “with the exception of death” would say the opposite of what is meant, so the English gives the legal sense. Other reading: life was granted subject to the reservation of death
- *ad hanc itur*: An impersonal form, literally “there is a going towards this”. “This” is most naturally death, the nearest noun, and the English names death so that the pronoun is clear. It could conceivably point to the proviso. Other reading: it is towards this proviso that one goes
- *dementis est*: Literally “is of a madman”, that is, the act or mark of one. The English says “is what a madman does”. Other reading: is the mark of a madman
- *certa exspectantur, dubia metuuntur*: A general maxim in the passive. The point rests on the contrast between waiting for and fearing, so both verbs are kept plain. Other reading: certain things are expected, uncertain things are dreaded

</details>

### 30.11

Death has a necessity that is fair and cannot be overcome: who can complain of being in a condition that no one is not in? And the first part of fairness is equality. But at this point it is needless to plead the case of nature, who wanted our law to be no other than her own: whatever she has put together she takes apart, and whatever she has taken apart she puts together again.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *necessitatem habet aequam et invictam*: Aequus means both “fair” and “equal, the same for all”. “Fair” is used so that it leads into “fairness” (aequitas) in the next sentence; the other sense is what the question about everyone’s condition goes on to state. Invictam is literally “unconquered”, in the usual sense of what cannot be conquered. Other reading: Death brings a necessity that is the same for all and invincible
- *in qua nemo non est*: A double negative, literally “in which no one is not”, meaning that everyone is in it. The negative form is kept, since the translator’s rules ask that every “not” be kept. Other reading: a condition that everyone shares
- *prima autem pars est aequitatis aequalitas*: A play on two words from one root: aequitas (fairness) and aequalitas (equality), both echoing aequam above. English cannot keep the echo. Prima pars may be “first part” or “chief part”. Other reading: and the chief element of fairness is equality
- *naturae causam agere*: Causam agere is the courtroom idiom for pleading a case as an advocate. The legal colour continues in lex (“law”) and condicio (“condition, terms”). Other reading: to act as counsel for nature
- *quid quid composuit resolvit*: Quid quid is a split quidquid, “whatever”. Resolvit can be present or past in form. The first is taken as present (“takes apart”), answering componit, and the second, which sits inside its “whatever” clause as composuit does, as past (“has taken apart”). Other reading: and whatever she takes apart she puts together again

</details>

### 30.12

And further, if it has been anyone’s lot that old age should let him go gently, not torn suddenly from life but drawn away little by little, oh, that man truly ought to give thanks to all the gods that, having had his fill, he has been brought through to a rest that is necessary for a human being and welcome to a tired one. You see some people wishing for death, and indeed more than life is usually begged for. I do not know which of the two I should think give us more courage, those who demand death or those who wait for it cheerful and quiet, since the first sometimes comes from frenzy and sudden indignation, while this calm comes from a settled judgment. Someone has come to death angry: no one has received death cheerfully when it came, except a man who had long made himself ready for it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *leviter emitteret*: Emittere is to send out or release; it is also used of freeing a slave and of discharging a soldier. The plain “let go” is kept. Leviter is “lightly, gently”. Other reading: should release him gently; should discharge him gently
- *o ne ille*: Ne here is the affirming particle (“truly, indeed”), not the negative. It is translated “truly”. Other reading: oh, he indeed
- *satiatus*: Literally “sated, filled full”, as after a meal. The picture is of a guest who has had enough. Other reading: having had enough of life
- *magis quam rogari solet vita*: Magis is simply “more”, and the English adds nothing to it. Rogari is to be asked or begged for, as a condemned man begs for his life. Other reading: more than people usually ask for life
- *maiorem nobis animum dare*: Animus is “mind” in the glossary, but here the sense is courage or heart, which the glossary allows. Other reading: give us greater spirit; put more heart into us
- *haec ex iudicio certo tranquillitas est*: The Latin sets a neuter “that” (the demanding of death) against “this calm”. “Calm” is the glossary’s word for tranquillitas and “judgment” for iudicium. Certo is “fixed, settled, sure”. Other reading: the latter is a calm that comes from firm judgment
- *Venit aliquis ad mortem iratus*: Venit can be present (“comes”) or past (“has come”); the spelling does not show which. The English takes it as past, to match the past forms in the second half of the sentence. The sentence plays on the man coming to death and death coming to the man, kept by “come” and “came”. Other reading: Someone comes to death angry
- *se ad illam diu composuerat*: Se componere is to compose, settle or arrange oneself. It is taken as making oneself ready over a long time. Other reading: had long been composing himself for it

</details>

### 30.16

He used to say that this torment at least we feel by our own doing: that we tremble just when we believe death is near us. For who is there that it is not near, ready as it is in every place and at every moment? “But let us consider,” he said, “just when some cause of dying seems to be approaching, how much nearer other causes are that are not feared.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *aiebat*: No subject is expressed. From the run of the letter the speaker is Bassus, the old man whose talk Seneca is reporting; the section does not name him, so the English has “He”. Other reading: Bassus used to say
- *nostra nos sentire opera*: The phrase says that the effort or agency is the sufferers’ own: the torment is self-made. Other reading: that we have ourselves to thank for feeling this torment
- *quod tunc trepidamus*: Quod can explain what the torment is (“namely that”) or give a cause (“because”). It is taken as explaining. Trepidare is to be in a flurry of fear; the English says “tremble”. Other reading: because we panic just when
- *tunc cum*: Tunc with cum is “then, when”, pointing at the moment. “Just when” is used in both places where the pair occurs. Other reading: at the time when
- *a quo enim non prope est*: Literally “for from whom is it not near?”. The question is in direct form, so it may be Seneca’s own aside or part of what Bassus said. It is left outside the quotation marks, as in the Latin. Other reading: (still reported speech) for, he said, who is there that it is not near
- *inquit*: The form serves as present and as past. Since the reporting verb before it is past, the English has “he said”. Other reading: he says

</details>

## Letter 31: On trusting yourself and scorning popular goods

### 31.5

Hard work feeds noble minds. So there is no reason for you to pick out, from that old prayer of your parents, what you would want to come to you, what you would wish for; and altogether, for a man who has already passed through the greatest things, it is shameful still to be wearying the gods. What need is there of prayers? Make yourself fortunate, you yourself; and you will, if you understand that those things are good which have virtue mixed into them, and those shameful which have wickedness joined to them. Just as nothing is bright without a mixture of light, and nothing is black unless it has darkness in it or has drawn something dark into itself; just as nothing is hot without the help of fire, and nothing cold without air; so it is partnership with virtue and with wickedness that makes things honourable and shameful.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Generosos animos labor nutrit*: Labor is toil, exertion, hardship; the section before has just said it is not itself a good. Generosus is “well-born” and so “noble”. “Minds” is kept for animos, though “spirits” is possible. Other reading: Toil nourishes spirits of good stock
- *ex illo vetere parentum tuorum*: As given, the Latin has no noun for illo vetere (“that old one of your parents”) to agree with. “Prayer” is supplied in the English because the next sentence asks what need there is of vota (“prayers”); printed texts are believed to read voto here (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: from that old (thing) of your parents
- *iam per maxima acto viro*: Literally “for a man already driven through the greatest things”. Maxima may be the highest offices, a full public career, or great affairs and trials generally. The English leaves that open and turns the passive into the plainer “passed through”. Other reading: for a man who has already been taken through the highest offices
- *etiam nunc*: The phrase is “even now” in the sense of “still”: he goes on doing it. The English says “still”. Other reading: even now
- *deos fatigare*: The stock phrase for pestering the gods with prayers. The image of tiring them is kept. Other reading: to keep importuning the gods
- *fac te ipse felicem*: Felix is “fortunate, favoured, prosperous”, the thing people pray for, and it can also be “happy”. “Fortunate” is used so that “happy” stays with beatus, as in the glossary. Ipse stresses that Lucilius, not the gods, is to do it. Other reading: Make yourself happy by your own doing; make your own good fortune
- *malitia coniuncta est*: Malitia is the opposite of virtus here, the Stoic term for badness of character, not “malice” in the English sense. “Wickedness” is used throughout the passage and “vice” is left for vitium, as in the glossary. Other reading: with which vice is combined; with which badness is joined
- *nihil sine aere frigidum*: In Stoic physics air is the cold element as fire is the hot one (from memory, unchecked), so cold things are cold by having air in them. The translation is literal. Other reading: nothing is cold without (an admixture of) air
- *virtutis ac malitiae societas*: Societas is partnership or association. The two nouns are taken as what a thing is associated with (virtue makes it honourable, wickedness shameful), not as virtue and wickedness associating with each other. “Honourable” follows the glossary’s rendering of honestum. Other reading: the company of virtue and of wickedness is what produces the honourable and the shameful

</details>

## Letter 32: On completing life before death

### 32.3

So hurry, dearest Lucilius, and think how much you would add to your speed if an enemy were bearing down on you from behind, if you suspected that cavalry were coming up and pressing on the tracks of those in flight. This is happening: you are being pressed. Speed up and get away, bring yourself through to safety, and consider again and again what a fine thing it is to complete one’s life before death, and then to wait free from care for the remaining part of one’s time, waiting for nothing for oneself, settled in possession of the happy life, which does not become happier if it is longer.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quantum additurus celeritati fueris*: A future participle with fueris, standing for an unreal “would add” inside an indirect question. It could also be heard as “would have added”. Other reading: how much you would have added to your speed
- *instaret*: Instare is to press hard upon, to threaten at close quarters. “Bearing down on” is used so that “press” is kept for premere alone, the verb that the next sentence repeats. Other reading: if an enemy were pressing on you from behind
- *equitem*: Singular, which can mean one horseman or, collectively, the cavalry. It is taken collectively. Other reading: that a horseman was coming up
- *fugientium premer vestigia*: Premer is a slip for premere (a letter dropped). Premere vestigia is to tread close on someone’s tracks. “Press” is used because the next sentence repeats the verb (premeris, “you are being pressed”). Other reading: and was hard on the heels of the fugitives
- *subinde*: Can mean “repeatedly”, “from time to time” or “immediately after”. The first is taken. Other reading: and from time to time consider; and then at once consider
- *consummare vitam ante mortem*: Consummare is to bring to its full sum, to finish off or complete. The thought is of a life made whole before it ends. Other reading: to bring life to its full sum before death
- *securum*: Glossary: securitas is freedom from care. The word describes the unnamed subject of “to wait”, a general “one”, which is kept. Other reading: to wait without anxiety
- *nihil sibi*: As given, these two words have no verb or participle to govern them and do not construe. The English repeats “wait” from exspectare as a stopgap (“waiting for nothing for oneself”). A word has probably fallen out, and printed editions are believed to mark or mend the text here (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: (literally) nothing for oneself; (with a word supplied) promising oneself nothing, or relying on oneself

</details>

### 32.5

I wish for you to have yourself at your own disposal: that your mind, driven about by wandering thoughts, may at last come to a stand and be settled; that it may be content with itself and, once it has understood the true goods, which are possessed the moment they are understood, may have no need of added years. That man alone has got beyond necessities, and is discharged from service and free, who lives with his life completed.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *tui facultatem*: Facultas is having something available, the means or free disposal of it. It answers the abundance of things that Lucilius’s parents prayed for: the wish is that he should have himself. “Command of yourself” is avoided because it suggests self-control. Other reading: possession of yourself; access to yourself; command of yourself
- *resistat et certa sit*: Resistere here is to halt, to stand still, not to resist. Certa, set against the “wandering” thoughts, is “fixed, settled”. Other reading: may at last stop and be sure
- *placeat sibi*: Literally “may please itself”, that is, be satisfied with itself. The English “pleased with itself” suggests smugness, which is not meant. Other reading: may be pleased with itself
- *aetatis adiectione*: Literally “an addition of lifetime”. The English says “added years”. Other reading: may not need any extension of its span
- *Ille demum*: Demum marks the one case that at last qualifies: “he and only he”, “he at length”. The English says “That man alone”. Other reading: He at last
- *necessitates supergressus est*: Necessitates may be needs, or the compulsions and constraints of life. The wider “necessities” is kept. Other reading: has risen above all needs
- *exauctoratus*: A military term: released from the soldier’s oath, discharged from service. “From service” is added in the English so that the image is heard. Other reading: released from his oath
- *vivit vita peracta*: A deliberate paradox: he lives when his life has already been carried through to the end. Peracta is “finished, played through”, as of a play or a task. Other reading: who lives after his life has been finished

</details>

## Letter 35: On steadiness and becoming a true friend

### 35.4

Hurry to me, but to yourself first. Make progress, and before all else take care of this: that you be consistent with yourself. Whenever you want to test whether anything has been achieved, watch whether you want the same things today as yesterday: a change in what one wants shows that the mind is adrift, turning up now in one place and now in another, as the wind has carried it. What is fixed and set on a foundation does not wander: that falls to the perfect wise man, and to some extent also to the one who is making progress and has advanced. What then is the difference? The latter is indeed shaken, yet he does not shift his ground, but sways in his own place; the former is not even shaken. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ut constes tibi*: Constare sibi is to stand firm with oneself, to be consistent. It is the verb behind constantia, which the glossary gives as “steadiness”; “be steady with yourself” is not natural English, so the English says “consistent”. Other reading: that you stay steady and true to yourself
- *an aliquid actum sit*: Literally “whether anything has been done”, that is, whether any ground has been gained. Other reading: whether you have got anywhere
- *mutatio voluntatis*: Voluntas is the noun of velle, “to want”, which has just been used (velis). The English keeps “want” for both so that the link is heard. The Latin noun is general and not in the second person, hence “one”. Other reading: a change of will
- *animum natare*: Natare is to swim or float; here to float about with nothing to hold it, as the wind in the next clause shows. “Adrift” keeps the image. Other reading: that the mind is afloat; that the mind is at sea
- *fixum atque fundatum*: Fundatum is “set on a foundation”. The image of a building is kept. Other reading: fixed and firmly grounded
- *proficienti provectoque*: Proficiens is the Stoic term for the person who is making progress but is not yet wise; it picks up the command Profice at the start. Provectus is “carried forward, advanced”. One person is meant, not two. Other reading: to the one who is progressing and well advanced
- *hic commovetur quidem*: Hic is the nearer of the two just named (the one making progress), ille the wise man. Commovetur is “is set in motion, shaken”; “shaken” is used for both occurrences. Other reading: the one is moved, to be sure
- *non tamen transit*: Transire is to go across. It is taken as leaving the spot where he stands, in contrast with swaying in his own place. Other reading: yet he does not cross over; yet he does not go over (to the other side)
- *suo loco nutat*: Nutare is to nod, sway or totter. He rocks but stays where he is. Other reading: he wavers where he stands

</details>

## Letter 36: On the benefits of a quiet life

### 36.10

But if so great a desire for a longer life has hold of you, consider that none of the things that go out of sight and are put back into the nature of things, from which they came out and will soon come forth, is used up: these things stop, they do not perish, and death, which we dread and refuse, interrupts life, it does not snatch it away; a day will come again to put us back into the light, a day that many would refuse if it did not bring them back with their memory gone.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *longioris aevi tenet?*: The copy puts a question mark after the if-clause. The English sets it aside and reads the clause as a condition leading into “consider”, which is believed to be the usual punctuation (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: But what if so great a desire for a longer life has hold of you? Consider that
- *in rerum naturam*: “The nature of things” is the glossary’s rendering of rerum natura. Here it means nature as the store from which things come and to which they return. Other reading: into nature; into the universe
- *ac mox processura sunt*: Two different verbs of coming out stand side by side, prodierunt and processura sunt, given as “came out” and “come forth”. The Latin has no “again”. Other reading: and from which they will soon come out again
- *reconduntur consumi*: Recondere is to put back in store, to lay away again. Consumi is to be used up or destroyed; “used up” keeps the store image. Other reading: are laid away again; is destroyed
- *desinunt ista, non pereunt*: Desinere is to leave off, to cease. The contrast is between stopping and being lost for good. Other reading: they cease, they are not lost
- *recusamus*: The same verb returns at the end (recusarent), so “refuse” is used both times. It is the word for declining or objecting to something offered. Other reading: which we dread and shrink from
- *veniet iterum qui nos in lucem reponat dies*: The Stoic teaching that the world and everything in it returns (from memory, unchecked). The Latin says that once again a day will come, a day such as to put people back, not that the same day returns. Reponat is “put back”, as into a place once held. Other reading: the day will come again that restores us to the light
- *nisi oblitos reduceret*: Oblitos is “having forgotten”, with no object stated; what is forgotten is presumably the earlier life. The English says “with their memory gone” and adds no object. The word agrees with an unexpressed object, either the many or the people put back into the light. Other reading: if it did not bring us back with no memory of the past

</details>

### 36.11

But later I will show more carefully that all the things that seem to perish are changed. A man who is going to come back ought to go out with an even mind. Watch the circle of things as they return upon themselves: you will see that nothing in this world is extinguished, but that things go down and rise by turns. Summer has gone, but another year will bring it; winter has fallen away, its own months will bring it back; night has buried the sun, but day will at once drive night itself away. That running about of the stars comes back to whatever it has passed; one part of the sky is continually being lifted, one part is being sunk.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *mutari*: Simply “are changed”. The Latin has no “only” or “merely”, so none is added. Other reading: undergo change
- *Aequo animo debet rediturus exire*: Aequo animo as in Ep. 30.4, “with an even mind”. Exire is to go out, to leave, used of dying; rediturus is “one who is going to return”. Other reading: He who will return should depart calmly
- *orbem rerum in se remeantium*: Orbis is a ring or circuit. Remeare is to travel back; in se is “into, upon themselves”. Other reading: the cycle of things returning on their own tracks
- *in hoc mundo*: Mundus is the ordered world, the universe, not just the earth. Other reading: in this universe
- *exstingui*: Literally to be quenched or put out, as a flame. “Extinguished” keeps the image. Other reading: is put out; is annihilated
- *Aestas abit*: Abit as written is a present (“goes”); it can also be a shortened past form (“has gone”). The English takes it as past, beside cecidit (“has fallen”), and treats obruit (“has buried”) the same way. Other reading: Summer goes; night buries the sun
- *hiemps cecidit*: Literally “winter has fallen”, that is, dropped away or ended, matching the going down and rising of the sentence before. “Away” is added because in English “winter has fallen” would be heard as “has arrived”, the opposite of the sense. Other reading: winter has gone down
- *Stellarum iste discursus*: Discursus is a running in different directions, here the stars moving on their several courses. “Running about” is used; the return is said by the verb that follows. Other reading: That running to and fro of the stars; that coursing of the stars
- *quidquid praeterit repetit*: Praeterit can be present (“passes”) or a shortened past form (“has passed”). Repetere is to make for again, to return to: the stars come round again to the points they have passed. Other reading: returns to whatever it passes; fetches back whatever has gone by

</details>

## Letter 37: On the oath to philosophy and reason

### 37.5

You will not show me anyone who knows how he began to want what he wants: he was not led to it by deliberation but flung into it by impulse. Fortune collides with us no less often than we with it. It is shameful not to go but to be carried, and suddenly, in the middle of the whirlwind of things, to ask in a daze, “How did I get here?” Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Neminem mihi dabis*: Literally “you will give me no one”, the idiom for producing an example. Other reading: You will not find me a single person
- *consilio adductus illo*: Illo can be the adverb “to that point” (led there by deliberation), which is the reading taken, or go with consilio (“by that plan”). Other reading: he was not brought by that plan
- *impetu impactus est*: A play of sound, and both words are of violent motion: impetus is a rush or impulse, impingere to dash one thing against another. He did not walk to his wish; he was flung at it. Other reading: but was hurled into it by a rush
- *fortuna in nos incurrit*: Incurrere is to run into, to collide with or charge at. Fortune stands first in the English sentence and is then “it”, since nothing here treats it as a person; the glossary allows “Fortune” and “her” where it is one. Other reading: Fortune charges at us no less often than we at her
- *non ire sed ferri*: Ire is to go on one’s own feet; ferri to be borne or swept along. Other reading: not to walk but to be swept along
- *in medio turbine rerum stupentem*: Turbo is a whirlwind or spinning eddy; res is “things, affairs, events”. Stupentem is “stunned, dazed”. Other reading: dumbfounded in the middle of the whirl of events

</details>

## Letter 41: On the god within us

### 41.1

You are doing a very good thing, and one that is healthy for you, if, as you write, you keep on making your way towards a sound mind. It is foolish to pray for this when you can obtain it from yourself. There is no need to lift hands to heaven, or to beg the temple-keeper to admit us to the ear of the statue, as if we could be heard better: god is near you, he is with you, he is within.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *rem optimam*: A superlative with no article: it can be “the best thing” or “a very good thing”. The English takes the weaker sense, since nothing is being compared. Other reading: You are doing the best thing
- *tibi salutarem*: Salutaris covers “healthy”, “wholesome” and “saving”. The English keeps the medical image, which returns at the end of the letter (ad salutem, 41.9). Other reading: one that will save you
- *bonam mentem*: Bona mens is a set phrase for a right state of mind. Mens, like animus, is “mind”, and the English reads “a sound mind”. Other reading: a good mind; good sense
- *quam stultum est optare*: Optare is “to wish for” and also “to pray for”. Since the next sentence is about prayer in a temple, the English reads “pray for”. The relative clause is made a separate sentence. Other reading: which it is foolish to wish for
- *Non sunt ad caelum elevandae manus*: The gerundive with non can mean “need not be” or “must not be”. The English takes it as “there is no need” and keeps it impersonal, as the Latin has it. Other reading: Hands must not be lifted to heaven
- *aedituus*: The attendant who keeps a temple and controls access to the cult statue. Other reading: the sacristan; the temple warden
- *prope est a te deus*: Latin has no article, so this can be “god”, “a god” or “the god”. The English reads “god” with a small letter, following the glossary. The last clause has no “you” in the Latin, so the English reads “within”. Other reading: a god is near you, with you, inside you

</details>

### 41.2

This is what I say, Lucilius: a sacred spirit sits within us, the observer and guardian of the bad and the good that are ours. As it has been treated by us, so it treats us in its turn. And indeed no one is a good man without god: or can anyone rise above fortune unless helped by him? It is he who gives advice that is grand and lofty. In each and every good man (which god is uncertain) a god lives.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *sacer intra nos spiritus*: Spiritus is “breath” as well as “spirit”, and may stand for the Stoic breath that runs through things. The English reads “sacred spirit” and avoids “holy spirit” because of its Christian ring. Other reading: a holy breath sits within us
- *malorum bonorumque nostrorum*: Literally “of the bad things and good things that are ours”. It can mean what is done badly and well, or the bad and good that befall a person. The English leaves it open. Other reading: of our bad and good deeds
- *observator et custos*: Custos is one who keeps watch over something, whether to protect it or to hold it to account. The English reads “guardian”. Other reading: watcher and keeper
- *sine deo*: Again no article: “without god” or “without a god”. The English keeps “god” so that “by him” in the next clause has a clear reference. Other reading: without a god
- *consilia magnifica et erecta*: Consilia can be advice given or plans and purposes formed. Erecta is literally “standing upright”, here in the sense of raised high, so the English reads “lofty”; “upright” would suggest honesty. Other reading: He gives purposes that are great and high-minded
- *[quis deus incertum est] habitat deus*: These words are from Vergil (Aeneid 8.352, said there of a grove and hill; from memory, unchecked), where “which god is uncertain” is a parenthesis. Square brackets in this copy elsewhere mark words an editor cuts; here they are taken to stand for Vergil’s parenthesis, since the words construe and belong to the line. The Latin has no quotation marks, so the English has none. Other reading: In each and every good man a god lives. (Bracketed words left out as a deletion.)

</details>

### 41.8

Praise in him what can neither be snatched away nor given, what is man’s own. You ask what that is? The mind, and reason made perfect in the mind. For man is a reasoning animal; and so his good is brought to completion if he has fulfilled what he is born for.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *in illo*: “Him” is the man of the section before, who is praised for his household, house and wealth, things that are around him and not in him. Other reading: Praise in that man
- *proprium hominis*: Proprium is “one’s own” and also “peculiar to”. Hominis can be “of the man” in question or “of man” as a kind. The English reads “man’s own”, with no article so that humankind is meant, and keeps the link with the section before, where what is praised is what is the thing’s own. Other reading: what is peculiar to a human being
- *animus et ratio in animo perfecta*: Animus is “mind” and ratio is “reason”, as in the glossary. Perfecta agrees with ratio only: it is reason that is perfected, not the mind. Other reading: mind, and complete reason in the mind
- *Rationale enim animal*: Rationale comes from ratio. The English reads “reasoning” so that a reader sees the link with “reason” in the sentence before. Animal is any living creature. Other reading: For man is a rational living creature
- *consummatur itaque bonum eius*: Consummatur is “is brought to its full sum”, “is completed”. Bonum is “the good”, here “his good”, meaning man’s. Other reading: and so his good is made complete
- *si id implevit cui nascitur*: Literally “if he has filled up that for which he is born”. The subject is man. Nascitur is present tense, a general truth. Other reading: if he has carried out the thing he is born to

</details>

### 41.9

And what is it that this reason demands of him? A very easy thing: to live according to his own nature. But the madness shared by all makes it hard: we push one another into vices. And how can people be called back to health when no one holds them back and the crowd drives them on? Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ab illo*: “Him” is man, the reasoning animal of the sentence before. Other reading: demands of a man
- *rem facillimam*: A superlative: “the easiest thing” or “a very easy thing”. The English takes the second, as it does for the same construction at the start of the letter (41.1). Other reading: The easiest thing of all
- *secundum naturam suam vivere*: The Stoic formula of living according to nature, here with suam, “his own”. Suam refers to the one who lives, that is man, not to reason. Other reading: to live in agreement with its own nature
- *communis insania*: Communis is “shared”, “held in common”. The English reads “shared by all”, because “common” can be heard today as “ordinary”. Other reading: the common madness
- *ad salutem*: Salus is “health”, “safety” and “rescue”. The English reads “health” to match “healthy” (salutarem) at the start of the letter. Other reading: called back to safety
- *populus impellit*: Populus is “the people” as a whole. The English reads “the crowd” because the image is of being pushed. The Latin sets the two clauses side by side with no “and”. Other reading: whom no one holds back, whom the people push on

</details>

## Letter 44: On true nobility coming from the mind

### 44.3

Socrates was not a patrician. Cleanthes drew water and hired out his hands for watering a garden. Philosophy did not receive Plato as a noble; it made him one. What reason is there for you to despair of being able to become the equal of these men? All of these are your ancestors, if you conduct yourself in a way worthy of them; and you will, if you persuade yourself of this at once: that you are surpassed by no one in nobility.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Patricius*: A Roman word for the old hereditary aristocracy, applied here to an Athenian. The English keeps it as “patrician”. Other reading: Socrates was not of high birth
- *Cleanthes aquam traxit et rigando horto locavit manus*: Cleanthes was the second head of the Stoic school, said to have earned his living by drawing water at night (from memory, unchecked). Locavit manus is literally “let out his hands for hire”. Other reading: Cleanthes hauled water and hired himself out as a labourer to water a garden
- *non accepit nobilem philosophia sed fecit*: Nobilem goes with both verbs: philosophy did not take him in already noble but made him noble. “Noble” and “nobility” stand for nobilis and nobilitas through the passage. Plato was in fact of high birth by ordinary reckoning (from memory, unchecked), so the claim is pointed. Other reading: Philosophy did not find Plato noble but made him so
- *maiores tui*: Maiores is the ordinary word for ancestors, literally “those greater” or “older”. Other reading: All these are your forefathers
- *protinus*: “At once” or “straight away”, taken with “persuade yourself”. It could also be “from the outset”. Other reading: if from the very start you persuade yourself of this
- *a nullo te nobilitate superari*: Passive in the Latin and kept passive. “No one” is absolute and is kept. Other reading: that no one outdoes you in nobility

</details>

### 44.5

Who is well born? The man who is well made by nature for virtue. This is the one thing to look at. Otherwise, if you go back to ancient times, there is no one who does not come from that point before which there is nothing. From the first beginning of the world down to this time we have been led all the way by a line in which the splendid and the lowly alternate. A hall full of smoke-darkened images of ancestors does not make a man noble. No one has lived for our glory, and what was before us is not ours. It is the mind that makes a man noble, and the mind may rise above fortune from any station whatever.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *generosus*: From genus, “stock”: of good birth. It is a different word from nobilis (“noble”) later in the passage, so it has a different English word. Other reading: Who is of good stock?
- *bene a natura compositus*: Compositus is “put together”, “arranged”. The English reads “well made by nature”. Other reading: well fitted by nature for virtue
- *si ad vetera revocas*: Literally “if you call back to old things”, with no object in the Latin. The English supplies none and reads “go back”. Other reading: if you call the matter back to antiquity
- *nemo non inde est ante quod nihil est*: A double negative, kept as one: everyone goes back to the very first beginning. Inde is “from there”, given as “from that point”. Other reading: everyone comes from the place before which nothing exists
- *perduxit nos*: Active in the Latin: the line “has led” the speaker and his reader the whole way. The English is passive so that the sentence can keep the Latin order, from the beginning of the world to the line. Other reading: a line in which the splendid and the lowly alternate has led us all the way from the first beginning of the world down to this time
- *ex splendidis sordidisque alternata series*: The adjectives can be of persons (illustrious and low-born ancestors) or of states. Splendidus is “shining”, “distinguished”, and sordidus is “dirty”, “of low rank”. “The splendid and the lowly” leaves both open. Other reading: a chain alternating between distinguished and mean conditions
- *atrium plenum fumosis imaginibus*: Imagines are literally “likenesses”. In the main hall of a noble Roman house they were the portrait masks of ancestors, darkened by smoke over the years (from memory, unchecked). The English reads “images of ancestors”. Other reading: a hall full of smoky ancestor masks
- *animus facit nobilem*: Animus is “mind”, as in the glossary. “Spirit” would also fit here, where the point is what a person is in himself. Other reading: it is the spirit that makes a man noble
- *ex quacumque condicione*: Condicio is one’s station or lot in life, such as slave, freedman or knight. The English reads “station”. Other reading: from whatever condition

</details>

## Letter 47: On treating slaves as fellow humans

### 47.17

“He is a slave.” But perhaps free in mind. “He is a slave.” Will that harm him? Show who is not one: one man is a slave to lust, another to greed, another to ambition, [gap in the Latin] all to fear. I will give you a man of consular rank who is a slave to a little old woman, I will give you a rich man who is a slave to a slave girl, I will show you young men of the noblest birth who are the bought slaves of pantomime dancers: no slavery is more shameful than voluntary slavery. So there is no reason why those disdainful people should deter you from showing yourself cheerful to your slaves, and superior without being arrogant: let them honour you rather than fear you.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Servus est.*: The words of an imagined objector, said twice. The English puts them in quotation marks as the Latin does. Other reading: “But he is a slave.”
- *liber animo*: Animus is “mind”, as in the glossary. “Free in spirit” is the other natural English. Other reading: But perhaps free in spirit
- *Hoc illi nocebit?*: Literally “Will this harm him?”. It can also be heard as “Is this to be held against him?”. Other reading: Is that to count against him?
- *alius libidini servit*: Servire is “to serve” and “to be a slave”. The English reads “is a slave to” each time, because the argument depends on the same word as servus and servitus. Libido is “lust” or more widely “desire”. Other reading: one man serves his desire
- *alius ambitioni, , omnes timori*: The doubled comma shows that something has fallen out of this copy, and the English marks the place in square brackets. From memory, unchecked, editions print a supplement here meaning “all to hope”. The sentence construes without it. Other reading: another to ambition, all to hope, all to fear
- *consularem aniculae servientem*: Consularis is a man who has been consul. Anicula is a slighting diminutive, given as “little old woman”; ancillulae, “slave girl”, is a diminutive of the same kind. Other reading: a former consul enslaved to some little old woman
- *mancipia pantomimorum*: Mancipium is a slave as a piece of bought and owned property, a harsher word than servus. Pantomimi were solo stage dancers with a large following; the English keeps the name and reads “pantomime dancers”. Other reading: the chattels of stage dancers
- *non est quod*: An idiom, “there is no reason why”, followed by what should not happen. The point is that Lucilius should not be put off, not that these people lack a motive. Other reading: So do not let those disdainful people deter you
- *fastidiosi isti*: Fastidiosus is one who turns up his nose at something. “Fastidious” today means careful over detail, so the English reads “disdainful”. Other reading: those squeamish people
- *non superbe superiorem*: A play on sound between superbe (“arrogantly”) and superiorem (“superior”). The English keeps the sense, not the sound. Other reading: their superior, but not haughtily so
- *colant potius te quam timeant*: Colere is “to cultivate”, “to honour”, “to show regard for”, and is used of the gods and of patrons. The English reads “honour”. The Latin names no subject; “them” is the slaves. Other reading: let your slaves respect you rather than fear you

</details>

## Letter 48: On logic puzzles versus real help

### 48.2

Am I talking like an Epicurean again? But in fact the same thing is in my interest as is in yours: or else I am no friend, if whatever matter concerns you is not mine. Friendship makes between us a partnership in all things. Nothing favourable and nothing adverse belongs to one alone; life is lived in common. And no one can live happily who looks only at himself, who turns everything to his own advantage: you must live for another, if you want to live for yourself.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Iterum ego tamquam Epicureus loquor?*: This looks back to the end of the section before, where Seneca said that one thing was in Lucilius’s interest and another in his own. He now takes that back as Epicurean talk. The copy punctuates it as a question; it could be an exclamation. Other reading: There I go again, talking like an Epicurean!
- *mihi vero*: Vero corrects the question just asked. The English reads “But in fact”. Other reading: No: in truth the same thing is in my interest
- *expedit*: “Is expedient”, “is in the interest of”. It repeats the verb of the sentence before this section and is kept distinct from utilitates (“advantage”) later. Other reading: the same thing is expedient for me as for you
- *aut non sum amicus, nisi quidquid agitur ad te pertinens meum est*: Aut is “or else” and nisi is “unless”, so the Latin states the condition twice. The English gives nisi as “if … not” to make one condition of it. Agitur can be “is being done” or “is at stake”, and “whatever matter” leaves that open. Other reading: or else I am not a friend, unless whatever is at stake that concerns you is mine
- *Consortium rerum omnium*: Consortium is a legal word for holding property jointly and undivided, as heirs might (from memory, unchecked). The English reads “partnership”. Other reading: a joint ownership of everything
- *nec secundi quicquam singulis est nec adversi*: Literally “neither is anything favourable for individuals singly, nor anything adverse”. Singulis is plural, “each one separately”, given as “one alone”. Other reading: neither good fortune nor bad comes to each separately
- *in commune vivitur*: An impersonal passive, “there is living in common”, kept impersonal. Other reading: we live in common
- *alteri vivas oportet*: Alter is “the other” of two, or “another person”. The “you” is general, though it also falls on Lucilius. Other reading: you have to live for the other man

</details>

### 48.9

Say what nature has made necessary and what superfluous, how easy the [laws] are that it has laid down, how pleasant life is, how unencumbered, for those who follow them, how bitter and entangled is the life of those who have trusted opinion more than nature [gap in the Latin] if you have first shown what part of them they are going to relieve. Which of those things takes away desires? Which moderates them? I wish it were only that they do no good! They do harm. Whenever you like I will make this perfectly clear to you: that a well-born character is crushed and weakened when it is thrown into those quibbles.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Dic quid natura necessarium fecerit*: The imperative is singular. The “you” may be Lucilius or an imagined teacher of the quibbles named at the end; the passage alone does not settle it. Other reading: Tell me what nature has made necessary
- *quam faciles posuerit*: The Latin as given does not construe: faciles (“easy”, plural) has no noun, and a feminine plural has dropped out. From memory, unchecked, editions have leges (“laws”) here. The English puts “laws” in square brackets as a supplement. Other reading: how easy the things are that it has laid down
- *quam expedita illas sequentibus*: Illas (“them”, feminine plural) refers to the missing noun. Expedita is “unencumbered”, said of a soldier without baggage, and is set against implicita (“entangled”) in the next clause. Other reading: how free of hindrance for those who follow them
- *opinioni plus quam naturae crediderunt*: Opinio is “opinion”, as in the glossary: what people suppose, as against what nature shows. Other reading: who have put more trust in belief than in nature
- **** si prius docueris quam partem eorum levatura sint*: The asterisks mark a gap in the text, so the main clause that this “if” depends on is lost, and the English says so in square brackets. The subject of levatura sint is neuter plural, probably the logical puzzles; eorum (“of them”) may be the troubles just described or the people who suffer them. Levare is “to lighten” or “to relieve”. Other reading: if you have first shown what share of those people’s burdens they will lighten
- *Quid istorum cupiditates demit?*: Istorum is “of those things”, with a hint of “of yours”: the puzzles the person addressed deals in. Quid is the subject: which of them removes desires. Other reading: What is there in those things of yours that takes away desires?
- *Utinam tantum non prodessent! nocent.*: Literally “Would that they only failed to help! They harm.” Tantum is kept as “only”. Other reading: If only they merely did no good! They do harm.
- *generosam indolem*: Indoles is inborn character or natural gift. Generosa is “of good stock”, given as “well-born” as in Ep. 44.5; “nature” is avoided because it stands for natura in this passage. Other reading: a noble nature
- *in istas argutias coniectam*: Argutiae are clever fine points, said with scorn, so “quibbles”. Istas can carry “of yours”. Coniectam is “thrown into”, and the image is kept. Other reading: when it is cast into those subtleties of yours

</details>

## Letter 49: On the shortness of life and wasted cleverness

### 49.10

Teach me something against these things. Bring it about that I do not flee from death, and that life does not escape me. Encourage me against what is hard, against what cannot be avoided; loosen the narrow limits of my time. Teach me that the good of life lies not in its length but in its use; that it can happen, indeed very often does happen, that a man who has lived long has lived too little. Say to me when I am about to sleep, “You might not wake up.” Say to me when I have woken, “You might not sleep again.” Say to me as I go out, “You might not come back.” Say to me as I come back, “You might not go out.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Adversus haec*: “These things” are the deadly things of the section before: death following and life fleeing. Other reading: Teach me something to meet this
- *ut ego mortem non fugiam, vita me non effugiat*: Fugere is “to flee” and effugere is “to flee out of reach”, “to escape”: two verbs from one root. The English reads “flee” and “escape”, so the step from one to the other is kept and the echo is lost. Other reading: that I do not flee from death, and that life does not flee from me
- *Exhortare adversus difficilia*: A deponent imperative, “encourage”, with no object in the Latin. The English supplies the speaker as object here and again with the second “Teach”. Other reading: Give encouragement in the face of difficulties
- *[de aequanimitate]*: Square brackets in this copy mark words an editor cuts, here probably a reader’s heading that got into the text. The two words mean “on evenness of mind” and do not construe where they stand, so they are left out of the English. The rule: bracketed words are translated only where they construe and the sentence needs them, as in Ep. 41.2. Other reading: Encourage me against what is hard, [on evenness of mind] against what cannot be avoided
- *angustias temporis mei laxa*: Angustiae is a narrow place or tight squeeze, and laxare is “to loosen”, “to widen”. The English keeps the image of something tight being loosened. Other reading: widen the narrowness of my time
- *in spatio eius sed in usu posse fieri*: This copy has no stop after usu, but the sense needs one: the first clause ends at “use”, and posse fieri (“that it can happen”) begins a second thing to be taught. The English marks the break with a semicolon and adds no connective. Bonum vitae is “the good of life”. Other reading: that the good of life lies not in its length but in its use, and that it can happen
- *parum vixerit*: Parum is “too little”, “not enough”. Other reading: has not lived enough
- *potes non expergisci*: Literally “you are able not to wake”, meaning it is possible that you will not. “Might not” is used in all four sayings because “may not” can be heard as a ban. Other reading: You may not wake up
- *exeunti*: “To one going out”, with the speaker carried over from the first pair. Going out is leaving the house, with a hint of leaving life. Other reading: Say to a man as he leaves

</details>

## Letter 51: On luxurious resorts and soft living

### 51.4

We ought to choose a place that is healthy not only for the body but also for our character. Just as I would not want to live among torturers, so I would not want to live among taverns either. To see drunk people wandering along the shores, and the revels of people out sailing, and the lakes noisy with the music of bands, and the other offences that self-indulgence, as if set free from the laws, not only commits but makes public: what need is there of that?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *moribus*: Mores is habits, conduct, character or morals. The English reads “character” and gives it a possessive that the Latin does not have, to balance “the body”. Other reading: healthy not only for the body but also for morals
- *inter tortores*: Tortor is one who inflicts torture, often the public executioner’s man. Other reading: among executioners
- *ne inter popinas quidem*: Popina is a cheap eating and drinking house with a low reputation, given as “tavern”. Ne … quidem is “not even” or “not … either”. The verb “live” is carried over from the first half. Other reading: so not even among cook-shops
- *comessationes navigantium*: Comessatio is a drinking party that roams about after dinner. Navigantium is “of people sailing”, here pleasure parties on the water. Other reading: the carousing of boating parties
- *symphoniarum cantibus strepentes lacus*: Symphonia is a group of musicians playing or singing together, and cantus covers both song and playing. The lakes would be those by Baiae, which the section before names (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: the lakes ringing with the singing of choirs
- *velut soluta legibus luxuria*: Luxuria is loose, extravagant living, given as “self-indulgence”; “luxury” today suggests comfort and costly goods. Soluta legibus is the phrase for being exempted from the laws, and it picks up the verb of loosening in the section before. Other reading: luxury, as though exempt from the laws
- *non tantum peccat sed publicat*: A play on sound, peccat and publicat. The relative “which” is the object of both verbs: the wrongs it does and then displays. “Offences” gives that object of peccat. Other reading: the other things in which self-indulgence not only sins but advertises its sin

</details>

### 51.9

Freedom is what has been set as the goal; it is for this prize that the toil is done. You ask what freedom is? To be a slave to no thing, to no necessity, to no accidents; to bring Fortune down to level ground. On the day I understand that she has more power, she will have no power: am I to put up with her, when death is in my hand?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Libertas proposita est*: Proposita is “set before” as an aim or as a prize put up in a contest. The Latin does not say by whom or for whom, and the English keeps it impersonal. Other reading: Freedom is the prize held out
- *ad hoc praemium laboratur*: An impersonal passive: “there is toiling for this prize”, kept impersonal. Other reading: this is the reward I am working for
- *Nulli rei servire*: Servire is “to be a slave to”. Res is any thing or circumstance, and “no thing” is written as two words to keep it wide. Other reading: To serve nothing
- *nullis casibus*: Casus is what befalls, an accident or chance event. It is plural in the Latin, and the English reads “accidents”. Other reading: to no chances
- *fortunam in aequum deducere*: A military idiom: to bring an enemy down from high ground to the level, where the fight is on equal terms. The image is kept. Fortune has a capital here, by the glossary’s rule, because she is treated as an enemy in this section and the one before. Other reading: to meet Fortune on equal terms
- *Quo die illam intellexero plus posse, nil poterit*: As given, this says that on the day Fortune is found to be the stronger she will be able to do nothing, which the next clause explains: death is to hand. From memory, unchecked, editions print illa me for illam, so that the speaker is the stronger one. The English translates the Latin as given, with “power” for both posse and poterit. Other reading: On the day I understand that I have more power than she, she will have no power
- *ego illam feram*: Feram can be future (“shall … bear”) or subjunctive (“am … to bear”). The sense is nearly the same, and the English takes the second. Other reading: Shall I endure her
- *cum in manu mors sit*: “In hand” means within one’s own power. The Latin has no possessive; English needs one with “hand”. The thought is that one can always choose to die. Other reading: when death is within reach

</details>

## Letter 52: On choosing guides and applauding philosophers

### 52.2

“It is folly,” you say, “and for folly nothing holds firm, nothing pleases for long.” But how, or when, shall we tear ourselves away from it? No one is strong enough by himself to come up to the surface; someone must stretch out a hand, someone must draw him out.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Stultitia*: The word answers the question of the section before (what is it that pulls a person the wrong way?), so the English makes “folly” the answer: “It is folly”. The Latin can also be read as a definition with folly as its subject. Other reading: “Folly,” you say, “is the thing for which nothing holds firm, which nothing pleases for long.”
- *cui nihil constat*: “constat” covers “stands firm”, “is settled”, “is consistent” and “is agreed”. The English reads “holds firm”, and turns the relative clause (“for which”) into “and for folly” so that the sentence can be followed. Other reading: for which nothing is consistent
- *ab illa revellemus*: “revellere” is to wrench off something that is stuck fast; the verb is a future (“shall”), not a wish. “illa” is folly. Other reading: how or when are we going to pull ourselves free of it?
- *emergat*: The verb is the opposite of “mergere”, to sink: to rise out of water or mud in which one is sunk. The image is kept with “come up to the surface”. Other reading: to get himself out
- *aliquis educat*: After “oportet” this is the subjunctive of “educere”, to lead or draw out, not “educare”, to bring up. The Latin has no object; “him” is supplied. “Draw” keeps both the leading and the pulling that are in the verb. Other reading: someone must lead him out

</details>

### 52.3

Epicurus says that some people have come out to the truth without anyone’s help, that they themselves have made their own road; these he praises most, the ones whose drive came from themselves, who brought themselves forward. Others, he says, need another’s aid: they will not go if no one has gone ahead, but they will follow well. Metrodorus, he says, is one of these; this too is an outstanding talent, but of the second rank. We are not of that first grade; we are well treated if we are admitted to the second. Do not look down even on the man who can be safe through another’s good service; this too is a great deal, to want to be saved.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quosdam ait Epicurus*: Seneca reports Epicurus in indirect speech, so there are no quotation marks. From memory, unchecked: the saying is known only from this letter and comes from a lost writing of Epicurus. Other reading: According to Epicurus, some people
- *exisse*: Literally “have gone out”, which continues the image of emerging in the section before. From memory, unchecked: some editions may print a different verb here (“contendere”, to strive towards); the English follows the text as given. Other reading: have made their way to the truth
- *fecisse sibi ipsos viam*: “sibi” is “for themselves” and “ipsos” is “they themselves”, with no one’s help. The English gives the two as “they themselves” and “their own road”. Other reading: that they have made a road for themselves
- *qui se ipsi protulerunt*: “proferre se” can be to bring oneself forward, to advance oneself, or to bring oneself out into view. The English takes it as moving forward under their own power, to match the road they made. Other reading: who brought themselves out into view
- *Metrodorum*: Metrodorus of Lampsacus, the closest pupil and friend of Epicurus (from memory, unchecked).
- *secundae sortis ingenium*: “ingenium” is inborn ability or cast of mind; the English reads “talent” because the glossary keeps “mind” for animus. “sors” is literally a lot drawn, here a rank or class. Other reading: this too is an outstanding mind, but of the second lot
- *ex illa prima nota*: “nota” is a mark or label, used of the grade marked on a wine jar, and so a class or quality. The English reads “grade”; the image of a stamped mark is lost. The same noun is understood with “the second”. Other reading: We do not bear that first stamp
- *bene nobiscum agitur*: An idiom, literally “it is dealt well with us”. The English reads “well treated”: the luck lies in what is granted, not in a performance. Other reading: it goes well with us if we are let into the second
