- *alieno beneficio*: “beneficium” is a good service or favour actually done, not a kindly disposition. “Help” and “aid” already stand for adiutorio and ope above, so the English reads “good service”. Other reading: by another’s kindness
- *esse salvus potest*: “salvus esse” is to be safe or sound; “servari” in the next clause is to be saved or kept safe. The two different words are kept apart as “safe” and “saved”. Other reading: who can be saved

</details>

### 52.9

Nor would I, on that account, forbid you to listen also to those whose habit it is to admit the public and lecture, provided that they come out before the crowd with this purpose, to become better and to make others better, if they do not practise this to court popularity. For what is more shameful than philosophy hunting for shouts? Surely a sick man does not praise the doctor who is cutting him?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nec ideo*: “On that account” points back to the advice just given: choose as a helper the man who teaches by his life rather than the fluent talker. Other reading: But that is no reason for me to stop you
- *admittere populum ac disserere*: Literally “to admit the people and to discourse”: philosophers who give open lectures to anyone who comes. “disserere” is to set out an argument at length; the English reads “lecture” here and for the same verb in Ep. 52.11. Other reading: to let the public in and hold discourses
- *faciantque meliores*: The Latin has no object: “become better and make better”. “Others” is supplied. Other reading: to become better and make their hearers better
- *ambitionis*: “ambitio” is first a candidate’s going round for votes, then the courting of favour and applause. The English reads “to court popularity”, because the English word “ambition” now means wanting to get ahead. Other reading: for the sake of ambition
- *exercent*: “hoc exercent” is “practise this”, the public lecturing. It can also mean carrying it on as a profession. Other reading: if they do not make a trade of this to court popularity
- *captante clamores*: “clamores” are the shouts of an audience, here shouts of approval; “captare” is to hunt or angle for something. “Shouts” is used for this word in Ep. 52.11 and 52.12 as well. Other reading: philosophy fishing for cheers
- *numquid aeger laudat medicum secantem*: “numquid” expects the answer no, which “Surely … not” gives. “secantem” is the doctor cutting, that is, operating with the knife; the plain “cutting” is kept. Other reading: Does a sick man praise the surgeon who is operating on him?

</details>

### 52.11

And how great is the madness of the man whom the shouts of the ignorant send away from the lecture hall cheerful! Why are you glad to have been praised by these people, whom you cannot yourself praise? Fabianus used to lecture to the public, but he was listened to with restraint; from time to time a great shout of praise would break out, but one that the greatness of what he was saying had called forth, not the sound of speech that had glided by without a stumble and softly.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *clamores imperitorum*: “imperiti” are those without knowledge or experience of the matter, the unqualified. The English reads “the ignorant”. Other reading: the shouts of people who know nothing about it
- *ex auditorio*: “auditorium” can be the hall or the audience gathered in it. The English takes it as the place. Other reading: send away from his audience
- *Quid laetaris*: The “you” here is the applauded lecturer, addressed directly, or any such person; it need not be Lucilius. The second person is kept as it stands. Other reading: Why should a man be glad
- *Fabianus*: Papirius Fabianus, a philosopher and speaker whom Seneca heard when young (from memory, unchecked).
- *modeste*: The adverb describes how the audience listened: within bounds, with self control. It could also be heard as “respectfully”. Other reading: but he was heard respectfully
- *rerum magnitudo*: Literally “the greatness of the things”: the substance of what was said, as opposed to how it sounded. Other reading: the greatness of his subject
- *sonus inoffense ac molliter orationis elapsae*: Literally “the sound of speech that has slipped out without striking against anything and softly”. “elapsae” may be speech slipping out, slipping by or slipping away; the English reads “glided by”. “molliter” (softly) can carry a hint of effeminacy. Other reading: the sound of speech that had slipped out smoothly and effeminately

</details>

### 52.12

Let there be some difference between the shouting of the theatre and that of the school: there is a certain good taste in praising too. Everything is a sign of everything, if it is watched, and one may take evidence of character even from the smallest things: the lewd man is shown by his walk, by a movement of his hand, sometimes by a single answer, by a finger brought to his head, by the turn of his eyes; the wicked man is given away by his laugh, the madman by his face and bearing. For those traits come out into the open through their marks: you will know what each person is like if you look at how he praises and how he is praised.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *scholae*: The “school” is a philosopher’s lecture room and the audience in it, set against the public theatre. Other reading: the shouting of the theatre and that of the lecture room
- *elegantia*: “elegantia” is discrimination or fineness of taste rather than elegance of dress or style. Other reading: there is a kind of refinement even in giving praise
- *Omnia rerum omnium, si observentur, indicia sunt*: Literally “all things are signs of all things, if they are observed”. “Omnia” can also be taken with “indicia”: all things have their signs, if one watches for them. The English takes “omnia” as the subject. Other reading: There are signs of everything, if one watches for them
- *impudicum*: “impudicus” is a man without sexual shame, and in Roman usage the word often points at a man thought effeminate or sexually passive, which the signs listed here suit. The English reads the general word “lewd”. Other reading: the effeminate man
- *relatus ad caput digitus*: Literally “a finger carried back to the head”. From memory, unchecked: scratching the head with one finger was a proverbial sign of effeminacy at Rome. The literal gesture is kept. Other reading: scratching his head with one finger
- *flexus oculorum*: “flexus” is a bending or turning: a sideways or rolling glance. Other reading: by a sidelong glance
- *improbum*: “improbus” is the general word for a morally bad man, the opposite of “probus”, upright; it can lean towards “dishonest” or “shameless”. The English keeps the broad word “wicked”. Other reading: the dishonest man
- *vultus habitusque*: “habitus” covers bearing, physical condition and dress. The English reads “bearing”. Other reading: by his expression and his dress
- *Illa enim in apertum per notas exeunt*: “Illa” is a neuter plural with no noun: the English takes it as the hidden traits of character just named and supplies “traits”. “notae” are distinguishing marks, kept apart from “indicia”, “signs”, above. Other reading: For those things come out into the open through marks

</details>

## Letter 53: On hidden faults and giving all to philosophy

### 53.8

Why does no one confess his vices? Because he is still in them: to tell a dream is for a man who is awake, and to confess one’s vices is a sign of health. Let us wake up, then, so that we can expose our errors. But only philosophy will rouse us, only philosophy will shake off the heavy sleep: dedicate yourself wholly to her. You are worthy of her, she is worthy of you: go into each other’s embrace. Refuse yourself to all other things, bravely, openly; there is no reason for you to do philosophy by someone else’s leave.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *vitia sua*: The glossary’s “vices” is kept. The section before treats them as diseases of the mind, so “faults” or “failings” is also possible. Other reading: Why does no one admit his faults?
- *sanitatis indicium*: “sanitas” is both health of body and soundness of mind. The section before speaks of diseases of the mind, and “health” answers that image. Other reading: a sign of sanity
- *errores nostros coarguere*: “coarguere” is to prove something wrong or convict it by evidence; the English reads “expose”. “errores” is “errors”, but also “wanderings”, which suits the dream image. Other reading: to convict our wanderings
- *illi te totum dedica*: “Philosophia” is feminine. Because the next sentence speaks of an embrace, the English reads “her” and “she” rather than “it”. Other reading: dedicate yourself wholly to it
- *ite in complexum alter alterius*: The command is plural, addressed to Lucilius and philosophy together: literally “go, the one into the embrace of the other”. Other reading: embrace one another
- *Omnibus aliis rebus te nega*: Literally “deny yourself to all other things”: say no to every other claim on you. Other reading: Say no to everything else
- *fortiter*: “fortiter” is “bravely”, and also “firmly, stoutly”. The English keeps the first sense. Other reading: firmly, openly
- *precario*: A term from law: holding something only at another’s request and by his leave, which can be withdrawn at any moment. Here it means doing philosophy only when other business allows. Other reading: there is no reason for you to do philosophy on sufferance

</details>

### 53.9

If you were ill, you would have set aside the care of your property, and your business in the forum would have dropped out of your mind, and you would not think anyone worth so much that you would go down as his advocate during a remission; with your whole mind you would work at this, to be freed from the illness as soon as possible. Well then? Will you not do the same now too? Let go of everything that hinders you and make yourself free for a sound mind: no one reaches it while he is busy. Philosophy exercises her own royal power; she gives out time, she does not accept it; she is not a thing for leftover hours; she is a thing for the regular hours, she is the mistress, she is present and gives orders.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *curam intermisisses rei familiaris*: The Latin moves from “would have” (set aside, dropped) to “would” (think, work); the shift is kept. “res familiaris” is one’s property, one’s private means. Other reading: you would have stopped looking after your estate
- *forensia tibi negotia*: “forensia” is “of the forum”, the place of the law courts and of public business in general. Other reading: your business in the courts
- *cui advocatus in remissione descenderes*: “descendere” is the usual word for going down to the forum; an “advocatus” is one called in to stand by a party at law, by this date often the one who pleads. “remissio” is an abatement of the illness, a let up in the fever. Other reading: for whom you would go down to court to support him when the illness eased
- *omnia impedimenta dimitte*: “impedimenta” are hindrances, and also the word for an army’s baggage. The plain sense is kept. Other reading: Send away all your baggage
- *vaca bonae menti*: “vacare” is to be free or at leisure for something. “bona mens” is a sound or good mind; “mens” is a different word from “animus”, which is “mind” just before. Other reading: give your free time to good sense
- *occupatus*: “occupatus” is “taken up”, busy with other affairs. In Seneca it is a set term for the man with no time of his own. Other reading: no one reaches it while preoccupied
- *Exercet philosophia regnum suum*: “regnum” is a king’s power or a kingdom: philosophy reigns in her own right. The English reads “royal power” so that the word is not taken for a regulation she follows. Other reading: Philosophy holds her own kingdom
- *dat tempus, non accipit*: Literally “she gives time, she does not receive it”. It can mean that she hands time out to other things and takes none of what they leave over, or that she appoints the hours and has none appointed for her; “gives out” leans to the first. From memory, unchecked: the letter goes on to make philosophy refuse the time left over from other things. Other reading: she sets the time, she does not have it set for her
- *non est res subsiciva; ordinaria est*: “subsicivus” is used of land left over after a survey and then of odd spare moments. “ordinarius” is what belongs to the regular order, as opposed to the extra. The English gives the contrast as leftover hours and regular hours; the surveyor’s image is lost. Other reading: she is not an odd moments affair; she is a regular one
- *domina est*: “domina” is the mistress of a household who commands its slaves. This personification is why the English reads “she” for philosophy throughout the sentence. Other reading: it is the master

</details>

## Letter 54: On asthma and rehearsing death

### 54.5

I ask you, would you not call it most foolish if someone thought that a lamp is worse off when it has been put out than before it is lit? We too are both put out and lit: in the time between we go through something, but on either side there is deep freedom from care. For this, my Lucilius, unless I am mistaken, is where we go wrong: we judge that death follows, when it has both gone before and will follow. Whatever was before us is death; for what does it matter whether you do not begin or you cease, when the result of both is this, not to be?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *stultissimum*: The form can be neuter (“a most foolish thing”) or masculine (“a complete fool”, said of the person who thinks so). The English takes it as neuter. Other reading: would you not call a man a complete fool if he thought
- *lucernae peius esse*: Literally “that it is worse for the lamp”: an oil lamp is spoken of as if it could be well or badly off. Other reading: that things are worse for a lamp
- *aliquid patimur*: “pati” is to undergo, to have something happen to one, and also to suffer. “Go through” is chosen because it can be heard either way. Other reading: in the time between we suffer something
- *alta securitas*: “securitas” is the state of being without care or trouble; the glossary’s “freedom from care” is kept. “alta” is “deep”. Other reading: on either side there is deep peace
- *mortem iudicamus sequi*: “sequi” has no object: death “follows”, that is, comes after life. “iudicamus” is “judge”, kept apart from “thought” above. Other reading: we suppose that death comes afterwards
- *non incipias an desinas*: The “you” is the general “you” (anyone), not Lucilius in particular; the second person is kept. Other reading: whether one never begins or one stops

</details>

### 54.7

Be assured of this about me: I shall not tremble at the end, I am already prepared, I give no thought to a whole day. As for you, praise and imitate the man who is not sorry to die though he is glad to live: for what virtue is there in going out when you are being thrown out? Yet there is virtue here too: I am indeed being thrown out, but as though I were going out. And the reason the wise man is never thrown out is that to be thrown out is to be driven from a place you leave unwillingly: the wise man does nothing unwillingly; he escapes necessity, because he wants what it is going to force on him. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Hoc tibi de me recipe*: “recipere” is the word for giving or taking a guarantee. Literally “take this for yourself about me”, that is, accept it as assured. Other reading: Take this as my pledge about myself
- *ad extrema*: Literally “at the last things”: the final moments of life. Other reading: when the last moment comes
- *nihil cogito de die toto*: Literally, no thought is given to “the whole day”. The likely sense is that he does not count on having a full day ahead of him; it could also be read as giving no thought to the day at all. Other reading: I do not count on a whole day
- *cum iuvet vivere*: “cum” is read as “although”: he is not unwilling to die even though living pleases him. Seneca sets this man against himself, old and ill, who is being pushed out anyway. Other reading: at a time when he enjoys living
- *quae est enim virtus*: The glossary’s “virtue” is kept. The passage is close to the sense “courage”, and “what merit is there” would also be possible. Other reading: for what courage is there
- *eicior quidem, sed tamquam exeam*: The argument rests on the pair “eici” (to be thrown out) and “exire” (to go out of one’s own accord). “Thrown out” and “going out” are kept throughout. Other reading: I am being evicted, it is true, but as if I were leaving
- *quia vult quod coactura est*: Literally “because he wants what it is going to compel”. The subject of “coactura est” is necessity; “on him” is supplied. Other reading: because he wills what it would force him to do

</details>

## Letter 55: On hiding versus truly living in retirement

### 55.5

For the man who has run away from affairs and from people, whom the ill success of his own desires has banished, who could not bear to see others more successful, who has gone into hiding out of fear like a timid and sluggish animal: that man does not live for himself but, which is the most shameful thing, for his belly, for sleep, for lust; a man who lives for no one does not, just by that, live for himself. Yet steadiness, and persistence in one’s purpose, is so great a thing that even stubborn sluggishness carries weight.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *res et homines*: “res” is “things”, here the business of public and private life. Other reading: the world and its people
- *fugit*: The form can be present (“runs away”) or perfect (“has run away”). The other verbs in the series are perfect, so the English takes it as perfect. Other reading: the man who runs away from affairs and from people
- *cupiditatum suarum infelicitas relegavit*: “infelicitas” is ill luck or failure: his ambitions came to nothing. It could also be the misery his desires brought him. “relegare” is the legal word for banishing someone to a distance; “ill success” lets “more successful” (feliciores) echo it. Other reading: whom the unhappiness of his own desires has sent into exile
- *videre non potuit*: Literally “was not able to see”, meaning he could not stand the sight; “bear” is supplied. “feliciores” is “more fortunate, more successful”; “happier” is avoided because the glossary keeps “happy” for beatus. Other reading: who could not look at others more fortunate than himself
- *oblituit*: The perfect of a verb meaning to hide oneself away, to go to ground. Other reading: has lain hidden
- *sibi non vivit*: “To live for oneself” is the phrase from the section before, where the crowd says it of any man of leisure and Seneca reserves it for the wise man. Other reading: does not live to himself
- *non continuo*: “continuo” here is “as an immediate consequence”: it does not follow at once that he lives for himself. Other reading: it does not follow that a man who lives for no one lives for himself
- *constantia*: The glossary’s “steadiness”: holding to one course. The verb is singular, treating steadiness and persistence as one thing. Other reading: constancy
- *inertia quoque pertinax*: “inertia” picks up “iners” said of the animal above, so the English reads “sluggish” and “sluggishness”. “pertinax” is “holding on obstinately”. “auctoritas” is weight or standing in others’ eyes. Other reading: even idleness, if it is obstinate, wins respect

</details>

### 55.8

But place does not contribute much to calm: it is the mind that makes everything agreeable to itself. I have seen people gloomy in a cheerful and pleasant country house, I have seen people in the middle of solitude who were like busy men. So there is no reason for you to think that you are not well enough composed because you are not in Campania. But why are you not? Send your thoughts all the way here.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *locus*: The Latin has no article. The English takes the word generally (“place”), not as the particular villa just described. Other reading: But the place does not contribute much to calm
- *qui sibi commendet omnia*: “commendare” is to recommend or make something acceptable. The verb is subjunctive, which may carry a shade of “is the thing to” or “should”; the English gives a plain statement. Other reading: it is the mind that must commend all things to itself
- *in villa hilari et amoena*: A “villa” is a country house or estate; “amoenus” is the usual word for a place with pleasant scenery. Other reading: in a bright and lovely villa
- *occupatis similes*: Literally “like the occupied”: men taken up with business, Seneca’s set word for those with no leisure. Other reading: who looked like men with their hands full
- *parum bene compositum*: “compositus” can be “composed, in good order” in oneself or “well placed, settled” in a situation. The passage is about calm and the mind, so the English reads “composed”. Other reading: that you are not well enough placed
- *in Campania*: The region around the Bay of Naples, where Seneca is writing from, near the villa of Vatia described in this letter. Other reading: here in Campania
- *Quare autem non es?*: The verb “are” has nothing after it in the Latin: “But why are you not (in Campania)?” The next sentence answers it: he can be there in thought. It may also be a real question about why Lucilius has not come. Other reading: And yet why are you not here?

</details>

## Letter 56: On noise and inner quiet

### 56.5

Besides, a sound that keeps breaking off is more of a nuisance to me than one that goes on without a break. But by now I have so hardened myself against all these things that I can listen even to a rowing-master giving the oarsmen their time in the harshest of voices. For I force my mind to be intent on itself and not to be called away to things outside. Let everything resound outside, so long as there is no uproar inside, so long as desire and fear do not brawl with each other, so long as greed and extravagance are not at odds and neither troubles the other. For what good is the silence of a whole district, if the passions are roaring?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *etiam nunc*: The section runs on from the list of noises before it. In Seneca these words often mean “furthermore” and not “even now”, and the English takes them so. The temporal sense is also possible, with “but by now” in the next sentence answering it. Other reading: Even now a sound that keeps breaking off is more of a nuisance to me
- *pausarium*: The pausarius is the man on a galley who gives the rowers the time for the stroke. English has no exact word; “rowing-master” is used, though it may suggest a higher rank than the Latin intends. Other reading: the man who calls the stroke; the boatswain
- *avocari*: The same verb runs through the section before, where a voice “calls away” more than a noise does, so “called away” is kept and “distracted” is not used. Other reading: and not to be distracted by things outside
- *luxuriaque*: Luxuria is the vice of lavish living and spending, set here against avaritia, the vice of getting and keeping. “Extravagance” keeps the opposition clear, though it narrows the word toward spending. Other reading: greed and luxury; greed and self-indulgence
- *regionis*: Regio can be a quarter of a city or a whole stretch of country. The English reads “district”. Other reading: the silence of the whole neighbourhood; of a whole region
- *affectus fremunt*: Affectus is the Stoic term for the passions. Fremere covers roaring, growling and a low angry murmur; the loud end is taken because it is set against silence. Other reading: if the passions are growling; if the feelings are in a roar

</details>

### 56.12

It is a lightweight character, and one that has not yet withdrawn into itself, that starts up at a voice and at whatever happens. It has something of anxiety inside, and it has something of a dread that has formed in it, which makes the man watchful, as our Vergil says: “And I, who a little while ago was moved by no weapons hurled at me, nor by Greeks crowding out of the opposing ranks, am now frightened by every breeze; every sound startles me, on edge and afraid alike for my companion and for my burden.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Leve illud ingenium*: “ingenium” is a person’s inborn character or temperament; the English reads “character”, keeping “mind” for animus and “nature” for natura. “Leve” is literally “light”, lacking weight and so easily moved; “lightweight” keeps the image of weight. Other reading: That is a light (unsteady, fickle) nature
- *nec sese adhuc reduxit introsus*: “introsus” is a spelling of introrsus, “inwards”. Literally “and has not yet led itself back inwards”; the English reads it as withdrawing into itself. The section before uses the military image of sounding the retreat, which “reduxit” may continue. Other reading: and has not yet drawn itself back inside
- *ad vocem et accidentia erigitur*: “vocem” can be a voice, a cry or a spoken word. “accidentia” is things that happen, or more narrowly things that fall on the ear, that is, chance noises; the English takes the broader sense. “erigitur” is literally “is raised up”: it can mean is roused, pricks up its ears, or starts up, and the English reads “starts up”. Other reading: that pricks up its ears at a word and at every chance noise
- *concepti pavoris*: Literally “of conceived dread”: a dread that has formed inside, not one absorbed from outside. “pavoris” names something stronger than plain fear, and “dread” keeps it apart from the two other Latin words for frightening and fearing in the verse. Other reading: something of a fear that it has taken in
- *quod illum*: “illum” is masculine and cannot agree with the neuter “ingenium”. The Latin slides from the character to the man who has it, or has the masculine word for mind, animus, in view, or the text is at fault. The English reads “the man” so that the shift is plain. Other reading: which makes it watchful
- *curiosum*: “curiosum” ranges from “careful” through “inquisitive” to “nervously on the watch”. Here it is the state of attending to every sound, so the English reads “watchful”; the anxiety is already named earlier in the sentence by another word. Other reading: which makes the man inquisitive (or: prying, always on the alert)
- *Vergilius noster*: The lines are from Vergil’s Aeneid, book 2, about lines 726 to 729 (from memory, unchecked). Aeneas speaks as he flees burning Troy. The Latin as given has no quotation marks and runs the verse on as prose; the English uses quotation marks because “ut ait” marks the lines as quoted.
- *dudum*: “dudum” can mean “a little while ago” or “for a long time before now”. The English takes the first, as the contrast with “now” suggests. Other reading: who for so long was moved by no weapons
- *movebant*: The Latin verb is a plain past and is active, with the weapons and the Greeks as its subject. The English turns it into “was moved by” to keep the sentence readable, and keeps “moved” for the image; the sense is “troubled” or “shook”. Other reading: whom no hurled weapons used to trouble
- *adverso glomerati e agmine Grai*: Vergil’s line is remembered as having “ex” where this copy has “e”, and the metre needs it (from memory, unchecked); the sense is the same. The phrase is literally “Greeks massed out of the opposing column”: either Greeks gathering from the enemy line against him, or Greeks in massed enemy formation. The English takes the first. Other reading: nor by the Greeks packed together in their opposing ranks
- *excitat*: Literally “rouses” or “stirs up”. The English reads “startles”, which suits a man on edge. Other reading: every sound rouses
- *suspensum*: Literally “hung up, in suspense”: anxious and uncertain. The English reads “on edge”. Other reading: in suspense
- *comitique onerique*: In the Aeneid the companion is Aeneas’s small son Ascanius, walking beside him, and the burden is his old father Anchises, carried on his shoulders (from memory, unchecked). Seneca’s Latin does not name them, so the English does not either. Other reading: fearing equally for the one who walks beside and for the one who is carried

</details>

### 56.14

Whichever you pick of those fortunate people, who drag much along and carry much, you will see him “fearing for both his companion and his load”. Know, then, that you are settled at the point when no shouting concerns you, when no voice shakes you out of yourself, not if it coaxes, not if it threatens, not if it is an empty voice making a din all round you with hollow sound.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *felicibus*: Felix is “lucky, prosperous”. The English reads “fortunate” and keeps “happy” for beatus, as the glossary has it. The word is ironic here. Other reading: of those prosperous people; those lucky ones
- *multa trahentibus, multa portantibus*: The two verbs answer the two halves of the Vergil line: Aeneas leads a companion along by the hand and carries a load on his shoulders. “Drag” and “carry” are kept so the match shows. Other reading: who have much in tow and much on their backs
- *comitique onerique timentem*: Vergil, Aeneid 2.729 (from memory, unchecked): Aeneas afraid for the son he leads and the father he carries. The line is quoted in full in the section before, which is not among the passages given. Other reading: afraid alike for the one who walks with him and the one he bears
- *compositum*: Literally “put in order, set at rest”; the English reads “settled”. The Latin has “then” and “when”, with no “only”, so none is added. Other reading: that you are composed; that you are at peace
- *ad te nullus clamor pertinebit*: Pertinere ad is “to reach” and also “to concern, to matter to”. The second is taken: the noise is still heard but is none of the hearer’s business, which is the letter’s point about living among noise. Other reading: when no shouting reaches you
- *te nulla vox tibi excutiet*: Literally “no voice will knock you away from yourself”. The image is kept as “shakes you out of yourself”. Other reading: when no voice robs you of yourself; makes you lose your self-possession
- *inani sono vana circumstrepet*: Vana can be nominative, agreeing with vox (“an empty voice”), or a neuter plural object (“dins empty things around you”). The English takes it with vox. Other reading: not if it dins meaningless things around you with hollow sound

</details>

### 56.15

“What then? Is it not sometimes more comfortable to be without the racket as well?” I admit it; and so I shall move out of this place. I wanted to test myself and train myself. What need is there to be tormented any longer, when Ulysses found so easy a remedy for his comrades even against the Sirens? Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quid ergo?*: The question in quotation marks is an imagined objector’s, as often in the letters; Seneca answers it with fateor, an admission. Other reading: “Well then,” you say,
- *et carere convicio*: Et here is “also”: besides being able to bear the noise, to be free of it, hence “as well”. Convicium is a loud clamour and also abuse; here it is the din. Other reading: also to be free of the uproar; to be spared the abuse as well
- *commodius*: Commodus covers “convenient”, “comfortable” and “advantageous”. The English reads “more comfortable”. Other reading: more convenient; better
- *Experiri et exercere me volui*: The pronoun is the object of both verbs, so “myself” is repeated and neither verb is left hanging. Other reading: I wanted to make trial of myself and give myself practice
- *Ulixes*: Ulixes is the Latin name of Odysseus; the English gives the usual English form of the Latin name, Ulysses. The remedy is the wax with which he stopped his crew’s ears against the Sirens’ song (Odyssey 12, from memory, unchecked). Other reading: Odysseus
- *invenerit Vale.*: The copy has no stop between invenerit and the closing Vale. The sentence is plainly a question (“what need is there”), so the question mark in the English is supplied.

</details>

## Letter 57: On instinctive fear in a dark tunnel

### 57.3

Still, that darkness gave me something to think about: I felt a kind of blow to the mind, and a change, without fear, which the newness and at the same time the foulness of an unfamiliar thing had brought about. I am not speaking to you now about myself, who am a long way from a tolerable man, let alone a perfect one, but about the man over whom Fortune has lost her authority: his mind too will be struck, his colour will change.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *illa obscuritas*: The darkness is that of the road tunnel described in the section before, with its dust and torches. Other reading: that gloom
- *ictum animi*: Literally “a blow of the mind”, that is, a jolt the mind receives. The image is kept and is echoed below, where the mind “will be struck” (ferietur). Other reading: a kind of shock to the spirit; a certain mental jolt
- *sine metu mutationem*: “A change without fear”: an alteration in him that was not fear. The Latin does not say what changed (mind, face or state), so the English leaves it open, and “change” is kept so that it answers “his colour will change” below. Other reading: an alteration of mood that was not fear
- *foeditas*: Foeditas is foulness, ugliness, anything repulsive to the senses. “Foulness” suits the dust and murk of the tunnel. Other reading: the ugliness; the unpleasantness
- *homine tolerabili*: Literally “a man who can be put up with”, a passably good man. Perfecto after it is the finished, complete man, that is, the wise man. Other reading: far from a passable man, let alone a complete one
- *in quem fortuna ius perdidit*: Ius is a legal right or power over a person, and “authority” gives that sense in plain words. Fortune acts as a person here, so the word has a capital, as the glossary allows. Other reading: over whom fortune has lost its right; its jurisdiction

</details>

### 57.4

For there are some things, my Lucilius, that no virtue can escape; nature reminds virtue of its own mortality. And so he will tighten his face at grim things, and shudder at sudden things, and his sight will go dim if he stands on the brink of a vast depth and looks down into it. This is not fear, but a natural feeling that reason cannot take by storm.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *nulla effugere virtus potest*: Virtus is “virtue”, as in the glossary, and not only “courage”: the man in view is the perfect man of the section before, so the claim is that no degree of virtue gets a person clear of these reactions. Other reading: that no courage can escape
- *mortalitatis suae*: Suae could strictly point back to natura, the subject, but the sense requires virtue’s own mortality: virtue is reminded that it lives in a mortal creature. The English names virtue again so that this is clear. Other reading: nature reminds it of nature’s own mortality
- *vultum adducet ad tristia*: The verbs have no stated subject. It is taken to be the man of the section before, over whom Fortune has lost her authority, because constitutus below is masculine and cannot go with virtus. Vultum adducere is to draw the face tight, to frown. Other reading: he will frown at sad things; it (virtue) will knit its brow at what is grim
- *caligabit*: Caligare is to be in a mist or darkness: of the eyes, to grow dim; of the head, to turn giddy. The image of sight is kept. Other reading: he will grow dizzy
- *vastam altitudinem in crepidine eius constitutus despexerit*: Altitudo is height or depth alike; since he looks down, the English reads “depth”. Eius refers to the altitudo: he stands on its brink. Other reading: if, standing on its brink, he looks down from a vast height
- *naturalis affectio*: The word is affectio, not affectus, and no fault is implied, so the English reads “feeling” and not “passion”. Other reading: a natural affection; a natural state of being affected
- *inexpugnabilis rationi*: A siege image: a place that cannot be stormed, with reason as the one who fails to storm it. The image is kept. Other reading: unconquerable by reason; which reason cannot overcome

</details>

## Letter 58: On being, Plato's ideas and old age

### 58.22

The fifth kind is of the things that exist in the common way; these begin to concern us; here are all things: people, cattle, objects. The sixth kind is of the things that, so to speak, exist, such as the void, such as time. Whatever we see or touch, Plato does not count among the things that he thinks exist in the proper sense; for they flow, and are in constant loss and gain. None of us is the same in old age as he was when young; none of us is the same in the morning as he was the day before. Our bodies are swept along in the manner of rivers. Whatever you see runs with time; nothing of the things we see remains; I myself, while I say that these things are changing, have changed.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quintum genus*: This closes a list of six ways in which things are said to be, which Seneca gives as Plato’s. Genus is “kind” or “class”. “Exist” stands for esse all through, so that the argument can be followed. Other reading: The fifth class is of the things that are in the common way
- *communiter sunt*: “Exist commonly”: in the ordinary, everyday sense of existing, as against existing “in the proper sense” (proprie) below. It could also be heard as “exist in common, generally”. Other reading: the things that exist in the ordinary sense; that exist generally
- *haec incipiunt ad nos pertinere*: After the higher kinds, these are the first that touch the world of human beings. Pertinere ad is “to concern, to have to do with”; it might also mean that these are the first to belong to the human level of reality. Other reading: with these the list begins to come to what has to do with us
- *homines, pecora, res*: Homines is human beings of either sex, hence “people”. Pecora is strictly herd animals and here seems to stand for animals at large; res is the widest word for a thing. Other reading: human beings, animals, things
- *quasi sunt*: “Exist as it were”: have a sort of existence. The Stoics classed void and time as things without body. Other reading: the things that quasi-exist; that have a kind of existence
- *in assidua deminutione atque adiectione*: Literally “in constant lessening and adding”. The English reads “loss and gain”. Other reading: in constant decrease and increase; subtraction and addition
- *dum loquor mutari ista*: Loquor with an accusative and infinitive: “while saying that these things change”. The present infinitive allows “change” or “are changing”. Other reading: while I speak of these things changing

</details>

### 58.23

This is what Heraclitus says: “Into the same river we go down twice and do not go down.” For the name of the river remains the same; the water has passed on. This is plainer in a river than in a human being; but a course no less swift carries us past too, and so I wonder at our madness, that we love so much a thing so quick to flee, the body, and fear that some day we may die, when every moment is the death of the state before it. Come, do not fear that what happens every day may happen once!

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *in idem flumen bis descendimus et non descendimus*: A saying of Heraclitus, the Greek philosopher of flux (a fragment known in Greek as well; from memory, unchecked). Bis, “twice”, may go with both verbs or only with the first, and the English leaves it placed as in the Latin. Other reading: we do and do not go down twice into the same river
- *aqua transmissa est*: Literally “the water has been sent across, let through”: the water that was there has gone by. Other reading: the water has flowed past; has been carried through
- *amne*: The Latin changes its word for the river here (amnis after flumen). Nothing turns on it, so “river” is kept. Other reading: This is plainer in a stream than in a human being
- *praetervehit*: Praetervehere is to carry past. The Latin does not say past what; the picture is of a current bearing its passengers by. Other reading: but a course no less swift sweeps us along too
- *dementiam*: Dementia is being out of one’s mind, stronger than stultitia, which the glossary renders “folly”. The English reads “madness”. Other reading: our folly; our senselessness
- *prioris habitus*: Habitus is the condition or state a thing is in. Each moment ends the state that held a moment before. Other reading: the death of our former condition
- *vis tu non timere*: Vis tu with an infinitive is an idiom of impatient urging, a command in the form of a question; the edition prints an exclamation mark. The English gives a brisk command, and “Come” stands for the tone, not for a Latin word. Other reading: Will you not stop fearing that what happens every day may happen once?
- *semel*: “Once”, set against “every day”: the single final death against the daily ones. Other reading: may happen once and for all

</details>

### 58.24

I have spoken of man, matter that is flowing and perishable and at the mercy of every cause. The world too, an eternal and unconquerable thing, changes and does not remain the same. For although it has in itself all that it had, it has it otherwise than it had it: it changes its order.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *fluvida materia et caduca*: The phrase is in the ablative and is taken in apposition to homine; it could also be an ablative of description, “man, of flowing and perishable matter”. Fluvida picks up “they flow” from the earlier section; caduca is “apt to fall”, so “perishable”. Other reading: man, a thing of fluid and frail material
- *omnibus obnoxia causis*: Obnoxius is “liable to, exposed to, at the mercy of”. Causis is left bare: any cause at all can act on man. Other reading: exposed to every influence; liable to every accident
- *mundus*: Mundus is the ordered whole, world or universe. The English reads “world”. Other reading: the universe too
- *invicta*: Invictus is “unconquered” and by extension “unconquerable”. Beside “eternal”, and set against man who is at the mercy of every cause, the stronger sense is taken. Other reading: an eternal and unconquered thing
- *in sc habeat*: The copy’s sc is a slip for se (“in itself”), and the English reads it so.
- *ordinem mutat*: Ordo is order or arrangement. The world keeps the same contents but arranges them differently. The Latin has no “its” or “their”, so the order may be that of the contents and not of the world as a whole. Other reading: it changes the arrangement; it changes their order

</details>

### 58.28

For all things remain, not because they are eternal, but because they are defended by the care of the one who governs them: immortal things would need no guardian. These things the craftsman preserves, overcoming the frailty of matter by his own power. Let us despise all the things that are so far from precious that it is doubtful whether they exist at all.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Manent enim cuncta*: Cuncta is “all things together”, the whole. Manent is the same verb as in the river passage, so “remain” is kept, though “last” would read more easily. Other reading: For the whole of things endures
- *cura regentis*: Literally “by the care of the one ruling”. This is the god of the section before, who defends from death what he could not make immortal. The Latin has no “them”. Other reading: by the care of their ruler
- *tutore*: Tutor is the legal guardian of a ward as well as a protector in general. “Guardian” keeps both. Other reading: would need no protector
- *Haec*: “These things” may be the same “all things” just mentioned, or more narrowly the things of this world. The Latin does not decide and the English leaves it open. Other reading: These things here below the craftsman preserves
- *artifex*: Artifex is a maker or craftsman; here it is the god as maker of the world. Vi sua is “by his own force”. Other reading: the maker; the artist, by his own strength
- *adeo pretiosa non sunt ut an sint omnino dubium sit*: Adeo non with ut is “so far from being that”. Sint is the bare verb “to be”, rendered “exist” as in the earlier sections of this letter, so the play between not being precious and perhaps not being at all is only half kept. Other reading: that are so little worth that it is in doubt whether they are at all

</details>

### 58.35

Do not listen to me unwillingly, as though this pronouncement already applied to you; and weigh what I am saying. I shall not leave old age, if it keeps the whole of me for myself, the whole, that is, as regards that better part. But if it begins to shake my mind, if it begins to tear its parts loose, if it leaves me not life but breath, I shall leap out of a building that is rotten and falling down.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ista sententia*: Sententia is an opinion stated, and also a judge’s sentence or a vote in the senate. “Pronouncement” leaves both open; the legal colour would suit “already applied to you”. Other reading: as though this verdict already applied to you; this opinion
- *non relinquam senectutem*: The same verb returns below (reliquerit, “if it leaves”). “Leave” is used both times so the turn shows: old age will not be left unless it leaves only breath. Other reading: I shall not abandon old age; I shall not desert old age
- *totum autem ab illa parte meliore*: Ab here is “in respect of, on the side of”: whole as far as the better part goes. The better part is the mind, as against the body. Other reading: whole, I mean, in that better part of me
- *mentem*: The word is mens, the thinking faculty, not animus; English “mind” serves for both. Other reading: to shake the understanding; the reason
- *partes eius convellere*: Eius is the mind’s. Convellere is to wrench loose or tear up, so the parts may be its faculties pulled away one by one or the whole pulled apart. The verb “begins” is carried over from the clause before, as the Latin leaves coeperit to be understood. Other reading: if it begins to pull its faculties to pieces
- *non vitam reliquerit sed animam*: Anima is here the bare breath of life, set against vita, a life worth the name. The glossary allows “breath” where that is the sense. Other reading: if it leaves me not life but mere existence; not life but only a soul
- *prosiliam ex aedificio putri ac ruenti*: The body in old age is pictured as a rotten house coming down, and leaving life as jumping clear of it. The image is kept and is not explained in the text. Other reading: I shall jump clear of a building that is crumbling and collapsing

</details>

## Letter 59: On pleasure, true joy and self-flattery

### 59.14

I shall now show you how you may understand that you are not a wise man. The wise man is full of joy, cheerful and peaceful, unshaken; he lives with the gods as an equal. Now consult yourself: if you are never sad, if no hope makes your mind anxious with expectation of the future, if by day and by night the course of your mind, lifted up and pleased with itself, is even and level, you have reached the height of human good. But if you go after pleasures, from everywhere and all of them, know that you fall as far short of wisdom as you do of joy. This is what you long to reach, but you go wrong in hoping that you will get there among riches, among honours, that is, in looking for joy among anxieties: those things, which you go after as though they would give gladness and pleasure, are causes of pain.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Sapiens ille*: Ille marks the wise man as the well-known ideal figure, “that wise man”. The English reads “the wise man”, since “the” after “a wise man” already points back and “that” would suggest a particular person. Other reading: That wise man is full of joy
- *gaudio*: Gaudium, “joy”, is in Stoic use the wise man’s settled gladness and is kept apart from voluptas, “pleasure”. The whole passage turns on that contrast, so “joy” and “pleasure” are held fixed. Other reading: full of gladness
- *placidus*: The English reads “peaceful” so as to keep “calm” for tranquillitas, as the glossary has it. Other reading: cheerful and calm; serene
- *cum dis ex pari vivit*: Ex pari is “on an equal footing”. The claim is strong and is not softened. Other reading: he lives on equal terms with the gods
- *ipse te consule*: Consulere with a person is to ask for an opinion; ipse stresses that the asking is done by oneself. Other reading: Now question yourself; now ask your own self
- *nulla spes animum tuum futuri exspectatione sollicitat*: The Latin as given has no “if” before this clause; it rides on the one before, and the English supplies “if”. “Makes anxious” is used so that it answers sollicitudines, “anxieties”, further down. Other reading: if no hope troubles your mind with looking forward to what is to come
- *par et aequalis animi tenor erecti et placentis sibi*: Tenor is an unbroken course. Erecti is “raised, lifted up”, of a mind that is high and confident, and may also be “alert”; “upright” is avoided because in English it means honest. Placentis sibi, “pleased with itself”, is meant in a good sense here. Other reading: the even and steady tenor of a mind that is alert and content with itself
- *humani boni summam*: Summa is the sum total and also the highest point; the English takes the second. Bonum is “the good” in the glossary. Other reading: the sum total of the good open to a human being
- *et undique et cmnes*: The copy’s cmnes is a slip for omnes, and the English reads it so: pleasures from every quarter, and every one of them.
- *Ad hoc cupis pervenire*: Hoc is joy, the nearest neuter noun. It could more loosely be the whole state just described. Other reading: It is this condition you long to reach
- *causae dolorum*: Dolor is “pain” in the glossary. The Latin is plural, which English “pain” covers; here the pains are of mind as much as of body, so “sorrows” would also fit. Other reading: are sources of sorrows; causes of grief

</details>

## Letter 60: On harmful prayers and human greed

### 60.4

So let us count these people, who are, as Sallust says, “obedient to the belly”, as animals, not as human beings, and some of them not even as animals, but as the dead. The one who is of use to many is alive; the one who makes use of himself is alive. But those who lie hidden and inert are in their house as they would be in a tomb. You may carve their name on marble at the very threshold: they have gone ahead of their own death. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ventri oboedientes*: From the opening of Sallust’s Catiline, where cattle are said to be made by nature bent downward and obedient to the belly (from memory, unchecked). Seneca fits the words to human beings. Other reading: slaves to the belly
- *animalium loco numeremus*: Literally “count in the place of animals”, that is, class them as animals. Animal is any living creature with breath. Other reading: let us reckon them among the living creatures, not among human beings
- *ne animalium quidem, sed mortuorum*: Since animal is “a living creature”, the step from animals to the dead is sharper in Latin: not even among the living. “Animals” is kept so the word matches the clause before. Other reading: not even among living creatures, but among the dead
- *Vivit is qui multis usui est*: Vivit stands first in each clause and carries the weight. “Is alive” is used for it because the next step in the passage is to the dead. Other reading: He lives who is of use to many
- *qui se utitur*: Se is ablative with utitur: “who uses himself”, puts himself to use. It answers “is of use to many” just before; the sense may be nearer “has command of himself”. Other reading: the one who has the use of himself; who is of use to himself
- *latitant et torpent*: Two verbs: to keep in hiding and to be torpid, without movement or activity. “Inert” is used for the second because it covers both idleness and the stillness of the dead that the tomb calls for. Other reading: those who skulk and are sluggish; who lie hidden and numb
- *conditivo*: Conditivum is a burial place, a tomb. It is a rare word and the sense is given from memory, unchecked. Other reading: as in a burial vault
- *in limine ipso nomen marmori inscribas*: The picture is an epitaph cut over the house door, as over a tomb. Licet is “you may”, a permission, not a command. Other reading: You may as well inscribe their name on a marble slab right at the doorway
- *mortem suam antecesserunt*: Literally “they have gone before their own death”: they are dead before it arrives. Other reading: they have died before their death; they have anticipated their own death

</details>

## Letter 61: On being ready and willing to die

### 61.1

Let us stop wanting what we have wanted. I at any rate am working at this: that as an old man I should not want the same things I wanted as a boy. To this one thing my days go, to this my nights; this is my work, this my thought: to put an end to old ills. I am working at this, that a day should be to me the equal of a whole life; and, by Hercules, I do not snatch at it as though it were my last, but I look at it as though it could even be my last.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *id ago senex eadem velim quae puer volui*: The Latin as given has no “ne” or “ut” after “id ago” and does not construe; taken loosely it would say the opposite of the first sentence. From memory, unchecked: editions print a supplied “ne” before “senex”, which the digitised copy seems to have lost. The English translates with the negative. Other reading: I at any rate am working at this: that as an old man I should want the same things I wanted as a boy.
- *Desinamus quod voluimus velle*: “voluimus” is a perfect and can be a simple past or a past reaching into the present. The English takes it as reaching into the present. Other reading: Let us stop wanting what we once wanted.
- *veteribus malis*: “mala” can be evils, ills or faults, and the Latin has no possessive here. The English reads “old ills”, a wide word that can cover his own long-standing failings; “fault” is kept in this project for “vitium”. Other reading: to put an end to my old faults
- *instar totius vitae*: “instar” with a genitive means the equivalent or image of something. The English reads “the equal of”. Other reading: that a day should count for me as a whole life
- *mehercules*: A common Roman oath by Hercules, the hero made a god. It is kept as an oath. Other reading: and indeed
- *rapio*: Literally “to snatch” or “to seize”: the picture is of grabbing greedily at a day because it is the last. The image is kept. Other reading: I do not seize it in haste as though it were my last
- *vel ultimus*: “vel” here strengthens: “even” or “actually”. It could also be taken more weakly as “perhaps”. Other reading: as though it could perhaps be my last

</details>

### 61.2

I write you this letter in this frame of mind: as though death were going to call me away at the very moment of writing. I am ready to go, and I shall enjoy life for this reason, that I do not hang in too much suspense over how long this will last. Before old age I took care to live well; in old age I take care to die well. And to die well is to die willingly.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Hoc animo*: “animus” is “mind” in the glossary; here it is a state of mind or mood. The English reads “frame of mind”, which keeps the glossary word. Other reading: In this spirit I write you this letter
- *cum maxime*: An idiom meaning “just now, at this very moment”, here with “scribentem”. Other reading: while I am actually writing
- *evocatura*: “evocare” is to call out or summon, used of calling someone out of a place or calling up soldiers, and “exire”, “to go out”, answers it in the next clause. “Call out” and “go out” mean something else in English today, so the English reads “call away” and “ready to go”, and the shared “out” of the two Latin verbs is lost. Other reading: as though death were going to call me out at the very moment of writing. I am ready to go out
- *fruar*: The form can be future indicative or present subjunctive. The English takes it as future. Other reading: and so let me enjoy life, because
- *non nimis pendeo*: “pendeo” is literally “to hang”: to be in suspense about something or to depend on it. The hanging image is kept, and “not too much” stays as the Latin has it, not “not at all”. Other reading: because I am not too anxious about how long this will be
- *in senectute ut bene moriar*: The Latin has one verb, the past “curavi”, for both halves, but the present subjunctive “moriar” shows that the care for dying well is going on now. The English supplies a present verb in the second half. Other reading: in old age, to die well
- *libenter*: “libenter” runs from “willingly” to “gladly”. The English reads “willingly” because the next section turns on willing and unwilling. Other reading: to die well is to die gladly

</details>

### 61.3

Take care that you never do anything unwillingly. Whatever is going to be necessary for one who resists is no necessity for one who wants it. This is what I mean: the man who takes orders willingly escapes the bitterest part of slavery: doing what he does not want to do. It is not the man who does something under orders who is wretched, but the man who does it unwillingly. So let us set our mind in order so that we want whatever the situation demands, and above all so that we think of our own end without sadness.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *invitus*: The section runs on one family of ideas: “invitus”, “volenti”, “libens”, “nolit”, “velimus”. The English uses “unwillingly” and “willingly” for “invitus” and “libens”, and “want” for every form of “velle” and “nolle”. Other reading: Take care that you never do anything against your will
- *quidquid necesse futurum est repugnanti, id volenti necessitas non est*: The point rests on “necesse” and “necessitas”, so “necessary” and “necessity” are kept. “necessitas” can also mean compulsion, which is the sharper sense here. Other reading: whatever will be forced on one who fights it is no compulsion for one who is willing
- *libens*: As with “libenter” in the section before, this can be “willingly” or “gladly”. “Willingly” holds the contrast with “invitus”. Other reading: the man who takes orders gladly
- *miser*: “miser” is wretched, pitiable or unhappy. The English reads “wretched”. Other reading: it is not the man who does something under orders who is unhappy
- *sic animum componamus*: “componere” is to put together, arrange or settle. The English reads “set in order”. Other reading: So let us compose our mind in such a way that
- *quidquid res exiget*: “res” is very wide: the situation, circumstances, the matter in hand. The verb is future; English uses the present after “whatever”. Other reading: whatever circumstances will require
- *finem nostri*: The phrase means the end of the speakers themselves, that is, death. “End” is kept and “death” is not written, since the Latin avoids the word here. Other reading: that we think of our death without sadness
- *tristitia*: “tristitia” is sadness or gloom. The English reads “sadness”. Other reading: without gloom

</details>

### 61.4

We must be prepared for death before we are prepared for life. Life is equipped well enough, but we are greedy for its equipment; something seems to us to be lacking, and always will seem so. That we have lived enough is brought about neither by years nor by days but by the mind. I have lived, dearest Lucilius, as much as was enough; having had my fill, I await death. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Ante ad mortem quam ad vitam*: “ante” with “quam” can mean earlier in time or first in importance. The English reads “before”, which leaves both open. Other reading: We need preparing for death more than for life.
- *instructa vita est, sed nos in instrumenta eius avidi sumus*: The Latin plays on “instructa” and “instrumenta”, the same root, kept as “equipped” and “equipment”. “instrumenta” are the supplies and furnishings of life. Other reading: Life is well enough provided for, but we are greedy for its provisions
- *ut satis vixerimus*: “satis” comes three times in the section and is “enough” each time. The clause is the object of “faciunt”: what makes it the case that a life has been long enough. Other reading: Whether we have lived enough depends not on years or days but on the mind.
- *animus*: “Mind”, as in the glossary. The sense is the state of the mind, its sense of having had enough. Other reading: but by the spirit
- *Vixi*: A perfect, which can carry the tone of an epitaph: a life summed up as complete. Other reading: My living is done
- *plenus*: Literally “full”, as of someone who has eaten enough; it describes Seneca, not death. The English uses the idiom of having had one’s fill; the Latin does not say full of what. Other reading: full, I await death

</details>

## Letter 62: On making time for study anywhere

### 62.1

They are lying, those who want it to look as though a crowd of business stands between them and liberal studies: they feign occupations, they make more of them, and they are the ones who keep themselves occupied. I have time, Lucilius, I have time, and wherever I am, there I am my own. For I do not hand myself over to affairs but lend myself to them, and I do not go hunting for reasons to waste time. And in whatever place I have come to a stop, there I work over my own thoughts and turn something wholesome over in my mind.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *studia liberalia*: Literally “studies fit for a free person”, the higher studies, here chiefly philosophy. The traditional “liberal studies” is kept. Other reading: serious study
- *simulant occupationes et augent et ipsi se occupant*: The Latin plays on “occupationes” and “occupant”, kept as “occupations” and “occupied”. “augent” can mean that they add to their occupations or that they make them out to be bigger than they are; “make more of them” can be heard both ways. Other reading: they pretend to be busy, they exaggerate it, and they make themselves busy
- *Vaco*: “vaco” is to be unoccupied, to have time, to be at leisure: the opposite of “occupant” just before. The English reads “have time” so that the contrast with being occupied is heard; “free” would suggest liberty in general. Other reading: I am free, Lucilius, I am free
- *meus sum*: Literally “to be one’s own”: to belong to oneself, to be one’s own master. It is kept close. Other reading: there I am my own master
- *non trado sed commodo*: “trado” is to hand over outright, “commodo” to lend something that stays one’s own. The contrast of handing over and lending is kept. Other reading: I do not surrender myself to affairs but put myself at their disposal
- *causas*: “causa” here can be a reason, an occasion or a pretext for wasting time. The English reads “reasons”. Other reading: I do not chase after occasions for wasting time
- *converso*: This is the first-person verb “converso”, “to keep turning over”, not a participle of “converto”. “salutare” is the adjective “wholesome, health-giving” going with “aliquid”. Other reading: and keep some health-giving thought revolving in my mind

</details>

### 62.2

When I have given myself to my friends, I still do not take myself away from myself, and I do not linger with those with whom some occasion has thrown me together, or some cause arising from a citizen’s duty, but I am with all the best men. To them, in whatever place, in whatever age they lived, I send my mind.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *non tamen mihi abduco*: The verb has no object in the Latin as given; the object has to be carried over from the clause before, and the English supplies it. Other reading: I do not, for all that, withdraw from myself
- *tempus aliquod*: Literally “some time”: some occasion or passing circumstance. The English reads “occasion”. Other reading: those with whom some circumstance of the moment has brought me together
- *causa ex officio nata civili*: “causa” may be a reason in general or a legal case; “officium civile” is the duty owed as a citizen, which could include appearing for someone in court. The general “cause” is kept. Other reading: or a case arising from public duty
- *cum optimo quoque*: An idiom, “with each best one”, meaning all the best. The Latin is masculine and the English reads “men”; from what follows, they are the great minds of any place and age. Other reading: I am with the best people, every one of them
- *fuerunt*: Literally “they were”. The English reads “lived”, which is what being in a place and an age amounts to. Other reading: in whatever place, in whatever age they were
- *animum meum mitto*: “animus” is “mind”, as in the glossary. The image is of sending the mind off to keep company with them. Other reading: I send my thoughts to them

</details>

## Letter 63: On keeping grief for a friend within bounds

### 63.15

So let us think constantly about our own mortality as much as that of all those we love. I ought to have said then: “My Serenus is younger than I am: what has that to do with it? He ought to die after me, but he can die before me.” Because I did not, Fortune struck me suddenly and unprepared. Now I think this: all things are mortal, and mortal under an uncertain law. Whatever can happen at any time can happen today.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *de nostra quam omnium*: As given, “quam” has nothing to answer it. From memory, unchecked: editions print a supplied “tam” before “de nostra”, which the digitised copy seems to have lost. The English translates the pair as “as much as”. Other reading: let us think constantly about our own mortality and that of all those we love
- *minor*: “minor” here is “younger” in years, as the section before makes clear. English needs a second term of comparison to complete it. Other reading: My Serenus is the younger man
- *Serenus meus*: Annaeus Serenus, the close friend whose death Seneca has just said he mourned beyond measure. From memory, unchecked: he is the man to whom several of Seneca’s shorter works are addressed. Other reading: my dear Serenus
- *debet*: “debet” against “potest”: what ought to happen in the due order of things against what can happen. “Ought” and “can” are kept. Other reading: he is due to die after me, but he may die before me
- *imparatum subito fortuna percussit*: The object of the blow is understood with “imparatum”. “Fortune” has a capital because it is shown dealing a blow; “subito” is the adverb “suddenly”. Other reading: fortune struck a sudden blow and found me unprepared
- *incerta lege mortalia*: Literally “mortal by an uncertain law”: there is a law, but the order and the day it sets are not fixed, so that the young may die before the old. “lex” can also mean the terms of an agreement. Other reading: and mortal on uncertain terms
- *cogito*: The same verb as “cogitemus” in the first sentence, so “think” is kept in both places. Other reading: Now I reflect that

</details>

## Letter 65: On causes and the worth of studying nature

### 65.2

Talk took the place of the pen, and from it I shall bring you the part that is in dispute. We have named you as arbiter. You have more work on your hands than you think: the case is threefold. Our Stoics say, as you know, that there are two things in the nature of things from which everything is made: cause and matter. Matter lies inert, a thing ready for everything, which will stay idle if no one moves it. But cause, that is, reason, shapes matter and turns it wherever it wishes, and brings varied works out of it. So there must be that from which a thing is made, and then that by which it is made: the second is cause, the first is matter.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *In locum stili sermo successit*: “stilus” is the writing tool, so writing; “sermo” is conversation. The friends who broke in on his writing in the section before stayed to talk. Other reading: Conversation took the place of writing
- *Te arbitrum addiximus*: Legal language: “addicere” is the formal word for assigning a judge or arbiter to a case. “lis”, just before, is a lawsuit as well as a dispute. Other reading: We have assigned the case to you as judge.
- *triplex causa est*: A play on “causa”, which is both a case at law and a cause: “case” here and “cause” in every sentence that follows are one Latin word, the very word in dispute. The English takes the legal sense, which fits “arbiter”; three views of cause are in dispute, the Stoic, Aristotle’s and Plato’s. Other reading: the cause is threefold
- *Stoici nostri*: Seneca counts himself one of the school, and the possessive is kept. Other reading: we Stoics
- *in rerum natura*: The phrase means in the whole of what exists, in the universe; it does not mean “inevitably”, as the English phrase can. The glossary rendering “the nature of things” is kept so that the phrase reads the same wherever it comes. Other reading: that there are two things in nature from which everything is made
- *ex quibus omnia fiant*: “fieri” is both “to be made” and “to come to be”. “Made” is used throughout, because the cause is later called the thing that makes. Other reading: from which all things come to be
- *cessatura*: Literally “going to be idle”: matter will do nothing unless something moves it. Other reading: which will lie unused if no one sets it in motion
- *id est ratio*: “ratio” is “reason”, as in the glossary: the Stoic active principle, which the letter later equates with god. Other reading: that is, the rational principle
- *hoc causa est, illud materia*: “hoc” points to the nearer item (that by which a thing is made) and “illud” to the farther (that from which). The English reads “the second” and “the first” to keep the Latin order. Other reading: the latter is cause, the former matter

</details>

### 65.10

You ask what god’s purpose is? Goodness. So, at any rate, Plato says: “What cause did god have for making the world? He is good; one who is good begrudges nothing good; so he made it as good as he could.” Give your verdict, then, as judge, and declare who seems to you to say what is most like the truth, not who says what is truest; for that is as far above us as truth itself.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *propositum deo*: “propositum” is literally “what is set before one”: an aim or purpose. In the section before it is the last in a list of causes, that for the sake of which god made the world, so the one word “purpose” is kept. Other reading: You ask what aim god set before himself?
- *Ita certe Plato ait*: “certe” can limit (“at any rate”, “at least”) or affirm (“certainly”). The English takes it as limiting: this is Plato’s answer, which Lucilius is then asked to weigh as judge. The passage paraphrased is in Plato’s Timaeus, about 29d to 30a (from memory, unchecked); Seneca gives it in Latin of his own. Other reading: That is certainly what Plato says
- *faciendi mundum fuit causa*: “causa” is the key term of this letter, which compares philosophers’ accounts of the causes of things, so “cause” is kept although “reason” would be more natural English here. “Reason” is also kept for ratio in this translation. Other reading: What was god’s reason for making the world?
- *bono nulla cuiusquam boni invidia est*: “invidia” is both envy and grudging; here it is grudging, the step that leads to the world being made as good as it could be. “cuiusquam boni” can mean “of any good thing” or “of anyone’s good”; the English takes the first, which also fits the Greek as remembered (from memory, unchecked). “Good” is repeated because the argument runs on the repeated word. Other reading: one who is good begrudges no one his good (or, with envy: one who is good envies nothing good)
- *quam optimum potuit*: “optimum” is the superlative of “bonus” and describes the world: literally “he made it as very good as he could”. The English reads “as good as he could”, which keeps the word “good” running through the argument and speaks of the quality of the world, not of the effort. Other reading: so he made it the best that he could
- *Fer ergo iudex sententiam*: The phrase is the courtroom idiom for giving a vote or verdict, and “iudex” stands in apposition to the “you” of the command: “you, as judge”. Lucilius is asked to judge between the accounts of cause that Seneca has set out. “Verdict” is used, leaving “judgment” for iudicium. Other reading: So cast your vote as the judge in the case
- *quis tibi videatur verisimillimum dicere, non quis verissimum dicat*: The Latin plays on “verisimillimum” (most like the truth, most probable) and “verissimum” (truest), followed by “veritas” (truth). The word “truth” is kept visible in all three so the play can be followed, at the cost of the more usual “most probable”. Other reading: who seems to you to say what is most probable, not who says what is most true
- *id enim tam supra nos est quam ipsa veritas*: “id” (“that”) most naturally points back to deciding who says what is truest; it could also mean simply “what is truest”. The English leaves it as “that”, as open as the Latin. Other reading: for to settle that is as far beyond us as truth itself is

</details>

### 65.12

But what we are asking about now is the first and general cause. This must be simple, for matter too is simple. Are we asking what the cause is? Reason that makes, of course: that is, god. For all those things you people have listed are not many separate causes, but hang from one, from the one that makes.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *primam et generalem*: “generalis” is “general” in the sense of belonging to the whole kind, the cause over all the particular ones. “General” is kept. Other reading: the first and universal cause
- *simplex*: “simplex” is simple in the sense of single and uncompounded, against the crowd of causes just listed. Other reading: This must be single; for matter too is single.
- *Quaerimus quid sit causa?*: This can be a question or a statement, and “quid sit causa” can be “what the cause is” (which thing it is) or “what cause is” (a definition). The English follows the copy’s question mark and takes the first sense, because the answer names the one cause that is sought. “quaerimus” is also the verb of the first sentence, so “asking” is used in both places. Other reading: We are asking what cause is.
- *ratio scilicet faciens*: Literally “reason making”, reason as the thing that makes; “scilicet” is “plainly, of course”. The verb “make” is kept to match “the one that makes” at the end. Other reading: Plainly, creative reason
- *rettulistis*: The verb is second person plural: not Lucilius but those who put forward the many causes, Plato and Aristotle with their followers, or the friends arguing their side. The English reads “you people” so that the plural can be heard. Other reading: all those things you have listed
- *multae et singulae*: Literally “many and single”: many causes each standing on its own. The English reads “many separate causes”. Other reading: are not many causes, each one independent

</details>

### 65.22

Never will this flesh drive me to fear, never to a pretence unworthy of a good man; never will I lie to do honour to this little body. When it seems right, I shall break up my partnership with it. And even now, while we are stuck together, we shall still not be partners on equal shares: the mind will take all the rights for itself. Contempt for one’s own body is sure freedom.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *caro ista*: “ista” is a slighting demonstrative pointing at his own flesh. The English reads “this flesh”, which makes plain whose flesh it is but loses some of the scorn. Other reading: Never will that flesh drive me to fear
- *indignam bono simulationem*: “bono” is taken as masculine, the person who is good, not the abstract good. “simulatio” is pretence or putting on a false front. Other reading: to a hypocrisy unworthy of what is good
- *corpusculi*: A diminutive of “corpus”, used with contempt: “little body”, “poor body”. Other reading: this puny body
- *Cum visum erit*: Literally “when it shall have seemed good”. The Latin does not say to whom, and the English leaves it impersonal. It looks forward to leaving the body by death. Other reading: When I see fit
- *distraham cum illo societatem*: “societas” is a partnership in the legal and business sense, and “distrahere” is the word for dissolving one. “socii aequis partibus” and “ius” carry the same legal image on. Other reading: I shall dissolve my association with it
- *haeremus*: Literally “to stick” or “to be fastened”: mind and body fixed together. “Stuck” is used because “cling” would suggest fondness, against the contempt of the passage. Other reading: while we cling together
- *animus ad se omne ius ducet*: “animus” is “mind”, as in the glossary, though here it is set against the body. “ius” is legal right, here the rights of a partner in the joint concern, and “ducet” is literally “will draw”; “omne” is kept as “all”. Other reading: the mind will claim all the authority for itself
- *certa libertas*: “certa” is sure, assured, not in doubt. Other reading: is a sure way to freedom

</details>

### 65.24

The place that god holds in this world, the mind holds in a human being; what matter is there, the body is in us. So let the worse serve the better. Let us be brave against the things of chance; let us not tremble at wrongs, at wounds, at chains, at want. What is death? Either an end or a crossing. I am not afraid of ceasing, for it is the same as not having begun; nor of crossing over, because nowhere shall I be so cramped. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *animus*: “Mind” is kept, as in the glossary, because the comparison is with god as the reason that rules matter. The end of the section, on crossing over at death, would also bear “soul”. Other reading: the soul holds in a human being
- *in hoc mundo*: “mundus” is the world as the whole universe; “illic”, “there”, in the next clause points back to it. Other reading: in this universe
- *Serviant ergo deteriora melioribus*: Neuter plurals: “the worse things” and “the better things”, that is, body and matter under mind and god. “serviant” is the word for serving as a slave. Other reading: So let the lower be slaves to the higher
- *fortes simus adversus fortuita*: The Latin has an echo of sound between “fortes” and “fortuita” that English loses. “fortuita” are things that happen by chance. Other reading: let us be brave against whatever fortune sends
- *iniurias*: “iniuria” is a wrong done to a person, an injury or an insult. The English reads “wrongs”. Other reading: let us not tremble at injuries
- *transitus*: A passing across to somewhere else. “Crossing” and “crossing over” are used for “transitus” and “transire” so that the pair shows. Other reading: Either an end or a passage.
- *nusquam tam anguste ero*: The Latin has an adverb, “so narrowly”: nowhere will be as tight a lodging as the body is now. The Latin leaves “as here” unsaid and so does the English. Other reading: because nowhere shall I be in such narrow quarters as I am now

</details>

## Letter 66: On all goods being equal

### 66.5

On the first day this was the question asked: how can goods be equal, if their condition is threefold? Some, as our people think, are first goods, such as joy, peace, the safety of one’s country. Some are second goods, shaped in unlucky material, such as endurance of torture and self-control in a serious illness.
