The former goods we shall wish for ourselves outright, the latter if it proves necessary. There are also third goods, such as a restrained way of walking, a composed and decent expression, and gestures that suit a man of good sense.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Hoc primo die quaesitum est*: Impersonal passive: the Latin does not say who asked. “Hoc” points forward to the question. The first day is the first of the few days Seneca has just said he spent with Claranus. Other reading: This is what was inquired into on the first day
- *paria bona*: “Bona” is read as the subject and “paria” as what is said of it: how goods can be equal. “Bona” is the Stoic term, rendered “goods” throughout the passage. Other reading: how there can be equal goods
- *triplex eorum condicio*: “Condicio” ranges over condition, situation, status and the terms on which a thing exists. The English keeps the neutral “condition”, because the passage is asking whether the three kinds differ in worth at all. Other reading: if their standing is of three kinds
- *ut nostris videtur*: “Nostris” is “to our people”, meaning the Stoics, the school Seneca follows. The English keeps the Latin’s plain wording and leaves the explanation to this note. Other reading: as the Stoic school holds
- *prima bona*: “Prima”, “secunda”, “tertia” are kept as “first”, “second”, “third” so the three classes stay visible. The Latin leaves open whether this is an order of rank or only of listing, and the question of the section turns on exactly that. Other reading: goods of the first rank, or primary goods
- *gaudium*: In Stoic usage “gaudium” is the settled joy of the wise mind, kept apart from “voluptas” (pleasure). “Joy” keeps that distinction. Other reading: gladness
- *in materia infelici expressa*: “Expressa” is a craftsman’s word: pressed out, moulded, as a figure in wax or clay, and “materia” is the stuff worked on. The English keeps that image. “Infelici” is “unlucky” rather than “unhappy”, so that “happy” stays free for “beatus”. The phrase can also be read without the image, as goods that show themselves in bad circumstances. Other reading: shown in wretched circumstances
- *temperantia*: The virtue of moderation or restraint. In the setting of an illness the English reads “self-control”. Other reading: moderation, or temperance, in a serious illness
- *Illa bona*: The Latin uses plain pointers here, “illa” (those, the ones named earlier) and “haec” (these, the ones just named), not the labels “prima” and “secunda”. “The former” and “the latter” keep them as pointers and keep the reference clear. Other reading: Those goods, and then: these
- *derecto*: Adverb: straight, directly. It is set against “si necesse erit”, so the English reads “outright”, that is, without a condition. It can also be taken as “directly, for their own sake”, a slightly different claim. Some editions print “directo”, with the same sense (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: directly, for their own sake
- *optabimus*: “Optare” is to wish for, to choose, and also to pray for. The English keeps the plainer “wish for”. Other reading: pray for
- *Sunt adhuc tertia*: “Adhuc” here means “besides, in addition”, a use found in the Latin of Seneca’s time, not “up to now”. Other reading: There are still third ones
- *modestus incessus*: “Modestus” is measured, kept within bounds, as well as unassuming. “Incessus” is a person’s manner of walking, not a walk taken. Other reading: a measured gait
- *compositus ac probus vultus*: “Vultus” is the look on the face rather than the face itself. “Compositus” is set in order, settled; “probus” is upright or decent in a broad sense, not only truthful. Other reading: a composed and honest face
- *prudenti viro*: “Prudens” is not “sapiens”. “The wise man” is kept for “sapiens”, and the English reads “a man of good sense” here. Other reading: a prudent man, or a wise man
- *gestus*: “Gestus” covers the carriage of the body in general and, more narrowly, the movements of hands and body. Walk and expression have just been named, so the English takes the narrower sense. The Latin is singular, used collectively. Other reading: a bearing that suits a man of good sense

</details>

### 66.12

Mortal things dwindle and fall, are worn away and grow, are drained and filled; and so, in a lot so uncertain, there is inequality among them. Divine things have one nature. Now reason is nothing other than a part of the divine breath sunk into a human body. If reason is divine, and no good is without reason, then every good is divine. Further, there is no difference between divine things; so there is none between goods either. So joy and the brave, unyielding endurance of torture are equal; for in both there is the same greatness of mind, in the one slackened and loose, in the other fighting and taut.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *minuuntur cadunt*: The other two pairs are opposites (worn away and grow, drained and filled), but “dwindle” and “fall” both point down, so the first pair is odd and the first verb may be corrupt in the transmitted text. The English translates what is given. From memory, unchecked: whether editors read something else here is not known. Other reading: Mortal things rise and fall
- *illis in tam incerta sorte inaequalitas est*: “sors” is a lot or allotted condition. The Latin says “in” so uncertain a lot, not “because of” it, and the English keeps that. Other reading: and so, their condition being so unsure, they are unequal
- *divini spiritus*: “spiritus” is breath, the Stoic warm breath that runs through all things; “spirit” would be the other word. “Breath” keeps it physical. “mersa” is “plunged” or “sunk”. Other reading: a part of the divine spirit plunged into a human body
- *bonum omne divinum est*: “bonum” is “the good”, “a good”, as in the glossary. The argument is a syllogism, and “good”, “reason” and “divine” are kept fixed through it. Other reading: everything good is divine
- *gaudium*: “gaudium” is joy, the wise man’s settled gladness, which the Stoics keep apart from “voluptas”, pleasure. Other reading: So gladness and
- *obstinata*: Here in a good sense: firm, resolved, not giving way. Other reading: the brave and stubborn endurance of torture
- *animi magnitudo*: The glossary allows “spirit” for “animus” where the sense is courage. “Mind” is kept here because the same greatness is said to be present in joy as well as in endurance, and because “spiritus” stands a few lines above, so that “spirit” would blur which Latin word is meant. Other reading: the same greatness of spirit
- *remissa et laxa*: An image of tension, as in a string or a muscle: slack in joy, stretched tight (“intenta”) under torture. It is kept with “slackened and loose” and “taut”. Other reading: in the one relaxed and at ease
- *pugnat et intenta*: As given, a finite verb (“it fights”) is joined to an adjective (“taut”), which breaks the match with the two adjectives before. From memory, unchecked: editions read “pugnax”, “given to fighting”, and the copy has a slip of one letter. The English reads the adjective, as “fighting”. Other reading: in the other it fights and is taut

</details>

## Letter 67: On whether enduring hardship is desirable

### 67.4

Draw distinctions among these things, my Lucilius, and you will understand that there is something in them to be wished for. I would want torture to be far from me; but if it has to be endured, I shall wish to carry myself in it bravely, honourably, with spirit. Why would I not prefer that war not break out? But if it does break out, I shall wish to bear wounds, to bear hunger and everything that the necessity of war brings, nobly. I am not so mad as to desire to be ill; but if I have to be ill, I shall wish to do nothing without self-control, nothing womanish. So it is not the hardships that are to be wished for, but the virtue by which the hardships are borne.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Distingue*: “Make a distinction”: separate the hardship from the way it is borne. “ista” are the things Lucilius listed in the section before (being tortured bravely, being ill patiently). Other reading: Take these things apart, my Lucilius
- *optandum*: The argument turns on “optare” (“optandum”, “optabo” three times, “optabilia”): to wish for, to pray for, to choose. “Wish” is used every time. The usual philosophical word would be “desirable”, and the question in the section before ties it to prayers and vows. Other reading: that there is something in them to be desired
- *velim*: A different verb from “optare”, so it has a different English word, “want”. The subjunctive is polite: “would want”. Other reading: I should like torture to be far from me
- *honeste*: The adverb of “honestum”, “what is honourable” in the glossary. “animose”, next to it, is “with spirit, with courage”. Other reading: bravely, nobly, with courage
- *non incidere bellum*: “bellum” is taken as the subject of “incidere”: that war should not befall or break out. It could also be read loosely as “not to fall into war”, though that would normally need “in”. Other reading: Why would I not prefer not to fall into a war?
- *generose*: Literally “like one of good birth”: nobly, with a high spirit. Other reading: I shall wish to bear them like a man of breeding
- *Ton sum*: “Ton” is not a Latin word; it is a slip in the digitised copy for “Non”, and the English reads “Non sum”.
- *effeminate*: Literally “in a womanish way”, the Roman term of blame for softness in bearing pain. The literal word is kept. Other reading: nothing unmanly
- *incommoda*: “incommoda” are disadvantages or discomforts, the Stoic word for things that are unwelcome but are not evils. The English reads “hardships”, which may be a little strong but fits torture, war and illness. Other reading: So it is not the inconveniences that are to be wished for

</details>

## Letter 68: On retiring without making a show

### 68.14

“And when,” you say, “will what you are learning on your way out be of use to you, or for what purpose?” For this one: so that I may go out better. But you have no reason to think that any age is better suited to a good mind than the age that has tamed itself by many experiences, by long and frequent regret over what it has done, and has come, with its passions softened, to what heals. This is the time for this good: whoever has reached wisdom as an old man has reached it by his years. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *in exitu discis*: “Exitus” is a going out, and so also the end of life. The plain image of leaving is kept because the answer picks it up with “exeam”. Other reading: what you are learning at the end of your life
- *ut exeam melior*: The Latin has only “better”, with no noun, and the English keeps it bare. “Go out” answers “on your way out” in the question. Other reading: so as to depart a better man
- *bonam mentem*: “Bona mens” is Seneca’s set phrase for a mind in good order, close to wisdom. The English reads “a good mind” so that “this good” (“huius boni”) two sentences later can be heard to point back to it. The word here is “mens”, not “animus”. Other reading: a sound mind
- *multis experimentis*: “Experimenta” are things tried: tests, and the experience gained from them. The English reads “experiences” because “trials” today suggests ordeals, which is darker than the Latin. Other reading: by many trials
- *longa ac frequenti rerum paenitentia*: “Rerum” is a bare “of things”: regret for things done or chosen, or a wider dissatisfaction with how things are. The English narrows it to “what it has done”, since “regret for things” says little in English. Other reading: by long and frequent dissatisfaction with things
- *domuit*: “Tamed” continues an image from the section before, where the same root is used of the vices of youth. Other reading: has subdued itself
- *ad salutaria*: Neuter plural, “things that bring health”. It can mean remedies in the medical sense or more generally what is good for one. “What heals” keeps the medical colour without naming medicines. Other reading: has come to what is good for it
- *mitigatis affectibus*: “Mitigare” is to make mild or soft. “Softened” is used and not “calmed” so that “calm” stays free for “tranquillitas”, as in the glossary. Other reading: with its passions calmed
- *annis pervenit*: “Annis” is read as the means: his years are what brought him there. “Pervenit” is spelled the same in the present and the perfect, and the English takes both verbs as perfect. Other reading: whoever reaches wisdom as an old man reaches it through the years

</details>

## Letter 70: On choosing the right time to die

### 70.3

We think that end is a rock, utter madmen that we are: it is a harbour, sometimes to be steered for, never to be refused, and if anyone has been carried into it within his first years, he should no more complain than a man who has made a quick voyage. For, as you know, sluggish winds toy with one man and hold him back and tire him out with the tedium of a very slow calm, while a persistent breeze carries another through very quickly.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Scopulum esse illum*: “Illum” points back to a masculine noun meaning “end” at the close of the section before, where it stands for death. “End” is supplied so that the sentence can stand alone. Other reading: that one is a rock
- *dementissimi*: A superlative, “most mad”, attached to the subject of “think”. The English gives it as an aside. Other reading: in utter madness
- *aliquando petendus*: “Aliquando” can be “sometimes” (on some occasions one should steer for it) or “at some time, one day”. The contrast with “never” and the letter’s subject, leaving life by choice, point to “sometimes”. Other reading: one day to be steered for
- *intra primos annos*: Literally “within the first years”. It can mean the first years of life or, in the image, the first years of the voyage. The English keeps it literal, and “his” leans towards life. Other reading: in the first years of the voyage
- *ludunt*: “Ludere” is to play with or make sport of someone, and also to cheat. The winds are taken as toying with the sailor. Other reading: mock one man
- *tranquillitatis lentissimae taedio*: “Tranquillitas” here is a literal calm at sea, not calm of mind; the glossary word “calm” fits both. “Lentissimae” is a superlative of “slow, dragging”, given as “very slow”, since “sluggish” already stands for “segnes” in the same sentence. Other reading: with the boredom of the most sluggish of calms
- *pertinax flatus*: “Flatus” is a blowing, a different word from the “venti” that hold the first sailor back, so the English reads “breeze”. “Pertinax” is holding on, persistent, sometimes stubborn: here a wind that does not drop. Other reading: an unrelenting blast

</details>

### 70.4

Consider that the same happens to us: life has brought some very quickly to the place that even those who hang back had to come to, and others it has soaked and stewed. Life, as you know, is not always to be held on to. For it is not living that is a good, but living well. So the wise man will live as long as he ought, not as long as he can.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *puta*: An imperative, “think” or “suppose”. It applies the image of the voyage to human lives. Other reading: Suppose the same happens
- *quo veniendum erat etiam cunctantibus*: The Latin is impersonal: “where there had to be a coming, even for those who hang back”. The English takes it as a general remark about everyone who delays. It can also be read of the same people who were brought quickly. Other reading: to the place they had to come to even if they hung back
- *maceravit et coxit*: Both verbs are kitchen words (to soften by soaking, to cook) and both are also used of wearing down and tormenting. The English keeps the kitchen image, with “stewed” for its hint of slow suffering. The contrast with “very quickly” shows a long, slow process. Other reading: others it has worn down and tormented
- *Quae*: The relative picks up “vita”. The English begins a new sentence and repeats “life” for clarity. Other reading: which, as you know, is not always to be held on to
- *non enim vivere bonum est, sed bene vivere*: “Bonum” is the Stoic term, the good, and “bene” (well) plays on it. The English reads “a good”, not the glossary’s “the good”, because the word is a predicate here: living is not one of the goods. Other reading: for the good is not living, but living well
- *quantum debet, non quantum potest*: “Quantum” is “as much as”. Of a life it comes to “as long as”, though the Latin does not mention time. Other reading: as much as he ought, not as much as he can

</details>

### 70.6

To die sooner or later does not matter; to die well or badly does matter. And to die well is to escape the danger of living badly. So I consider most unmanly the saying of that man of Rhodes who, when he had been thrown into a cage by a tyrant and was being fed like some wild animal, said to someone who advised him to refuse food: “A human being should hope for everything while he lives.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Citius mori aut tardius*: “Tardius” is “more slowly” or “later”. Paired with “citius” it means the timing of death, not how long the dying takes. Other reading: To die more quickly or more slowly
- *ad rem non pertinet*: An idiom, literally “does not bear on the matter”, that is, makes no difference. The phrase is repeated in the positive, so the English uses “does not matter” and then “does matter”. Other reading: is not to the point
- *male vivendi periculum*: “Periculum” is danger or risk. “Male vivere” answers “bene mori” and could mean living wretchedly as well as living wrongly. “Badly” is kept to hold the pairing. Other reading: the risk of living in misery
- *effeminatissimam vocem*: Literally “most effeminate” or “most womanish”, a superlative of a word that reproaches softness in a man. “Unmanly” is the plainer word today. “Vox” is a thing said, an utterance. Other reading: the most womanish of utterances
- *illius Rhodii*: “Illius” points to the man as one the reader is expected to know, and the English gives it as “that”. From memory, unchecked: this is Telesphorus of Rhodes, mutilated and caged by King Lysimachus, whose story Seneca also tells in On Anger. Other reading: of that famous man of Rhodes
- *abstineret cibo*: Literally “to abstain from food”. The adviser is recommending that he starve himself to death. “Refuse food” says as much without adding the word death, which the Latin does not have. Other reading: to starve himself
- *homini*: “Homo” is a human being, not a male, and it stands against the wild animal the speaker was being fed like. “He” in “while he lives” follows the Latin singular. Other reading: A man should hope for everything while he lives.
- *speranda sunt*: The form says what is to be done: “everything is to be hoped for”. It can also be heard more weakly as “may be hoped for”. The English takes the stronger sense, a rule of conduct, which is what Seneca calls unmanly. Other reading: A human being may hope for everything while he lives.

</details>

## Letter 71: On virtue as the only good

### 71.2

Whenever you want to know what is to be avoided or what is to be aimed at, look to the highest good, the purpose set before your whole life. For whatever we do ought to agree with it: no one will put the particulars in order unless he already has the whole of his life set before him. No one, though he has his colours ready, will produce a likeness unless it is already settled what he wants to paint. The reason we go wrong is that we all deliberate about the parts of life, and no one deliberates about the whole.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quid fugiendum sit aut quid petendum*: The verb petere comes back in the next section for the archer’s target and the sailor’s harbour. “Aim at” is used here and there so that the link shows. Other reading: what is to be fled or what is to be sought
- *propositum totius vitae tuae*: Propositum is literally “the thing set before”, and the same word returns as a verb in “proposita est”. The English reads “the purpose set before” here and “set before him” there, so that the echo can be heard. Other reading: the purpose of your whole life
- *vitae suae summa proposita est*: Summa can be the whole or sum of a thing, or its chief point, and it echoes summum bonum (the highest good). The English takes “the whole of his life”, set against the particulars and preparing the parts and whole at the end, though the Latin word there (tota) is a different one. Other reading: unless the chief end of his life is already set before him
- *non disponet singula, nisi cui*: The Latin has no stated subject: literally “he will not arrange the particulars, except one for whom”. The English supplies “no one … unless”. Other reading: only a man who already has the whole of his life before him will arrange the particulars
- *quamvis paratos habeat colores*: Quamvis can be a plain “although” or a matter of degree, “however ready”. The English takes the plain sense. Other reading: however ready he has his colours
- *nisi iam constat quid velit pingere*: Constat is impersonal, “it is settled”. It could be settled for the painter himself or simply settled; the impersonal form is kept. Other reading: unless he is already clear what he wants to paint
- *Ideo peccamus*: Peccare ranges from making a mistake to doing wrong. “Go wrong” covers both. Other reading: This is why we make mistakes

</details>

### 71.3

The man who wants to shoot an arrow must know what he is aiming at, and then direct and guide the shaft with his hand: our plans go astray because they have nothing to be directed at; no wind is favourable to one who does not know what harbour he is aiming for. Chance is bound to have great power in our life, because we live by chance.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quid petat*: The same verb as “petendum” in the section before and “quem portum petat” below. “Aim at” and “aim for” are kept so that the three uses read as one thought. Other reading: what he is seeking
- *derigere ac moderari manu telum*: Telum is any missile or weapon; here it is the arrow, so the English has “shaft”. Derigere returns as “derigantur”, and “direct” is kept for both. Other reading: to point and control the weapon with his hand
- *errant consilia nostra*: Errare is both to wander and to miss, so the plans stray like badly aimed arrows. Consilia can be plans, decisions or pieces of advice; the letter began from a request for advice, but here the word is general. Other reading: our counsels wander
- *nullus suus ventus est*: Literally “no wind is his own”. A wind that is “one’s own” is an idiom for a favourable wind, and the English gives the sense with “favourable”. Other reading: no wind is his own wind
- *quia vivimus casu*: Casus is “chance” in both halves of the sentence, and one word is kept. The ablative could also be heard as “at random”. Other reading: because we live at random

</details>

### 71.7

Socrates, who called the whole of philosophy back to conduct and said that this is the highest wisdom, to distinguish goods from evils, says: “Follow them, if I have any authority with you, so that you may be happy, and let yourself seem a fool to someone. Let anyone who wishes insult you and wrong you; you will still suffer nothing, so long as virtue is with you. If you want to be happy,” he says, “if you want to be truly a good man, let someone despise you.” No one will achieve this unless he has first despised all things himself, unless he has put all goods on one level, because there is no good without what is honourable, and what is honourable is equal in all.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *revocavit ad mores*: Mores is conduct, character, morals: the part of philosophy now called ethics. The English takes “conduct”. Other reading: brought the whole of philosophy back to morals
- *hanc summam dixit esse sapientiam*: Summam can be an adjective with sapientiam (“the highest wisdom”) or a noun (“the sum”). The English takes the adjective. Other reading: said that the sum of wisdom is this
- *illos*: The pronoun (“those”, masculine plural) has no antecedent inside this section. In the section before, Seneca sets the people who discovered these teachings against those who only teach them, and “them” is taken to be the discoverers. Other reading: Follow those men
- *Si vis inquit*: The quotation marks in the Latin as given are defective here: a mark opens after “inquit” with none closed before it. The English treats everything from “sequere” to “aliquis” as one speech of Socrates, with “he says” put in twice. The words seem to echo the end of Plato’s Gorgias (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: “If you want,” he says, “to be happy …” as a second, separate saying
- *tibi contumeliam faciat et iniuriam*: Contumelia is an insult, an affront to a person’s standing; iniuria is a wrong or injury done. The English turns the two nouns into verbs. Other reading: Let whoever wishes do you insult and injury
- *fide bona*: A legal phrase, literally “in good faith”, here meaning really and without pretence, so the English has “truly”. The verb “to be” is understood from the clause before. Other reading: if you want to be a good man in good faith
- *contempserit*: The same verb as “contemnat” just before: he who is to bear being despised must first have despised everything. “Despise” is kept for both. Other reading: unless he has first looked down on all things himself
- *omnia bona exaequaverit*: Literally “has made all goods equal”, that is, has come to count every good as equal to every other. “Put on one level” shows that the levelling is in how he rates them. Other reading: has made all goods equal
- *honestum in omnibus par est*: Honestum is the Stoic term for what is honourable, the morally fine. “In omnibus” can be “in all goods”, “in all things” or “in all cases”; the English “in all” leaves this open, though the goods just mentioned are the nearest sense. Other reading: what is honourable is equal in every case

</details>

### 71.13

All things move through fixed periods: they must be born, grow, be extinguished. Whatever you see running above us, and these things on which we lean and have been set as though they were the most solid of things, will be worn away and will cease. There is nothing that does not have its own old age. Nature sends these things off to the same place after unequal spans: whatever is will not be, yet it will not perish but will be dissolved.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Certis eunt cuncta temporibus*: Tempora can be times, seasons or set periods. The English takes “fixed periods”, with “move” for eunt (“go”), so that “run” is kept for currere in the next sentence. Other reading: All things go at fixed times
- *exstingui*: The image is of a flame going out. “Be extinguished” keeps it; the plain sense is “die”. Other reading: die out
- *supra nos vides currere*: This means the heavenly bodies, which “run” their courses overhead. The English leaves it as Seneca has it, without naming them. Other reading: whatever you see moving in its course above us
- *quibus innixi atque impositi sumus veluti solidissimis*: This is the earth underfoot. “Veluti solidissimis” is taken as “as though they were the most solid of things”; it could also be heard as a plain comparison. Other reading: on which we lean and are set as on the most solid of things
- *carpentur ac desinent*: Carpere is to pluck or wear away bit by bit. The English takes “worn away”. Other reading: will be plucked away and come to an end
- *Inaequalibus ista spatiis eodem natura dimittit*: Spatium is a stretch of distance or time, and also a lap of a racecourse; dimittere is to send off or dismiss. “Eodem” is “to the same place”, the same end. “Spans” can be heard of time or of distance. Other reading: Nature dismisses these things to the same end over courses of unequal length
- *nec peribit sed resolvetur*: Nec here turns against what has just been said, so the English has “yet”. Resolvi is to be loosened or broken up into what a thing was made of; the next section of the letter turns on this word, so “dissolved” is used. Other reading: and it will not perish but be broken up

</details>

### 71.15

And so, like Marcus Cato, when he has run through the ages in his mind, he will say: “The whole human race, both the part that is and the part that will be, is condemned to death. All the cities that anywhere hold supreme power, and all those that are the great ornaments of empires not their own: one day it will be asked where they were, and they will be done away with by one kind of destruction or another. Wars will pull some down; others will be eaten away by idleness and peace turned to sloth, and by a thing destructive to great wealth, luxury. All these fertile plains will be hidden by a sudden flood of the sea, or carried off into a sudden chasm by the slipping of the ground as it sinks. What reason is there, then, for me to be indignant or to grieve, if I go a brief moment ahead of the common fate?”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Itaque ut M. Cato, cum aevum animo percucurrerit, dicet*: The verb “dicet” has no stated subject; the section before speaks of the mind that would bear its end more bravely if it saw further. The English supplies “he”, a person of such a mind who speaks “like Marcus Cato”, because a mind running through time “in its mind” (animo) would be strange; “it will say” and “as Marcus Cato will say” are also possible. Cato is Cato the Younger, who killed himself rather than live under Caesar. Other reading: And so, as Marcus Cato will say when he has run through the ages in thought
- *aevum*: Aevum is time in its whole length, or an age. The English has “the ages”, without an “all” that the Latin does not have. Other reading: when he has run through time in his mind
- *morte damnatum est*: A legal phrase: sentenced, with death as the penalty. The English keeps “condemned to death”. Other reading: has been sentenced to death
- *rerum potiuntur*: An idiom for being master of affairs, holding the highest power. The English has “hold supreme power”. Other reading: that anywhere are masters of the world
- *alienorum imperiorum magna sunt decora*: Taken of cities that do not rule but are the glory of empires belonging to others. The phrase is compressed and could be read more loosely. Other reading: and those that are the great glories of foreign empires
- *ubi fuerint aliquando quaeretur*: Impersonal in the Latin, and kept so. “Aliquando” is “one day”. Other reading: some day people will ask where they stood
- *magnis opibus exitiosa res, luxus*: Opes is wealth, resources or power, and of ruling cities “great power” is possible. The English takes “great wealth”, the sense that goes most closely with luxury. “Exitiosa” picks up “exitii” (destruction) a line earlier, so the English has “destructive”. Other reading: and luxury, a thing ruinous to great power
- *repentini maris inundatio*: Literally “the flooding of a sudden sea”: the Latin attaches “sudden” to the sea. The English moves it to the flood. Other reading: the flood of a sudden sea
- *indigner aut doleam*: Dolere is to feel pain or to grieve. Here it is the grief of a man facing his own end, so the English has “grieve”. Other reading: why I should be indignant or feel pain
- *exiguo momento publica fata praecedo*: “Publica fata” is the fate shared by all people and all states; for Cato the word also suggests the fate of the republic. “Exiguo momento” is the small measure by which he goes first. Other reading: if I go before the fate of all by a tiny margin

</details>

### 71.16

Let a great soul obey god and undergo without hesitation whatever the law of the universe commands. Either it is sent out into a better life, to remain among divine things in greater light and greater calm, or at least it will be without any trouble, if it is mixed back into nature and returns into the whole. So Marcus Cato’s honourable life is not a greater good than his honourable death, since virtue cannot be stretched any further. Socrates used to say that truth and virtue are the same thing. As truth does not grow, so virtue does not either: it has its full complement, it is full.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Magnus animus*: The glossary prefers “mind” for animus, with “soul” where it is what survives the body. That is exactly the question in this sentence, where the animus is sent out into a better life or mixed back into nature, so the English has “soul”. Other reading: Let a great mind obey god
- *patiatur*: Pati is to suffer, bear, undergo. The English takes “undergo”, since the sentence is about accepting what is commanded. Other reading: and suffer without delay whatever the law of the universe orders
- *lucidius tranquilliusque inter divina mansurus*: The Latin has two comparative adverbs, “more brightly and more calmly”. The English turns them into nouns to be readable. Other reading: to stay more brightly and more calmly among divine things
- *sine ullo futurus incommodo*: Incommodum is a mild and general word: inconvenience, disadvantage, trouble. “Trouble” keeps it mild without narrowing it to bodily feeling. Other reading: or at least it will be free of any harm
- *si naturae remiscebitur et revertetur in totum*: The “if” clause is attached to the second alternative only: the soul does not live on but is blended back into the whole. Other reading: if it is to be blended with nature again and go back into the universe
- *M. Catonis maius bonum*: The genitive can be “Cato’s” good or a good “for Cato”. The English attaches it to the life and the death. Other reading: So for Marcus Cato an honourable life is not a greater good than an honourable death
- *non intenditur virtus*: Intendere is to stretch or tighten, as a string or a bow. The Stoic point is that virtue has no degrees and cannot be raised higher, which is why the next sentences say it does not grow. The English keeps the image with “stretched any further”. Other reading: since virtue cannot be increased
- *habet numeros suos*: An idiom: a thing that “has its numbers” has every element that makes it whole. The English gives the sense with “its full complement”, and keeps “full” for plena. Other reading: it has its numbers

</details>

### 71.27

I do not take the wise man out of the number of human beings, nor do I move pains away from him as from some rock that lets in no feeling. I remember that he is made up of two parts: one is without reason, and this part is bitten, burned, feels pain; the other has reason, and this part holds unshaken beliefs, is fearless and unconquered. In this part is placed that highest good of a human being. Before that good is brought to fullness, there is an unsure wavering of the mind; but when it has been made complete, the mind has a stability that does not move.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *summoveo*: Summovere is to clear away or keep at a distance. Seneca denies that he puts the wise man out of reach of pain. Other reading: nor do I fence pains off from him
- *nullum sensum admittente*: Sensus is feeling in the sense of sensation. “Feeling” is used here and for “sensus” in section 29. Other reading: that admits no sensation
- *irrationalis*: “Without reason” and “has reason” keep the glossary word “reason” (ratio) in view. Other reading: one is irrational … the other rational
- *mordetur, uritur, dolet*: Two passives and then an active verb. The English keeps the change. Other reading: is stung, is scorched, hurts
- *inconcussas opiniones*: The glossary gives “opinion” for opinio and allows “belief” for a settled belief. These are the firm convictions of the rational part, so the English has “beliefs”. Other reading: holds unshaken opinions
- *indomita*: Indomita is “untamed, unconquered”, and such forms can also mean “not to be tamed, unconquerable”. The English keeps the first sense. Other reading: is fearless and unconquerable
- *Antequam impleatur*: The subject is the highest good (the neuter “perfectum est” confirms it), and the English names it as “that good” so that “it” is not taken as the rational part. Impleri is to be filled up, brought to its full measure. Other reading: Before it is filled out
- *incerta mentis volutatio*: Volutatio is a rolling or tossing about. Mens is “mind” here as animus is elsewhere. Other reading: there is an uncertain tossing of the mind
- *immota illi stabilitas est*: “Illi” can be the mind, the good itself or the man. The English takes the mind, as the contrast with the wavering mind suggests. Other reading: it has an unmoved stability

</details>

### 71.28

And so the man who is only begun and is moving toward the heights, one who cultivates virtue, even if he is coming near to the complete good but has not yet put the finishing touch to it, will from time to time go backward and relax something of the exertion of his mind; for he has not yet crossed over the unsure ground and is still on slippery footing. But the happy man, whose virtue is perfected, loves himself most when he has put himself to the test most bravely, and the things the rest fear, if they are the price of some honourable duty, he not only bears but embraces, and he much prefers to hear “so much the better man” than “so much the more fortunate”.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inchoatus*: Literally “the begun one”: a person who is himself begun but not finished, like a piece of work, set against the man of perfected virtue below. Other reading: the man who is still unfinished
- *cultorque virtutis*: Cultor is one who tends or cultivates, and also a worshipper. Other reading: and a devotee of virtue
- *summam manum imposuit*: An idiom from the workshop: literally “put the last hand to it”. Other reading: has not yet put the last hand to it
- *ibit interim cessim*: Interim can mean “meanwhile, for the time being” or, in Seneca’s time, “sometimes”. The English takes “from time to time”. Cessim is backward, giving ground. Other reading: will for the time being go backward
- *ex intentione mentis*: Intentio is the tension of a stretched string, hence effort or concentration. “Exertion” is used here and for the same word in section 35. Other reading: something of the tension of his mind
- *nondum enim incerta transgressus est*: “Incerta” picks up the “unsure” wavering of the section before. “Ground” is supplied to go with the slippery footing that follows. Other reading: for he has not yet passed beyond what is uncertain
- *virtutis exactae*: A genitive of description: a man “of perfected virtue”. Exactus is finished off, brought to exactness; it is a different word from perfectum, which is “complete” in this letter. Other reading: the happy man of finished virtue
- *cum fortissime expertus est*: The verb has no stated object. “Himself” is understood from “se … amat” just before; the trial could also be of fortune or of hardship, or the sense could be passive. Other reading: when he has been tried most bravely
- *metuenda ceteris*: Literally “things to be feared by the rest”: things everyone else finds fearful, not things they are obliged to fear. Other reading: the things that others dread
- *si alicuius honesti officii pretia sunt*: Pretium is price or reward. The fearful things are taken as what an honourable duty costs. Other reading: if they are the reward of some honourable service
- *tanto melior*: A colloquial cry of approval, something like “well done” (from memory, unchecked). The English keeps the literal comparison so that it answers “tanto felicior”, where felix is “fortunate” and not the “happy” of beatus. Other reading: “well done” rather than “lucky you”

</details>

### 71.29

I come now to the point to which your expectation calls me. So that our virtue may not seem to wander outside the nature of things: the wise man will tremble, and feel pain, and turn pale, for all these are feelings of the body. Where, then, is the disaster, where is that true evil? There, of course: if these things drag the mind down, if they bring it to a confession of slavery, if they make it dissatisfied with itself.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *illo quo me vocat exspectatio tua*: Literally “to that place where your expectation calls me”: the thing Lucilius has been waiting to hear. Other reading: I come now to what you are waiting for
- *Ne extra rerum naturam vagari virtus nostra videatur*: A purpose clause that explains why Seneca makes the admission that follows; the colon marks that loose link. “Virtus nostra” most likely means virtue as the Stoic school describes it, not the virtue that Seneca and Lucilius possess; the English keeps the bare possessive of the Latin. Other reading: Lest virtue as the Stoics teach it seem to stray beyond nature
- *et tremet sapiens et dolebit et expallescet*: The triple “et” piles the verbs up; the first could also be heard as “even”. Dolebit is physical pain here. Other reading: the wise man will even tremble and suffer and grow pale
- *corporis sensus*: Sensus is sensation. “Feeling” is kept, as in section 27. Other reading: sensations of the body
- *illud malum verum*: “Verum” is the adjective “true”, set after the noun for weight. Other reading: where is the real evil
- *animum detrahunt*: Detrahere is to pull down or to pull away. The English takes “drag down”, which fits the slavery that follows. The verb is slightly odd with this object and the text may be doubted (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: if these things pull the mind away
- *illi paenitentiam sui faciunt*: “Illi” is the mind (or the wise man). Literally “produce in it regret of itself”: it becomes sorry to be what it is. Other reading: if they make him sorry to be what he is

</details>

### 71.30

The wise man, it is true, conquers fortune by virtue, but many who have professed wisdom have at times been terrified by the slightest threats. Here the fault is ours, who demand the same thing of the wise man and of the man who is making progress. I am still urging on myself the things I praise; I have not yet convinced myself. Even if I had convinced myself, I would not yet have them so ready or so practised that they would run out to meet whatever happens.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *vincit virtute fortunam*: Fortune is an opponent here, but it is not addressed or described as a person, so it has no capital. Other reading: defeats Fortune by virtue
- *professi sapientiam*: Profiteri is to declare openly, and so to claim or to make a profession of something. These are people who announced themselves as philosophers. Other reading: many who have laid claim to wisdom
- *nostrum vitium est*: The glossary gives “vice” for vitium, and “fault” where it is small. Here it is a mistake of judging, so “fault”. Other reading: On this point the vice is ours
- *a proficiente*: The proficiens is the Stoic term for one who is advancing toward wisdom but is not yet wise. Other reading: and of the learner
- *Suadeo adhuc mihi ista quae laudo, nondum persuadeo*: A play on suadeo (urge, advise) and persuadeo (urge with success, convince). English has no such pair on one root, so the English has “urging” and “convinced”. The Latin verb is a present, “do not yet convince”. Other reading: I am still recommending to myself what I praise; I do not yet persuade myself
- *ad omnes casus procurrerent*: A military image: troops running forward to meet an attack. Casus is the word rendered “chance” in section 3; here, in the plural, it is the things that befall a person. Other reading: that they would run out to meet every chance

</details>

### 71.31

Just as wool takes on some colours in one go, and others it does not drink in fully unless it has been soaked and boiled again several times, so there are other branches of learning that intellects make good on at once, as soon as they have received them: this one, unless it has gone down deep and settled for a long time and dyed the mind rather than coloured it, makes good on nothing of what it had promised.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *semel ducit*: Ducere colorem is to take on a colour; semel is “once”, at a single dyeing. Other reading: absorbs some colours at the first dipping
- *saepius macerata et recocta*: Saepius is “rather often, repeatedly”. It is taken with both participles. Other reading: steeped again and again and boiled a second time
- *non perbibit*: The prefix per- means “through and through”, hence “drink in fully”. Other reading: does not soak up
- *alias disciplinas ingenia*: Disciplinae are branches of learning; “alias” (other) is answered by “haec” (this one) after the colon. Ingenium is inborn ability, and “intellects” is used so that “mind” stays free for animus later in the sentence. Other reading: so there are some studies which natural talents
- *protinus praestant*: Praestare is to make good, deliver or show in practice; it returns in “praestat” at the end, and “make good on” is kept for both. Other reading: can show at once
- *haec*: “This one” is the branch of learning Seneca has been speaking of, philosophy or wisdom. It is not named in this section and the name is not added. Other reading: but this branch of learning, philosophy
- *alte descendit et diu sedit*: Sedere is to sit or settle, as a dye sets. Other reading: has sunk deep and sat long
- *animum non coloravit sed infecit*: The contrast is between colour on the surface and dye taken right through; literally “has not coloured the mind but dyed it”. Inficere is to dye or steep; it can also mean to stain, but there is no bad sense here. Other reading: has not tinted the mind but steeped it

</details>

### 71.35

Things that are incomplete are bound to totter, and now go forward, now slip down or collapse. And they will slip down, unless they keep on going and striving; if they slacken anything of their zeal and faithful exertion, they have to go backward. No one finds his progress where he had left it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Imperfecta*: Neuter plural, “incomplete things”, though the sense is people who are not yet made perfect. The neuter is kept, and “incomplete” answers “complete” for perfectum in sections 27 and 28. Other reading: Those who are not yet perfect
- *labent*: From labare, to totter or be unsteady, not from labi, to slip. Other reading: are bound to be unsteady
- *sublabantur aut succidant*: Sublabi is to slip or sink down from under; a backward direction is stated only later, in “retro”. Succidere is to sink under oneself, as knees do. “Sublabentur” in the next sentence repeats the first verb, and “slip down” is repeated. Other reading: now slip back or give way
- *ire et niti*: Niti is to strain, push or climb with effort. Other reading: to go on and to struggle
- *fideli intentione*: Intentio is tension, and “laxaverint” (slacken) keeps the image of a string. “Exertion” is used as in section 28. Studium is zeal or devoted study. Other reading: their eagerness and loyal tension
- *retro eundum est*: An impersonal form, “there must be a going back”. The English gives it to the same subject, “they”, since no new subject is meant. Other reading: one has to go backward
- *Nemo profectum ibi invenit ubi reliquerat*: Profectus is progress, the noun of proficere, which the next section plays on. Other reading: No one picks up his progress at the point where he dropped it

</details>

### 71.36

So let us press on and persevere. More remains than we have got through, but a great part of progress is wanting to make progress. This I know of myself: I want to, and I want to with my whole mind. I see that you too have been roused and are hurrying with great force toward the finest things. Let us hurry: only so will life be a gift. Otherwise it is a delay, and a shameful one at that, for those who live among foul things. Let us see to it that all time is ours; but it will not be, unless we have first begun to be our own.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *plus quam profligavimus restat*: Profligare is to strike down, and so to bring a task or a war near its end. The English takes the plain “got through”. Other reading: more is left than we have dispatched
- *magna pars est profectus velle proficere*: Profectus (genitive) and proficere are noun and verb of one root. “Progress” is kept for both. Other reading: a large part of advancing is the will to advance
- *Huius rei conscius mihi sum*: An ordinary Latin phrase for knowing something of oneself at first hand. The literal wording, “conscious to myself of this thing”, is stiff in English. Other reading: This I can vouch for in my own case
- *mente tota*: Mens, not animus. “Mind” is used for both. Other reading: with all my heart
- *instinctum*: Instinctus is goaded, fired, set in motion. Other reading: that you too have been spurred on
- *ad pulcherrima*: Literally “toward the most beautiful things”, with a moral sense: the noblest aims. They are set against the “foul things” (foeda) below. Other reading: toward what is most beautiful
- *ita demum vita beneficium erit*: Beneficium is a kindness or favour done to someone, a word Seneca uses with weight. Life will count as something given only on this condition; “gift” is used because “benefit” sounds like a mere advantage. Other reading: only so will life be a benefit
- *alioquin mora est*: The subject can be life (“it is a delay”, a mere waiting) or nothing (“there is delay”). The English takes life. Other reading: otherwise there is delay
- *inter foeda versantibus*: Versari is to move about in a place, to spend one’s days there. The participle has no pronoun: the English keeps the general “for those who”, though with “let us” on both sides it may well mean Seneca and Lucilius themselves. Other reading: for us who spend it among foul things
- *nostrum omne tempus sit*: Literally “that all time be ours”, with the possessive once. The sense is most likely all the time a person has, not all the ages. Other reading: that all our time is ours
- *nisi prius nos nostri esse coeperimus*: A play on the possessive: time will belong to a person only when he himself is “his own”, that is, belongs to himself. Other reading: unless we first begin to belong to ourselves

</details>

## Letter 72: On not letting business postpone philosophy

### 72.3

It is not when you have time free that you are to do philosophy, but you are to make time free so that you may do philosophy. Everything else is to be neglected so that we may sit in attendance on philosophy, for which no time is long enough, even if life is drawn out from boyhood to the farthest limits of a human lifetime. It does not matter much whether you give philosophy up or leave it off for a time; for it does not stay where it was interrupted, but, like things stretched tight that spring apart, it runs back all the way to its beginnings, because it has left off being continuous. We must resist the things that keep us busy: they are not to be untangled but pushed out of the way. There is in fact no time that is ill suited to a study that brings health; and yet many do not study in the midst of the things on account of which one ought to study.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Non cum vacaveris philosophandum est, sed ut philosopheris vacandum est*: The sentence turns on one verb, vacare (to have time free, to be at leisure), used twice and crossed with “do philosophy” twice. Both duties are impersonal in the Latin (“philosophy is to be done”, “time is to be made free”), while the two other verbs are second person. The English makes both duties personal (“you are to”) so that it reads plainly, and keeps “time free” for vacare both times. Other reading: Philosophy is not to be done when you have leisure; leisure is to be made so that you may do philosophy.
- *ut huic assideamus*: Assidere is to sit beside someone in attendance, as at a sickbed or beside a teacher. The image of sitting is kept. The Latin says only “this”, and the English names philosophy so that the reference is clear. Other reading: in order to attend closely to this
- *humani aevi*: Aevum is a span of life or an age. Here it is the length of a human life, so “a human lifetime”; the same word is “lifetime” in Ep. 74.10. Other reading: the farthest limits of the human span
- *utrum omittas philosophiam an intermittas*: A play on two compounds of one verb: omittere (let go altogether) and intermittere (leave off for a time). English cannot keep the echo; “give up” and “leave off for a time” keep the difference between stopping for good and stopping for a while. Other reading: whether you drop philosophy or interrupt it
- *eorum more quae intenta dissiliunt*: The image is open: things under tension that “leap apart” may be things stretched tight that snap and fly back (a cord, a spring) or things strained that burst. The English takes them as stretched things springing apart, since the point is the recoil to the starting point. Other reading: like things that burst when they are strained
- *quod a continuatione discessit*: Quod can be “because”, with philosophy still the subject, or “whatever”, starting a general statement with a new subject. The English takes it as “because”, since the sentence is built as “it does not stay…, but runs back…” and such a pair normally keeps one subject. Other reading: but, like things stretched tight that spring apart, whatever has broken its continuity runs back all the way to its beginnings
- *occupationibus*: The word picks up the busy days and the fresh business of the section before: whatever fills one’s time. “Occupations” is the nearer English word in form, but today it suggests jobs. Other reading: Occupations are to be resisted
- *nec explicandae sed summovendae sunt*: Explicare is to unfold, disentangle or sort out in order; summovere is to clear out of the way, the word used of clearing a crowd from someone’s path. Both are kept as physical images. Other reading: they are not to be settled one by one but removed
- *inter illa non student propter quae studendum est*: “The things” are not named. They are most likely the troubles and pressures that make the healing study necessary in the first place, though they could be the busy affairs themselves; the English leaves them unnamed as the Latin does. Studere and studium are “study” throughout, though the word also means earnest pursuit in general. Other reading: many fail to study in exactly the circumstances that make study necessary
- *studio salutari*: Salutaris is giving health or saving; the study meant is philosophy as a cure. The English reads “a study that brings health”. Other reading: a wholesome pursuit

</details>

### 72.4

“Something will come up to get in the way.” Not, at any rate, in the way of the man whose mind is glad and lively in every piece of business. In those who are still imperfect, gladness is cut apart; but the wise man’s joy is woven in one piece: it is torn by no cause, by no fortune. He is calm always and everywhere. For he does not hang on what is not his own, and he does not wait for the favour of fortune or of a man. His happiness is home-grown: it would go out of the mind if it came into it. It is born there.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Incidet aliquid quod impediat*: An imagined objection, marked as quoted in the Latin; the speaker could be Lucilius or anyone. In the reply, “the man” is the object of “get in the way”, so the English repeats the phrase to make that clear. Other reading: “Something will happen to hinder you.” Not him, at least, whose mind…
- *imperfectis adhuc interscinditur laetitia, sapientis vero contexitur gaudium*: Seneca sets laetitia against gaudium, and the mind in the sentence before is called laetus. “Glad” and “gladness” stand for laetus and laetitia and “joy” for gaudium, so that the contrast shows. The verbs come from cloth: one is cut apart, the other woven together, which the English gives as “woven in one piece”. Other reading: the cheerfulness of the imperfect is interrupted, but the joy of the wise man is woven together
- *imperfectis adhuc*: Literally “those still not completed”: people who are making progress but are not yet wise. “Imperfect” is the same word in English dress and keeps the sense of something not yet brought to completion. Other reading: for those who are still unfinished
- *nulla causa rumpitur, nulla fortuna*: Causa may be “cause” or “occasion, circumstance”; fortuna here is any turn of fortune and could be personified. The English keeps “cause” and “fortune” with a small letter. Rumpere is to break or burst; “torn” keeps the cloth of the verb before. Other reading: no circumstance breaks it, no stroke of Fortune
- *ex alieno pendet*: Alieno can be neuter (what is another’s, what lies outside him) or masculine (another person). The English takes it as neuter, “what is not his own”, and keeps the image of hanging, where plainer English would say “depend on”. Other reading: he does not depend on another person
- *favorem fortunae aut hominis*: Fortune stands beside “a man” as a giver of favour, so it is close to personified; the small letter is kept. Hominis is singular: “of a man” or “of any person”. Other reading: the favour of Fortune or of any human being
- *Domestica illi felicitas est*: Felicitas is good fortune, prosperity or happiness; domestica means belonging to his own house, produced at home. “Happiness” is used here so that “fortune” stands only for fortuna in this section; the same word is “good fortune” in Ep. 74.18 and 75.15. “Happy” is otherwise this project’s word for beatus, which does not occur in this section. Other reading: His good fortune is of his own household
- *exiret ex animo si intraret*: A compressed thought: what comes into the mind from outside can also leave it, so his happiness is not something that entered. The English is literal and leaves the inference to the reader, as the Latin does. Other reading: if it had come into the mind from outside, it would also leave it

</details>

## Letter 74: On virtue as the only safe good

### 74.10

Whoever resolves to be happy should think that the only good is what is honourable. For if he considers anything else a good, first, he passes an unfavourable judgment on providence, because many troubles happen to just men, and because whatever it has given us is short and slight, if you compare it with the lifetime of the whole world.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *unum esse bonum putet quod honestum est*: The Stoic thesis that what is honourable (honestum) is the only good (bonum). Unum is “one” and here amounts to “the only”, as the next clause (“anything else”) shows. Other reading: should think that there is one good, what is honourable
- *constituet*: Future tense in the Latin (“shall resolve”); English uses the present in such a clause. Constituere is to decide or settle on. Other reading: Whoever shall have made up his mind to be happy
- *primum*: “First” opens a list whose next member falls outside this section, so the English “first” stands alone here as the Latin does. Other reading: in the first place
- *male de providentia iudicat*: Literally “judges badly about providence”: forms a low opinion of it. “Judgment” is kept because iudicat is the verb of iudicium. “Unfavourable” shows that the judgment is a low one and not a mistaken one, though the Latin could mean either. Other reading: he thinks ill of providence
- *incommoda*: Incommoda is the Stoic word for disadvantages, the opposite of commoda (advantages): things that are unwelcome but not truly evils. “Troubles” is the plain English; the technical colour is lost. Other reading: many disadvantages befall just men
- *quidquid nobis dedit*: The subject of “has given” is not stated; it is taken to be providence, carried over from the clause before, hence “it”. Other reading: whatever she has given us
- *mundi totius aevo*: Aevum is a lifetime or an age; mundus is the world in the sense of the whole ordered universe, not the earth. “World” is kept for mundus so that it matches Ep. 74.20, where “universe” is needed for another word. Other reading: the lifetime of the entire universe
- *si compares*: The sentence moves from “he” to a general “you”. The second person is kept as the Latin has it. Other reading: if one compares it

</details>

### 74.18

Let all those things come to us as additions and not stick fast to us, so that, if they are led away, they may leave without tearing us at all. Let us use them, not boast of them, and let us use them sparingly, as things deposited with us that are going to leave. Whoever has possessed them without the use of reason has not kept them long; for good fortune itself, unless it is kept within measure, bears itself down. If he has trusted in goods that are the quickest to run away, he is soon deserted, and, in order to be deserted, he is struck down. Few have been allowed to lay their good fortune down gently: the rest fall together with the things among which they stood high, and the very things that had lifted them up weigh them down.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Omnia ista*: “Those things” are the advantages of the section before: things called goods by opinion, which Seneca has just said should be called advantages and treated as property, not as parts of a person. Other reading: All those advantages
- *accedant, non haereant*: Accedere is to come to, to be added; haerere is to stick or cling. “As additions” brings out the sense of something added on from outside, which a bare “come” would lose. Other reading: Let all these things come near us, not cling to us
- *si abducentur*: Abducere is to lead away, the verb used of taking off slaves or cattle; the section before has just called these things slaves. The verb is future: Seneca expects it to happen. Other reading: if they are taken away
- *sine ulla nostri laceratione*: Literally “without any tearing of us”: the image is of something stuck to the flesh that tears it when pulled off. The image is kept, with a verb in place of the noun. Other reading: without any wound to us
- *tamquam depositis apud nos et abituris*: A legal image: a deposit is property left in one’s keeping, to be returned on demand. The same verb returns below in “lay down” good fortune (deponere), an echo English cannot keep with one word. Other reading: as things entrusted for safe keeping and soon to depart
- *sine ratione*: Ratio is “reason” in the glossary, and the neighbouring sections speak of reason as what moderates wealth. “The use of” is added so that the phrase is not read as “for no reason”. The Latin can also mean simply “without method”. Other reading: Whoever has possessed them without a plan
- *ipsa enim se felicitas, nisi temperatur, premit*: Felicitas is good fortune or prosperity; premere is to press hard on or weigh down. “Good fortune” is used here and in Ep. 75.15; in Ep. 72.4, where the word stands beside fortuna, it is “happiness”. Temperare is to keep in due measure. Other reading: for prosperity, unless it is moderated, crushes itself
- *Si fugacissimis bonis credidit, cito deseritur*: The subject is not stated. By grammar it could be good fortune, the subject just before; by sense and tense (the same past form as “has possessed”) it is the person, and the English takes it so. “Goods” is used loosely here, of things Seneca has just said are goods only in name. Other reading: If it has trusted in the most fleeting goods, it is soon deserted
- *ut deseratur, affligitur*: The clause is doubtful. The English takes ut as purpose: for the goods to leave him he is knocked down, which fits the next sentence about how few lay good fortune down gently. It can also be read as a concession. Other reading: and, even granted that he is deserted, he is struck down
- *cito deseritur, et, ut deseratur, affligitur*: As given, the second clause comes close to repeating the first, and the text may be at fault. A “not” (non) may have dropped out before deseratur; this is from memory, unchecked. The English follows the Latin as given. Other reading: he is soon deserted, and, even if he is not deserted, he is battered
- *deponere felicitatem molliter*: Deponere is to lay or set down, like a load, and echoes the “deposited” things above. Molliter is softly, gently, without a crash. Other reading: to put off their prosperity without a shock

</details>

### 74.20

Let him not be indignant at anything that happens to him, and let him know that the very things by which he seems to be harmed serve to preserve the universe and are among those that bring the course of the world and its task to completion. Let whatever has pleased god please man. For this very reason let him admire himself and what is his: that he cannot be conquered, that he holds evils themselves beneath him, that by reason, and nothing is stronger than reason, he subdues chance and pain and wrong.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nihil indignetur sibi accidere*: The subject “he” is the man of the section before, and these wishes answer the question there about what the inner equipment is. Nihil may be the subject of “happen” (“that anything happens to him”) or adverbial (“let him in no way resent what happens”). The sense is close either way. Other reading: Let him in no way resent what befalls him
- *laedi videtur*: “Seems to be harmed”: the seeming may be his own impression or how it looks to others. The English leaves it as open as the Latin. Other reading: by which he thinks he is harmed
- *ad conservationem universi pertinere*: Pertinere ad is to bear on or tend towards. The Latin has a noun, “the preservation of the universe”; the English turns it into a verb. Other reading: have to do with the preservation of the universe
- *cursum mundi officiumque consummant*: Officium is duty, function or appointed task; it is said here of the world itself. Consummare is to bring to a full total, to complete. Mundus is “world” and universi just before is “universe”; Seneca may mean no difference between them. Other reading: that fulfil the course and function of the universe
- *placeat homini quidquid deo placuit*: One verb, placere, is used of god and of man; “please” is kept for both. Deus is singular, “god”, as in the glossary. Other reading: let man approve whatever god has approved
- *ob hoc ipsum suaque miretur*: As given, the Latin does not quite construe: the “and” in suaque has no first object to join. “Himself” is supplied on the assumption that se has dropped out before suaque; editions are believed to print se suaque (from memory, unchecked). If the Latin is read as it stands, ipsum has to serve as “himself” and “very” goes. Other reading: For this let him admire himself and what is his
- *miretur*: Mirari is to admire or to wonder at. “Admire” is used because the sense is to think highly of, not to be puzzled by. Other reading: let him wonder at himself and what is his
- *mala ipsa*: “Evils themselves”, said from the ordinary point of view: for a Stoic these are not truly evils. The plain word is kept. Other reading: the very things called bad
- *casum doloremque et iniuriam*: Casus is chance, accident or mishap; dolor is “pain” in the glossary and could be grief; iniuria is a wrong or injury done to one by another. The English reads “chance and pain and wrong”. Other reading: misfortune, grief and injustice

</details>

## Letter 75: On plain writing and stages of progress

### 75.15

Let us give some thought to this rank: it will go well with us if we are admitted to this group. The second step is won through great good fortune in one’s nature and through great and unremitting effort in study; but not even this third colour is to be despised. Think how much evil you see around you; look how there is no outrage without a precedent, how much progress wickedness makes every day, how much wrong is done in public and in private: you will understand that we attain enough if we are not among the worst.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *De hoc loco aliquid cogitemus*: Locus is place or rank; here it is the third class of people making progress, described in the section before. It could also mean “topic”. Cogitare de can mean to think about or to set one’s mind on as an aim; “thought” is kept so that it matches “Think” below (cogita). Other reading: Let us aim at this rank
- *bene nobiscum agetur*: An idiom, literally “it will be dealt well with us”: to be well treated, to be doing well. Other reading: it will be a good outcome for us
- *in hunc admittimur numerum*: Numerus is a number in the sense of a class or company. The verb is present tense in the Latin, where English also uses a present after “if”. Other reading: to be admitted to this number
- *Magna felicitate naturae*: Felicitas is good fortune; with naturae it means a lucky natural endowment. “Good fortune” matches Ep. 74.18; “one’s” is added for plain English. Other reading: by great gifts of nature
- *intentione studii*: Intentio is a straining or stretching, so effort or application; studium is study or earnest pursuit, kept as “study”. Other reading: by great and constant application to study
- *secundus occupatur gradus*: Occupare is to seize or take possession of; gradus is a step or rank. The English reads “is won”, with good fortune and effort as the means. The Latin has no “only”, though the sense leans that way, and none is added. Other reading: the second rank is reached
- *color tertius*: Literally “third colour”, where “class” or “kind” would be expected: the third class of people making progress is meant. What the image is drawn from (a shade, a cast, a mark of rank) is not clear, and “colour” is kept as an image. The text may be doubted. Other reading: this third kind
- *quam nullum sit nefas sine exemplo*: Exemplum can be a precedent (every crime has been done before), an instance (every crime is in fact being committed) or a model to copy. Sine exemplo usually means “unprecedented”, so the English reads “without a precedent”. Nefas is a crime in the strong sense of something abominable. Other reading: how no abomination lacks its example
- *nequitia proficiat*: Proficere, to make progress, is Seneca’s usual word for moral progress; here it is wickedness that progresses, which looks like deliberate irony. The English keeps “progress”. Other reading: how far wickedness advances every day
- *publice privatimque peccetur*: An impersonal passive: “how much wrongdoing there is”. Publice and privatim can mean in public and in private, or by communities and by individuals. The English keeps the passive and takes the first. Other reading: how much wrong is done by states and by individuals

</details>

## Letter 76: On learning in old age and the only good

### 76.4

But I am ashamed of the human race every time I go into the school. As you know, anyone making for Metronax’s house has to pass right by the theatre of the Neapolitans. That place, to be sure, is packed, and with enormous eagerness judgment is passed on who is a good piper; a Greek trumpeter and a herald draw a crowd as well. But in the place where a good man is what is inquired into, where a good man is what is learned, very few sit, and to most people these seem to have no good business to be about; they are called silly and idle. May that mockery fall to me: the abuse of the ignorant must be heard with a calm mind, and one who is heading for what is honourable must hold contempt itself in contempt.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *scholam intravi*: Schola here is the philosopher’s lecture room, that of Metronax at Naples, whose house the next sentence names. The perfect with quotiens marks a repeated action, so the English has a present. Other reading: whenever I have entered the lecture hall
- *pythaules*: A Greek word for a player of the pipe (aulos), by its make-up one who plays the Pythian piece; from memory, unchecked, it is also used of a solo piper in the theatre. The English narrows it to “piper”. Other reading: Pythian piper
- *quis sit pythaules bonus iudicatur*: Impersonal passive, literally “it is judged who is a good piper”. Bonus is then repeated for the good man and for “good business”, so “good” is kept each time. Other reading: they decide who is a fine piper
- *habet tubicen quoque Graecus et praeco concursum*: Graecus agrees with tubicen, but it may be meant to colour the herald too. The English leaves it with the trumpeter only. Other reading: a Greek trumpeter and a Greek herald also draw a crowd
- *in quo vir bonus quaeritur, in quo vir bonus discitur*: Literally “where a good man is sought, where a good man is learned”. Quaeritur can mean “is looked for” or “is inquired into”; set against the theatre, where the question judged is who is a good piper, the English takes the second. Discitur leaves open whether what is learned is what a good man is or how to be one, and the English keeps that open and keeps the two clauses parallel. Other reading: where a good man is looked for, where one learns to be a good man
- *nihil boni negotii habere quod agant*: Literally “to have nothing of good business that they might do”. Boni carries on the play on “good”, so the word is kept. Other reading: to have nothing worthwhile to occupy them
- *aequo animo*: An idiom for taking something calmly, literally “with a level mind”. “Mind” is kept for animus, as the glossary prefers, and “calm” stands for aequus; the same phrase is given the same way at 76.23. Other reading: calmly
- *inperitorum convicia*: Inperitus is one without knowledge or skill. “The ignorant” fits here, where the point is that they do not know what the school is for; at 76.34 the same word is “the inexperienced”. Other reading: the jeers of the inexperienced
- *contemnendus est ipse contemptus*: A play on contemnere and contemptus, kept with “contempt” twice. Other reading: being despised is itself to be despised

</details>

### 76.5

Keep going, Lucilius, and hurry, so that what has happened to me does not happen to you: to be learning as an old man. Or rather, hurry all the more for this reason, that you have now taken on something you could hardly learn through to the end as an old man. “How much progress shall I make?” you say. As much as you attempt.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ne tibi accidat quod mihi, ut senex discas*: The ut clause spells out what has happened to Seneca: he is learning as an old man. The English sets it after a colon so that it attaches to the thing that happens and to nothing else. Other reading: so that it does not happen to you, as it has to me, that you learn as an old man
- *quod perdiscere vix senex possis*: Open: either the subject is so large that one can hardly finish learning it by the time one is old, or it is one that a man already old could hardly master. The English adds nothing and so leaves both readings standing, as the Latin does. Other reading: something that you could hardly learn thoroughly if you were an old man
- *proficiam*: The form is either future indicative or present subjunctive; the English takes the future. Proficere is the usual word for a learner’s progress in philosophy. Other reading: How far am I to get?
- *Quantum temptaveris*: Future perfect, literally “as much as you will have attempted”. English uses a present in such a clause. Other reading: As far as you will have tried.

</details>

### 76.8

Every thing rests on its own good. What recommends the vine is its fruitfulness and the flavour of its wine; what recommends the stag is speed. Of pack animals, whose one use is this, to carry a load, you ask how strong they are in the back. In a dog the first thing is keenness of scent if it has to track wild animals, running if it has to catch them, boldness if it has to bite and attack. In each thing, what ought to be best is that for which it is born, that by which it is rated.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Omnia suo bono constant*: Constare with the ablative can mean “consist in”, “depend on” or “stand firm by”. The English takes it that each thing rests on the good that is its own, and “rests” keeps the sense of standing. Bonum is “good” as in the glossary. Other reading: All things consist in their own good.
- *commendat*: One verb serves both the vine and the stag, and the English repeats it. It means what makes a thing valued. Other reading: Fruitfulness and the flavour of its wine commend the vine, speed the stag
- *quam fortia dorso iumenta sint quaeris*: A general “you”, kept as the Latin has it. Iumenta are beasts of burden. Other reading: one asks how strong in the back beasts of burden are
- *sagacitas*: Of a dog this is keenness of scent; of a person it would be shrewdness. Other reading: sharpness
- *cui nascitur, quo censetur*: Cui is a dative of purpose, “for which it is born”. Censeri is the word for being assessed in the census, so “rated”. Other reading: that for which it comes into being and by which it is valued

</details>

### 76.9

What is best in a human being? Reason: by this he goes ahead of the animals and comes next after the gods. So perfected reason is the good peculiar to him; everything else he has in common with animals and plants. He is strong: so are lions. He is handsome: so are peacocks. He is swift: so are horses. I say nothing of his being outdone in all these; I am not asking what he has in him that is greatest, but what is his own. He has a body: so do trees. He has impulse and voluntary movement: so do beasts and worms. He has a voice: but how much louder is the dogs’, how much shriller the eagles’, deeper the bulls’, sweeter and more supple the nightingales’?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *In homine*: Homo is the human being as a kind, not the male. The English has “a human being” and then “he” for the pronouns. Other reading: In man
- *hac antecedit animalia, deos sequitur*: Sequitur can mean “comes next after” in rank or “follows” as one follows a leader. Paired with going ahead of the animals, it is taken as rank. Other reading: by this he goes ahead of the animals and follows the gods
- *Ratio ergo perfecta proprium bonum est*: Ratio is “reason” (the Stoic term). Perfecta is reason brought to completion; “perfect” is used for this verb through 76.9 and 76.10 because the argument turns on it. Proprium is what belongs to him and to nothing else, so “peculiar to him”. Other reading: So reason brought to completion is his proper good
- *satisque*: Sata are literally “things sown”, that is crops or plants. Other reading: and with crops
- *Non dico, in his omnibus vincitur*: The Latin sets the plain statement that he is outdone beside non dico, with nothing joining them. The English takes this as passing over a point that is granted; it could be read as declining to make the claim. Other reading: I am not saying that in all these he is outdone
- *impetum ac motum voluntarium*: Impetus is the Stoic term for the impulse that sets a living thing moving, and “impulse” is kept. Other reading: drive and movement at will
- *clariorem canes, acutiorem aquilae*: Clarior can be “louder” or “clearer”; for dogs the English takes “louder”. Acutior is high and piercing. Other reading: how much clearer the dogs’, how much higher the eagles’
- *luscinii*: A masculine form of the usual luscinia, nightingale. It is read as nominative plural, parallel to the other animals; it could be genitive singular. Other reading: the nightingale’s

</details>

### 76.10

What is peculiar to a human being? Reason: when this is right and brought to its full measure, it has made a human being’s happiness complete.
