So if every thing, when it has perfected its own good, is praiseworthy and has reached the goal of its nature, and if a human being’s own good is reason, then if he has perfected this he is praiseworthy and has touched the goal of his nature. This perfected reason is called virtue, and it is also what is honourable.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *recta et consummata*: Consummata is “brought to its sum”, finished off. It is kept distinct from perfecta, for which the English has “perfected”. Other reading: upright and consummate
- *felicitatem hominis implevit*: Felicitas, not beata vita; “happiness” is used all the same, since “good fortune” would mislead here. The perfect implevit states a general truth; literally “has filled up”. Other reading: fills up the measure of human good fortune
- *cum bonum suum perfecit*: Perficere is to carry through to completion. “Perfect” is used for it each time (perfecit, perfecit, perfecta) so that the chain of the argument shows. Other reading: when it has brought its own good to completion
- *si hanc perfecit*: The subject is not stated; it is taken to be the human being, with hanc as reason. Other reading: if it has perfected this
- *finem naturae suae tetigit*: Finis is the end in the sense of goal, and “goal” is used both times so that it is not read as a stopping. Tetigit is literally “has touched”, and the image is kept. Other reading: has attained the end of his nature
- *eademque honestum est*: Literally “and the same thing is the honourable”. Honestum is “what is honourable”, as in the glossary. Other reading: and this same thing is what is honourable

</details>

### 76.23

You must grant that the good man is a man of the highest piety towards the gods. So whatever happens to him he will bear with a calm mind; for he will know that it has happened by the divine law by which all things go forward. If that is so, the one good for him will be what is honourable; for in this lies obeying the gods, and not flaring up at sudden events or bewailing his lot, but patiently accepting fate and doing what is commanded.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *summae pietatis erga deos*: Pietas is dutiful devotion, wider than the English “piety”. “Piety” is kept because the gods are named. Other reading: of the greatest dutifulness towards the gods
- *aequo animo sustinebit*: Aequo animo is an idiom for taking something calmly, literally “with a level mind”. The English has “with a calm mind”, keeping the glossary’s “mind” for animus, as at 76.4. Other reading: he will bear calmly
- *lege divina qua universa procedunt*: Universa is “all things together”, close to “the universe”. Procedunt is “go forward, take their course”. Other reading: by the divine law under which the whole proceeds
- *unum illi bonum erit quod honestum*: Unum is “one” in the sense “the only”. Illi can be “for him” or “in his eyes”; the English has “for him”. Other reading: in his eyes the only good will be what is honourable
- *in hoc enim positum*: The verb est is understood. Hoc may point back to what is honourable, or forward to the list of infinitives; it is taken as pointing back. Other reading: for what is honourable consists in this: to obey the gods
- *excandescere ad subita*: Excandescere is literally “to grow white hot”, used of flaring up in anger. Subita are things that come suddenly. Other reading: to blaze up at the unexpected
- *facere imperata*: A phrase with a military ring, “to carry out orders”. Other reading: carrying out orders

</details>

### 76.29

But even the man from whom this joy too is snatched away, the joy that the handling of his greatest and last work gives, will leap down into death without any hesitation, content to act rightly and dutifully. Go further and set against him many things to dissuade him; say: “Your deed will be followed by early oblivion and by a less than grateful verdict from your fellow citizens.” He will answer you: “All those things lie outside my work; I am looking at the work itself. I know that this is honourable; so wherever it leads and calls, I come.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *etiam hoc gaudium*: “This joy” is the one in the section before: the pleasure a brave man takes in looking ahead to the deed he is about to do. Other reading: even this joy
- *tractatio operis maximi et ultimi*: Tractatio is “handling”. It can be handling in thought, which the section before describes, or the actual carrying out of the deed; “handling” leaves both open. Other reading: dwelling on his greatest and last work; or, the carrying out of his greatest and last work
- *desiliet in mortem*: The image is of jumping down, as from a height or a horse, and it is kept. Other reading: will plunge into death
- *recte pieque*: Pie is the adverb of pietas, acting as duty to gods, country and kin requires. The English has “dutifully”; “piously” would be too narrow here. Other reading: rightly and piously
- *Oppone etiamnunc*: Etiamnunc is “even now” or “still further”. It is taken as adding a further test. Other reading: Even now set against him
- *parum grata existimatio civium*: Existimatio is what the citizens think of the deed; “verdict” is used because the glossary keeps “judgment” for iudicium and “opinion” for opinio. Grata can be “grateful” or “favourable, pleasing”, and parum is “too little”; the English takes the citizens’ view as short of grateful. Other reading: a verdict from your fellow citizens that is far from favourable
- *quocumque ducit ac vocat venio*: The subject of ducit and vocat is not stated: it is either what is honourable or the work, and “it” leaves that open. Venio is kept as “come”, in the present tense. Other reading: wherever what is honourable leads and calls, I go

</details>

### 76.31

None of those whom you see dressed in purple is happy, any more than any of those to whom the plays assign a sceptre and a cloak on the stage: when they have walked before the people with a broad swagger and in high boots, the moment they have gone off, their boots are taken off and they return to their own stature. None of those whom riches and offices set at a greater height is great. Why then does he seem great? You are measuring him together with his pedestal. A dwarf is not great though he has taken his stand on a mountain; a colossus will keep its greatness even if it stands in a well.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *purpuratos*: Men in purple, that is courtiers and men of high rank, purple being the dress of kings and their courts. Other reading: the courtiers in purple
- *felix*: Felix, not beatus. It leans towards “fortunate”, but “happy” is used to match “happiness” for felicitas at 76.10. Other reading: fortunate
- *in scaena fabulae adsignant*: Fabulae is taken as nominative plural, the subject of adsignant. It could be genitive singular with scaena, leaving the verb with an unnamed “they”. Other reading: to whom they assign a sceptre and a cloak on the stage of a play
- *chlamydem*: A chlamys is a Greek cloak, here the mantle of a stage king. The English has only “cloak”, as the Latin does. Other reading: a royal cloak
- *lati incesserunt et coturnati*: Lati is “broad”, of a walk and bearing that take up room, or of the padded figure of the tragic actor; the English takes it of the walk. From memory, unchecked, some editions print elati, “raised up”. Coturnati is “wearing the cothurnus”, the tragic actor’s boot with a thick sole. Other reading: have walked raised up and in high boots
- *in altiore fastigio*: Fastigium is a gable or peak, and so high rank. The English keeps the image as “height”, and statura in the sentence before is “stature” so that the two are not confused. Other reading: on a higher summit
- *cum basi illum sua metiris*: Basis is the pedestal of a statue. The sentence answers the question and is taken as a statement. Other reading: Because you measure him pedestal and all.
- *Non est magnus pumilio*: Magnus means both “great” and “big, tall”, and the passage moves from one to the other. “Great” and “greatness” are kept throughout so that the argument can be followed. Other reading: A dwarf is not tall
- *colossus*: A giant statue, like the one at Rhodes. Other reading: a giant statue

</details>

### 76.32

This is the error we suffer from, this is how we are taken in: we value no one by what he is, but add to him the things he is decked out with as well. Yet when you want to make a true valuation of a human being and to know what he is like, examine him naked; let him lay aside his estate, let him lay aside his offices and the other lies of fortune, let him strip off even his body: look at his mind, what it is like and how great, whether it is great by what is another’s or by what is its own.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *sic nobis inponitur*: Impersonal passive of imponere with the dative, in the sense “to deceive, to impose on”. Other reading: this is the trick played on us
- *aestimationem inire*: A phrase from reckoning accounts, “to enter on a valuation”. “Value” and “valuation” are kept for aestimamus and aestimationem. Other reading: to arrive at a true estimate of a human being
- *patrimonium*: A man’s estate, often but not only property that has come down to him from his father. The English has “his estate” and does not press the inheritance. Other reading: his inherited wealth
- *fortunae mendacia*: Mendacia are lies or shams. Fortune is close to being a person here; the English leaves it in lower case, and a capital would also be defensible. Other reading: Fortune’s other shams
- *corpus ipsum exuat*: Exuere is to take off clothing; the body is treated as one more garment. Other reading: let him take off his very body
- *animum intuere*: Animus is set against the body here, but not as what survives it, so the glossary’s “mind” is kept. Intuere is “look at”, distinct from inspice (“examine”) earlier in the sentence. Other reading: look at his soul
- *alieno an suo magnus*: Alieno and suo are neuter ablatives, “by what belongs to another or by what is his own”. Magnus could agree with the mind (animus is masculine) or with the man; the English takes the mind. Other reading: whether he is great by another’s goods or by his own

</details>

### 76.34

When an evil has been thought out beforehand, its blow comes soft. But to fools and to those who trust fortune every face that events show seems new and unexpected; and for the inexperienced a great part of an evil is its newness. So that you may know this: the things they had thought harsh they bear more bravely once they have grown used to them.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Praecogitati mali*: Malum is “an evil”, the opposite of bonum; here it is a misfortune that falls on someone. “Evil” is kept for both occurrences. Other reading: a trouble foreseen
- *fortunae credentibus*: Those who trust fortune, that is, who count on their luck lasting. Other reading: those who put their faith in luck
- *nova rerum et inopinata facies*: This takes up the words of the verse quoted in the section before, where no face of hardship rises new or unexpected (Vergil, Aeneid 6, about lines 103 to 105; from memory, unchecked). “Face”, “new” and “unexpected” are kept so that the echo shows, and rerum is given as “events”. Other reading: every turn of events seems strange and unforeseen
- *apud inperitos*: At 76.4 the same word is “the ignorant”. Here the point is lack of experience of trouble, as the next sentence on growing used to things shows, so “the inexperienced” fits the argument better. Other reading: among the ignorant
- *Hoc ut scias*: An idiom that introduces a proof, literally “so that you may know this”. The second person, which is Lucilius, is kept. Other reading: To see that this is so
- *fortius*: “More bravely” or “more firmly”. Other reading: with more strength

</details>

## Letter 77: On a friend's calm chosen death

### 77.4

A journey will be incomplete if you stop in the middle of it or short of the place you were making for. A life is not incomplete if it is honourable; wherever you leave off, if you leave off well, it is whole. But often one must leave off both bravely and for reasons that are not the greatest; for the reasons that hold us are not the greatest either.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inperfectum*: “Not carried through”, unfinished. The English has “incomplete” both times; “imperfect” would suggest a flaw, which is not the point. Other reading: unfinished
- *citra petitum locum*: Literally “on this side of the place sought”. Other reading: before reaching your destination
- *ubicumque desines, si bene desines, tota est*: Desinere is “to stop, to leave off”, here to leave off living. Both verbs are future, and the English uses the present. The subject of tota est is the life. Other reading: wherever you will end, if you end well, it is a whole life
- *et fortiter desinendum est et non ex maximis causis*: The two et are taken as “both” and “and”, pairing “bravely” with “not for the greatest reasons”. The first et could instead mean “also”, adding bravely to the “well” of the sentence before. Other reading: But often one must leave off bravely too, and not for the greatest reasons
- *nec eae maximae sunt quae nos tenent*: Eae picks up causae: the reasons that hold a person in life are not the greatest either. The Latin does not say “in life” and the English does not add it. Other reading: for neither are those the greatest reasons that keep us here

</details>

### 77.6

Our Stoic friend, an outstanding person and, to praise him in the words in which he deserves to be praised, a brave and energetic man, seems to me to have urged him on best. For he began like this: “Do not torment yourself, my Marcellinus, as if you were deliberating about a great matter. Living is not a great matter: all your slaves live, all animals do. The great thing is to die honourably, sensibly, bravely. Think how long you have now been doing the same thing: food, sleep, lust. This is the circle one runs round. Not only a sensible man or a brave one or a wretched one can want to die, but also one who is fed up.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Amicus noster Stoicus*: The friend is not named. Noster may mark him simply as a friend the two men share, or as a particular Stoic whom Lucilius will recognise; the English takes the second. Other reading: A Stoic friend of ours
- *vir fortis ac strenuus*: Seneca flags this as a set form of praise. From memory, unchecked, it is an old Roman formula of commendation for a soldier or citizen. Other reading: a gallant and vigorous man
- *optime illum cohortatus*: Illum is Tullius Marcellinus, the sick man of the section before, who was deliberating about death. Optime may be “best of them all” or “excellently”; cohortari is to urge on or exhort, so “urged him on”. Other reading: to have exhorted him excellently
- *mi Marcelline*: Mi with the name is the affectionate form of address. The English keeps the bare possessive and adds no “dear”, which the Latin does not have. Other reading: my dear Marcellinus
- *torqueri*: Passive in form, “to be tormented”. It is taken in a reflexive sense. Other reading: do not be tormented
- *prudenter*: With practical good sense and foresight. The English has “sensibly”, and “sensible” for prudens in the last sentence, since “prudent” now suggests caution. Other reading: prudently
- *libido*: Sexual desire, given as “lust”. Other reading: sex
- *per hunc circulum curritur*: Impersonal passive, literally “through this circle there is running”. It is kept impersonal with “one”. Other reading: this is the round we run
- *fastidiosus*: One who has had too much and turns away in distaste; the word can also mean squeamish or hard to please. “Fed up” gives the first sense without supplying an object that the Latin leaves unsaid. Other reading: the fastidious man

</details>

### 77.19

“But I,” he says, “want to live, I who do many things honourably; it is against my will that I am leaving the duties of life, which I carry out faithfully and diligently.” What? Do you not know that to die is also one of the duties of life? You are leaving no duty; for no fixed number is laid down that you must complete.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquit*: An imagined objector, spoken of in the third person and then answered directly as “you”. Both persons are kept as the Latin has them. Other reading: someone says
- *qui multa honeste facio*: The relative clause may give his reason for wanting to live. It is kept as a plain relative and no “because” is added. Other reading: since I do many honourable things
- *invitus relinquo officia vitae*: Relinquo states that he is leaving; invitus says that it is against his will. The English keeps the leaving as a fact, since the reply takes up the same verb. Other reading: I am unwilling to leave the duties of life
- *officia vitae*: Officium is a duty or function owed. “Duty” is kept for all three occurrences, since the reply turns on the word. Other reading: the tasks of life
- *unum esse ex vitae officiis et mori*: Et here is “also” or “even”. Other reading: that even dying is one of life’s duties
- *non enim certus numerus quem debeas explere finitur*: The Latin does not say a number of what. A number of duties follows from the sentence before, and a number of years is possible; the English leaves it unstated as the Latin does. Finitur is “is marked off, is fixed as a limit”. Other reading: for no definite number of years is set that you must complete

</details>

## Letter 78: On bearing illness and pain

### 78.2

I often had the impulse to break off my life: the old age of my most loving father held me back. For I thought not of how bravely I could die, but of how far he was from being able to miss me bravely. So I ordered myself to live; for sometimes even to live is to act bravely.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *impetum cepi abrumpendae vitae*: Impetus is a sudden impulse more than a settled plan, and abrumpere is to break or snap off. The English keeps the impulse and the image of breaking. Other reading: formed the resolve to cut life short
- *indulgentissimi*: The word covers kind, tender and indulgent. The English takes the sense of affection, not of spoiling. Other reading: most indulgent father
- *quam ille fortiter desiderare non posset*: Desiderare is to miss someone who is gone; its object is not in the Latin and is supplied. Quam is taken as measuring how far he was from being able, and fortiter is “bravely” all three times in the section. Other reading: but of how bravely he could not miss me
- *et vivere fortiter facere est*: Fortiter can go with facere (to live is to act bravely) or with vivere (to live bravely is a deed). The English takes it with facere, which answers the bravery of dying in the sentence before. Other reading: sometimes even to live bravely is a deed

</details>

### 78.7

Now let us go back to that trouble which is illness’s own: illness has great torments, but intervals make them bearable. For the intensity of pain at its height finds an end; no one can be in pain both severely and for long; nature, who loves us most dearly, has so arranged us as to make pain either bearable or short.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *illud nunc proprium incommodum*: Proprium means “its own”, and “illness’s” is supplied. The section before listed three burdens of illness and said the fear of death belongs to nature, not to illness, so this is the trouble that is illness’s own, bodily pain. Other reading: that particular trouble
- *summi doloris intentio*: Intentio is a stretching or tension, the pitch to which the pain is strained. “Intensity” is used so that the strain is clearly the pain’s own and not the sufferer’s. Other reading: the strain of pain at its height
- *invenit finem*: Literally “finds an end”. It can mean that the pain stops, or that it reaches a limit beyond which it cannot go; the literal phrase is kept. Other reading: reaches its limit
- *valde dolere et diu*: Dolere is the verb of dolor, which the glossary gives as “pain”, so the English says “be in pain” where “suffer” would be more usual. Other reading: no one can suffer both severely and long
- *amantissima nostri natura*: Nature is personified as one who loves humankind. The superlative can be a true superlative or simply “very loving”. Other reading: nature, in her great love for us

</details>

### 78.10

So this is the consolation for immense pain: that you are bound to stop feeling it if you feel it too much. But what troubles the untrained when the body is in distress is this: they have not become used to being content with the mind; they have had much to do with the body. That is why a great and prudent man draws his mind apart from the body and spends much time with the better and divine part; with this complaining and fragile one, as much as is necessary.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *vasti doloris*: Vastus is huge, and can also suggest something that lays waste. The English takes size. Other reading: of devastating pain
- *inperitos*: Literally those without skill or experience, here people with no training in philosophy. “The untrained” is used. Other reading: the inexperienced
- *male habet*: An idiom: to trouble or distress someone, to put someone in a bad way. Other reading: what puts the untrained in a bad way
- *animo esse contenti*: Animus is “mind” by the glossary. It is set against the body here and called the better and divine part, but not as what survives death, so “soul” is not used. Other reading: to be satisfied with the soul alone
- *multum illis cum corpore fuit*: Literally “there has been much for them with the body”, an idiom for having dealings with someone. The same multum cum returns for the great man and the better part, so “much” is kept both times. Other reading: their dealings have been mostly with the body
- *prudens*: Prudens is a man of practical wisdom and foresight. “Prudent” is used, though the English word today leans toward caution; “wise” is kept for sapiens, which Seneca did not write here. Other reading: a great and wise man
- *animum diducit a corpore*: Diducere is to draw apart, to separate. The texts may vary here between diducit and deducit (from memory, unchecked); the English follows what is given. Other reading: leads his mind away from the body
- *quantum necesse est*: The Latin has no “only”; the limit is implied by the contrast with “much” and is left implied. Necesse est also stands in the first sentence, where it is “bound to”: there it is what is certain to happen, here what cannot be avoided. Other reading: only as far as he has to

</details>

### 78.13

Do not make your own ills heavier for yourself and load yourself with complaints: pain is light if opinion has added nothing to it. If, on the other hand, you begin to encourage yourself and to say, “It is nothing, or at least it is slight; let us hold out; it will stop soon”, you will make it light in thinking it so. Everything hangs on opinion; it is not only ambition and extravagance and greed that look to it: we feel pain according to opinion.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *mala tua*: Mala are bad things. In Stoic argument they are “evils”, but here they are a sick man’s sufferings, so “ills” is used here and in the next section. Other reading: your evils
- *levis est dolor si nihil illi opinio adiecerit*: Opinio is “opinion” by the glossary, meaning what one believes about the pain. The section runs on an image of weight (heavier, load, light), which is kept. Other reading: pain is slight if belief has added nothing to it
- *duremus*: Durare is to harden oneself and so to last out. The plural is the sick man talking to himself. Other reading: let us harden ourselves
- *dum putas*: Dum with the present beside a future verb is normally “while”, here close to “by”: the pain is made light in the act of thinking it light. It can also be read as a limit of time. Other reading: you will make it light for as long as you think it so
- *Omnia ex opinione suspensa sunt*: Literally “all things are hung from opinion”. The image of hanging is kept. Other reading: Everything depends on opinion
- *ad illam respicit*: Respicere ad is to look to, to have regard for. With ambition, extravagance and greed the opinion looked to may be other people’s, while with pain it is one’s own; the Latin uses the one word and so does the English. Other reading: it is not only ambition that has an eye on what people think
- *luxuria*: Luxuria is the vice of showy, self-indulgent living, not the costly goods themselves. “Extravagance” is used. Other reading: luxury
- *ad opinionem dolemus*: Ad here is “in accordance with, to the measure of”. Dolemus is the verb of dolor, so the English keeps “pain”. Other reading: our suffering is measured by our opinion

</details>

### 78.14

Everyone is as wretched as he has believed himself to be. I think complaints about past pains should be done away with, and these words too: “No one has ever had it worse. What torments, what great ills I have gone through! No one thought I would get up again. How many times my own people gave me up for lost, how many times the doctors abandoned me! Men put on the rack are not pulled apart like this.” Even if those things are true, they are over: what good does it do to go back over past pains and to be wretched because you have been? And what of the fact that there is no one who does not add a great deal to his own ills and lie to himself? Then again, what was bitter to bear is pleasant to have borne: it is natural to be glad at the end of one’s own ill. So two things must be cut away, both the fear of what is to come and the memory of old trouble: the old trouble no longer concerns me, what is to come does not yet.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Tam miser est quisque quam credidit*: Credidit is perfect and has no object, so “himself to be” is supplied. It could also be a general perfect, better put as a present. Other reading: Everyone is just as wretched as he believes he is
- *Detrahendas*: Detrahere is to pull off or take away. The Latin is impersonal, and the English keeps it so with a passive. Other reading: complaints about past pains are to be stripped away
- *surrecturum*: Literally “would rise”, that is, get up from the sickbed. Other reading: No one thought I would recover
- *deploratus sum a meis*: Deplorare is to weep for someone as already lost, and so to give up hope of them. Mei are one’s own people, family and household; the Latin passive is turned into an active in English. Other reading: How many times I was wept over by my family as a dead man
- *In eculeum inpositi*: The eculeus (“little horse”) was a frame on which a person was stretched under torture. “Rack” is the nearest English. Other reading: Men set on the torture horse
- *retractare*: Literally to handle again. It can also suggest touching a wound again. Other reading: to reopen past pains
- *Quid quod*: An idiom that brings in a further point: “what of the fact that”. Other reading: Besides, everyone adds a great deal to his own ills
- *Circumcidenda*: Literally “to be cut round”, as in pruning or surgery. The cutting is kept. Other reading: So two things must be pruned back
- *hoc ad me iam non pertinet, illud nondum*: Hoc and illud are neuter and point to the things themselves, the nearer one just named (the old trouble) and the farther one (what is to come), not to the memory and the fear. The English names them so that this is plain. Other reading: this no longer concerns me, that does not yet

</details>

### 78.15

Set in the very midst of difficulties, let him say, “Perhaps one day it will be pleasant to remember even these things.” Let him fight back with his whole mind; he will be conquered if he gives way, he will conquer if he braces himself against his pain. As things are, what most people do is this: they pull down on themselves the collapse they ought to be standing against. That thing which presses, which hangs overhead, which bears down: if you begin to draw yourself out from under it, it will follow and weigh more heavily; if you stand against it and choose to push back, it will be driven back.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *dicat*: The subject is not stated in this section, and ille follows in the next sentence. It is taken as the man in pain in general; the Latin then moves to “you” in the last sentence, and that shift is kept. Other reading: let a man say
- *forsan et haec olim meminisse iuvabit*: A line of Vergil, Aeneid 1.203 (from memory, unchecked). The Latin as given has no quotation marks; they are added because the line is direct speech after dicat. Other reading: let him say that perhaps one day it will be pleasant to remember even these things
- *iuvabit*: Iuvare is both “to please” and “to help”. “Pleasant” is taken, in line with “pleasant to have borne” in the section before, although quid iuvat there has the sense of use. Other reading: it will be of use
- *Toto contra ille pugnet animo*: Animus is “mind” by the glossary, though toto animo is also the ordinary phrase for “with all one’s heart”. Contra is an adverb with no object, so the English says “fight back”. Other reading: Let him fight against it with all his spirit
- *se contra dolorem suum intenderit*: Literally “has stretched, strained himself against his pain”. It is the verb behind intentio in 78.7. Other reading: if he strains every nerve against his pain
- *nunc hoc plerique faciunt*: Nunc is “as things are” here, not “at this moment”, and hoc points forward to what follows. The English begins a new sentence at this point. Other reading: but now most people do this
- *adtrahunt in se ruinam cui obstandum est*: Ruina is the falling of a building or wall. The image is of pulling down on oneself what should be stood against, and it is kept as a collapse. Other reading: they draw upon themselves the ruin they ought to resist
- *quod premit, quod inpendet, quod urguet*: The three verbs have no object in the Latin, and the English gives them none. Istud is “that thing of yours”, the pain or trouble at hand. Other reading: which presses on you, which hangs over you, which bears down on you
- *si subducere te coeperis*: Subducere is to draw away from underneath, as from under a weight. Other reading: if you begin to back away
- *obniti volueris*: Volueris is an act of will: “choose to”, “make up your mind to”. “Are willing to” would be weaker. Other reading: if you stand against it and are willing to push back

</details>

### 78.18

This too will help: to turn the mind away to other thoughts and to step away from the pain. Think of what you have done honourably, what you have done bravely; go over the good parts of your life in your own mind; scatter your memory across the things you have most admired; then let all the bravest men, the conquerors of pain, come before you: the man who went on reading a book while he offered his varicose veins to be cut out, the man who did not stop laughing while the torturers, angered by that very thing, tried every instrument of their cruelty. Will pain not be conquered by reason, when it has been conquered by laughter?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *a dolore discedere*: Literally to walk away, to withdraw from the pain. The movement is kept. Other reading: to take your thoughts off the pain
- *quid honeste, quid fortiter feceris*: Honeste is the adverb of honestum, “what is honourable” in the glossary, so “honourably”. Other reading: what you have done nobly, what bravely
- *bonas partes tecum ipse tracta*: Partes can be “parts, sides” (the good side of a life or a character) or a role in a play, and tractare can be “go over” or “play (a role)”. The English takes the first, supplies “of your life” from the sentence before, and gives tecum as “in your own mind”. Other reading: play the good role to yourself
- *memoriam in ea quae maxime miratus es sparge*: Spargere is to scatter or sprinkle. The image is kept, so that memory is sent out to many things at once. Other reading: let your memory range over the things you have most admired
- *fortissimus quisque et victor doloris*: Literally “each bravest man and conqueror of pain”, one group. Victor, victus and vincetur are one word in the Latin, so “conquer” is used throughout. Other reading: every one of the bravest, every conqueror of pain
- *ille qui dum varices exsecandas praeberet legere librum perseveravit*: Seneca does not name this man or the one who laughed under torture, and the English does not identify them. A story of varicose veins cut without flinching is told of Marius, though without a book (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: the man who kept on reading his book while he held out his leg for the varicose veins to be cut away
- *hoc ipsum irati*: Hoc ipsum is taken with irati: angry at that very thing, his laughing. Other reading: the torturers, furious for that very reason
- *Non vincetur dolor ratione, qui victus est risu?*: Ratio is “reason” by the glossary. The relative clause gives the ground (“seeing that it has been”); the English has “when” and does not add “since”. Other reading: Shall pain, which laughter has conquered, not be conquered by reason?

</details>

### 78.20

“But illness,” he says, “lets me do nothing; it has taken me away from all my duties.” Ill health holds your body, not your mind as well. So it slows the feet of a runner, it hampers the hands of a cobbler or a smith: if your mind is what you are used to using, you will advise and teach, listen and learn, inquire and remember. And another thing: do you believe you are doing nothing if you are self-controlled in your sickness? You will show that illness can be overcome, or at least borne.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquit*: Third person: an imagined objector. The answer then turns to “you”, and both are kept as the Latin has them. Other reading: you say
- *qui me omnibus abduxit officiis*: Officia are duties, including the social and public tasks of a Roman of rank. The Latin has a relative clause, which the English joins with a semicolon, and the object of “lets” in the first clause is supplied. Other reading: which has drawn me away from all my occupations
- *valetudo*: Valetudo is state of health, here bad health. The Latin has morbus in the objection and valetudo in the answer, so the English has “illness” and “ill health”. Other reading: It is your body that sickness holds
- *non et animum*: Literally “not also the mind”. Other reading: not your mind too
- *Itaque*: “So”: because it holds the body alone, it stops those whose work is of the body. The Latin does not spell out that step, and the English does not add it. Other reading: That is why
- *sutoris aut fabri*: Faber is any worker in hard materials: a smith, a carpenter, a builder. “Smith” is chosen because the general word “craftsman” would take in the cobbler as well. Other reading: of a cobbler or a carpenter
- *si animus tibi esse in usu solet*: Literally “if the mind is usually in use for you”. Animus stands first in its clause, and the English stresses it against the feet and hands. Other reading: if you are in the habit of using your mind
- *suadebis docebis, audies disces, quaeres recordaberis*: Six verbs in three pairs with no “and”. “And” is added inside each pair, since bare pairs do not read as English. Other reading: you will advise, you will teach; you will listen, you will learn; you will inquire, you will remember
- *Quid porro*: A short question that moves on to a further point, literally “what further?”. The English gives it as a phrase leading into the next question. Other reading: What then?
- *temperans aeger*: Temperans is the man who has temperantia, self-control or moderation. Aeger can be a noun (“a sick man”) or describe the state in which he is self-controlled; the English takes the second. Other reading: if you are a temperate patient

</details>

### 78.26

For a life that is surveying so many varied, great, divine things cannot be overtaken by the sense of having had its fill of itself: what usually brings it to hatred of itself is sluggish leisure. To one who ranges through the nature of things the truth will never become distasteful: it is false things that he will have his fill of.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *satietas sui*: Satietas is having had enough, to the point of being sick of something. The last word, satiabunt, is the same root, so “fill” is used in both places; “the sense of” is added to make the phrase a noun. Other reading: satiety with itself cannot take hold of a life
- *percensentem*: Agrees with vitam: it is the life that goes through these things one by one, reviews them. “Surveying” narrows the sense a little. Other reading: a life that is reckoning up so many varied, great, divine things
- *in odium illam sui adducere solet iners otium*: Otium is leisure, time free of business, and iners is sluggish, doing nothing. The Latin puts the phrase last for weight, and the English keeps that order. Other reading: it is idleness with nothing to do that makes life loathe itself
- *Rerum naturam peragranti*: Peragrare is to travel all through a country. “The nature of things” is the glossary’s rendering, meaning the natural world as an object of study. Other reading: For one who travels through the whole of nature
- *in fastidium*: Fastidium is the distaste of someone who has eaten too much. It carries on the image of having had one’s fill. Other reading: the truth will never come to disgust him
- *falsa satiabunt*: Two words in the Latin, with no object: “false things will sate”. The English supplies “he”, the one who ranges through nature, and adds the emphasis of “it is” to bring out the contrast with truth. Other reading: false things will cloy

</details>

### 78.29

Meanwhile hold on to this, grip this with your teeth: not to give in to adversity, not to trust prosperity, to keep before your eyes all that Fortune is free to do, as though she were going to do whatever she can do. Whatever has been long expected is lighter when it comes. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *hoc morde*: Literally “bite this”: get the teeth into it and do not let go. The bite is kept. Other reading: bite on this
- *adversis non succumbere, laetis non credere*: Two neuter plurals: things that go against a person and things that gladden. The English uses the abstract nouns, and keeps the infinitives as “not to”. Other reading: not to give in to adverse things, not to trust glad ones
- *omnem fortunae licentiam*: Licentia is freedom to do as one pleases, unchecked power; the English word “licence” could be taken for a permit. Fortune acts as a person here (factura sit), so she has a capital, as the glossary allows. Other reading: the whole of Fortune’s licence
- *tamquam quidquid potest facere factura sit*: Literally “as if she is going to do whatever she is able to do”. Other reading: as though she will do everything that is in her power
- *levius accedit*: Accedit is “comes up, arrives”, and levius is “more lightly”, the weight image of the earlier sections. The verb is easily confused with accidit (“happens, falls”), which would also give sense; the English follows what is given. Other reading: falls less heavily

</details>

## Letter 79: On Etna, virtue and late fame

### 79.17

No virtue lies hidden, and to have lain hidden is not virtue’s own loss: a day will come to make public the virtue that was buried and held down by the spite of its own age. The man who thinks of the people of his own time is born for few. Many thousands of years, many thousands of peoples will come after: look to those. Even if envy has imposed silence on all who are alive with you, there will come those who judge without ill will, without favour. If virtue has any reward from fame, this does not perish either. The talk of those who come after will indeed not concern us at all; yet it will honour us and keep returning to us even though we do not feel it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nulla virtus latet*: A flat present, though the next clause grants that virtue may have been hidden for a time. The sense is that none stays hidden; the flat statement is kept because the Latin repeats the same verb in latuisse. Other reading: No virtue stays hidden
- *non ipsius est damnum*: Ipsius is “of virtue itself”: the loss is not virtue’s own, with the hint that it is the age’s. The hint is not added. Other reading: to have been hidden does it no harm
- *conditam et saeculi sui malignitate conpressam*: Condere is to put away, store or bury, and malignitas is ill will, grudging meanness. The two participles are given as a plain relative clause. Other reading: stored away and suppressed by the meanness of its own century
- *Paucis natus est*: “Born for few”: his life serves or reaches only a few people. The Latin has no “only”. Other reading: He is born for only a few
- *multa populorum*: Milia is taken as understood again: many thousands of peoples. Other reading: many generations of peoples
- *silentium livor indixerit*: Livor is the bluish colour of a bruise, and so envy. Indicere is the word for an official proclamation, so envy “decrees” silence. Other reading: even if spite has decreed silence
- *sine offensa, sine gratia*: Offensa is dislike or resentment, taken here as the judge’s own feeling, and gratia is favour, partiality. The phrase means with no bias either way. Other reading: without taking offence, without partiality
- *Si quod est pretium virtutis ex fama, nec hoc interit*: The “if” leaves open whether virtue gets any reward from fame at all. Nec hoc is “not even this” or “this, too, not”. Other reading: If there is any price paid to virtue by fame, not even that is lost
- *colet ac frequentabit*: No object in the Latin; the first person plural is supplied from non sentientes. Colere is to honour or pay court to, and frequentare is to visit often or in numbers. Other reading: it will pay us honour and throng about us

</details>

## Letter 80: On training the mind and false happiness

### 80.3

What I turn over most in my own thoughts is this: if the body can be brought by training to this endurance, by which it bears the fists and equally the kicks of more than one man, by which someone holding out under the most blazing sun in the most scorching dust, and drenched in his own blood, lasts out the day, how much more easily the mind can be strengthened so that it takes the blows of Fortune unconquered, so that thrown down, so that trampled on, it rises up. For the body needs many things in order to be in good condition: the mind grows from itself, feeds itself, trains itself. Those men need much food, much drink, much oil, and long labour besides: virtue will come to you without equipment, without expense. Whatever can make you good is with you.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *revolvo mecum*: Literally “roll over with myself”. “Thoughts” is used and not “mind”, which in this section is the word for animus. Other reading: What I turn over with myself most is this
- *ad hanc patientiam*: Patientia is the capacity to bear things, endurance, not “patience” in the mild English sense. Hanc is kept as “this”, pointing to what is on show in the stadium; it could also be the “such” that prepares the clause after it. Other reading: to such a power of endurance that it bears
- *pugnos pariter et calces non unius hominis*: Calces are heels, so kicks, which points to a fighting contest in which both were allowed. “Not of one man” means of several opponents; pariter may be “alike” or “at the same time”. Other reading: the punches and at the same time the kicks of more than a single opponent
- *diem ducat*: Literally “draws out the day”: gets through the whole day. Other reading: spends the day
- *quanto facilius animus conroborari possit*: The subjunctive depends on the opening verb of pondering. It is kept as a statement of what is being pondered, not a question to Lucilius. Other reading: how much more easily might the mind be strengthened
- *fortunae ictus invictus excipiat*: Fortune is the opponent who lands blows here, so she has a capital as in 78.29. Invictus is “unconquered” or “unbeaten”. Other reading: so that it takes fortune’s blows unbeaten
- *ut proiectus, ut conculcatus exsurgat*: Ut is said three times in a row, and the English repeats “so that” to match. Exsurgat is “rises up”. Other reading: so that, thrown down and trampled on, it rises again
- *ut valeat*: Valere is both to be in health and to be strong. “In good condition” keeps both. Other reading: in order to be strong
- *Illis*: “Those men” are the athletes of the section before, whose noise from the stadium set off the thought. Other reading: The athletes
- *multo oleo*: Oil for rubbing the body before exercise, as athletes did. Other reading: much oil for rubbing down
- *Quidquid facere te potest bonum tecum est*: Bonum is taken with te: “make you good”. It is the adjective here, not “the good” of the glossary; tecum est is “is with you”, already in your possession. Other reading: Whatever can make you a good man you have with you

</details>

### 80.10

Do you see that king of Scythia or Sarmatia, adorned with the mark of rank on his head? If you want to value him and to know him whole, what he is like, undo the band: much that is bad lies hidden under it. Why do I speak of others? If you want to weigh yourself up, set aside money, house, standing; look at yourself within, for yourself: as it is, you take the word of others for what you are. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Scythiae Sarmatiaeve regem*: Scythia and Sarmatia are the lands north of the Black Sea, for a Roman the far edge of the world. “Or” shows that any such distant king is meant, not a particular one. Other reading: that Scythian or Sarmatian king
- *insigni capitis decorum*: Insigne capitis, “the mark of rank on the head”, is the diadem, a band of cloth tied round the head. Decorus is “adorned” or “handsome”. Other reading: splendid in his diadem
- *fasciam solve*: Fascia is both the cloth band of a diadem and a bandage. The section before spoke of a buyer who has a bandaged leg or arm uncovered to see what the dealer is hiding, so the royal band is treated as one more bandage; “band” is kept to hold both senses. Other reading: untie the bandage
- *aestimare*: The buyer’s word from the section before: to put a value on, to appraise. Other reading: If you want to appraise him
- *totumque scire qualis sit*: Totum agrees with illum: to know him whole, what sort he is all through. Other reading: and to know the whole of what he is like
- *multum mali*: Literally “much of bad”. It can be moral evil or, keeping the picture of the bandage, something unsound underneath; “much that is bad” leaves it open. Other reading: a great deal of evil
- *perpendere te*: Perpendere is to weigh carefully, a word of valuing like aestimare above. “Weigh yourself up” is used because the bare “weigh yourself” means the scales. Other reading: If you want to assess yourself
- *dignitatem*: Standing in public life, not “dignity” as a personal quality. “Standing” is used and not “rank”, which appears earlier in the section for another Latin word. Other reading: rank
- *intus te ipse considera*: Ipse is “you yourself”, the one who is to do the looking, and is given as “for yourself”. The Latin has no “and” before this clause. Other reading: and look at yourself within
- *nunc qualis sis aliis credis*: Nunc is “as things are”. Literally “you believe others as to what you are like”. Other reading: at present you trust others to tell you what you are

</details>

## Letter 81: On gratitude and repaying favours

### 81.1

You complain that you have run into an ungrateful man. If this is now the first time, give thanks either to fortune or to your own carefulness. But in this matter carefulness can do nothing except make you grudging; for if you want to avoid this risk, you will not give benefits; and in this way, so that they are not lost in someone else’s hands, they will be lost in yours. Let them fail to bring a return rather than fail to be given: even after a bad harvest one must sow. Often whatever had been lost through the persistent barrenness of a poor soil has been restored by the richness of a single year.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *si hoc nunc primum*: The clause has no verb; “has happened” is understood. The command that follows, to give thanks (gratias), plays on the ungrateful man (ingratum) just named. Other reading: if this has happened now for the first time
- *diligentiae tuae*: Diligentia here is carefulness in choosing whom to help, not hard work. One word, “carefulness”, serves for both occurrences. Other reading: to your own diligence
- *malignum*: Malignus can mean spiteful, but with giving it means stingy, grudging. The English takes the second sense, which the next clause explains. Other reading: except make you spiteful
- *beneficia*: Beneficium is the key term of this letter: a kindness or favour done. It is “benefit” throughout the passages from letter 81. Other reading: you will not do favours
- *apud te peribunt*: Literally “they will perish at your house”: by never being given, the benefits are wasted in the giver’s own keeping. Apud is given as “in someone’s hands”, and “lost” is kept because the same verb (perire) returns in the last sentence. Other reading: they will go to waste with you
- *Non respondeant potius quam non dentur*: Respondere is used of a field or a loan that answers with a return, which prepares the image of sowing. The subjunctives express a preference. Other reading: Let them go unanswered rather than ungiven
- *infelicis soli*: Infelix of soil means unfruitful; its common sense is “unlucky”. The English has “poor” so as not to say “barren” twice. Other reading: of an unlucky soil

</details>

### 81.19

We must do everything to be as grateful as we can. For this good is our own, in the same way that justice is not, as is commonly believed, something that has to do with others: a great part of it comes back to itself. There is no one who, when he does good to another, has not done good to himself. I do not say this for the reason that the man who has been helped will want to help and the man who has been defended will want to protect, or that a good example comes back round in a circle to the one who sets it (just as bad examples fall back on their authors, and no pity comes to those who suffer wrongs that they taught could be done, by doing them), but because the reward of all the virtues is in the virtues themselves. For they are not practised for a prize: the pay for a right deed is to have done it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nostrum enim hoc bonum est*: “This good” is gratitude; nostrum says that it belongs to the grateful person, not to the one thanked. Bonum is “the good”, as in the glossary. Other reading: For this is a good thing for ourselves
- *quemadmodum iustitia non est (ut vulgo creditur) ad alios pertinens*: The comparison is compressed: gratitude belongs to the grateful in the same way that justice is not, as people think, a matter concerning others. The Latin has no “only”, and none is added, though the next clause (“a great part”) shows that is meant. Other reading: just as justice does not, as is commonly believed, concern other people only
- *in se redit*: Se can be justice itself or, loosely, the just person. The English keeps the literal “itself”. Other reading: a great part of it comes back to the one who is just
- *Nemo non, cum alteri prodest, sibi profuit*: A double negative for “everyone”; the perfect profuit says the gain is already made at the moment of the act. Prodesse is given as “does good to”; it is a different word from bonum (“the good”) and beneficium (“benefit”). Other reading: Everyone who is useful to another has been useful to himself
- *eo nomine*: A bookkeeping phrase, “under that heading”, continuing the language of accounts from the section before. The English gives the plain sense, “for the reason”. The Latin sets the two reasons side by side with no joining word; “or” is supplied. Other reading: under this heading
- *circuitu*: Literally “by a circuit”: the example goes round and returns to its source. Other reading: comes back by a roundabout way
- *virtutum omnium pretium in ipsis est*: Pretium can be price, worth or reward. With praemium (prize) and merces (pay) following, the English takes “reward” and gives each of the three its own word. Other reading: the worth of all the virtues is in themselves
- *recte facti fecisse merces est*: Merces is wages or pay. The Latin is very compressed: “of a thing rightly done, to have done it is the pay”. Other reading: the wages of a good deed is having done it

</details>

### 81.22

So let us avoid being ungrateful, not for another’s sake but for our own. The smallest and lightest part of wickedness spills over onto others: what is worst in it and, so to speak, thickest stays at home and presses on the one who has it, as our Attalus used to say: “Vice itself drinks the greatest part of its own poison.” That poison which snakes bring out for another’s destruction, and hold without destruction to themselves, is not like this one: this one is worst for those who have it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *nequitia*: Seneca uses nequitia here and malitia in the saying, apparently for the same thing. Two English words, “wickedness” and “vice”, let the reader see that there are two Latin ones. Other reading: worthlessness
- *malitia ipsa maximam partem veneni sui bibit*: Malitia is the general opposite of virtue (virtus) in the section before, not only ill will, so the English has “vice”. The glossary gives “vice” to vitium, which does not occur in this section. Other reading: Malice itself drinks most of its own poison
- *spississimum*: “Thickest” or “densest”: the image is of a liquid whose light part spills over while the dregs stay in the vessel. Seneca marks the image with “so to speak”. Other reading: most concentrated
- *redundat*: Literally “overflows”, of a liquid running over the rim. It belongs to the same image as “thickest”. Other reading: overflows onto others
- *Attalus noster*: Attalus was a Stoic philosopher whose lectures Seneca attended when young (from memory, unchecked). Noster marks him as one of Seneca’s own school, or as his teacher. Other reading: Attalus of the Stoic school
- *sine sua continent*: Elliptical: sua takes up perniciem, “without their own [destruction]”. The English supplies the noun again. Snakes hold their venom without harm to themselves. Other reading: and keep in without harm to themselves
- *premit habentem*: Premere is to press, weigh down or choke. The English keeps the plain “presses on”. Other reading: weighs down its owner

</details>

### 81.24

Bad men have one pleasure, and that a short one, while they are receiving benefits; from those benefits a long joy, one that does not fail, stays with the wise man. For what delights him is not receiving but having received, which is undying and constant. He scorns the things that have hurt him, and he forgets not through carelessness but by choice.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Malis*: Dative plural of “the bad” (people), set against the wise man. It could in form be “for evils”, which makes no sense here. Other reading: For the wicked there is one pleasure
- *longum gaudium*: Seneca sets voluptas (pleasure), which the bad have, against gaudium (joy), which the Stoics allow the wise man. The English keeps two distinct words. Other reading: a long gladness
- *perenne*: Literally “lasting the year through”, used of springs that never run dry. Other reading: and everlasting
- *quod inmortale est et adsiduum*: The relative refers to “having received”: a past fact cannot be taken away. It could loosely refer to the delight. Other reading: a delight that is undying and constant
- *contemnit*: Contemnere is to think little of, to hold of small account; it does not mean to hate. “Scorn” is used here and for the same verb in Ep. 82.16. Other reading: He makes light of the things that have hurt him
- *volens*: Literally “willing”: the forgetting is an act of will, not a lapse. The verb has no stated object. Other reading: but willingly

</details>

### 81.25

He does not turn everything to the worse, nor does he look for someone to blame for an accident, and he puts men’s wrongdoings down to fortune instead. He does not put a bad meaning on words or looks; whatever happens, he lightens it by interpreting it kindly. He does not remember an offence rather than a service; as far as he can he keeps himself in the earlier and better memory, and his mind does not change toward those who have done well by him unless the bad deeds far outstrip the good and the difference is plain even to one who shuts his eyes; and even then only this far, that after the greater wrong he is such as he was before the benefit. For when the wrong is equal to the benefit, some goodwill remains in the mind.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *cui inputet casum*: Inputare is an accounting word, to enter something to a person’s account; the English gives the plain “blame”. Casus is a chance event. “He” throughout is the wise man of the section before. Other reading: someone to whose account he can put a mischance
- *ad fortunam potius refert*: Referre ad is also used of entering an item under a heading. Human faults are booked to chance and not to the person. Other reading: he prefers to set men’s faults down to chance
- *calumniatur*: Calumniari is to bring a false or quibbling charge. Here it means reading ill will into what was said or how someone looked; the Latin does not name what comes of it. Other reading: He does not find fault with words or looks
- *offensae potius quam offici*: The two nouns are chosen for their like sound, which English cannot keep. Officium is a service or good turn. Other reading: a slight rather than a kindness
- *in priore ac meliore se memoria detinet*: “Earlier” because the benefit came before the offence. Literally “he holds himself in the earlier and better memory”. Other reading: he dwells on the earlier and better recollection
- *mutat animum*: Animus is “mind”, as in the glossary, and returns at the end of the passage. Here it is closer to attitude or feeling toward a person, so the English avoids the idiom of changing one’s mind, which means altering a decision. Other reading: he does not change his feeling toward
- *bene meritos*: Literally “those who have deserved well”, that is, who have earned thanks by what they did for him. “Service” is not used, because it already stands for officium in this sentence. Other reading: those who have done him good service
- *multum male facta praecedunt*: Praecedunt is taken as “go ahead of, surpass”: the bad deeds far exceed the good, and “the good” is supplied as the thing surpassed. It can also mean “go before” in time, which fits the context less well. Other reading: unless many bad deeds have come first
- *coniventi*: Conivere is to close the eyes, and so to overlook on purpose. The English keeps the image. Other reading: even to one willing to overlook it
- *talis sit post maiorem iniuriam qualis ante beneficium*: Even when the wrong is greater than the benefit, he only goes back to where he stood before the benefit, that is, to neutral, not to enmity. Other reading: that after the greater injury he is what he was before the kindness

</details>

### 81.26

Just as a defendant is acquitted when the votes are equal, and humane feeling always tips whatever is doubtful toward the better side, so the mind of the wise man, when the services are equal to the misdeeds, does indeed cease to owe, but does not cease to want to owe, and does what those do who pay after a cancellation of debts.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *sententiis paribus*: Refers to the rule that a tied vote of the jurors acquits (from memory, unchecked). Sententiae are the jurors’ votes. Other reading: when the verdicts are evenly divided
- *humanitas*: Humanitas ranges over human feeling, kindness and civilised decency. The English has “humane feeling”. Other reading: kindness
- *in melius*: “Toward the better”, that is, toward the more favourable view. “Side” is supplied by the English. Other reading: toward the more lenient view
- *paria maleficiis merita sunt*: Merita are the good services a person has done, maleficia the bad deeds. They answer to beneficium and iniuria in the section before, but the Latin words are different, so the English has “services” and “misdeeds”, not “benefit” and “wrong”. Other reading: when the good turns balance the ill turns
- *tabulas novas*: Literally “new account books”, the Roman term for a general cancellation of debts (from memory, unchecked). The English gives the sense so that the comparison can be followed. Other reading: those who pay after new account books

</details>

## Letter 82: On facing death without logical tricks

### 82.5

Philosophy must be set around us, a wall that cannot be stormed, which Fortune does not get past, though she has assailed it with many siege engines. The mind that has abandoned external things stands in a place that cannot be scaled, and claims its freedom in its own citadel; every missile falls below it. Fortune does not have the long hands we think she has: she seizes no one except the one who clings to her.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *circumdanda est*: The Latin does not say around whom. The English supplies the general first person plural of the section before. Other reading: One must put philosophy around oneself
- *fortuna multis machinis*: Fortune takes a capital and “she” where she acts as a person, as here with her siege engines and hands. Where fortuna is plain chance, as in Ep. 81.1 and 81.25, the word is in lower case. Other reading: which fortune does not get past
- *lacessitum*: The participle belongs to the wall: “the wall, though attacked with many engines”. The English makes Fortune the one attacking, which the Latin implies. Other reading: which, though it has been battered by many engines, Fortune does not cross
- *insuperabili loco*: Insuperabilis is “that cannot be climbed over” as well as “that cannot be defeated”. The English takes the first, since the image is of high ground below which the missiles fall. Other reading: on ground that cannot be conquered
- *arce se sua vindicat*: Se vindicare is a legal phrase, to claim oneself as free; it can also mean simply to protect oneself. The ablative arce sua can be “in” or “by means of” its citadel. Other reading: and defends itself in its own citadel
- *ut putamus*: The parenthesis gives the common belief, that Fortune has long hands, which Seneca denies. The English is worded so that it cannot be read as a belief that she has none. Other reading: Fortune does not, as people suppose, have long hands
- *longas manus*: Long hands, that is, a long reach. A proverb said that kings have long hands; a line of Ovid has it (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: a long reach
- *haerentem sibi*: Sibi refers to Fortune: the one who clings to her. By form it could refer to the person, which gives poor sense. Other reading: except one who holds fast to her gifts

</details>

### 82.15

Among these things too, Lucilius, which we call middle, there is a great difference. For death is not a matter of indifference in the same way as whether you have an even number of hairs: death is among those things that are not indeed evils, yet have the look of an evil. There is love of self, and an inborn will to last and to preserve oneself, and a shrinking from dissolution, [gap in the Latin text] because it seems to snatch many goods away from us and to lead us out of this plenty of things to which we have grown used. This too estranges us from death, that we already know the things here, while we do not know what the things are like to which we are going to cross over, and we shudder at the unknown. Besides, there is a natural fear of the dark, into which, it is believed, death will lead us.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quae appellamus media*: Media, “middle things”, is the Stoic term for things neither good nor evil, also called indifferentia in the section before. The English keeps “middle” for media and “indifference” for indifferens. Other reading: which we call intermediate
- *mors indifferens est*: Indifferens is the Stoic term for what is in itself neither good nor evil. “A matter of indifference” is used, here and in Ep. 82.16, because “death is indifferent” would read as if death did not care. Other reading: For death is not indifferent in the same way
- *utrum capillos pares habeas*: Pares is taken as “even in number”, the stock Stoic example of something wholly indifferent (from memory, unchecked). It could also mean hairs of equal length. The Latin as given does not say “or odd”; later editions print words for it after pares (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: whether your hair is cut evenly
- *mali speciem*: Species is outward look or appearance. Malum is “an evil”, the opposite of “a good” (bonum). Other reading: the appearance of something bad
- *sui amor est*: Est is taken as “there is”. With the gap that follows, this clause stands without a link to what comes before or after, and the first shape of the sentence cannot be recovered. Other reading: it is love of self
- *insita voluntas*: Voluntas is will or wish. Here it is an inborn drive to go on existing. Other reading: an implanted desire
- *aspernatio dissolutionis*: Dissolutio is a loosening or coming apart: here, the coming apart of the living creature at death. Aspernatio is a turning away or rejection. Other reading: a recoil from being broken up
- ** * **: The edition marks a gap here: some words are lost. The gap is marked in the English and not filled. Other reading: (nothing supplied)
- *quia videtur*: After the gap the subject of “seems” is not stated. It is presumably death, and the English has “it”. Other reading: because death seems
- *morti nos alienat*: Literally “makes us strangers to death”, with death in the dative. Other reading: sets us against death
- *adductura*: A future participle, “going to lead to”, with no object stated; the English supplies the first person plural. Other reading: into which death, it is believed, will lead

</details>

### 82.16

So even if death is a matter of indifference, it is still not a thing that can easily be disregarded: the mind must be hardened by much training to bear the sight of it and its approach. Death ought to be scorned more than it usually is; for we have believed many things about it; the talents of many have competed to increase its bad name; a prison in the underworld has been described, and a region weighed down by everlasting night, in which the huge doorkeeper of Orcus, lying on half-eaten bones in his bloody cave, barking for ever, frightens the bloodless shades. Even when you have persuaded people that these are stories and that nothing is left for the dead to fear, another fear comes up: for they are equally afraid of being in the underworld and of being nowhere.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *indifferens mors est*: Indifferens is the Stoic term for what is neither good nor evil in itself, given as “a matter of indifference” as in Ep. 82.15. Other reading: even if death is indifferent
- *exercitatione*: Exercitatio is practice or drill, as of a soldier or athlete. Other reading: by long practice
- *contemni*: “Scorned” in the sense of held of small account, not hated. It is the same verb as in Ep. 81.24. Other reading: Death ought to be thought less of than it usually is
- *multorum ingeniis certatum est*: Impersonal: “there has been a contest by the talents of many”. The poets are meant. Other reading: many gifted men have vied with one another
- *ingens ianitor Orci ossa super recubans antro semesa cruento aeternum latrans exsangues terreat umbras*: These words are verse, woven into Seneca’s sentence without being marked as a quotation, so the English has no quotation marks. They join lines of Vergil’s Aeneid about Cerberus, from book 8 and book 6 (from memory, unchecked), as a sample of what the talents of many have written. Orcus is the god, or the realm, of the dead. Other reading: the huge gatekeeper of the underworld, crouched over half-gnawed bones in a gory cave
- *terreat*: The subjunctive is carried over from the verse and may give the sense “is there to frighten”. The English has a plain “frightens”. Other reading: is to frighten the bloodless shades
- *persuaseris*: Second person singular with no object: a general “you”, or Lucilius. The following verbs are third person plural, so “people” is supplied. A less likely reading is that “you” have become convinced. Other reading: Even when you have convinced them
- *defunctis*: Literally “those who have finished”, a usual word for the dead. Other reading: for those who have done with life
- *aeque enim timent ne apud inferos sint quam ne nusquam*: Literally “they fear being among those below equally as being nowhere”. The English keeps the Latin order and the even balance of the two fears. Other reading: for they fear being nowhere just as much as being in the underworld

</details>

## Letter 83: On a daily routine and drunkenness

### 83.1

You tell me to report to you each of my days, and the whole of each: you judge well of me if you think there is nothing in them that I would hide. Certainly we must live as if we were living in plain sight, and think as if someone could look into the inmost heart: and someone can. For what is the use of something being kept secret from a human being? Nothing is shut to god; he is present in our minds and comes into the midst of our thoughts. I say “comes into” as if he ever went away.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *et quidem totos*: “And whole ones at that”: Lucilius wants the entire day accounted for, not a selection. Other reading: and in full
- *Sic certe vivendum est*: Certe can affirm (“certainly”) or limit (“at any rate”: whatever is true of Seneca’s own days, this is how one must live). The English takes the first. The gerundive is an obligation, “must”. Other reading: At any rate, this is how one must live
- *in conspectu*: “In view”, with no word saying whose. It may be in the sight of everyone or of some observer. Other reading: in public
- *et potest*: Two words: “and [someone] can”. The subject is the “someone” just mentioned, who turns out to be god. Other reading: and there is one who can
- *ab homine*: Homo is a human being, set against god in the next sentence; the contrast is not between male and female. Other reading: from a man
- *clusum*: A spelling of clausum, “closed, shut”. Other reading: Nothing is closed off from god
- *interest animis nostris et cogitationibus medius intervenit*: Two verbs in inter-: interest, “is among, is present in”, and intervenit, “comes among”. Medius stresses that he comes right into the middle. Other reading: he is in our minds and steps in among our thoughts
- *dico tamquam aliquando discedat*: Seneca corrects his own word: to say that god “comes in” wrongly implies that he is sometimes away. The edition’s dash is rendered as a new sentence. Other reading: “comes in”, as though he sometimes withdrew

</details>

### 83.2

So I will do what you tell me, and I will gladly write to you what I do and in what order. I will watch myself at once and, what is most useful, I will review my day. This is what makes us very bad, that no one looks back at his own life; we think about what we are going to do, and that rarely; we do not think about what we have done; and yet a plan for the future comes from the past.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *protinus*: Protinus can mean “at once” or “straight on, without a break”. The English takes “at once”. Other reading: without a break, from now on
- *recognoscam*: Recognoscere is to go over and inspect, as one checks accounts or musters troops. Other reading: go back over the day
- *pessimos*: A superlative, which can be “the worst” or “very bad”. With nothing to compare it to, the English takes “very bad”. Other reading: the worst of men
- *respicit*: Literally “looks back at”. The image of turning round to look is kept. Other reading: no one reviews his own life
- *consilium futuri*: Consilium is both a plan and advice. The genitive is “of the future”. Other reading: advice about what is to come

</details>

## Letter 84: On reading and digesting what you read

### 84.11

“How,” you say, “will it be possible to bring this about?” By constant attention: if we do nothing except at reason’s urging and avoid nothing except at reason’s urging. If you are willing to listen to reason, it will say to you: leave at once those things that people run about after; leave riches, either a danger or a burden to those who own them; leave the pleasures of body and of mind, they soften and weaken; leave ambition, it is a swollen thing, empty, windy, it has no limit, it is as anxious not to see anyone ahead of it as not to see anyone level with it, it suffers from envy, and a double envy at that. And you see how wretched a man is if he who is envied also envies.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *hoc effici poterit*: “This” is the state described in the section before: a mind in which many arts, precepts and examples sound together as one. The verb is future, “will be able to be brought about”, which the English turns as “will it be possible”. Other reading: how can that be achieved
- *Adsidua intentione*: Intentio is a stretching or straining of the mind, so attention or effort. The English takes “attention”. The “if” clauses that follow hang on it with no main verb. Other reading: By unremitting effort
- *ratione suadente*: Literally “with reason advising”. Ratio is “reason”, as in the glossary; the phrase is repeated word for word, and so is the English. Other reading: unless reason advises it
- *Hanc*: A feminine pronoun for reason. The English names reason again and then has “it”, not “her”. Reason’s speech has no quotation marks in the Latin, so none are added. Other reading: If you are willing to listen to her, she will tell you
- *iamdudum*: With an imperative iamdudum means “at once, without more delay”, and it stands here before the relative clause, beside the imperative. It could instead be taken with the relative clause in its usual sense of “for a long time now”. Other reading: leave those things that people have long been running after
- *animi voluptates*: Pleasures of the mind as well as of the body are to be given up. Animus is “mind”, as in the glossary. Other reading: the pleasures of body and soul
- *enervant*: Literally “take the sinews out”. Other reading: they soften and unstring
- *ambitum*: Ambitus is properly going round canvassing for office, and so the pursuit of position. Other reading: leave the chase after office
- *ne secum*: Elliptical: “lest [it see anyone] with itself”, that is, alongside it as an equal. The English fills in the verb. Other reading: as not to see anyone beside it
- *invidia et quidem duplici*: Invidia is both the envy one feels and the ill will one draws. “Double” is explained by the next sentence: the ambitious man envies and is envied. Other reading: it is troubled by ill will, and of two kinds
- *si is cui invidetur et invidet*: The Latin is slightly loose: “how wretched he is, if the one who is envied also envies”. The English supplies “a man” as the one who is wretched; it could also be ambition itself, spoken of as a person. Other reading: how wretched it is when the man who is envied is envious too

</details>

### 84.13

Whatever things seem to stand high in human affairs, though they are tiny and stand out by comparison with the lowest, are still reached by hard and steep paths. The road to the peak of rank is rough; but if you care to climb this summit, the one before which Fortune has lowered herself, you will indeed see beneath you everything that is counted as loftiest, and yet you will come to the top over level ground. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *comparatione humillimorum exstent*: They rise above the surface only relative to what is lowest. The Latin has no “only”, and none is added. Humillimorum can be the lowest things or the lowest people. Other reading: and stand out by comparison with the humblest people
- *fastigium dignitatis*: Fastigium is a gable or peak; dignitas is rank or standing in public life. Other reading: to the height of high office
- *hunc verticem*: “This summit” is wisdom, to which the section before has just directed Lucilius; it is not the peak of rank just named. The English adds “the one” so that the relative clause marks it off. Other reading: this peak of wisdom
- *cui se fortuna summisit*: Literally “to which Fortune has lowered herself”, that is, has submitted. Fortune is treated as a person and so takes a capital. Other reading: to which Fortune has submitted
- *per planum*: “Over the level”: the paradox is that the true height is reached without a climb. Other reading: by a level road
- *venies ad summa*: Summa is neuter plural, “the highest things”. Other reading: you will arrive at the highest things

</details>

## Letter 85: On rejecting even moderate passions

### 85.11

Next, if you grant any rights to sadness, to fear, to desire, to the other wrong movements, they will not be in our power. Why? Because the things that provoke them are outside us; and so they will grow according to whether the causes that stir them up are great or lesser. Fear will be greater if it has seen more to terrify it, or seen it nearer; desire will be keener to the degree that the hope of a larger thing has called it out.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *si das aliquid iuris*: A legal turn of phrase: literally “if you give anything of right”, that is, any legal standing. The legal colour is kept with “grant any rights”. Other reading: if you allow any authority to
- *tristitiae*: The word ranges from gloom to grief. The English reads “sadness” and leaves “pain” and “grief” for dolor, as the glossary does. Other reading: grief, or distress
- *motibus pravis*: Motus is “movement”, here a movement of the mind, Seneca’s general word for an emotion; pravus is literally “crooked”. The Latin does not say “of the mind”, and the English does not add it. Other reading: the other crooked movements of the mind
- *in nostra potestate*: The Stoic phrase for what depends on a person. The subject of “will not be” is the passions just listed. Other reading: they will not be under our control
- *prout magnas habuerint minoresve causas*: The Latin sets a plain adjective beside a comparative, “great or lesser causes”, and the English keeps the pair. Literally “according as they have had great or lesser causes”. Other reading: in proportion as the causes that stir them up are large or small
- *si plus quo exterreatur aut propius aspexerit*: Very compressed: literally “if it has seen more by which to be terrified, or nearer”. Fear itself is the subject of “has seen”, and the English keeps that personification. Propius could also be “has looked more closely”. Other reading: if a person has seen more to terrify him, or seen it nearer at hand
- *quo illam amplioris rei spes evocaverit*: Quo answers the comparative acrior: literally “by as much as the hope of a larger thing has called it out”. The English keeps “the hope of a larger thing” as the subject. Other reading: the larger the thing that it has been roused to hope for

</details>
