### 85.13

But what madness this is: to believe that where the beginnings of things are placed outside our control, their limits are within our control! How am I strong enough to set a limit to a thing that I was not strong enough to prevent, when it is easier to shut things out than to hold them down once they have been let in?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Illud vero cuius dementiae est*: Literally “but that, of what madness it is”, an exclamation. Vero marks a step up in the argument and is given as “But”. Other reading: And how mad this is
- *nostri esse arbitri terminos*: Arbitri is the short genitive of arbitrium, the same word as a few words earlier, not arbiter, “judge”. The English reads “control” both times so that the repetition shows; the word is literally a person’s say or decision. Terminos, literally boundary marks, is set against principia, “beginnings”. Other reading: that their limits are ours to decide
- *ad id finiendum*: Finire can mean to set a bound to something or to bring it to an end. Because the argument is about how large the passions grow, and terminos has just been used, the English reads “set a limit to”. Other reading: to put an end to a thing
- *parum valui*: The same verb valere as in satis valeo just before: “am strong enough” against “was too little strong”. The English keeps “strong enough” in both halves. Other reading: that I had too little strength to prevent
- *admissa conprimere*: Admissa is a general neuter plural, “things let in”; conprimere is to press down, hold in check. No object is named for “shut out” either, so the English reads “things”. Other reading: to suppress them once admitted

</details>

### 85.33

I judge that a different answer should be given: that neither the helmsman’s art nor the actual practice of the art is made worse by any storm. The helmsman did not promise you good luck, but useful effort and the knowledge of how to steer a ship; this knowledge is all the more visible, the more some chance force has stood in his way. Anyone who has been able to say this, “Neptune, never this ship except on a straight course”, has done enough for his art: a storm hinders not the helmsman’s work but his success.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *aliter respondendum*: Seneca has just reported and set aside another reply to the comparison of the wise man with a helmsman; here he gives his own. “Should be given” renders the impersonal gerundive. Other reading: that one must reply differently
- *ipsam administrationem artis*: Administratio is the carrying out or handling of something; the word can also be used of steering. Ipsam goes with administrationem and sets the practice beside the art, so the English reads “the actual practice of the art”. Other reading: nor the exercise of the art itself
- *felicitatem*: Here a lucky result, not happiness of mind. “Happy” is kept for beatus and “fortune” for fortuna, so the English reads “good luck”. Other reading: a fortunate outcome
- *utilem operam*: Opera is effort or pains given to a task. “Effort” keeps it apart from opus, “work”, later in this section and from ministerium, “service”, in 85.36. Other reading: useful service
- *navis regendae scientiam*: Regere, “to steer”, is the root of rectam in the saying that follows and of rector in 85.38. Other reading: the science of guiding a ship
- *haec eo magis apparet quo illi magis aliqua fortuita vis obstitit*: Haec is the knowledge just named, so the English says “this knowledge”. Illi may be the helmsman or the knowledge itself; the English takes it as the helmsman because the pronoun changes. Other reading: the more some chance force has stood in its way
- *Neptune, numquam hanc navem nisi rectam*: The saying has no verb; something like “will you sink” is left unsaid, and the English keeps the gap. It is a helmsman’s saying, sometimes given to a Rhodian pilot (from memory, unchecked). Rectam agrees with the ship and can mean “straight, on course” or “upright”; the English takes the first, which suits the steering just mentioned. Other reading: Neptune, never this ship except upright
- *arti satis fecit*: Satis facere is to do enough for, to meet a claim, as one settles a debt. The art is treated as the thing he owed something to. Other reading: has met what his art requires
- *successum*: The outcome, how it turns out, set against opus, the work itself. “Work” is kept for opus here and in the sections that follow. Other reading: the result

</details>

### 85.36

Next, the helmsman’s art is a good that belongs to others: it is for those he carries, as the doctor’s is for those he treats. [The wise man’s] is a shared good: it is both the good of those he lives with and his own. And so the helmsman may perhaps be harmed, whose service, promised to others, is hindered by a storm.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *alienum bonum*: Literally a good that is another’s. The phrase comes back in 85.37, where it is rendered the same way. Other reading: another’s good
- *ad eos quos vehit pertinet*: Pertinere ad can mean to concern, to belong to or to be for the benefit of. The English reads “is for”, here and for the same verb in 85.37. Other reading: it concerns those he carries
- *quomodo medici ad eos quos curat*: “Art” is understood again with medici: “as the doctor’s [art is for] those he treats”. Other reading: just as medicine concerns the doctor’s patients
- *commune bonum est: et eorum cum quibus vivit et proprium ipsius*: The Latin as given has no subject for this clause and would say that the helmsman’s art is both a good that belongs to others and a shared one, which contradicts the sentence before. From memory, unchecked: editors supply sapientis (“the wise man’s”) before commune. 85.37 shows that wisdom is meant, and the supplied words stand in square brackets. Other reading: it is a shared good: both of those he lives with and his own
- *proprium ipsius*: Proprium is “one’s own” as against commune, “shared”; ipsius is “of the man himself”. The English reads simply “his own”. Other reading: and his own in particular
- *fortasse noceatur*: An impersonal passive in the subjunctive of possibility: “harm may perhaps be done to the helmsman”. Seneca concedes the point without asserting it. Other reading: perhaps the helmsman would be harmed
- *cuius ministerium aliis promissum*: The relative clause may carry a reason (“since his service”); the English keeps the plain “whose” and adds no “because”. Ministerium is “service”, the work of one who serves. Other reading: since the service he promised to others
- *tempestate inpeditur:*: The Latin sentence does not end here: after the colon it runs on into 85.37 (“the wise man is not harmed”). The English closes with a full stop so that the section can stand alone as a paragraph. Other reading: is hindered by a storm: the wise man is not harmed

</details>

### 85.37

The wise man is not harmed by poverty, nor by pain, nor by the other storms of life. For not all his work is prevented, but only the work that is for others: he himself is always in action, greatest in what he achieves at the time when fortune has set itself against him; for then he is doing the business of wisdom itself, which we said is both a good that belongs to others and his own.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *sapienti non nocetur*: This begins in lower case because it finishes the sentence of 85.36 (“the helmsman may perhaps be harmed:”). The English begins a new sentence with a capital. Other reading: no harm is done to the wise man
- *aliis tempestatibus vitae*: The image is kept: poverty and pain are “storms”, like the helmsman’s storm. Other reading: the other tempests of life
- *prohibentur*: Prohibere recurs through 85.38. The English uses “prevent” for it every time, and “hinder” for inpedire. Other reading: are blocked
- *opera eius omnia*: Opera is the plural of opus, “work”, the word used of the helmsman in 85.33 and of the wise man in 85.38. The English reads “all his work” in the singular, because “works” suggests finished products. Other reading: not all his works are prevented
- *ad alios pertinentia*: The same verb as in 85.36, rendered “is for” in both places. Other reading: only those that concern others
- *ipse semper in actu est, in effectu tunc maximus*: In actu is “in action, at work”; in effectu is “in what is brought about”, and the two words answer each other. The English takes in effectu with maximus (“greatest in what he achieves”); it could instead go with est. Other reading: he himself is always in action and in effect, and greatest then when
- *fortuna se opposuit*: Fortune is pictured as an opponent who stands in the way. The English keeps the small letter and “itself”, to match the plain “fortune” of 85.38 and 85.40; a capital and “herself” would press the personification further. Other reading: when Fortune has set herself against him
- *ipsius sapientiae negotium agit*: Ipsius can go with sapientiae (“of wisdom itself”) or stand alone as “his own”. The English takes the first. Negotium agere is to carry on someone’s business. Other reading: he is doing the business of his own wisdom
- *quam diximus et alienum bonum esse et suum*: The verb of saying points back to 85.36, exactly where the Latin as given has lost its subject; this clause is the evidence that wisdom was meant there. Suum could be wisdom’s “its own” or a general “one’s own”; the English reads “his own”, the wise man’s, as in 85.36. Other reading: which, as we said, is both another’s good and one’s own

</details>

### 85.38

Besides, he is not prevented from being of use even to others at the time when some constraints press on him. Because of poverty he is prevented from teaching how the state should be handled, but he teaches this: how poverty should be handled. His work stretches through the whole of life. So no fortune, no circumstance shuts out the wise man’s actions; for he is busy with the very thing that prevents him from being busy with other things. He is fitted for either turn of events: he steers good things, he conquers bad ones.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *aliquae necessitates premunt*: Necessitates are things that cannot be avoided and that force a person’s hand. The English reads “constraints”, because “necessities” today means things one needs. Premere is “press”, and the same verb is “pressed” in 85.40. Other reading: when some unavoidable circumstances weigh on him
- *tractanda res publica*: The same verb tractare is used of the state and then of poverty, so the English reads “handled” for both. Res publica may be “the state” or “public affairs”. Other reading: how public affairs should be managed
- *Per totam vitam opus eius extenditur*: Totam vitam can be “the whole of life” (every condition of it) or “his whole life” (its whole length). The English takes the first, which suits the argument. Other reading: His work extends over his whole life
- *actus sapientis excludit*: Actus is plural, “actions”, and picks up in actu of 85.37. Excludit is “shuts out”. Other reading: bars the wise man from acting
- *id enim ipsum agit quo alia agere prohibetur*: Agere is used twice, and the English reads “be busy with” both times. The sense is that his poverty, which stops other activity, is itself what he works on. The Latin is passive: literally “by which he is prevented”. Other reading: for he is doing the very thing by which he is prevented from doing other things
- *utrosque casus*: Casus is what befalls, a chance or outcome; “either” means good fortune and bad. Other reading: for both kinds of chance
- *bonorum rector est, malorum victor*: Bonorum and malorum are taken as neuter, “good things” and “bad things”, not good and bad men. Rector and victor are nouns, “one who steers or rules” and “conqueror”; the English turns them into verbs. Rector is taken as the one who steers, from the verb used of the ship in 85.33. Other reading: he is the ruler of good things, the conqueror of bad ones

</details>

### 85.40

You think he is pressed by bad things? He uses them. It was not only out of ivory that Phidias knew how to make statues; he made them out of bronze. If you had offered him marble, if you had offered him a still cheaper material, he would have made the best that could be made out of it. In the same way the wise man will display virtue in riches if he is allowed, and if not, in poverty; in his own country if he can, and if not, in exile; as a general if he can, and if not, as a soldier; able-bodied if he can, and if not, disabled. Whatever fortune he receives, he will make out of it something worth remembering.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Tu illum premi putas malis*: Premere is the verb used in 85.38 (“constraints press on him”), so the English keeps “pressed”. Malis is rendered “bad things”, to match “bad ones” in 85.38. The expressed Tu is emphatic, which the English shows by opening with “You think”. Other reading: Do you think he is crushed by evils?
- *Non ex ebore tantum Phidias sciebat facere simulacra*: Phidias was the Athenian sculptor of the fifth century BC, famous for statues of gods in gold and ivory. Simulacra are images, especially of gods; the English reads “statues”. Tantum, “only”, goes with “out of ivory”. Other reading: Phidias knew how to make images of the gods, and not from ivory alone
- *faciebat ex aere*: The Latin has no “also”, and none is added. The imperfect may mean “he used to make them”. Other reading: he used to make them in bronze as well
- *quale ex illa fieri optimum posset*: Literally “such a thing as could be made best out of it”. Materiam, “material”, is also Seneca’s word in this letter for the material that virtue works on. Other reading: the best statue that material allowed
- *explicabit*: Explicare is to unfold, unroll or deploy, as a general deploys a line of battle. The English reads “display”, which is clearer than “unfold” and comes from the same image. Other reading: will unfold virtue, or will deploy virtue
- *imperator*: Here a commanding general, set against the common soldier. In Seneca’s time it was also a title of the emperor, which is not the sense here. Other reading: as a commander
- *integer, si minus, debilis*: Integer is whole, unimpaired; debilis is disabled or weakened in body. Both are about the body, though the Latin does not say “in body”. Other reading: whole if he can, and if not, maimed
- *memorabile*: Worth recording or telling. The English reads “worth remembering”. Other reading: something notable

</details>

## Letter 88: On the limits of liberal studies

### 88.19

For what is there that is liberal, I ask you, about those people who vomit on an empty stomach, whose bodies are stuffed fat and whose minds are thin and sluggish? Or do we believe that this is a liberal study for our youth, whom our ancestors trained upright, to throw javelins, to hurl a stake, to ride a horse, to handle weapons? They taught their children nothing that had to be learned lying down. But neither these arts nor those teach or nourish virtue; for what use is it to control a horse and moderate its pace with the bridle, yet be dragged away by the most unbridled passions? What use is it to beat many at wrestling or boxing, yet be beaten by a hot temper?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *liberale*: The whole letter turns on this word: liberalis means befitting a free person (liber), and it gives “liberal arts” and “liberal studies”. It does not mean generous or politically liberal here. The English keeps “liberal” so that the argument about the word can be followed. Other reading: worthy of a free man
- *isti ieiuni vomitores*: These are the wrestlers and athletes dismissed just before. Literally “those fasting vomiters”: ieiuni is “on an empty stomach”, and it may point to a training regime of fasting, vomiting and heavy feeding, though the Latin does not say so. It could also be a slur, “starved” or “meagre”, in line with the thin minds that follow. Other reading: those starved vomiters
- *corpora in sagina, animi in macie et veterno*: Sagina is fattening or cramming, the word used of animals and of gladiators. Macies is leanness and veternus is torpor or drowsiness. The Latin has nouns in parallel, literally “bodies in fattening, minds in leanness and torpor”; the English uses adjectives. Other reading: whose bodies are in the feeding stall and whose minds are wasted and drowsy
- *istuc*: “That thing”, with a note of contempt: the athletes’ training just dismissed. The English reads “this”. Other reading: that pursuit of theirs
- *quam maiores nostri rectam exercuerunt*: Rectam agrees with the youth: “trained it upright”, that is, not on the ground. It is set against iacentibus, “lying down”, in the next sentence. It could also give the result, “trained to be upright”. Other reading: whom our ancestors trained to stand straight
- *quod discendum esset iacentibus*: “Lying down” is not explained: it may be wrestling on the ground or reclining at ease. There may also be a play between iacere, “to throw”, just before and iacere, “to lie”; the English cannot show it. Other reading: nothing that could be learned by people reclining
- *liberos suos*: Liberi, “children”, is the same word as “the free”, so it echoes liberale. The echo is lost in English. Other reading: their freeborn children
- *nec hae nec illae*: Feminine plural with no noun. “Arts” (artes) is supplied from the argument just before; the two groups are the athletes’ training and the ancestors’ military exercises. Other reading: neither the one kind nor the other
- *freno temperare, adfectibus effrenatissimis abstrahi*: Effrenatissimis plays on freno, kept as “bridle” and “unbridled”. The two infinitives stand side by side with no connecting word, as a contrast; the English adds “yet” to carry the contrast, here and before “be beaten”. Other reading: to moderate its pace with the bridle and be dragged away by passions utterly unbridled
- *luctatione vel caestu*: The caestus is the boxer’s strapping of leather thongs, so “with the caestus” means “at boxing”. Vincere and vinci are kept as one verb, “beat” and “be beaten”. Other reading: to defeat many in wrestling or with the boxing glove
- *ab iracundia vinci*: Iracundia is the disposition to anger, a hot temper, as distinct from ira, a fit of anger. Seneca does not always keep the two apart. Other reading: and be beaten by anger

</details>

## Letter 89: On the divisions of philosophy

### 89.3

The people is distributed into tribes, the army into centuries; whatever has grown large is more easily made out if it has separated into parts, which, as I said, ought not to be countless and tiny. For too much division has the same fault as none: whatever has been cut all the way down to dust is like a jumble.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Discribitur in tribus populus*: Tribus here is “tribes” (accusative plural), the divisions of the Roman people, not the number three. Discribitur is to mark off or distribute into classes; “distributed” keeps it apart from divisio, “division”, in the last sentence. Other reading: The people is marked off into tribes
- *in centurias exercitus*: A century is a Roman army unit of nominally a hundred men, not a hundred years. The Roman term is kept, as “tribes” is. Other reading: the army into companies
- *in maius crevit*: Literally “has grown into something larger”. No point of comparison is stated, and the English reads “has grown large”. Other reading: whatever has grown to a larger size
- *agnoscitur*: To recognise, to come to know, to make out. The English reads “made out”, since the point is taking in something too big to see whole. Other reading: is more easily recognised
- *ut dixi*: Points back to what comes just before this section, where he promised to divide philosophy into parts and not into scraps. Other reading: as I have said
- *simile confuso est*: Confusum is what has been poured together and not sorted. The thought is that dust is as hard to take in as an undivided heap. Other reading: is like what is mixed together

</details>

### 89.14

So, since philosophy has three parts, let us begin by setting out its moral part first. It has been held that this in turn is divided into three: the first is the examination that assigns to each thing its own and assesses how much each is worth, useful in the highest degree (for what is so necessary as putting prices on things?); the second is about impulse, the third about actions. For the first thing is that you judge how much each thing is worth; the second, that you take up toward those things an impulse that is ordered and moderated; the third, that your impulse and your action fit together, so that in all of these you agree with yourself.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *tripertita*: The three parts named earlier in the letter are the moral, the natural and the rational (ethics, physics, logic). Other reading: since philosophy is threefold
- *primum incipiamus disponere*: Primum is “first”, slightly doubled by “let us begin”. The English keeps both. Disponere is to set out in order. Other reading: let us first begin to arrange
- *dividi placuit, ut prima esset*: Placuit is impersonal, “it has been held” or “it has been agreed”, the settled view of the school; Seneca does not say whose. The ut clause spells out the division, so the English uses a colon. Other reading: It has been decided to divide this in turn into three, so that the first is
- *inspectio suum cuique distribuens*: Inspectio is a looking into, the examining part of ethics. Suum cuique echoes the formula of justice, “to each his own”; cuique can be “to each thing” or “to each person”. The English takes “each thing” because the next words are about what each thing is worth. Other reading: the examination that gives each person his due
- *maxime utilis*: Agrees with inspectio. It can mean “extremely useful” or “the most useful of the three”; the English reads “useful in the highest degree”, which leaves the comparison with the other two unstated, as the Latin does. Other reading: the most useful of the three
- *pretia rebus inponere*: Literally to put prices on things. The image of pricing is kept. Other reading: to set values on things
- *de impetu*: Impetus is Seneca’s Latin for the Stoic term for the impulse toward action (Greek horme; from memory, unchecked). “Impulse” is used for it throughout 89.14 and 89.15. Other reading: about the drive to act
- *ut in omnibus istis tibi ipse consentias*: This second ut may give the aim or result of all three points (“so that”) or restate the third (“that is, that”). The English reads “so that”, because “in all of these” looks back over all three. Tibi consentias is to be in agreement with yourself. Other reading: that is, that in all of these you are consistent with yourself

</details>

### 89.15

Whichever of the three is missing throws the rest into disorder as well. For what use is it to have everything assessed, if you go too far in impulse? What use is it to have checked your impulses and to have your desires in your own power, if in the action itself you are ignorant of the timing and do not know when each thing should be done, and where, and how? For it is one thing to know the worth and the prices of things, another to know the critical moments, another to rein in impulses and to go to what has to be done, not rush at it. So a life is in harmony with itself when action does not desert impulse, and impulse takes its start from the worth of each thing, slack [or] keener in proportion as that thing is worth seeking.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inter aestimata habere omnia*: Literally “to have all things among things assessed”. Aestimare is the verb of 89.14, so “assess” is kept. Other reading: to hold everything at its assessed value
- *si sis in impetu nimius*: Literally “if you are too much in impulse”. Other reading: if you are excessive in impulse
- *in sua potestate*: Sua is third person because the infinitive is general (“to have desires in one’s own power”). The English reads “your own” to run on with the “you” of the if clauses. Other reading: to have desires in one’s own power
- *in ipsa rerum actione*: Literally “in the very action of things”, that is, in the doing of deeds. The English reads “in the action itself”, keeping “action” for actio as elsewhere in 89.14 and 89.15. Other reading: in the actual doing of things
- *tempora ignores*: Literally “you are ignorant of the times”, that is, of when to act. The English reads “the timing”. Other reading: you do not know the right times
- *dignitates et pretia rerum*: Dignitas, dignitate and digna all belong to one word family here, as does dignum in 89.14. “Worth” is used for all of them and “prices” for pretia. Other reading: the values and prices of things
- *aliud articulos*: Articulus is a joint, and so a turning point in time, the exact moment. “To know” is carried over from the clause before. It answers the “timing” mentioned just above. Other reading: another to know the junctures
- *actio non destituit impetum*: Destituere is to abandon, to leave in the lurch, to fail someone. Action is pictured as a companion that might leave impulse stranded. Other reading: when action does not fall short of impulse
- *concipitur*: To be conceived, to be taken up, to take its start. Impulse is to arise out of the worth of the thing. Other reading: is conceived from the worth of each thing
- *proinde remissus acrior prout illa digna est peti*: The Latin as given has no “or” between remissus and acrior and does not construe as it stands; a connective seems to have dropped out, and the English supplies “[or]” in square brackets. Remissus is a plain adjective and acrior a comparative, and the English keeps that difference. Proinde with prout is “correspondingly, according as”. Other reading: slacker or keener in proportion as that thing is worth seeking

</details>

### 89.19

“I was the one who ought to be saying ‘How long will you keep doing the same wrong things?’ Do you want the remedies to stop before the vices do? I, for my part, will say it all the more, and because you refuse I will keep on; medicine begins to do good at the point when, in a body that has lost its feeling, a touch has forced out pain. I will say things that will help even the unwilling. Let some voice that does not flatter reach you at last, and because you are not willing to hear the truth each on your own, hear it all together.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Ego debebam dicere*: This opens the reply that Lucilius is told to give to people who complain “how long the same things?”. The speech runs on past this section, so the Latin has an opening quotation mark and no closing one, and the English quotation is left open too. Debebam is “it was for me to say”. Other reading: I should have been the one to say
- *quousque eadem peccabitis?*: This throws the complaint “how long the same things?” back at the complainers, with peccare, to go wrong or commit a fault. “You” is plural here and through the whole passage: it is the complainers, not Lucilius. Other reading: How long will you keep making the same mistakes?
- *Remedia ante vultis quam vitia desinere?*: Both remedies and vices are subjects of “stop”: do you want the cure to give up before the disease does. Vitia is kept as “vices”. Other reading: Do you want remedies to cease before faults do?
- *Ego vero eo magis dicam*: Dicam has no object in the Latin; the English supplies “it”, and keeps “say” for dicere all three times it comes in this section. It is taken as future, parallel to perseverabo. Other reading: I, for my part, will speak all the more
- *in corpore alienato*: Alienatus, “estranged”, is used of a body or limb that has lost sensation, gone dead (medical usage, from memory, unchecked). The English reads “that has lost its feeling”. Other reading: in a deadened body
- *dolorem tactus expressit*: Literally “touch has pressed out pain”: the pain shows that feeling is coming back. Dolor is “pain”. Other reading: when a touch has drawn a cry of pain
- *Dicam etiam invitis profutura*: Etiam invitis, “even to the unwilling”, can go with the saying or with “things that will help”. The English takes it with the helping, because prodesse regularly takes such a dative and the medical image has just been used. The Latin is general and has no “you”. Other reading: I will say, even to unwilling hearers, things that will help
- *Aliquando aliqua ad vos non blanda vox veniat*: Aliquando can be “at last”, “for once” or “at some time”; the English reads “at last”. Non blanda is “not coaxing, not flattering”. Other reading: Let a voice that is not soothing reach you for once
- *quia verum singuli audire non vultis, publice audite*: Singuli is “each one separately, as individuals”, set against publice, “as a community, all together”. Publice can also be heard as “in public, openly”. Other reading: since you are not willing to hear the truth as individuals, hear it in public

</details>

## Letter 90: On philosophy and the invention of crafts

### 90.29

Then it went back to the beginnings of things, to the eternal reason set within the whole, and to the force of all seeds, which shapes each single thing in its own way. Then it began to inquire about the soul: where it comes from, where it is, how long it lasts, into how many members it is divided. Then it moved from bodies to things without body, and examined truth and its proofs thoroughly; after that, how the ambiguities of life or of speech were to be told apart; for in both, false things are mixed in with true.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *redit*: The form can be a present (“goes back”) or a shortened perfect (“went back”). The English reads the perfect, because the verbs that follow are perfects and the questions that hang on them are in past sequence. The subject is not expressed; it is carried over from the earlier sections and is wisdom (sapientia), from memory, unchecked. Other reading: Then it goes back to the beginnings of things
- *aeternamque rationem toti inditam*: “ratio” is the Stoic reason that runs through the universe; “toti” is “the whole”, meaning the universe. The English keeps “the whole” and “set within”. Other reading: the eternal reason implanted in the universe
- *vim omnium seminum singula proprie figurantem*: This points to the Stoic teaching of principles like seeds that give each thing its form. “proprie” is rendered “in its own way”; it can also mean “specifically, each one separately”. Other reading: the power of all seeds, which gives each single thing its particular form
- *de animo*: The glossary prefers “mind” for “animus”. “Soul” is used here because the questions are about where it comes from and how long it lasts, which is the soul as what may survive the body. Other reading: about the mind
- *quamdiu*: Only “how long” stands in the Latin, with no verb. “It lasts” is supplied. Other reading: for how long
- *in quot membra divisus*: “membra” is literally “limbs” and is used here for the divisions of the soul. “Members” keeps the bodily word, which suits the next sentence, where the soul is counted among bodies. Other reading: into how many parts it is divided
- *a corporibus se ad incorporalia transtulit*: For the Stoics the soul itself is a body, so the move “from bodies” includes leaving the topic of the soul. The plain words are kept. Other reading: it passed from corporeal things to incorporeal ones
- *veritatemque et argumenta eius excussit*: “argumenta” can be “proofs”, “signs” or “tests” of truth; “excussit” is literally “shook out”, meaning examined closely. Other reading: and shook out truth and the signs by which it is known
- *post haec quemadmodum discernerentur vitae aut vocis ambigua*: This clause has no main verb of its own; it hangs on the verb of examining before it. “vocis” can be “of a word”, “of speech” or “of language”; the ambiguities of life are things in life that can be taken two ways. Other reading: after this it examined how what is doubtful in life or in a word might be distinguished

</details>

### 90.44

But although their life was outstanding and free of deceit, they were not wise men, since that name now belongs to the greatest of works. Still, I would not deny that they were men of lofty spirit and, so to speak, fresh from the gods; for there is no doubt that the world brought forth better things when it was not yet worn out with bearing. But while all of them had an inborn character that was stronger and readier for toil, their natural abilities were not, in all of them, brought to completion. For nature does not give virtue: to become good is an art.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *illis vita fuerit*: “They” are the people of the earliest age, described in the sections before. Other reading: the life of those first people was
- *carens fraude*: “fraus” is first of all deceit, but it can also be wrongdoing or harm in general. The English keeps the first sense. Other reading: free of wrongdoing
- *quando hoc iam in opere maximo nomen est*: Literally “since this name is now in the greatest work”. The English takes it to mean that the name “wise” by now belongs to the greatest of works; “work” leaves open whether the task or its result is meant. Other reading: since that name is now given only for the greatest achievement
- *a dis recentes*: Literally “fresh from the gods”, that is, newly sprung from them. The image is kept. Other reading: newly come from the gods
- *mundus nondum effetus*: “effetus” is used of a body worn out by bearing young, or of exhausted soil. The image of childbearing is kept. Other reading: the world, not yet exhausted
- *indoles fortior*: “indoles” is inborn quality, set against “ingenia” later in the sentence. The English has “inborn character” for the one and “natural abilities” for the other. Other reading: a sturdier native constitution
- *ita non erant ingenia omnibus consummata*: The reach of “non” is open. The English follows the Latin order, “were not, in all of them”, which most naturally says that not all of them had finished abilities; the Latin can also say that none of them had, which fits “they were not wise men”. The pair of words that frames the sentence, literally “just as … so”, sets the two facts against each other, so the English has “while”. Other reading: so in none of them were the natural abilities brought to completion
- *ars est bonum fieri*: “bonum” here is “a good man”, not “the good”. “ars” is a skill that is learned, set against what nature gives. Other reading: becoming a good man is a skill

</details>

### 90.46

What then? They were innocent through ignorance of things; but it makes a great difference whether someone does not want to do wrong or does not know how. They lacked justice, they lacked prudence, they lacked self-control and courage. Their unschooled life had certain things that resembled all these virtues: virtue does not come to a mind unless that mind has been trained and taught thoroughly and brought to the highest point by constant exercise. We are born for this, it is true, but without it, and even in the best people, before you school them, there is the material of virtue, not virtue. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Ignorantia rerum innocentes erant*: “innocentes” is both “harmless” and “guiltless”; “rerum” is “of things”, meaning of the world and of what there is to do wrong with. Other reading: They were harmless because they did not know the world
- *utrum peccare aliquis nolit an nesciat*: “nesciat” with the infinitive is “does not know how to”. “peccare” is to do wrong; “sin” is avoided. Other reading: whether a person is unwilling to do wrong or has no knowledge of it
- *prudentia*: “Prudence” is the usual English name for this one of the four chief virtues, and it stays apart from “wisdom”, which the glossary keeps for “sapientia”. It means good sense in action, not caution. Other reading: practical wisdom
- *temperantia ac fortitudo*: With justice and prudence these make up the four chief virtues. “temperantia” could also be “temperance”. Other reading: temperance and bravery
- *rudis vita*: “rudis” is raw, unworked, unschooled. “Unschooled” is used so that the link with “erudias” at the end of the section can be heard: that verb means to take the rawness out. Other reading: their rough life
- *Ad hoc quidem, sed sine hoc nascimur*: “hoc” (“this”) is neuter and points loosely to virtue, or to the trained state just described. Other reading: We are born for virtue, certainly, but without virtue
- *antequam erudias*: A general “you”, meaning anyone who teaches. The second person is kept, and “school” answers “unschooled” above. Other reading: before they are educated

</details>

## Letter 91: On the burning of Lyon and expecting disaster

### 91.3

All these things bend the spirit of our friend Liberalis, a spirit firm and unbowed against his own troubles. And he has not been shaken without cause: what is unexpected weighs more heavily; newness adds weight to disasters, and no mortal has failed to feel more pain at what also took him by surprise.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Haec omnia*: “All these things” are the circumstances told just before: the city of Lyon destroyed in a single night, in a time of peace. Other reading: All this
- *Liberalis nostri*: The Latin names him with the possessive that marks a friend; “friend” is supplied in the English. He is Aebutius Liberalis, a friend of Seneca from Lyon (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: of our Liberalis
- *adfectum inclinant*: “adfectus” here is his whole state of feeling, with no fault implied, and it is singular. The glossary’s word would be “feeling”, but a thing that is bent and that stands firm and unbowed is better called “spirit” in English. “inclinant” is to bend or tilt, and can also mean to sway. Other reading: sway the feeling
- *adversus sua firmum et erectum*: The adjectives agree with “adfectum”. “sua” is only “his own things”; “troubles” is supplied, set against the disaster of his city. “erectum” is standing straight, the opposite of bent, so “unbowed”. Other reading: a feeling steadfast and upright where his own affairs are concerned
- *novitas*: “novitas” is newness, the fact that a thing has not been met before. Other reading: strangeness
- *nec quisquam mortalium non magis quod etiam miratus est doluit*: A double negative, which is kept. “miratus est” is “wondered at” or “was astonished at”. “doluit” is pain, and here also grief. Other reading: and every mortal has grieved more over what he also wondered at

</details>

### 91.4

That is why nothing ought to be unforeseen by us; the mind must be sent ahead to everything, and what must be thought of is not whatever usually happens but whatever can happen. For what is there that Fortune, when she has wished it, does not drag down from its fullest flowering? What is there that she does not attack and shake all the more, the more splendidly it shines? What is an uphill climb for her, or what is hard?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *in omnia praemittendus animus*: Literally “the mind must be sent ahead into all things”, like a scout sent before an army. Other reading: the mind must be sent forward into everything
- *cogitandumque*: An impersonal form (“it must be thought”). It is kept impersonal in the English. Other reading: and we must think
- *fortuna, cum voluit*: Fortune is treated as a person who wills, so the English has a capital and “she”. The perfect “voluit” is “has wished”, “once she has decided”. Other reading: fortune, when it pleases her
- *ex florentissimo detrahat*: Literally “drags down out of the most flourishing”, a neuter used as a noun for the most flourishing state. Other reading: pulls down from the height of prosperity
- *quo speciosius fulget*: “speciosius” is “more showily”, with a hint of mere outward show. Other reading: the more brilliant its show
- *arduum*: “arduum” is literally steep, a hard climb. “An uphill climb” keeps the image beside the plain word “hard”. Other reading: What is steep for her

</details>

### 91.19

Still, there is no reason for you to believe those people who make a din all around you: none of these things is an evil, none is unbearable or hard. Their fear comes from general agreement. You fear death in the same way that you fear what people say: but what is more foolish than a man who is afraid of words? Our friend Demetrius has a neat way of saying that the voices of the ignorant count for the same with him as the noises of the belly breaking wind. “For what is it to me,” he says, “whether those people make their sound up above or down below?”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *nihil horum*: “These things” are the ones listed just before: pain, thirst, hunger, old age, illness, loss, death. Other reading: nothing of all this
- *Ex consensu istis metus est*: Literally “their fear is from agreement”: these things are feared because everyone agrees to fear them. “Opinion” is avoided, since this translation keeps it for another Latin word. Other reading: Their fear is a matter of common consent
- *Sic mortem times quomodo famam*: “fama” covers rumour, common talk and reputation. “What people say” is taken because the next clause speaks of fearing words. It could also be fear of a bad name. Other reading: You fear death as you fear for your reputation
- *Demetrius noster*: Demetrius the Cynic philosopher, a contemporary whom Seneca admired (from memory, unchecked). The possessive marks him as a friend. Other reading: our Demetrius
- *eodem loco sibi esse*: An idiom: “to be in the same place for him”, that is, to be held in the same regard. The English gives the sense. The literal “place” may prepare the joke about above and below. Other reading: are in the same place for him
- *ventre redditos crepitus*: Literally “noises given back by the belly”, a roundabout Latin phrase for farts. The English keeps the roundabout wording but says plainly what kind of noise it is. Other reading: farts
- *mea, susum isti an deosum sonent*: “mea” stands for a fuller phrase meaning that a thing matters to the speaker, with the verb left out. “susum” and “deosum” are everyday forms of the words for “upward” and “downward”, here “up above” and “down below”. “isti” is masculine and most likely the people, though it could be the noises. Other reading: what do I care whether those noises go upward or downward

</details>

## Letter 92: On reason and virtue sufficing for happiness

### 92.1

I think you and I will agree that external things are acquired for the body, that the body is cared for in honour of the mind, that in the mind there are serving parts, through which we move and are nourished, given to us for the sake of the ruling part itself. In this ruling part there is something without reason, and there is also something with reason; the first serves the second, and the second is the one thing that is not referred to anything else but refers everything to itself. For that divine reason too is set over all things and is itself under nothing; and this reason of ours, which comes from that one, is the same.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Puto, inter me teque conveniet*: Two verbs stand side by side: a verb of thinking in the first person, then an impersonal “it will be agreed” between the two friends. The English joins them into one clause. Other reading: I suppose it will be agreed between me and you
- *externa corpori adquiri*: “externa” are external things such as possessions. “corpori” is “for the body”, for its sake. Other reading: that outward things are sought for the body’s sake
- *in animo esse partes ministras*: “animus” here is the whole soul, including the powers of movement and nourishment. The glossary’s “mind” is kept, because nothing is said here of survival and the same word runs on into the next section. “Soul” would be the more natural English for this wider sense. Other reading: that in the soul there are servant parts
- *ipsum principale*: “principale” is Seneca’s Latin for the Stoic term for the leading or commanding part of the soul. “The ruling part” is used throughout the passage. Other reading: the chief part itself
- *est aliquid inrationale, est et rationale*: “Without reason” and “with reason” are used so that the link with “reason” (ratio) in the next sentences shows in English. Other reading: there is something irrational, and there is also something rational
- *alio non refertur sed omnia ad se refert*: “referre” is to refer one thing to another as its end or measure. The same verb is used twice and one English verb is kept. Other reading: is not directed to something else but directs all things to itself
- *sub nullo*: “nullo” can be neuter (“under nothing”) or masculine (“under no one”). The English takes the neuter, to match “set over all things”. Other reading: is itself under no one
- *et haec autem nostra eadem est, quae ex illa est*: “eadem” (“the same”) can mean the same in kind as the divine reason, or the same in being set over all and under nothing. The relative clause is kept as a plain “which”; it may also be giving the ground. Other reading: and this reason of ours is the same, since it comes from that one

</details>

### 92.2

If we agree on this, it follows that we also agree on that: the happy life rests on this one thing, that reason in us should be perfect. For it alone does not let the mind sink; it stands against fortune; in whatever state things are, it keeps [the next word is corrupt in the Latin]. And that alone is good which is never broken off. That man, I say, is happy whom nothing makes smaller; he holds the heights, and leans on no one at all except himself; for a man who is held up by some support can fall. If it is otherwise, things that are not ours will begin to have great power in us. But who wants to owe his standing to fortune, or what sensible man admires himself for things that are not his own?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ut in nobis ratio perfecta sit*: “perfecta” is both “perfect” and “brought to completion”. “Perfect” is kept, as the description of reason at its full growth. Other reading: that reason in us should be brought to completion
- *non summittit animum*: Literally “does not lower the mind”. The phrase is the usual one for losing heart, so “spirit” would also be right. “Mind” is kept with the glossary. Other reading: does not let the spirit droop
- *in quolibet rerum habitu ~servitus~ servat*: The copy marks “servitus” (“slavery”) as corrupt, and the words as given do not construe: “servat” (“it keeps”) has no object. The English leaves the gap and says so in square brackets. From memory, unchecked: editors have proposed a word meaning “safe” or “free from care” here. Other reading: in whatever state things are, it keeps us free from care (a guess at the lost word)
- *Id autem unum bonum est quod numquam defringitur*: This can be “that alone is good which …” or “the one good is that which …”. “defringitur” is “is broken off”, as a branch is. The image is kept. Other reading: But the one good is that which is never broken
- *tenet summa*: Literally “he holds the highest things”: the top, the summit. Other reading: he occupies the highest place
- *ne ulli quidem nisi sibi innixus*: “ulli” can be “anyone” or “anything”. The English takes “no one”, the commoner sense of the word standing alone and the one that matches “himself”; the “support” of the next clause would also suit “nothing”. Other reading: leaning on nothing at all except himself
- *constare fortuna*: “constare” with the ablative can be “to stand firm by means of”, “to depend on” or “to consist of”. The English takes it as standing by fortune’s gift, which echoes “stands against fortune” earlier. Other reading: to be made up of what fortune gives
- *prudens*: “prudens” can be “a sensible man” or “knowingly, with his eyes open”. Other reading: or who, knowing what he does, admires himself for what is another’s

</details>

### 92.11

“What then?” he says. “If good health and rest and freedom from pains are not going to hinder virtue in any way, will you not seek them?” Why would I not seek them? Not because they are goods, but because they are according to nature, and because they will be taken by me with good judgment. What then will be good in them? This one thing: being well chosen. For when I take the kind of clothing that is fitting, when I walk as I ought, when I dine as I should, it is not the dinner or the walk or the clothing that is a good, but my purpose in them, the purpose of one who keeps, in each thing, the measure that agrees with reason.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquit*: “He says”: an imagined objector, not a named person. Other reading: someone says
- *quies*: “quies” is rest or quiet; the section before uses it for a state of repose. Other reading: quiet
- *non quia bona sunt*: “bona” is neuter, while the three things named are feminine, so it is taken as the noun “goods” and not the adjective “good”. The same form at the end of the section is rendered the same way, “a good”. Other reading: Not because they are good
- *secundum naturam sunt*: A Stoic term: things “according to nature” are worth choosing without being goods. The literal phrase is kept. Other reading: they are in accord with nature
- *bono a me iudicio sumentur*: “sumere” is “to take” and is the same verb used just below for putting on clothing. “Take” is used in both places so that the link shows. Other reading: they will be chosen by me with sound judgment
- *hoc unum, bene eligi*: A passive infinitive: “to be well chosen”. The good lies in the choosing, not in the thing chosen. Other reading: this alone, that they are chosen well
- *meum in iis propositum servantis*: “servantis” is a genitive that agrees with the speaker implied in “meum”, so the purpose is that of a person who keeps the measure; the English repeats “purpose” to make this plain. “propositum” is purpose, intention, or the plan one sets oneself. Other reading: my intention in them, as I keep
- *rationi convenientem modum*: “modus” is measure or limit; “convenientem” is “agreeing with”, “fitting”. Other reading: the limit that suits reason

</details>

### 92.12

I will add this as well: the choice of clean clothing is something a human being should seek; for a human being is by nature a clean animal and one that chooses with care. And so clean clothing is not a good in itself, but the choice of clean clothing is, because the good is not in the thing but in a choice of a certain kind; it is our actions that are honourable, not the things themselves that are done.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Etiamnunc adiciam*: “Etiamnunc” here is “further, besides”, not “even now”. Other reading: Even now I will add
- *mundae vestis electio*: “mundus” is clean, neat, well kept. “Clean” is used throughout the passage. Other reading: the choice of neat clothing
- *adpetenda est homini*: “adpetenda” is “to be sought, to be reached for”; “homini” can be “by a human being” or “for a human being”. Other reading: is a thing for a human being to desire
- *mundum et elegans animal*: “elegans” is discriminating, having taste. It is related to the verb “to choose” and so answers “electio” (“choice”) beside it, as “mundum” answers “mundae”. “One that chooses with care” keeps that link. Other reading: a clean and discriminating animal
- *non est bonum per se munda vestis*: “per se” (“in itself”) can go with “a good” or with “clean clothing”. The English leaves it as open as it can: it says that clean clothing is not a good in itself and that the choice is a good, without settling whether the choice is a good in itself. Other reading: And so what is good in itself is not clean clothing but the choice of clean clothing
- *in electione quali*: Literally “in a choice of some sort”, that is, in what kind of choice it is. Read the other way, “quali” could mean “in the choice, such as it is”. Other reading: in the quality of the choice
- *actiones nostrae honestae sunt, non ipsa quae aguntur*: The noun “actiones” and the verb “aguntur” share a root: the acting is honourable, not what is acted. The English has “actions” and “done”. Other reading: it is our acts that are honourable, not the very things that are enacted

</details>

### 92.18

In the same way, the things set in the way of virtue take nothing away from it: it is not smaller, but it shines less. To us, perhaps, it does not show and gleam as much; to itself it is the same, and like the sun when it is obscured it exerts its force out of sight. And so disasters and losses and wrongs have the same power against virtue that a mist has against the sun.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Eodem modo*: This carries on the comparison in the section before, where clouds stand in front of the sun without hindering it. Other reading: In the same manner
- *virtuti opposita*: Literally “things placed opposite virtue”, as clouds are placed in front of the sun. It could also be heard as “things opposed to virtue”. Other reading: the things opposed to virtue
- *non aeque apparet ac nitet*: “aeque” is “equally”, with the comparison (“as before, as when nothing is in the way”) left unsaid, and “ac” is taken as joining the two verbs. The two words together can also make a comparison of their own, “as much as”. Other reading: it does not show as much as it gleams
- *sibi eadem est*: “To itself” is set against “to us”: in itself, for its own part. Other reading: in itself it is the same
- *more solis obscuri in occulto*: “obscuri” is “obscured, hidden from view” by what stands in front, not dimmed in itself; “in occulto” is “in a hidden place, unseen”. Other reading: in the manner of a hidden sun, it works in secret
- *iniuriae*: “iniuriae” are wrongs done to a person; “injuries” in the older sense. Other reading: injuries
- *nebula*: “nebula” is mist, fog or thin cloud. The image is the same as the clouds in the section before. Other reading: a cloud

</details>

### 92.33

When it has raised itself to this height, it is, towards the body too, as towards a necessary burden, not a lover but a manager, and it does not put itself under the thing it has been set over. No one is free who is a slave to the body; for, even if you pass over the other masters that too much anxiety on the body’s behalf finds, the body’s own rule is hard to please and fussy.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Cum se in hanc sublimitatem tulit*: The subject is not expressed. It is taken to be the mind (animus), which the section before is speaking of, so the English has “it”. The masculine forms would also fit “he”, a man. Other reading: When he has raised himself to this height
- *procurator*: A “procurator” manages property on another’s behalf: an agent or steward. He looks after a thing without being attached to it. Other reading: a steward
- *nec se illi cui inpositus est subicit*: A play on “under” and “over”: the one set over the body (as its overseer) does not place himself under it. Other reading: and does not subject itself to that which it has been put in charge of
- *corpori servit*: “servit” is “is a slave to”, stronger than “serves”. The slavery is kept because the sentence is about being free. Other reading: who serves the body
- *ut alios dominos quos nimia pro illo sollicitudo invenit transeas*: Literally “granted that you pass over the other masters”, with a general “you”, which the English keeps. “invenit” is “finds”, that is, comes upon or gets for itself; the form can be present or perfect. The other masters are those a person falls under through worrying about the body. Other reading: to leave aside the other masters that excessive worry about the body has found
- *morosum imperium delicatumque*: “morosus” is peevish, hard to please; “delicatus” is pampered, fussy, self-indulgent. Both describe a difficult master and are applied here to his rule. Other reading: its own command is peevish and pampered

</details>

## Letter 93: On the quality not length of life

### 93.2

Which, I beg you, do you judge the fairer: that you should obey nature, or that nature should obey you? And what does it matter how soon you leave a place that must be left in any case? What we must take care of is not that we live long but that we live enough; for to live long you need fate, to live enough you need the mind. A life is long if it is full; and it is made full when the mind has given back to itself its own good and has transferred to itself the power over itself.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *aequius*: The word answers the fairness (aequitas) that Seneca said he missed in Lucilius in the section before, so “fairer” is kept. It can also mean more reasonable or more just. Other reading: more reasonable
- *unde utique exeundum est*: The Latin names no place, only “from where”; life is meant. The verb is impersonal, a leaving that falls on everyone, so the English reads “must be left” and not “you must leave”. “Utique” can be “in any case” or “without fail”. Other reading: from where one must go out without fail
- *sed ut satis*: The verb is left to be supplied from the clause before. “Satis” is “enough”: the point is a life that is sufficient, not merely a fairly long one. Other reading: but that we live sufficiently
- *animo*: Animus is “mind” by the glossary, and the next sentence speaks of the mind (animus) recovering its own good, so one word is kept. Here it also leans towards spirit or resolve. Other reading: to live enough you need spirit
- *sibi bonum suum reddidit*: “Reddere” is to give back or to pay what is due. The English takes it as the mind restoring to itself the good (bonum) that belongs to it; it could be the weaker “has delivered”. Other reading: has rendered to itself the good that is its own
- *ad se potestatem sui transtulit*: The wording is that of a legal transfer of authority: the mind takes over control of itself from outside owners. “Transferred” and “power” are kept to show this. Other reading: has taken control of itself into its own hands

</details>

### 93.6

I would not on that account refuse to have more years added to me; yet I will say that I lacked nothing for the happy life if its span is cut short; for I have not made myself ready for the day that greedy hope had promised me as my last, but there is no day I have not looked on as if it were my last. Why do you ask me when I was born, or whether I am still registered among the younger men? I have what is mine.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nec ideo*: “Not for that reason”: it looks back to the praise, in the section before, of a man whose short time was well spent. Seneca now speaks in the first person. Other reading: And yet I would not therefore refuse
- *dicam*: The form can be future or subjunctive. The English takes the future, “will say”, because the “if” clause that follows has a plain present, “is cut short”; after the mild “would not refuse” the subjunctive is also possible. Other reading: yet I would say
- *si spatium eius inciditur*: “Eius” most naturally refers to the happy life just named, though it may mean life in general. The verb is present, “is cut short”, literally “is cut into”. Other reading: if the span of my life is cut short
- *ad eum diem me aptavi*: Literally “fitted myself to that day”: he did not arrange himself with a view to one far-off final day. The English reads “made myself ready for”, which is one plain sense of the verb. Other reading: I have not arranged myself around that day
- *nullum non tamquam ultimum aspexi*: A double negative, “none not looked on as the last”, set against the single day that hope had promised. The English keeps both negatives; the possessive with “last” is carried over from the clause before. Other reading: but have looked on every day as if it were my last
- *an inter iuniores adhuc censear*: A term of the Roman census: the “iuniores” were the men of military age, up to about forty-five (from memory, unchecked). “Censear” is “be entered at the census”, so the English reads “registered” and keeps “the younger men” as the plain name of the class. Other reading: or whether I am still entered on the roll of the men of military age
- *habeo meum*: Two words: the verb “have” and a bare possessive with no noun. The noun is left open: a share, a due, what belongs to the speaker. The English is kept as open as the Latin. Other reading: I have my share

</details>

### 93.7

Just as a person can be complete in a smaller build of body, so too a life can be complete in a smaller measure of time. Years are among the external things. How long I am to be is not mine: to be, for as long as I will be, is mine. Demand this of me: that I do not travel the whole length of an obscure lifetime as if through darkness, that I lead my life, not that I am carried past it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *in minore corporis habitu*: “Habitus” is the build or condition of the body; with “smaller” it means a small stature. Other reading: in a body of smaller stature
- *perfectus*: The word means finished, complete, and so perfect. “Complete” is used for both the person and the life, since the argument is about nothing being missing. Other reading: perfect
- *Aetas inter externa est*: “Aetas” is age or lifetime; here it means how many years one gets, and “years” says that without an added explaining word. “Externa” is the Stoic term for things outside a person and not in that person’s control. Other reading: Length of life is one of the externals
- *Quamdiu sim alienum est*: “Alienum” is literally “belonging to another”, the set opposite of “meum”, “mine”, in the next clause. The English reads “not mine” so that a reader does not look for some particular other owner. Other reading: How long I am to be belongs to another
- *quamdiu ero, ut sim, meum est*: Very compressed: the verb “to be” stands twice, and the second time it seems to carry a full sense, to be truly alive and not merely to last, which is what the next sentence asks for. The English keeps the bare verb and adds no “truly”. Editors have doubted the wording here and a word may have dropped out (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: that I should truly be alive, for as long as I am alive, is in my power
- *aevum ignobile emetiar*: “Emetiri” is to measure out and so to travel the whole length of; with “through darkness” and the travel verb that follows, the English takes the travelling sense. “Ignobile” is unknown, obscure, without distinction. Other reading: that I do not measure out an inglorious span of years
- *ut agam vitam, non ut praetervehar*: A play of active against passive: “agere vitam” is the common phrase for living a life but literally “to drive” it, while “praetervehi” is to be carried past, as in a ship or a carriage. “Lead” keeps the active sense. The Latin does not say what one is carried past; “it” is supplied. Other reading: that I drive my life, not that I am carried along past

</details>

## Letter 94: On the usefulness of practical advice

### 94.26

You know that friendships are to be cultivated religiously, but you do not do it. You know that a man is shameless who demands chastity of his wife while he himself is a seducer of other men’s wives; you know that, just as she ought to have nothing to do with an adulterer, so you ought to have nothing to do with a mistress, and you do not do it. So you must be brought back to remembering again and again; for these things ought not to be stored away but at hand. Whatever is wholesome ought to be often stirred, often turned over, so that it is not only known to us but also ready. Add now that even plain things usually become plainer.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Scis*: The “you” here is the general “you” of the argument about the use of reminders: anyone who knows the rule and does not keep it. It need not be Lucilius in particular. The second person is kept. Other reading: One knows
- *sancte colendas*: “Sancte” is an adverb, “in a holy way”, and so scrupulously, faithfully; “religiously” keeps both the manner and the sacred colour. “Colere” is to cultivate, tend or honour. Other reading: that friendships are to be tended scrupulously
- *inprobum*: The word ranges from dishonest to shameless to wicked. “Shameless” is chosen because the word is often used of someone who asks for more than is fair, which is the case described. Other reading: that a man is in the wrong
- *ut illi nil cum adultero*: “Illi” is the wife. The verb “ought to have” is supplied from the second half of the comparison. Other reading: as she has no business with a lover
- *paelice*: The “paelex” is a woman kept by a married man, the rival of the wife. “Mistress” is the nearest plain word. Other reading: a concubine
- *subinde ad memoriam reducendus es*: “Subinde” can be “repeatedly” or “from time to time”. The English takes the stronger, since the next sentences say “often”. Literally “you must be led back to memory”. Other reading: from time to time you must be reminded
- *in promptu*: “In promptu” is in view and within reach; “parata”, in the next sentence, is prepared for use. The English keeps the two apart as “at hand” and “ready”. Other reading: but ready to hand
- *saepe agitari debent, saepe versari*: Two verbs of physical movement: to keep in motion or stir, and to turn over. They may be meant of discussion or of thinking over, and the English leaves that open. Other reading: ought to be often discussed, often dwelt on
- *aperta quoque apertiora fieri solent*: “Aperta”, things that lie open, answers the objector’s word in the section before. One English word, “plain”, is kept for both forms. Other reading: even what is obvious tends to become more obvious

</details>

### 94.55

So let there be some guardian, and let him tug at our ear again and again, and drive off the common talk, and shout back against the praise of the crowds. For you are wrong if you think that vices are born with us: they have come upon us, they have been forced into us. So let the opinions that sound all around us be driven back by frequent reminders.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *custos*: A guard, keeper or watcher, the word used of a boy’s attendant as well as of a sentry. The English reads “guardian”. Other reading: some keeper
- *aurem subinde pervellat*: Tugging the ear was a Roman gesture for reminding someone (from memory, unchecked). The Latin has only “the ear”; the possessive is supplied from the first person plural of the next sentence. “Subinde” is again “repeatedly” or “from time to time”. Other reading: and pluck the ear from time to time
- *rumores*: Not only false reports but what people in general are saying, popular talk and reputation. Other reading: drive away rumours
- *populis laudantibus*: Plural, literally “the peoples praising”; the plural of this word can mean whole peoples, as in the section before, or simply crowds. The English takes crowds and puts the weight on the praise, which is the crowd’s praise of the wrong things. Other reading: against the peoples as they praise
- *supervenerunt, ingesta sunt*: The first verb is “came on top” or “came in addition”, set against being born with a person. The second is “were poured in”, “heaped on” or “forced upon”. Both say that vices arrive from outside. Other reading: they arrived later, they were heaped upon us
- *monitionibus crebris*: “Monitio” is a reminder, a piece of advice or a warning. This letter defends the use of such reminders, so “reminders” is used. Other reading: by frequent warnings
- *opiniones quae nos circumsonant*: “Opinio” is “opinion” by the glossary: here the mistaken beliefs of the crowd. The verb is an image of noise on every side, and it is kept. Other reading: the beliefs that din around us

</details>

### 94.61

Many are found who carry fire into cities, who lay low what the centuries could not storm and what had been safe through several generations, who raise a siege mound level with citadels, and with rams and engines batter walls that had been built up to an astonishing height. There are many who drive armies before them and press hard on the backs of the enemy and come to the great sea drenched in the slaughter of nations; but these too, in order to conquer the enemy, were conquered by desire. No one withstood them as they came, but neither had they themselves withstood ambition and cruelty; at the time when they seemed to be driving others, they were being driven.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inexpugnabilia saeculis*: “Saeculis” can be a dative (“not to be stormed by the centuries”, with time itself as the besieger) or an ablative of time (“impregnable for centuries”). The English takes the dative, since the word stands without any “many” or “so many” and the phrase about generations that follows already speaks of length of time. Other reading: what had been impregnable for centuries
- *aequum arcibus aggerem*: The “agger” is the earth ramp a besieging army builds up against a wall. “Arx” is a citadel or a height. Other reading: a rampart as high as the strongholds
- *ante se agant agmina*: “Agmina” are columns on the march. With the enemy’s backs in the next phrase they are taken to be enemy forces driven in flight, but they could be the conqueror’s own troops. The verb is the one played on at the end, so “drive” is used in both places. Other reading: who lead their columns before them
- *[et]*: The square brackets are the editor’s sign that this word should be deleted. It is left out; it would not change the sense. Other reading: and who also press hard on the backs of the enemy
- *ad mare magnum*: “The great sea” may mean the outer Ocean at the edge of the world, the goal of conquerors such as Alexander. The phrase is left as it stands. Other reading: to the Ocean
- *perfusi caede gentium*: Literally “drenched with the slaughter of nations”, slaughter standing for blood. The image is kept as it is. Other reading: soaked in the blood of slaughtered peoples
- *ut vincerent hostem, cupiditate victi sunt*: The same verb stands in active and passive, so “conquer” is used twice. “Ut” is taken as purpose: they had first to be beaten by desire in order to go out and beat the enemy; a looser “though they conquered” is possible. “Cupiditas” is desire or greed. Other reading: though they conquered the enemy, they were themselves conquered by greed
- *tunc cum agere alios visi sunt, agebantur*: Again active against passive of one verb, “agere”, to drive. The next section, 94.62, opens with the same verb, so “drive” is kept there as well. Other reading: when they seemed to be chasing others, they were themselves being chased

</details>

### 94.62

Wretched Alexander was driven by a madness for laying waste what belonged to others, and it sent him off to unknown lands. Or do you think a man sane who begins first with the disasters of Greece, where he was educated? Who snatches from each the best thing it has: orders Sparta to be a slave, Athens to be silent? Not content with the overthrow of so many states, which Philip had either conquered or bought, he throws down others, some in one place and some in another, and carries his arms around the whole world; and his cruelty, like savage beasts that bite more than hunger demands, nowhere tires and comes to a halt.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Agebat*: The same verb as at the end of 94.61 (“agebantur”), so “driven” again. The Latin is active, “madness drove Alexander”; the English is passive to keep Alexander first and the two verbs close to their subject. Other reading: A madness for laying waste what belonged to others drove wretched Alexander
- *infelicem*: The opposite of “felix”, the word the crowd uses of such men just before: not fortunate, and so wretched or ill-starred. “Wretched” is used because “unhappy” today reads as merely sad. Other reading: Unhappy Alexander
- *furor aliena vastandi*: “Furor” is frenzy or madness. “Aliena” is what is another’s, which can also mean foreign lands. Other reading: a frenzy for devastating foreign lands
- *a Graeciae primum cladibus, in qua eruditus est*: “Clades” are disasters or defeats, here those he inflicted on Greece; the plural is kept. “In qua” refers to Greece. The allusion is probably to the destruction of Thebes and to his schooling under Aristotle (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: who starts with the defeats of Greece, where he had his schooling
- *quod cuique optimum est eripit*: “Cuique” is “from each”, each city or people. The two examples follow with no linking word, so the English sets them after a colon: Sparta (in the Latin, Lacedaemon) loses its freedom, Athens its speech. Other reading: who robs each state of what it prizes most
- *quas aut vicerat Philippus aut emerat*: Philip is Philip II of Macedon, Alexander’s father. “Bought” refers to his winning cities by bribery (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: which Philip had either defeated or purchased
- *alias alio loco proicit*: Literally “he throws forth others in another place”, the idiom for “one in one place, another in another”. “Proicere” can be to throw down, to fling away or to cast out, and it is not certain which is meant. Other reading: he flings others aside, each in a different place
- *nec subsistit usquam lassa crudelitas*: “Lassa” (tired) can fall under the negative: his cruelty nowhere tires and stops. Or it can stand apart: his cruelty is tired and still does not stop. The English takes the first; the beast simile fits either. Other reading: and his cruelty, though worn out, nowhere comes to a halt

</details>

### 94.64

Gnaeus Pompeius, too, was urged to wars abroad and at home not by virtue or reason but by an insane love of false greatness. Now he was going into Spain and against the arms of Sertorius, now to bind the pirates and pacify the seas: these were the causes held out as a cover, in order to keep his power going without a break.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Ne Gnaeo quidem Pompeio*: “Ne” with “quidem” is “not even” or “not this one either”, and either way it stresses the man. After Alexander the English takes it as adding a second case, “Gnaeus Pompeius, too”. Gnaeus Pompeius is Pompey the Great, the Roman general and rival of Caesar. Other reading: Not even Gnaeus Pompeius was urged to wars abroad and at home by virtue or reason
- *externa bella ac domestica*: “Domestica” wars are wars at home, that is, civil wars. Other reading: foreign and civil wars
- *virtus aut ratio*: “Virtus” could be courage in a passage about a general, but paired with reason (ratio) and set against a mad love it is the moral term, so “virtue”, as in the glossary. In the Latin these two are the subject of the verb; the English turns the sentence into the passive so that Pompeius comes first, as in the Latin. Other reading: courage or reason
- *insanus amor magnitudinis falsae*: “Insanus” answers the question in 94.62 whether Alexander was sane, so “insane” is used and not “mad”. Other reading: a mad passion for a greatness that was not real
- *Sertoriana arma*: Literally “the Sertorian arms”: the armed forces of Sertorius, the Roman commander who held Spain against the government at Rome (from memory, unchecked). “Against” is added, as the Latin preposition allows, so that “arms” cannot be heard as an embrace. Other reading: the war with Sertorius
- *ad colligandos piratas*: As given, the verb is “colligare”, to tie up or bind, and so to bring under restraint. A form differing by one letter, from “colligere”, would give “to gather”; which the editions prefer is not known here (from memory, unchecked). The English translates the text as given and keeps the image. Other reading: to round up the pirates
- *hae praetexebantur causae ad continuandam potentiam*: “Praetexere” is to weave a border in front, and so to put forward as a pretext: “held out as a cover”. “Causae” is “causes” here because “reason” is in use for “ratio” in the sentence before. “Continuare” is to make continuous: one command joined to the next, with no gap. Other reading: these were the pretexts put forward for prolonging his power

</details>

### 94.65

What dragged him into Africa, what into the north, what against Mithridates and into Armenia and every corner of Asia? Plainly a boundless desire to grow, since to himself alone he seemed not great enough. What sent Gaius Caesar headlong into his own doom and the state’s alike? Glory and ambition and no limit to standing out above the rest. He could not bear one man ahead of him, though the state was bearing two above it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *illum*: “Him” is Pompey, carried on from 94.64. Other reading: What dragged Pompeius into Africa
- *in septentrionem*: “Into the north”. Seneca does not say which campaign he means; the English is left as general as the Latin. Other reading: to the northern lands
- *omnis Asiae angulos*: “Omnis” can be accusative plural with “corners” (“all the corners of Asia”) or genitive with “Asia” (“the corners of all Asia”). The English takes the first. Mithridates was the king of Pontus against whom Pompey fought (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: the corners of the whole of Asia
- *infinita scilicet cupido crescendi*: “Scilicet” is “of course”, “plainly”, often with an edge of irony. “Cupido” is desire, close to the “cupiditas” of 94.61, so the same English word is used. Other reading: an endless craving to grow bigger, of course
- *cum sibi uni parum magnus videretur*: A play on Pompey’s surname Magnus, “the Great”: everyone called him great, and only he thought himself not great enough. “Cum” here gives the ground, “since”. Other reading: because he was the one man to whom he seemed too little “the Great”
- *in sua fata pariter ac publica inmisit*: “Fata” is “fate” by the glossary, but the plural here means death and ruin, Caesar’s own and the state’s, so “doom” is used. “Inmittere” is to let loose or launch, as a horse or a weapon. “Publica” is “of the public”, of the state; “the state’s” matches the next sentence. Other reading: What sent Gaius Caesar headlong into his own fate and the public fate alike?
- *gloria et ambitio*: “Glory” here means the pursuit of glory. “Ambitio” is the striving for office and standing. Other reading: love of fame and ambition
- *Unum ante se ferre non potuit, cum res publica supra se duos ferret*: The same verb, “to bear”, stands twice, so one English word is used. “Cum” may be “though” or “at a time when”, and “res publica” could also be “the republic”. The “one” is Pompey; the “two” are presumably Pompey and Caesar himself as masters over the state, which the Latin does not say. Other reading: He could not endure one man in front of him at a time when the republic was enduring two over its head

</details>

## Letter 95: On why advice needs underlying principles

### 95.8

All those arts are occupied with the equipment of life, not with the whole of life; and so many things check them from outside and hinder them: hope, desire, fear. But this one, which professes the art of life, cannot be prevented by anything from exercising itself, for it shatters hindrances and tosses aside what stands in its way. Do you want to know how unlike the condition of the other arts is to the condition of this one? In them it is more excusable to go wrong on purpose than by accident; in this one the greatest fault is to do wrong of one’s own accord.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>
