- *artes*: Ars is any skilled craft with rules, such as the helmsman’s in the section before. “Art” is kept throughout this passage and the next because the argument sets the “art of life” against the others. Other reading: All those crafts
- *instrumenta vitae*: Instrumenta are tools, gear, the means one works with. The English has “equipment”; the contrast is with life as a whole. Other reading: the tools of life
- *multa illas inhibent extrinsecus et inpediunt, spes, cupiditas, timor*: Hope, desire and fear stand in apposition to “many things” and are said to act “from outside”, which is taken to mean from outside the art, not from outside the person. The bare list is kept as the Latin has it. Other reading: and so many things outside them hold them back and get in their way, such as hope, desire, fear
- *haec quae artem vitae professa est*: “This one” is wisdom (sapientia), named in the section before. Profiteri is what a teacher does when he announces his subject; the Latin form describes a claim that still stands, so the English has the present “professes”. Other reading: But this one, which has declared itself the teacher of the art of life
- *nulla re quominus se exerceat vetari potest*: Vetare is usually to forbid. Here nothing gives an order, so the English has “prevented”. Other reading: cannot be forbidden by anything to exercise itself
- *discutit enim inpedimenta et iactat obstantia*: Iactare is to toss or fling, often to toss about. It is taken here as tossing obstacles out of the way. “Hindrances” repeats “hinder” (inpediunt) from the sentence before, as the Latin does. Other reading: for it breaks up hindrances and flings obstacles about
- *voluntate peccare*: Peccare covers both a slip in a craft and a moral wrong, and the argument turns on that range. “Go wrong” is used for it here and in the next section. Voluntate is “by will”, that is, on purpose. Other reading: to make a mistake deliberately
- *sponte delinquere*: A different verb from peccare, so it has a different English phrase, “do wrong”. Delinquere is to fall short, to fail in what one ought to do. Other reading: to fail deliberately

</details>

### 95.9

What I mean is this. A grammarian will not blush at a mistake in grammar if he made it knowingly; he will blush if he made it unknowingly. A doctor, if he does not realise that his patient is failing, goes wrong more, as far as his art is concerned, than if he pretends not to realise it. But in this art of living the fault of those who go wrong willingly is the more shameful. Add now the fact that most arts too, or rather the most liberal arts of them all, have their own doctrines, not only precepts, as medicine does; and so the school of Hippocrates is one, that of Asclepiades another, that of Themison another.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Grammaticus non erubescet soloecismo*: The grammaticus is the teacher of language and literature. The Latin word is “solecism”, a fault in the construction of a sentence; the English says “a mistake in grammar” because “solecism” is no longer a common word. Other reading: A grammarian will not blush at a solecism
- *si se intellegere dissimulat*: Dissimulare is to hide what is in fact the case: he does realise it and acts as if he did not. The English puts this as “pretends not to realise it”. Other reading: than if he conceals that he realises it
- *quantum ad artem*: This limits the claim to the doctor’s skill, leaving aside the moral side. The limit is kept explicit. Other reading: so far as skill goes
- *turpior volentium culpa est*: Literally “the fault of the willing is the more shameful”, the comparison being with those who go wrong unwillingly. The English supplies “those who go wrong”, the verb already used of the doctor, so that “the willing” is not misread. Other reading: the fault of the willing is the more shameful
- *pleraeque*: Plerique can mean “most” or, more loosely, “very many”. The English has “most”. Other reading: a great many arts too
- *immo ex omnibus liberalissimae*: Liberalis is “worthy of a free person”, the word behind “liberal arts”. The English keeps “liberal” in that old sense and sets it beside “arts” so that the term can be heard. The Latin marks the phrase off as an aside; the English uses commas. Other reading: or rather those most worthy of a free person among them all
- *decreta sua, non tantum praecepta*: The two technical terms of this letter. Decreta are the general tenets of a school (Seneca elsewhere in the letter equates them with the Greek dogmata; from memory, unchecked) and praecepta are particular instructions. The English uses “doctrines” and “precepts” and keeps “precept” in the later sections. Other reading: their own principles, not only rules
- *alia est Hippocratis secta, alia Asclepiadis, alia Themisonis*: Three Greek physicians treated as heads of rival medical schools: Hippocrates of Cos, Asclepiades of Bithynia, who practised at Rome, and his pupil Themison (from memory, unchecked). Secta is a school one follows; “school” is used here and in Ep. 97.14. Other reading: the sect of Hippocrates is one thing, that of Asclepiades another, that of Themison another

</details>

### 95.45

Marcus Brutus, in the book he titled “Peri kathekontos” (“On Duty”), gives many precepts to parents and to children and to brothers. No one will carry these out as he ought unless he has something to refer them to. We must set before us the goal of the highest good, towards which we may strive, towards which every deed and word of ours may look, just as those who sail must direct their course towards some star.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *M. Brutus*: Marcus Junius Brutus, the assassin of Caesar, who also wrote philosophical works (from memory, unchecked). The first name is written out. Other reading: M. Brutus
- *peri kathekontos*: Seneca gives the title in Greek, which this copy prints in Latin letters. The English keeps the Greek title and adds its meaning in brackets, which is not in the Latin. Kathekon is the Stoic term for what it is fitting to do, Latin officium. Other reading: the book he titled “On Appropriate Action”
- *haec nemo faciet quemadmodum debet*: “These” are the precepts, or the things they prescribe. Facere here is to do what they say, so the English has “carry these out”. Other reading: no one will do these things in the way he should
- *nisi habuerit quo referat*: Literally “unless he has that to which he may refer (them)”, a fixed point of reference. No object is expressed; “them” is supplied. Other reading: unless he has a standard to measure them against
- *finem summi boni*: The genitive can define (“the end that is the highest good”) or depend more loosely. “The goal of the highest good” keeps the Latin shape and leaves this open. Summum bonum is the Stoic term, “the highest good”. Other reading: We must set before us an end, namely the highest good
- *ad aliquod sidus*: English more often says that one steers “by” a star. “Towards” is used because the Latin has the same small word three times, for striving towards the goal, looking towards it and steering towards the star. Other reading: must direct their course by some star

</details>

### 95.47

It is usual to give precepts on how the gods are to be worshipped. Let us forbid anyone to light lamps on the sabbath, since the gods have no need of light and not even human beings take pleasure in soot. Let us forbid the performing of morning greetings and sitting at the doors of temples: it is human vanity that is won by such attentions; whoever knows god worships him. Let us forbid the bringing of towels and bath scrapers to Jupiter and the holding of a mirror for Juno: god does not look for servants. Why would he? He himself serves the human race; he is at hand everywhere and for everyone.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *solet praecipi*: Impersonal passive, “it is usual for precepts to be given”. The English stays impersonal and keeps the word “precepts”, since the letter’s argument is about them. Other reading: People usually lay down rules on how the gods are to be worshipped.
- *prohibeamus*: This verb form, and the two that follow (vetemus), can be an exhortation or a concession, with the point, made in the next section, that such bans are not enough without a right idea of god. The English takes the exhortation, which is the plainer reading of the form. Other reading: We may forbid anyone to light lamps on the sabbath
- *sabbatis*: The Jewish sabbath, here in the plural (“on sabbaths”). Seneca is speaking of a foreign rite practised at Rome. Other reading: on sabbaths
- *salutationibus matutinis fungi*: The salutatio, literally a greeting, was the morning call a Roman dependant had to pay at his patron’s house. Here the same attendance is paid to a god at his temple. Fungi is to carry out a duty, so “performing”. Other reading: Let us forbid the paying of morning calls
- *humana ambitio istis officiis capitur*: Ambitio here is the wish to be courted and honoured, not the wish to rise, so “vanity”. Capitur is “is caught, won over”. Officia are the dutiful attentions just listed. Other reading: it is human ambition that is caught by such services
- *deum colit qui novit*: The second verb has no object in the Latin; “god” is understood from the first. The English order is turned round for clarity. Other reading: he worships god who knows him
- *lintea et strigiles Iovi ferre et speculum tenere Iunoni*: Worshippers wait on the statues as slaves wait on a master at the bath and a mistress at her dressing. Lintea are linen towels, strigiles the scrapers used at the bath. Jupiter is the chief Roman god and Juno his wife. Other reading: to carry linen cloths and strigils to Jupiter and to hold up a mirror to Juno
- *non quaerit ministros deus*: The Latin moves from “the gods” to “god” in the singular in this passage, and the English keeps that. “Servants” and “serves” render ministros and ministrat, one word repeated. Other reading: god does not need attendants
- *Quidni?*: Literally “why not?”, an idiom that confirms what has just been said. After a negative statement English needs the question turned round. Other reading: Of course not.

</details>

### 95.49

What cause do the gods have for doing good? Nature. Anyone who thinks they are unwilling to do harm is mistaken: they are not able to. They can neither suffer a wrong nor do one, for to hurt and to be hurt are joined together. Those whom that nature, the highest and most beautiful of all, has taken out of danger it has not made dangerous either.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quae causa est dis bene faciendi?*: Causa is “cause” or “reason, motive”. “Cause” is kept, since the answer is not a motive but nature. Other reading: What reason do the gods have for doing good?
- *natura*: The answer is one bare word with no possessive. It can be the gods’ own nature or nature in general. The English leaves it bare, so that the last sentence can take it up as “that nature”. Other reading: Their nature.
- *nocere nolle*: “To be unwilling to harm”, that is, to refrain by choice. The correction that follows is that it is not a matter of choice. Other reading: that they choose not to do harm
- *Nec accipere iniuriam queunt nec facere*: Iniuria is a wrong done to someone. The literal verb is “receive”; English says “suffer a wrong”. Other reading: They can neither receive an injury nor do one
- *laedere etenim laedique coniunctum est*: The thought is that the power to hurt and the liability to be hurt go together. “Hurt” is kept for laedere here and in Ep. 95.52, and “harm” for nocere. Other reading: for hurting and being hurt belong together
- *Summa illa ac pulcherrima omnium natura*: It is open whether this is the gods’ own nature or the nature of things that made them, and whether “of all” goes with the adjectives or with “nature”. The English keeps the phrase as open as the Latin. Other reading: That supreme and most beautiful nature of all things
- *ne periculosos quidem fecit*: Ne … quidem is “not even” or “not … either”. Here the second fits: out of danger, and not dangerous either. The Latin plays on periculo and periculosos, kept as “danger” and “dangerous”. Other reading: it has not even made dangerous

</details>

### 95.50

The first way to worship the gods is to believe in the gods; next, to give them the majesty that is theirs, to give them the goodness without which there is no majesty; to know that it is they who preside over the world, who govern all things by their power, who have the guardianship of the human race, sometimes without concern for individuals. They neither give evil nor have it; yet they chastise some people and restrain them and impose punishments, and sometimes they punish by what looks like a good. Do you want to win the favour of the gods? Be good. Whoever has imitated them has worshipped them enough.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Primus est deorum cultus*: Literally “the first worship of the gods”. English needs a noun such as “way” or “act”; “first” is answered by “next”. Other reading: The first act of worship of the gods
- *deos credere*: Credere with a plain object: to believe in the gods, to believe that they exist. The English takes the shorter form. Other reading: to believe that there are gods
- *reddere illis maiestatem suam*: Reddere is to give what is owed, or to give back, and suam marks the majesty as their own. “Give” is repeated as the Latin repeats the verb. Other reading: to give them back their majesty
- *scire illos esse qui praesident mundo*: This can be “to know that they are the ones who preside” or “to know that there are those who preside”. The English takes the first, since the gods’ existence was the first point. Other reading: to know that there are beings who preside over the world
- *universa vi sua temperant*: Universa is “all things together”; temperare is to regulate, keep in due order. Vi sua is “by their own force”. Other reading: who regulate the universe by their own force
- *interdum incuriosi singulorum*: As given the text says the gods are sometimes careless of individuals. Some editions are thought to read or conjecture “curiosi” here, without the negative, which gives the opposite sense (from memory, unchecked). The English translates the text as given. Other reading: and who sometimes take thought for individuals
- *nec dant malum nec habent*: Malum is “evil” or “harm”. The two plain verbs, “give” and “have”, are kept. Other reading: They neither do harm nor suffer it
- *specie boni puniunt*: Literally “under the appearance of a good”. The English takes it that the thing which looks like a good is itself the punishment. It could also mean that they punish while seeming to do a kindness. Other reading: sometimes they punish under a show of kindness

</details>

### 95.52

All this that you see, in which things divine and human are enclosed, is one; we are limbs of a great body. Nature brought us forth as kin, since she begot us from the same things and for the same things. She planted in us love for one another and made us fit for fellowship. She established what is fair and just; by her arrangement it is more wretched to harm than to be hurt; by her command let our hands be ready for those who need help.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *omne hoc quod vides*: The passage begins in lower case because it is the formula of human duty announced at the end of the section before. “You” is singular, the person to whom the formula is handed. Other reading: this whole that you see
- *membra sumus corporis magni*: Membra are limbs or members. The bodily image is kept. Other reading: we are members of a great body
- *cognatos*: Cognati are blood relatives. The English has “kin”. Other reading: Nature produced us as blood relatives
- *cum ex isdem et in eadem gigneret*: Literally “from the same things and into the same things”. The second half can mean for the same ends or back into the same elements; the English takes the ends and keeps the wording loose. Cum is taken as “since”; it could be “when”. Other reading: when she begot us from the same elements, to return into the same
- *sociabiles*: “Able to join in fellowship.” The English has “fit for fellowship” so that it links with societas, “fellowship”, in the next section. Other reading: and made us sociable
- *ex illius constitutione*: Constitutio is how something has been set up, an arrangement or a ruling. The English has “arrangement”, beside “command” (imperio) in the next clause. Other reading: by her ruling
- *miserius est nocere quam laedi*: Two different verbs, kept as “harm” and “be hurt”. Miser is “wretched”, the word also used in Ep. 96.1. Other reading: it is more pitiable to do harm than to suffer it
- *paratae sint iuvandis manus*: Iuvandis has no noun: “for those (or the things) that are to be helped”. The English takes it of persons. The verb is a command, and the Latin has no possessive with “hands”; one is supplied. Other reading: let hands be ready to help

</details>

### 95.53

Let that verse be both in the heart and on the lips: “I am human: I count nothing human foreign to me.” Let us have it in common: we have been born. Our fellowship is very like an arch of stones, which would fall if the stones did not block one another, and is held up by that very thing.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *et in pectore et in ore*: Literally “both in the breast and in the mouth”. No possessive is expressed. The English uses the pair “heart” and “lips”. Other reading: both in the breast and in the mouth
- *homo sum, humani nihil a me alienum puto*: A line of the comic poet Terence, from his play Heauton Timorumenos (line 77; from memory, unchecked). Homo is a human being, not specifically a male, and the line plays on homo and humani, so the English has “human” twice. Other reading: “I am a man: nothing that concerns man do I think foreign to me.”
- *Habeamus in commune: nati sumus*: As given the Latin does not construe: the first verb has no object and the statement of being born stands bare, making no point. The English is word for word, with “it” supplied, which may point back to the verse or forward. Editors are thought to supply a second “in commune” or to repunctuate so that “in common” goes with “born” (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: Let us hold to this: we were born for the common good.
- *Societas nostra*: Societas is partnership, the bond between associates. The English has “fellowship”, matching “fit for fellowship” in Ep. 95.52. Other reading: Our society
- *lapidum fornicationi*: Fornicatio is an arching or vaulting, from fornix, an arch. It is an arch built of stones. Other reading: a vault of stones
- *casura nisi in vicem obstarent*: The verb is plural, so its subject is the stones. Obstare is to stand against something: each stone blocks its neighbour’s fall. Other reading: which would collapse if the stones did not hold one another back
- *hoc ipso sustinetur*: “By this very thing”, that is, by the stones blocking one another. The English is left as bare as the Latin. Other reading: is kept up by precisely this mutual pressure

</details>

### 95.54

After gods and human beings, let us look into how things are to be used. We shall be tossing out precepts to no purpose unless this has come first: what opinion we ought to hold about each thing, about poverty, about riches, about glory, about disgrace, about homeland, about exile. Let us assess each thing one by one with its reputation set aside, and ask what they are, not what they are called.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quomodo rebus sit utendum*: Uti is to use or to deal with. The same phrase was used of people in the section before Ep. 95.52. Res are “things” in the widest sense, such as the poverty and exile listed next. Other reading: how we should deal with things
- *In supervacuum praecepta iactabimus*: Iactare is to toss or fling about, the same verb as in Ep. 95.8. In supervacuum is “to no use”. Other reading: We shall be scattering precepts uselessly
- *opinionem*: Opinio, the Stoic term for the view held of a thing’s worth. “Opinion” throughout. Other reading: what belief we ought to hold
- *de patria*: Patria is one’s native country. The Latin has no possessive in any item of the list, and the items are kept bare. Other reading: about one’s country
- *Aestimemus singula*: Aestimare is to judge what a thing is worth, which may be little. Singula is “each thing one by one”. The Latin then moves to the plural (“what they are”), and the English follows it. Other reading: Let us value each thing singly
- *fama remota*: Fama is what people commonly say of a thing, its repute. The English has “reputation”, kept apart from “glory” (gloria) in the list. Other reading: setting aside what is commonly said of them

</details>

## Letter 96: On accepting hardship without complaint

### 96.1

Yet you are indignant at something, or complain, and do not understand that there is nothing evil in these things except this one thing, that you are indignant and complain? If you ask me, I think nothing is wretched for a man except that there is anything in nature which he thinks wretched. I shall not put up with myself on the day when I am not able to put up with something. I am in poor health: it is part of fate. The slaves have taken to their beds, the interest has gone wrong, the house has cracked, losses, wounds, hardships, fears have come rushing in: it commonly happens. That says too little: it had to happen.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *nihil esse in istis mali*: Malum is “evil” or simply “bad”. “Evil” is kept, as in Ep. 95.50. The letter opens with “yet” (Tamen), as if answering a complaint of Lucilius. Other reading: that there is nothing bad in these things
- *viro*: Vir is a man in the full sense, with a hint of “a real man”. The plain “a man” is kept. Other reading: for a true man
- *nisi aliquid esse in rerum natura quod putet miserum*: The one wretched thing is his thinking anything wretched; the English keeps the Latin shape. In rerum natura is also the ordinary Latin for “in existence”. The project’s usual “in the nature of things” would mean “inherently” in English, so the English has “in nature”. Other reading: except that there is anything in the world which he thinks wretched
- *Non feram me quo die aliquid ferre non potero*: The sentence turns on ferre used twice, of bearing oneself and of bearing a hardship. “Put up with” is used both times. Other reading: I shall not bear myself on the day when I am not able to bear something.
- *Male valeo*: First person and a plain statement. It may be Seneca’s real state or a supposed case, like the list that follows. The plain statement is kept. Other reading: Suppose I am ill
- *Familia decubuit*: Familia is the household, chiefly its slaves. Decumbere is to take to one’s bed with illness. The Latin has no possessive here or with the interest and the house, and the English has none. Other reading: My household has fallen sick
- *fenus offendit*: Fenus is interest, or money out at interest. Offendere is to stumble or meet a setback, or to give trouble. The English leaves open whether it is income from lending that has failed or interest owed that presses on him. Other reading: the money lent at interest has gone bad
- *Hoc parum est*: Literally “this is too little”. “This” is the phrase just used, that such things commonly happen, which Seneca then replaces with a stronger one. Other reading: That is too little
- *debuit fieri*: Debuit can be necessity, “it was bound to happen”, or what is owed or right, “it was due, it ought to have happened”. The English takes necessity, in line with the opening of the next section, where such things are said to be decreed. The other sense would prepare the taxes of that section, things that are owed. Other reading: it ought to have happened

</details>

### 96.2

These things are decreed; they are not accidents. If you trust me at all, I am at this very moment uncovering my inmost feelings to you: in all the things that seem adverse and hard, this is how I have been formed: I do not obey god but agree with him; I follow him from the heart, not because it is necessary. Nothing will ever befall me that I shall receive gloomily, that I shall receive with a sour face; I shall pay no tax unwillingly. And all the things at which we groan, at which we take fright, are the taxes of life: from these, my Lucilius, do not hope for exemption and do not ask for it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Decernuntur ista, non accidunt*: Decernere is to decree, as a senate or a judge does. Accidere is to befall, with a sense of chance. English “happen” alone loses the contrast, so the English has “are not accidents”, which is the same word in origin. Other reading: These things are decreed; they do not merely happen.
- *intimos adfectus meos*: Adfectus is usually “passion” in this project. Here no fault is implied, so “feelings”. Other reading: my inmost passions
- *cum maxime*: An idiom, “at this very moment, right now”. Other reading: just now
- *sic formatus sum*: Passive, “have been shaped thus”. It can imply that he has shaped himself by training. The passive is kept. Other reading: this is how I have trained myself
- *non pareo deo sed adsentior*: Adsentiri is to agree, to give one’s assent, a word with a Stoic ring. The English has the plain “agree with him”. Other reading: I do not obey god but assent to him
- *ex animo*: An idiom for “sincerely, willingly”. Animus is “mind” in this project, but English says “from the heart”. Other reading: with my whole mind
- *nullum tributum invitus conferam*: Tributum is a levy paid to the state, and conferre is to pay in one’s share. The English has “tax”, repeated in “the taxes of life”. Other reading: I shall pay no tribute against my will
- *nec speraveris immunitatem nec petieris*: A direct prohibition in the second person: do not hope, do not ask. Immunitas is the legal term for exemption from a tax, which carries on the image. Other reading: do not hope for immunity from these or seek it

</details>

## Letter 97: On vice in every age and guilty conscience

### 97.11

A helmsman does not rejoice when his ship has been overturned, a doctor does not rejoice when his patient has been carried out for burial, an orator does not rejoice if the defendant has lost through the advocate’s fault; but on the other hand to everyone his own offence is a pleasure. One man delights in an adultery to which he was provoked by its very difficulty; another delights in cheating and theft, and the fault does not displease him until the fault’s fortune does. This comes about through perverse habit.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *navigio gubernator everso*: Evertere is to overturn or wreck. No possessive is expressed; “his” is supplied here and with the patient. Other reading: A helmsman does not rejoice when the ship is wrecked
- *aegro medicus elato*: Efferre is the set word for carrying a body out to burial. “For burial” is added to make that plain. Other reading: when the sick man has been carried out
- *orator si patroni culpa reus cecidit*: The orator and the patronus (advocate) are the same man: he is not glad when his own mistake loses the case. The Latin has no possessive with “advocate”. Cecidit, “fell”, is the idiom for losing in court. Other reading: if the accused was convicted through the fault of his counsel
- *omnibus crimen suum voluptati est*: Crimen is a charge and so the offence charged. “Everyone” keeps omnibus at full strength. Voluptas is “pleasure”. Other reading: all men take pleasure in their own guilt
- *in quod inritatus est ipsa difficultate*: Inritare is to goad or excite. The difficulty itself is what spurred him on. Other reading: to which the very difficulty of it incited him
- *circumscriptione furtoque*: Circumscriptio is defrauding someone, especially by sharp practice. The English has the plain “cheating”. Other reading: in fraud and theft
- *nec ante illi culpa quam culpae fortuna displicuit*: Literally “nor has the fault displeased him before the fault’s fortune did”: he is sorry only when it turns out badly. The past form states a general truth, so the English uses the present. “Fortune” keeps fortuna; here it means how the thing turned out. Other reading: and he is not sorry for the fault until it turns out badly for him
- *prava consuetudine*: Pravus is crooked, perverse, stronger than “bad”. Consuetudo can be a person’s habit or general custom. The English takes habit. Other reading: from corrupt custom

</details>

### 97.12

Apart from that, so that you may know that a sense of the good lies underneath even in minds that have been drawn away into the worst things, and that what is shameful is not unknown but disregarded: all people conceal their wrongdoings and, however luckily these have turned out, enjoy the fruit of them while keeping the deeds themselves out of sight. But a good conscience wants to come forward and be seen; wickedness is afraid even of the darkness.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Alioquin*: “Otherwise, in other respects”, or more loosely “besides”. The section before ends by blaming perverse habit, and the English takes the word as “apart from that”. Other reading: Besides,
- *ut scias*: A purpose clause hanging on the act of speaking: “(this is said) so that you may know”. The Latin shape is kept and the turn is marked with a colon. Other reading: As proof that
- *boni sensum*: Sensus is a perception or awareness; bonum is “the good”. Other reading: a feeling for what is good
- *nec ignorari turpe sed neglegi*: Turpe is what is shameful or base, the opposite of what is honourable. Neglegi is to be disregarded, not merely overlooked. Other reading: and that the base is not unrecognised but ignored
- *peccata*: The noun from peccare. In this letter the sense is moral, so “wrongdoings”, and “do wrong” in Ep. 97.14. Other reading: all people hide their sins
- *quamvis feliciter cesserint*: Feliciter is “with good luck, successfully”. “Happy” is kept for beatus, so the English has “luckily”. Other reading: even though they have turned out successfully
- *fructu illorum utuntur, ipsa subducunt*: Two short clauses with no connective: they use the proceeds and withdraw the deeds from view. The English joins them with “while”. Other reading: they use the profit of them and hide the acts themselves
- *ipsas nequitia tenebras timet*: Ipsas is emphatic and has the force of “even”: wickedness fears the very darkness that hides it. Nequitia is wickedness, worthlessness. Other reading: wickedness is afraid of the darkness itself

</details>

### 97.14

I do not judge that this conflicts with our school, if it is explained in this way. Why? Because the first and greatest punishment of those who do wrong is to have done wrong, and no crime, even if Fortune adorns it with her gifts, even if she protects and defends it, is unpunished, since the penalty of crime is in the crime. But nonetheless these second punishments too press hard upon that first one and follow it: always to be afraid, to take fright, and to have no trust in one’s own security. Why should I free wickedness from this penalty? Why should I not leave it always in suspense?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Hoc ego repugnare sectae nostrae*: “This” is the saying of Epicurus quoted in the section before, that a guilty man may stay hidden but cannot be confident of it. The school meant is the Stoic one, to which Epicurus was a rival. Other reading: I do not think this is at odds with our sect
- *si sic expediatur*: Expedire is to disentangle, set out. “In this way” may point back to the paraphrase in the section before or forward to the account that follows. The English leaves it open. Other reading: provided it is set out as follows
- *prima illa et maxima peccantium est poena peccasse*: The sentence repeats peccare, kept as “do wrong … have done wrong”. Poena is “punishment” throughout the passage and supplicium is “penalty”. Other reading: the first and greatest punishment of sinners is to have sinned
- *licet illud fortuna exornet muneribus suis*: Fortune is treated as a person who gives gifts and protects, so the English has a capital and “her”. Other reading: however much fortune decks it out with its gifts
- *tueatur ac vindicet*: Vindicare is to claim as one’s own, to champion, or to rescue from a charge. The English has “defends”. Other reading: even if she protects it and takes its side
- *sceleris in scelere supplicium est*: Supplicium is a penalty exacted, often a severe one. It is kept distinct from poena. Other reading: the punishment of crime lies in the crime itself
- *et hae illam secundae poenae premunt ac sequuntur*: Illam is feminine singular and the word for crime is neuter, so it is taken as the first punishment, and “first” is supplied to say so: the second punishments follow close upon the first. It could instead look ahead to wickedness (nequitiam), named two sentences later. The two verbs together mean to follow hard upon. Other reading: these second punishments too press hard on wickedness and pursue it
- *securitati diffidere*: Securitas is “freedom from care” in this project’s glossary. Here the wrongdoer distrusts something he seems to have, and that phrase makes the clause hard to follow, so the English has “security”, which like the Latin word covers both safety and ease of mind. The section before, which breaks off in this copy, seems to set safe crimes against carefree ones (from memory, unchecked). The possessive is supplied. Other reading: and to put no trust in freedom from care

</details>

## Letter 98: On not depending on fortune's gifts

### 98.2

All the things that Fortune looks on become fruitful and pleasant on this condition: that the one who has them also has himself and is not in the power of his own things. For those people are wrong, Lucilius, who judge that Fortune gives us anything good or anything evil: she gives the material for goods and evils, and the beginnings of things that, in our keeping, will turn out for evil or for good. For the mind is stronger than any fortune, and itself leads its own things in either direction, and is for itself the cause of a happy life and of a wretched one.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *fortuna intuetur*: The verb means to look at or have regard to. It may mean everything in Fortune’s field of view, or the things she looks on with favour. The English keeps the plain image of looking. Other reading: All the things that Fortune looks on with favour
- *ita fructifera ac iucunda fiunt si*: The pair ita ... si is restrictive: “on these terms, if”. The English reads “on this condition” so as not to add an “only” that is not a separate word in the Latin. Other reading: become fruitful and pleasant only if
- *se quoque habet*: Literally “also has himself”, meaning he is in possession of himself. “Has” is kept because the sentence turns on the repeated verb habet. Other reading: is also master of himself
- *in rerum suarum potestate*: Res is wide: things, possessions, circumstances, affairs. It is “things” all three times in this passage (rerum suarum, initia rerum, res suas), so that the answer at the end can be heard: the owner must not be in the power of his things, and the mind leads its things. Other reading: and is not under the control of his own possessions
- *materiam dat bonorum ac malorum*: Materia is raw stuff or timber, what a thing is made from. Bonum and malum are “good” and “evil” throughout this passage so the chain can be followed. Other reading: she supplies the raw stuff of good things and bad things
- *apud nos in malum bonumve exiturarum*: Apud nos is “with us”, “at our house”, “in our possession”. It says where the outcome is decided. The English reads “in our keeping”, the wording used for the same preposition in 98.10. Other reading: things that in our hands will end as an evil or as a good
- *Valentior enim omni fortuna*: Omni can be “every” or “any”. The noun is the same fortuna as before, but with omni it means any kind of fortune, good or bad, which fits “in either direction”, so it has no capital here. Other reading: For the mind is stronger than anything Fortune can do
- *ipse res suas ducit*: Ducit is “leads” or “draws”. Ipse stresses that the mind does this itself, not Fortune. Res suas would more naturally be “its own affairs” in English; “things” is kept for the echo of rerum suarum. Other reading: it steers its own affairs itself to one side or the other

</details>

### 98.5

Or rather, by Hercules, so that I may reach for a line that is braver and more just, one with which you may prop up your mind more, say this whenever something has turned out otherwise than you had reckoned: “The gods decided better.” Nothing will happen to one so settled. And he will be so settled if he has reckoned, before he has felt it, with what the changeableness of human affairs can do, if he has children and wife and property as one who will not necessarily have them always, and as one who will not be more wretched on that account if he stops having them.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Immo mehercules*: Immo corrects what was just said: the section before recommended a line of verse about the gods seeing a matter otherwise. Mehercules is the everyday oath by Hercules. Other reading: No, indeed
- *carmen fortius ac iustius*: Carmen can be a verse, a chant, a set form of words or a charm. The section before quoted a line of Vergil (Aeneid 2, from memory, unchecked) and the replacement is two bare words, not verse, so the English reads “line”, which covers both. Fortius is “braver” or “stronger”; iustius is “more just”, that is, fairer to the gods. Other reading: a formula (or charm) that is stronger and fairer
- *ut carmen fortius ac iustius petam*: Petam is first person: the one who goes for the better line is Seneca, and the one who is to say it is Lucilius. Petere is to make for a thing, to go and get it. Other reading: to look for a line that is braver and more just
- *magis fulcias*: Magis is “more”. The English avoids “better” here so that it is not confused with the “better” of the two quoted words, which is a different Latin word (melius). Fulcire is to prop or shore up. Other reading: with which you may better support your mind
- *di melius*: Two words with no verb. In ordinary use they are a wish (“may the gods grant better”, almost “heaven forbid”). Here they answer the line of verse in the section before, in which the gods saw the matter otherwise, and are offered as braver and more just, so the English makes them a statement and supplies the verb “decided”. Other reading: “May the gods grant better.”
- *Sic composito nihil accidet*: Composito has no noun: it may be a person or the mind (animus, also masculine); the English reads “one so settled” and means a person, because what follows speaks of having wife and children. Accidet is “will happen to”, with the sense of something falling on a person as an accident. The Latin shifts here from “you” to “he”, and the English keeps that. Other reading: To a mind so composed nothing will come as an accident.
- *cogitaverit antequam senserit*: The same verb as cogitabas above: to think over, to plan, to count on. “Reckon” is used in both places so the link can be heard. Senserit is to feel or experience. Other reading: if he has thought about it before he has experienced it
- *humanarum rerum varietas*: Varietas is variety, here in the sense of shifting and change. Other reading: the vicissitudes of human affairs
- *non utique semper habiturus*: Non utique is “not necessarily”, “not for certain”: keeping them always is not guaranteed. It does not say that they will certainly be lost. Other reading: as one who is not sure to have them for ever
- *patrimonium*: Strictly the estate inherited from a father; more loosely one’s property. Other reading: inheritance

</details>

### 98.9

So I think Metrodorus put it excellently in the letter in which he consoles his sister after the loss of her son, who was of the finest promise: “Every good of mortals is mortal.” He is speaking of these goods that people rush to in crowds. For that true good does not die; it is sure and everlasting: wisdom and virtue. This is the one immortal thing that falls to mortals.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Metrodorus*: A close friend and follower of Epicurus (from memory, unchecked). The sentence quoted is a fragment of a letter of his, which would have been in Greek. Other reading: Metrodorus the Epicurean
- *adloquitur*: The verb is “speaks to”, “addresses”, and is regularly used of words of comfort after a death. The English reads “consoles”. Other reading: in which he addresses his sister
- *amisso optimae indolis filio*: The Latin does not say whose son. Latin often leaves out “his” or “her” with family words, and the English takes the son to be the sister’s. Indoles is inborn quality, and of the young it means promise. Other reading: after the loss of a son of the finest character
- *omne mortalium bonum*: Mortalium can be “of mortals” (people) or “of mortal things”. The English takes people, because the passage ends with mortalibus, “to mortals”. Other reading: Every good that belongs to mortal things is mortal.
- *ad quae concurritur*: An impersonal passive: “to which there is a running together”. English needs a subject, so “people” is supplied, and “in crowds” stands for the “together” of the verb. Other reading: the goods the crowd rushes after
- *illud verum bonum*: Illud, “that”, sets the true good apart from “these goods” (his bonis) of the sentence before. Other reading: For the true good does not die
- *certum est sempiternumque*: Certum is sure, fixed, to be relied on. Other reading: it is fixed and lasts for ever
- *sapientia et virtus*: Two nouns set beside the singular “true good” with no connecting word. The English keeps that with a colon. Other reading: that is, wisdom and virtue
- *hoc unum contingit inmortale mortalibus*: Contingit is “falls to the lot of”. Unum is “one” or “alone”. Other reading: This alone comes to mortals as something immortal.

</details>

### 98.10

But they are so shameless, and so forgetful of where they are going, of where each single day is pushing them, that they are surprised to lose anything themselves, though on one day they are going to lose everything. Whatever it is that you are written down as owner of is in your keeping; it is not yours. Nothing is firm for one who is not firm, nothing eternal and unconquerable for one who is fragile. It is as necessary to perish as to lose, and this very fact, if we understand it, is a comfort. Lose with an even mind: one must perish.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Ceterum tam inprobi sunt*: “They” has no noun here; it picks up the mortals of the sentence before (98.9). Inprobi ranges over shameless, unreasonable, greedy beyond bounds; the English reads “shameless”. Other reading: Yet people are so presumptuous
- *amissuri uno die omnia*: A future participle: “being about to lose”. Uno die is “on one day” or “in a single day”. Other reading: when in a single day they will lose it all
- *cui dominus inscriberis*: The language of a title or register: the thing has your name entered as owner. The form can be present or future; the English takes the present. Other reading: Whatever you will be registered as master of
- *apud te est*: “Is with you”, “is at your house”: the language of a thing left in someone’s keeping, set against owning it. The English reads “in your keeping”. Other reading: is with you
- *nihil firmum infirmo*: A play on firmum and infirmo (firm, weak). The English keeps the echo with “firm” and “not firm”. Other reading: nothing is solid for the weak
- *invictum*: Invictus can be “unconquered” or “unconquerable”. Beside “eternal” the English takes the second. Other reading: nothing eternal and unconquered
- *perire quam perdere*: A play that English cannot keep in full: perire (to perish) serves in Latin as the passive of perdere (to lose). The same pair returns in the last sentence. Other reading: It is as necessary to be lost as to lose
- *Aequo animo*: The set phrase for calm acceptance. The glossary’s “mind” is kept for animus. Other reading: Lose calmly
- *pereundum est*: An impersonal form: “there must be perishing”. The English keeps it impersonal with “one”, though the command before it is addressed to “you”. Other reading: you must perish

</details>

### 98.11

What help, then, do we find against these losses? This: that we hold in memory the things lost, and do not let the fruit we gathered from them drop away along with the things themselves. Having is snatched away; having had, never. It is a thoroughly ungrateful man who, when he has lost a thing, owes nothing for having received it. Chance snatches the thing from us; the use and the fruit of it chance leaves with us, and this we ourselves have lost by the unfairness of our longing.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *amissiones*: A rare noun, “losings”. English “lose” covers two Latin verbs in this passage: amittere (to let go, lose) and, in the last clause, perdere (to lose by ruining or wasting). Other reading: these bereavements
- *invenimus*: The form is the same for present and perfect. The English takes the present. Other reading: What help, then, have we found
- *fructum excidere patiamur*: Fructus is fruit, yield, enjoyment; “fruit” is kept because the legal pair “use and fruit” follows. Excidere is to fall out, and is also used of things slipping from memory. Other reading: nor allow the enjoyment to slip from our minds
- *Habere eripitur, habuisse numquam*: Two infinitives used as nouns. No person is named, and none is added. Other reading: To have can be taken from us, to have had never can.
- *pro accepto nihil debet*: Bookkeeping language: acceptum is what is entered as received. “Owes nothing” means he behaves as if he owed nothing. Other reading: counts himself as owing nothing for what he was given
- *casus*: “Chance”, not fortuna, so not “Fortune”. Other reading: accident
- *usum fructumque*: A legal term, usus fructus: the right to use a thing and take its yield without owning it. The two plain words are kept so that “fruit” repeats. Other reading: the usufruct
- *quem nos iniquitate desiderii perdidimus*: Quem is singular and picks up the fruit (or use and fruit as one thing). The perfect is kept as it stands, though it may be meant generally. Desiderium is longing for what is gone; iniquitas is unfairness, the opposite of an even mind. Other reading: which we throw away by the injustice of our regret

</details>

### 98.14

Why have we given up? Why do we despair? Whatever it has been possible to do, it is possible to do. Only let us cleanse our mind and follow nature: one who strays from her must desire and fear and be a slave to the things of chance. We may return to the road, we may be restored to our original condition. Let us be restored, so that we can bear pains to the end, in whatever way they invade the body, and say to Fortune: “It is a man you have to deal with. Look for someone you can conquer.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *defecimus*: A perfect beside the present desperamus. The verb ranges over failing, giving out, losing heart and deserting. Other reading: Why have we failed?
- *Quidquid fieri potuit potest*: Potuit is past and potest present: what it was possible to do then is possible now. The past looks back to the examples in the section before (Fabricius, Tubero, Sextius). Fieri can also be “to happen”. Other reading: Whatever was able to happen is able to happen.
- *nos modo purgemus animum*: Modo with the subjunctive can be an exhortation (“only let us”) or a proviso (“provided that”). The English takes the exhortation and splits the sentence. Other reading: provided only that we cleanse our mind
- *aberranti cupiendum timendumque est*: Impersonal forms of necessity with a dative: “for one who strays there must be desiring and fearing”. No objects are given. Other reading: for anyone who wanders from it, craving and fear are unavoidable
- *fortuitis serviendum*: Fortuita are things that come by chance. Servire is to be a slave. Other reading: and must serve accidents
- *in integrum restitui*: A legal phrase (from memory, unchecked): a magistrate’s order putting a person back in the position he held before a loss. Integer is “whole”, “untouched”. The English gives the legal sense plainly and repeats “restored” as the Latin repeats its verb. Other reading: we may be made whole again
- *invaserint*: The form looks forward: in whatever way pains shall have invaded. English puts this in the present. Invadere is to invade or attack. Other reading: in whatever way they have attacked the body
- *cum viro tibi negotium est*: Vir is “man” in the strong sense, the word behind virtus. Negotium is business, dealings. Other reading: “Your business is with a real man.”
- *quaere quem vincas*: Literally “look for one whom you may conquer”, implying: not this one. Other reading: “go and find someone to defeat”

</details>

### 98.17

This, my Lucilius, is learning philosophy in action and training on the real thing: seeing how much spirit a man of good sense has against death, against pain, when the one is coming near and the other is pressing on him. What is to be done must be learned from one who is doing it.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Hoc est*: “This” points back to the example in the section before, the man who goes on living under torment. Other reading: This is what it means
- *in opere*: Literally “in the work”: on the job, in the doing, as against in the lecture room. “In practice” is avoided because the next verb is itself about practising. Other reading: at the work itself
- *ad verum exerceri*: “To be exercised against the real”: training on the real thing, not in practice bouts. Verum can also be “the truth”. Other reading: to be trained by the truth
- *quid homo prudens animi habeat*: Animi depends on quid: “what of spirit”, that is, how much. The glossary prefers “mind” for animus, but the sense here is courage, so the English reads “spirit”. Prudens is a man of good sense, not the technical “wise man” (sapiens). Other reading: how much courage a sensible man has
- *cum illa accedat, hic premat*: Illa is death and hic is pain. Premat has no object in the Latin; “on him” is supplied. Other reading: when death approaches and pain bears down
- *a faciente*: A present participle: from someone in the act of doing it. Other reading: from the doer

</details>

## Letter 99: On excessive grief for a lost child

### 99.4

But most people do not reckon up how much they have received, how much they have rejoiced. Among its other evils that grief has this one: it is not only needless but ungrateful. So then, that you had such a friend: is it wasted effort? In so many years, in so close a sharing of life, in so intimate a partnership of pursuits, was nothing achieved? Do you carry the friendship out to burial along with the friend? And why do you grieve at having lost him, if having had him does you no good? Believe me, a great part of those we have loved stays with us, even though chance has taken the people themselves away. The time that has gone by is ours, and nothing is in a safer place than what has been.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *conputant*: An accounting word: to add up, to reckon. Other reading: do not count
- *quanta perceperint*: Percipere is to take in or receive, and is the regular verb for gathering crops or income. With “fruit” in 98.11 it is “gathered”; here and in 99.5, where no fruit is its object, the English reads “received”. Other reading: how much they have gathered
- *dolor iste*: “Grief”, since the subject is mourning. Iste points at the person addressed or at the kind just mentioned. From memory, unchecked: most of letter 99 is a copy of a letter Seneca wrote to Marullus on the death of his small son, so “you” in this section is Marullus, and the lost friend is a supposed case. Other reading: this grief of yours
- *perit opera*: The idiom for wasted effort or trouble. Perit can be present or a shortened perfect. Other reading: has it all gone to waste?
- *tanta coniunctione vitae*: Vitae is singular and has no “your”: one life joined or shared. Other reading: in so close a joining of your lives
- *studiorum societate*: Studia can be studies or, more widely, the things a person gives himself to. “Pursuits” covers both. Other reading: so intimate a partnership in studies
- *effers*: The usual verb for carrying a body out to burial. The image is kept. Other reading: Do you bury the friendship with the friend?
- *magna pars ex iis quos amavimus*: This can mean a great part of each person, or a great many of them. The English leaves the wording open. Other reading: much of those we have loved
- *quod praeterit tempus*: As spelled, praeterit is present (“passes”); it can also be a shortened perfect (“has passed”). The English takes the perfect, because “what has been” follows. Other reading: the time that is passing is ours

</details>

### 99.5

We are ungrateful for what we have received, out of hope for what is to come, as if what is to come, if it does turn out well for us, were not going to pass quickly into what is past. Whoever is glad only at what is present sets narrow bounds to the fruits of things. Both what is to come and what is past give delight, the one by expectation, the other by memory; but the one is in suspense and may not come about, while the other cannot not have been. What madness is it, then, to lose hold of what is surest? Let us rest in the things we have already drawn in, provided that we were not drawing them in with a mind full of holes, one that let through whatever it had received.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *spe futuri sumus*: Futuri is taken as “of the future”, depending on spe, and sumus goes with ingrati. The words could be read together as “are going to be”, but then spe hangs loose. Other reading: We are going to be ungrateful for what we have received, through hope
- *percepta*: The verb of 99.4, here again “received”. It is the regular verb for gathering fruit, and “fruits” follows in the next sentence. The last word of the section, acceperat, is a related verb and is also “received”. Other reading: ungrateful for what we have gathered
- *si modo successerit nobis*: Succedere with a dative of the person is to turn out well for someone; it can also be simply to come next. The English takes “turn out well”, the usual sense when a thing hoped for is the subject. Other reading: if indeed it comes to us at all
- *fructus rerum*: The “fruit” of 98.11, here plural: yield or enjoyment. Determinat is to mark out boundaries. Other reading: the enjoyment of things
- *haec expectatione, illa memoria*: By the usual rule haec would be the nearer word (the past) and illa the further (the future), but the sense requires the reverse: the future by expectation, the past by memory. The English follows the sense. Other reading: the latter by expectation, the former by memory
- *pendet et non fieri potest*: Pendet is “hangs”, is in suspense; there are no scales in the Latin. Non goes with fieri: “is able not to happen”. It does not mean “cannot happen”. Other reading: is still hanging and can fail to happen
- *certissimo excidere*: Excidere with the ablative is to fall out of a thing, to be put out of possession of it. The English gives the sense with “lose hold of”. It is the verb used of the fruit in 98.11. Other reading: to fall away from what is surest
- *Adquiescamus iis*: To come to rest in a thing, to be content with it. Other reading: Let us be content with
- *hausimus*: Haurire is to draw a liquid and also to drink it up. With a mind full of holes that lets through what it receives, the picture is of drawing water in a leaking vessel, so the English reads “drawn in” and “drawing them in”. Other reading: the things we have already drunk in
- *perforato animo*: The mind as a vessel pierced with holes, so that what is poured in runs out. The image recalls the leaking jar of the Danaids (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: with a punctured mind

</details>

### 99.6

There are countless examples of men who carried out their children, young men, to burial without tears, who went back from the pyre to the senate or to some public duty and at once turned to other business. And not without reason. For first, it is needless to grieve if you gain nothing by grieving. Next, it is unfair to complain of a thing that has happened to one and is waiting for all. Next, the complaint that longing makes is foolish where very little lies between the one lost and the one who longs for him. So our mind ought to be all the more even, because we are following those we have lost.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Innumerabilia sunt exempla*: The Latin as given opens with a quotation mark that is not closed in this section. It marks the passage as part of the letter to Marullus that Seneca is copying out (from memory, unchecked), so “you” here is the reader of that letter. In every section of letter 99 the English leaves this outer mark out, because it is never closed inside a section. Other reading: The same sentence with an opening quotation mark left open
- *liberos iuvenes*: Iuvenes are young adults, not small children: sons who were grown. Other reading: their young children
- *extulerint*: The verb for carrying a body out to burial, as in 99.4. Other reading: who buried
- *statim aliud egerint*: Literally “at once did something else”. Other reading: and immediately attended to something else
- *si nihil dolendo proficias*: A general “you”, kept as in the Latin. Other reading: if one gains nothing by grieving
- *uni accidit, omnibus restat*: “One” is a single person, set against “all”. Accidit can be present or perfect, and the English takes the perfect. Restat is “remains”, is still to come. Other reading: what happens to one man and is in store for everyone
- *desiderii stulta conquestio est*: Literally “the complaint of longing is foolish”. The genitive can be the source (the complaint that longing makes) or the thing complained of (a complaint about one’s longing). The English takes the source, and keeps “complaint” to match “complain” (queri) just before. Other reading: it is foolish to complain of longing
- *minimum interest*: “The least lies between”: the gap may be of time (the mourner follows soon) or of condition. Other reading: where the difference is very slight
- *Eo itaque aequiore animo*: Eo ... quod with a comparative is “all the more ... because”. “Even mind” is kept for aequus animus, as in 98.10. The same root is in iniquum, “unfair”, two clauses earlier, an echo the English does not keep. Other reading: So we ought to be all the calmer because

</details>

### 99.10

“But he died a boy.” I am not yet saying that the one who is done with life is better off. Let us pass to the one who has grown old: by how little he beats the infant! Set before yourself the vastness of deep time and take in the whole; then compare this thing we call a human lifetime with the immeasurable. You will see how small a thing it is that we wish for, that we stretch out.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Sed puer decessit*: An imagined objection. The Latin as given has an outer opening mark (the letter being copied, as in 99.6) and inner marks round these words; the English keeps only the marks round the objection. Puer is a boy or a child. Other reading: “But it was a child who died.”
- *melius agi cum eo qui vita defungitur*: As given there is no word for “early” or “quickly”, and the English translates what stands. From memory, unchecked, printed editions have cito (“quickly”) here, which answers the objection more directly. Melius agi cum is the idiom for being better off; defungi is to be done with a thing, as with a duty. Other reading: that the one who is quickly done with life is better off
- *quantulo vincit infantem*: Quantulo is “by how tiny an amount”. Infans is a baby not yet speaking. Other reading: how small the margin by which he outdoes the baby
- *temporis profundi vastitatem*: Profundi is taken as an adjective with temporis: deep, bottomless time. It can also be a noun (“the abyss of time”). Vastitas is vastness, with a shade of emptiness. Other reading: the vastness of the abyss of time
- *universum conplectere*: Conplectere is an imperative: “embrace”, “take in”. Universum is taken as the whole of time; it can also mean the universe. Other reading: and embrace the universe
- *aetatem vocamus humanam*: Aetas is an age or a span of life. Other reading: what we call the age of man
- *compara immenso*: Immensum is the unmeasured, the boundless. Other reading: compare it with infinity
- *quod extendimus*: “What we stretch out”: what people try to make longer. The image is kept. Other reading: what we try to prolong

</details>

### 99.31

Let us also say this to one who weeps and longs for someone snatched away in his earliest years: all of us, as far as shortness of lifetime goes, if you compare it with the whole of time, both young and old, are on a level. For less comes to us out of all time than what anyone would call the least, since the least is at any rate some part. This span that we live is next to nothing; and yet, oh our madness, it is planned out on a wide scale.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *in aetate prima raptum*: The object is an unnamed masculine “one snatched”. Aetas prima is the earliest age, childhood or first youth. Other reading: a child carried off in infancy
- *quantum ad brevitatem aevi*: “As far as concerns”. Aevum is a lifetime or span. Other reading: so far as the brevity of life is concerned
- *si universo compares*: Universo is taken as the whole of time, as in 99.10, since aevi stands just before and “all time” just after; it can also mean the universe. What is compared is left unsaid, and “it” is supplied for the lifetime. The “you” is general. Other reading: if you compare us with the universe
- *ex aetate omni*: Aetas here is time at large, not an age of life. Other reading: out of the whole of eternity
- *quam quod minimum esse quis dixerit*: “Than what anyone would say is the least.” The next clause gives the ground: the least is still a part, and the human share is less than that. Other reading: than the smallest thing one could name
- *hoc quod vivimus*: Literally “this which we live”. English needs a noun, so “span” is supplied. Other reading: This that we live
- *o dementiam nostram*: An exclamation in the accusative, set inside the sentence. Other reading: how mad we are
- *late disponitur*: Disponere is to set out, arrange, plan; late is “widely”. The sense is that this next to nothing is planned as if it were a broad space. The passive is kept. Other reading: it is laid out far and wide

</details>

## Letter 101: On sudden death and living each day

### 101.7

But what is more foolish than to be surprised that something has happened on any one day which can happen on every day? A boundary does stand for us, where the necessity of the fates, which no plea can move, has fixed it; but none of us knows how near he is to the boundary. So let us shape our mind as though the end had been reached. Let us put nothing off. Let us balance our account with life every day.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quammirari*: Read as “quam mirari” (two words run together in the copy). “Mirari” ranges from “wonder at” to “be surprised”; the English takes “be surprised”, since the section before speaks of brief astonishment at other people’s deaths. Other reading: than to marvel that something has happened
- *ullo die factum quod omni potest fieri*: “Ullo die” is “on any day at all”, set against “omni”, “on every (day)”. The English reads “on any one day” so that the contrast with “every day” is heard. Other reading: that a thing has happened on some day which can happen on every one
- *Stat quidem terminus*: “Terminus” is a boundary mark, the stone that fixes the edge of a piece of land; “stat” (“stands”) and “fixit” (“has fixed”) keep that picture. “Boundary” is used for both occurrences in the passage. Other reading: A limit is indeed set
- *inexorabilis fatorum necessitas*: “Fatorum” is plural and the English keeps “the fates” (lower case), though it may be no more than “fate”. “Inexorabilis” is literally “not to be moved by entreaty”, given as “which no plea can move”. Other reading: the inexorable necessity of fate
- *nostrumquam prope versetur a termino*: Read as “nostrum quam”: “nostrum” goes with “nemo” and “quam” with “prope”. “Versetur” is “moves about, finds himself”; the English narrows it to “is”. Other reading: how close to the boundary he is moving
- *tamquam ad extremaventum sit*: Read as “ad extrema ventum sit”, an impersonal passive: literally “as though it had been come to the last things”. The English keeps it impersonal. Other reading: as though we had arrived at the very end
- *cum vita paria faciamus*: “Paria facere” is a bookkeeping idiom: to make the two sides of an account equal, to settle up. The English keeps the accounting image. Other reading: let us be quits with life every day

</details>

### 101.8

The greatest fault of life is that it is always unfinished, that some part of it is put off. The man who has put the finishing touch to his life every day is not in need of time. But from this need fear is born, and a craving for the future, one that eats away the mind. Nothing is more wretched than doubt about where the things that are coming will end up. The mind, anxious about how much is left or what kind of thing it is, is driven about by a fear that cannot be untangled.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *vitae vitium*: The glossary prefers “vice” for “vitium”, but this is a defect in life itself, not a moral failing, so the English has “fault”. The Latin also has a play of sound between “vitae” and “vitium” that English loses. Other reading: The greatest vice of life
- *inperfecta semper est*: “Inperfecta” is “not brought to completion”, given as “unfinished”; it does not mean morally imperfect here. Other reading: it is always incomplete
- *quod [in] aliquid ex illadiffertur*: Read as “ex illa differtur”. The square brackets around “in” mark a word the edition deletes, and the English is translated without it. With “in” the clause would say that life is put off to something. Other reading: that something of it is always being postponed to some later point
- *summam manum inposuit*: “Summa manus”, literally “the last hand”, is the craftsman’s idiom for the final touch on a finished work. The English keeps the image as “the finishing touch”. Other reading: has laid the last hand on his life
- *ex hac autem indigentia*: “Indigentia” picks up “indiget” just before, and “need” is used for both. “This need” is the need for more time, which the man just described does not have and everyone else does. Other reading: but from this want
- *cupiditas futuri exedens animum*: “Futuri” (“of the future”) is taken with the craving only, as the word order suggests, and “exedens” (“eating away”) with the craving too. “Futuri” could also depend on “timor”, and “exedens” could be felt with the fear as well. “Animus” is “mind” as in the glossary. Other reading: fear of the future and a craving for it, which eat away the mind
- *dubitatione venientium quorsus evadant*: “Venientium” is “of things that are coming”, taken as events, not persons. “Quorsus evadant” is literally “in what direction they will come out”, given as “where they will end up”. Other reading: than uncertainty about how what is coming will turn out
- *quantum sitillud quod restat aut quale*: Read as “quantum sit illud”. The two indirect questions have no governing verb of their own; the English hangs them on “sollicita” (“anxious about”). They could depend loosely on the whole clause. Other reading: How much is left, and of what kind: over this the anxious mind is driven about
- *inexplicabili formidine*: “Inexplicabilis” is literally “that cannot be unfolded or disentangled”; it can also mean “from which there is no way out” or “that cannot be explained”. The English keeps the image of something knotted and names no one who fails to untangle it. Other reading: by a dread that has no way out

</details>

### 101.10

So hurry to live, my Lucilius, and count each single day as a single life. The man who has fitted himself in this way, for whom his life has been whole every day, is free from care. From those who live looking towards hope, each moment slips away as it comes nearest, and in come a greedy hunger and the fear of death, most wretched itself and making everything most wretched. Hence that most shameful prayer of Maecenas, in which he does not refuse crippling, or deformity, or last of all the sharp cross, provided that among these evils his breath is prolonged:

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *singulos dies singulas vitas puta*: The repeated “singulos” and “singulas” pair one day with one life each time. “Single” is repeated to keep the pairing. Other reading: think of each day as a separate life
- *cui vita sua cotidie fuit tota*: Literally “for whom his own life has every day been whole”. It can mean that each day’s life was complete, or that each day was his whole life; the English keeps the literal order, which leaves both open. Other reading: for whom each day has been his entire life
- *securus est*: The glossary gives “freedom from care” for “securitas”, so “securus” is “free from care”, not “safe”. Other reading: is secure
- *inspem viventibus*: Read as “in spem viventibus”, literally “those living into hope”, that is, with their life directed at what they hope for. The dative is taken with “elabitur”: time slips away from them. Other reading: for those who live on hope
- *proximum quodque tempus elabitur*: “Proximum quodque tempus” is “each nearest time”: either the moment immediately at hand or each next period as it arrives. The English reads “each moment as it comes nearest”, which narrows “tempus” (“time”) to “moment”. Other reading: each next stretch of time slips away
- *subitque aviditas*: “Subit” is “comes up from below, steals in”, often “takes the place of”. “Aviditas” is greedy wanting with no object stated; from the context it is hunger for more time or more life, and “greed” alone would suggest money. Other reading: and greed for life takes its place
- *miserrimus ac miserrima omnia efficiens metus mortis*: The Latin repeats the same superlative: the fear of death is “most wretched” and makes all things “most wretched”. The English repeats the word. Other reading: the fear of death, the most miserable of things and the thing that makes everything else most miserable
- *illud Maecenatis turpissimum votum*: Maecenas was the friend of Augustus and patron of poets, who also wrote verse himself. “Votum” is a prayer or vow; the verses that the colon introduces follow in the next section of the letter and are not part of this passage. Other reading: that most disgraceful wish of Maecenas
- *acutam crucem*: “Crux” is the instrument of execution, whether a cross with a beam or a single stake, and “acuta” is “sharp, pointed”. A pointed stake on which a man is set may be meant (the verses that follow in the letter speak of sitting on it: from memory, unchecked). The English keeps the literal words. Other reading: the pointed stake
- *spiritus prorogetur*: “Spiritus” is breath, and so life; “prorogare” is the official word for extending a term of office or a deadline. The English keeps “breath” and adds nothing to it. Other reading: provided his life is given an extension

</details>

## Letter 102: On fame after death and the soul's immortality

### 102.3

You say that in my earlier letter I did not work through the whole inquiry in which I was trying to prove what our school holds: that the renown which comes after death is a good. For, you say, I did not resolve the objection that is made against us: “No good,” they say, “is made up of things that stand apart; but this one is made up of things that stand apart.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *epistula prima*: Literally “in the first letter”. It is taken as the first of the letters on this question, that is, the earlier one, so that a reader does not think of the first letter of the collection; the passage does not say which letter is meant. Other reading: in the first letter
- *totam quaestionem explicuisse in qua probareconabar*: Read as “probare conabar”. “In qua” (“in which”) can refer to “quaestionem”, the nearer word, or to “epistula”. The English takes the nearer word and gives “quaestio” as “inquiry”, since one proves something in an inquiry, not in a question. Other reading: in my earlier letter, in which I was trying to prove what our school holds, I did not work through the whole question
- *id quod nostris placet*: “Nostris” is the dative of the word for those on one’s own side, here the Stoics. “Placet” is the regular word for a school’s settled doctrine. Other reading: the view that our people accept
- *claritatem*: Later sections of this letter set “claritas” apart from “fama” and “gloria”, so the three need three English words: “renown” for “claritas” throughout, “fame” for “fama” and “glory” for “gloria”. Other reading: distinction
- *bonumesse*: Read as “bonum esse”. The question is whether renown counts as “a good” in the Stoic sense, so the English has “a good”, not “good”. Other reading: is good
- *Id enim me non solvisse quod opponitur nobis*: The accusative and infinitive carries on what Lucilius says, so “you say” is repeated in the English though the Latin does not repeat it. “Solvere” is to untie a knot, and so to resolve an objection. Other reading: For the knot that is put in the way has not, you say, been untied
- *ex distantibus constat*: “Ex distantibus” is a Stoic technical phrase for a whole whose members are physically separate, such as an army or a people (from memory, unchecked). The first clause has no verb in the Latin; “is made up” is supplied from the second, and the same rendering of “constat” returns in section 17 of this letter. Other reading: consists of separate things

</details>

### 102.17

Fame in any case needs a voice; renown can come even without a voice, content with a judgment. It is complete not only among people who are silent but even among people who cry out against it. I will say what the difference is between renown and glory: glory is made up of the judgments of the many, renown of the judgments of the good.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Fama vocem utique desiderat*: “Fama” is what is said about someone, talk, and is related to the Latin verb for speaking; that is why it needs a voice. “Utique” is a word of necessity, “in any case, without exception”, not of time. Other reading: Talk about a man necessarily requires speech
- *citra vocem*: “Citra” is literally “on this side of”, that is, stopping short of. The English gives it as “without”. Other reading: even short of any utterance
- *contenta iudicio*: “Iudicium” is “judgment” as in the glossary: here a verdict held in the mind, even if it is never spoken. The Latin does not say whose judgment. Other reading: satisfied with a favourable opinion
- *plena est*: “Plena” is literally “full”, taken as “complete, lacking nothing”. Other reading: it is at its full
- *inter reclamantis*: “Reclamantis” (accusative plural) is “those who shout back, cry out in protest”. The Latin gives no object; “against it” is supplied. Other reading: even among those who shout in opposition
- *gloria multorum iudicisconstat*: Read as “iudiciis constat”: as printed, “iudicis” looks like “of a judge”, but the ablative plural of “iudicium” is needed. “Multorum” is “of many people”, given as “the many” because it is set against “the good”. “Constat” is “is made up of”, as in section 3 of this letter, where the objection turns on what a good is made up of. Other reading: glory rests on the judgments of many people
- *claritas bonorum*: “Bonorum” is “of good men”, persons, not “of good things”. “Judgments” is repeated in the English where the Latin leaves it understood. Other reading: renown on those of good men

</details>

### 102.18

“Whose good,” he says, “is renown, that is, praise rendered to a good man by good men? Is it the good of the one praised or of the one praising?” Of both. It is mine, as the one who is praised: because nature bore me as someone who loves all people, I both rejoice to have done good and am glad to have found grateful interpreters of the virtues. That they are grateful is a good of several people, but it is mine too. For my mind is so made that I judge the good of others to be mine, at any rate the good of those for whom I am myself the cause of the good.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquit*: “He says”: an imagined objector, in the singular, and the English keeps the singular. Other reading: someone says
- *laus bono a bonis reddita*: “Reddita” is “rendered, paid as something due”, which looks ahead to the argument in the next section about justice paying what is owed. Other reading: praise given to a good man by good men
- *quia natura meamantem omnium genuit*: Read as “me amantem”. The “because” clause is taken with what follows (the copy has a semicolon before it); it could go with the words before it. “Omnium” can be “of all people” or “of all things”, and the argument that the good of others is one’s own points to people. Other reading: because nature bore me as a lover of all things
- *bene fecisse gaudeo*: “Bene fecisse” can be “to have acted well” or “to have done a kindness”. The gratitude that follows points to the second, and “done good” leaves both audible. Other reading: to have acted well
- *gratos me invenisse virtutuminterpretes*: Read as “virtutum interpretes”; the Latin has only “of virtues”, with no word for whose. An “interpres” is one who explains or reads the meaning of a thing, here people who read virtuous acts rightly. “Judges” is not used, because “judge” is kept for “iudicare” in this letter. Other reading: to have found people who gratefully understand my virtues
- *Hoc plurium bonum est quod grati sunt*: “Plurium” is “of several, of more than one”: the gratitude is a good belonging to a number of people. It could also be pressed to “of the majority”. Other reading: This, their being grateful, is the good of the greater number
- *ita enim animo compositus sum*: Literally “for in mind I am so put together”. The English makes the mind the subject for the sake of natural English; “animus” is “mind” as in the glossary. Other reading: for such is the disposition of my mind
- *quibus ipse sum boni causa*: “Boni causa” is taken as “the cause of (their) good”, with “causa” nominative. In other contexts “boni causa” could mean “for the sake of good”, which does not fit the dative here. Other reading: those whose good has its source in me

</details>

### 102.19

That is a good of those who praise, for it is done by virtue, and every act of virtue is a good. This could not have come to them unless I were such a man. So to be praised deservedly is a good of both, just as much, by Hercules, as to have judged well is a good of the one who judges and of the one in whose favour judgment was given. Surely you do not doubt that justice is both a good of the one who has it and also a good of the one to whom it pays what is owed? To praise one who deserves it is justice; therefore it is a good of both.
