<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Est istud laudantium bonum*: “Istud”, “that”, is the praising, or the gratitude just spoken of; this sentence takes the praisers’ side after the last section took the side of the one praised. Other reading: It is the praisers’ good as well
- *virtute enimgeritur*: Read as “enim geritur”. “Geritur” is “is carried on, performed”; “virtute” is ablative of means. Other reading: for it is an action carried out through virtue
- *nisi ego talis essem*: “Talis”, “of such a kind”, means a man who deserves the praise. The Latin leaves that unstated and so does the English. Other reading: if I were not the kind of man I am
- *merito laudari*: “Merito” is taken as the adverb “deservedly”. It could be felt as “for a merit”. Other reading: to be praised for what one has deserved
- *mehercules*: An everyday oath by Hercules, used for emphasis. The name of the god is kept, as the translator’s brief asks for the names of gods. Other reading: just as surely as
- *secundumquem iudicatum est*: Read as “secundum quem”. This is the courtroom idiom for a verdict given in someone’s favour. Other reading: the one on whose side the verdict went
- *Numquid dubitas*: “Numquid” marks a question that expects the answer no, given as “Surely you do not doubt”. Other reading: Do you doubt at all
- *sitet autem sit*: Read as “sit et autem sit”. The Latin as given does not construe naturally: “autem” cannot follow “et” in this way, and the text is likely faulty here. The English gives the sense, which is not in doubt, as “both” and “and also”, with no separate weight on “autem”. Other reading: is a good of the one who has it and, on the other hand, is a good of the one

</details>

### 102.21

Say rather how natural it is to stretch one’s mind out into what has no measure. The human soul is a great and noble thing. It lets no boundaries be set for it except those it shares with god as well. First, it does not accept a lowly homeland, Ephesus or Alexandria or any ground there may be that is still more crowded with inhabitants or richer in buildings. Its homeland is whatever encloses the highest things and the whole of things in its circuit: this entire vault within which the seas and the lands lie, within which the air, while it separates the divine from the human, also joins them, in which so many divine powers are posted and stand watch, ready for their own work.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Dic potius*: A singular imperative, “say rather”, set against the quibbling arguments dismissed in the section before. The English keeps it as a command to the one addressed. Other reading: Speak instead of
- *humanus animus*: The glossary gives “mind” for “animus” and allows “soul” where it is set against the body as what survives. That is the case here: in the next section this “animus” leaves the body and returns to the gods. “Mind” is kept for “mens” in the sentence before, so the two Latin words stay apart. Other reading: The human mind
- *generosa*: “Generosus” is “of good birth, noble”, not “generous” in the modern sense. Other reading: high-born
- *nullos sibi poni nisi communeset cum deo terminos patitur*: Read as “communes et cum deo”. “Et” is “also”: the only boundaries it accepts are ones that god has too. “Terminos” is the boundary mark again, as in Ep. 101.7. Other reading: it allows no limits to be set for it other than those common to it and to god
- *humilem non accipit patriam*: “Humilis” is literally “low, near the ground”, and so “humble”. Both senses work here, since the true homeland turns out to be the sky; “lowly” keeps both. Other reading: it does not accept a homeland down on the ground
- *frequentius accolis laetiusvetectis solum*: Read as “laetiusve tectis”. “Accolae” are those who live in or near a place; “tecta” are literally roofs, and so houses or buildings; “laetius” is “more flourishing, richer”. Ephesus and Alexandria stand for the great crowded cities of the day. Other reading: any soil more thickly settled or more richly roofed
- *quodcumque suprema et universa circuitu suocingit*: Read as “suo cingit”. Because the verb is singular, “quodcumque” is taken as its subject and “suprema et universa” as its objects: the homeland is the outermost thing that goes round everything. The forms would also allow “suprema et universa” as subject, but the verb does not agree with that. Other reading: whatever the highest and the whole enclose in their circuit
- *humanis divina secernens etiam coniungit*: The air lies between earth and heaven, so it both divides and links them. “Humanis” is taken as ablative, “from human things”. Other reading: the air, which parts things divine from things human, yet also unites them
- *disposita tot numinain actus suos excubant*: Read as “numina in actus suos”. “Numina”, divine powers, probably means the stars and planets. “Disposita” and “excubant” are military words (posted, keeping night watch); “in actus suos” with the accusative is “towards, for their own actions”, so the powers stand ready for their work and the work is not the thing guarded. Other reading: stand watch over their own tasks

</details>

### 102.22

Next, it does not let a narrow span of life be given to it. “All the years are mine,” it says. “No age is closed to great intellects, and there is no time that is not open to thought. When that day comes which is to separate this mixture of the divine and the human, I will leave the body here where I found it, and myself I will give back to the gods. Even now I am not without them, but I am held back by what is heavy and earthly.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *artam aetatem*: “Aetas” is a lifetime or a period of time; “artam” is “tight, narrow”. This answers the “lowly homeland” of the section before: first no narrow place, now no narrow time. Other reading: a cramped stretch of time
- *inquit*: The speaker is the human soul (“animus”) of the section before, so the English has “it says”. Other reading: he says
- *magnis ingeniis*: “Ingenium” is inborn talent or intellect. “Minds” is avoided because “mind” is this project’s word for “mens” and, elsewhere, for “animus”. Other reading: to great minds
- *nullum non cogitationi pervium tempus*: A double negative, “no time not passable to thought”, and the English keeps the two negatives. “Pervius” is “that can be travelled through”. Other reading: every time can be travelled by thought
- *qui mixtum hoc divini humanique secernat*: The subjunctive “secernat” gives the day its purpose or character, “which is to separate”. “Secernat” picks up “secernens” in the section before, and “separate” is used in both. Other reading: the day that will part this blend of divine and human
- *ipse mediis reddam*: As printed, “mediis” would mean “to the middle things”, which gives no good sense and leaves “reddam” without an object. The English reads “me diis” (“myself to the gods”), two words run together in the copy; the “them” of the next sentence then refers to the gods. “Ipse” sets the speaker against the body left behind, which is why “myself” comes first in its clause. Other reading: will give it back to those in between
- *sine illis*: “Illis”, “them”, is the gods of the sentence before, on the reading taken there. Other reading: without those
- *gravi terrenoque detineor*: “Gravi terrenoque” are taken as neuter adjectives used as nouns, “what is heavy and earthly”. They could instead be felt with an unexpressed “body”. Other reading: held down by a heavy and earthly body

</details>

## Letter 103: On the danger people pose to each other

### 103.1

Why do you look about for those things that can perhaps happen to you but can also not happen? I mean fire, the collapse of a building, other things that fall on us but do not lie in wait for us. Look rather at those, avoid those: the things that watch us, that try to catch us. Being shipwrecked, being overturned in a carriage: these are rare accidents, even if serious ones; from man to man the danger is daily. Make yourself ready against this, and keep your eyes fixed on this. No evil is more frequent, none more persistent, none more ingratiating.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *circumspicis*: “Circumspicere” is to look around on all sides, here anxiously, for dangers that may or may not come. “Look about for” keeps both the looking round and the fact that the things are not yet present. Other reading: Why do you keep an anxious watch for
- *ruinam*: “Ruina” is a fall or collapse, in this context the collapse of a building. The Latin says only “collapse”; “of a building” narrows it. Other reading: a collapse
- *incidunt, non insidiantur*: A play of sound and sense: “incidunt” (fall upon, befall) against “insidiantur” (lie in ambush). The English keeps the two images and loses the echo. Other reading: things that befall us but do not plot against us
- *illa potius vide, illa [vide] vita [illa] quae nos observant*: The square brackets mark words the edition deletes. The English follows the text without them: “illa potius vide, illa vita”, “look rather at those, avoid those”. With the bracketed words the sentence repeats itself and does not run; the text is disturbed here and another restoration is possible. Other reading: look rather at those, look at those, avoid those that watch us
- *captant*: “Captare” is to try to catch, as a hunter or a trapper does. Other reading: that hunt us
- *naufragium facere*: An idiom: “to make shipwreck” means to suffer one, not to cause one. Other reading: to be wrecked at sea
- *abhomine homini cotidianum periculum*: Read as “ab homine homini”. There is no verb in the Latin; “is” is supplied. “Homo” is a human being, not a male; “man” keeps the short form of the saying. Other reading: the danger that comes to one human being from another is an everyday thing
- *te expedi*: “Se expedire” is to free oneself of baggage and get ready for action, a soldier’s phrase. Other reading: strip for action against this
- *blandius*: “Blandus” is coaxing, caressing, winning one over: this evil comes with a friendly face. “Ingratiating” is used because “flattering” today mostly means “showing to advantage” and “smooth-tongued” would narrow it to speech. Other reading: none more coaxing

</details>

### 103.3

For your part, however, think about what danger comes from man in such a way that you think about what the duty of man is. Keep your eyes on the one so that you are not harmed, on the other so that you do not harm. Be glad when things go well for anyone, be moved when they go badly, and remember what you ought to fulfil and what you ought to guard against.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Tu tamen*: “Tamen”, “however”, answers the section before, which says that people take pleasure in destroying one another. “Tu” is emphatic, given as “for your part”. Other reading: Even so, you
- *quod ex homine periculum sit*: “Quod” can be “what” (an indirect question, “what danger there is from man”) or “that” (“that there is danger from man”). It is taken as “what”, parallel to “what the duty of man is” in the next clause. Other reading: think that there is danger from man
- *hominis officium*: “Officium” is duty, what it is fitting for someone to do; “hominis” is “of a human being”, the same word as in “danger from man”. “Man” is kept in both so that the pairing shows. Other reading: what a human being’s proper part is
- *alterum intuere ne laedaris, alterum ne laedas*: “The one” is the danger and “the other” is the duty. “Intuere” is the verb of Ep. 103.1 (“keep your eyes fixed on”), so “keep your eyes on” is used here. “Laedere” appears in the passive and then the active, with “harm” for both. Other reading: look to the first so as not to be injured, to the second so as not to injure
- *Commodis omnium laeteris,movearis incommodis*: “Commodis” and “incommodis” are a matched pair in Latin, things that suit and things that do not; the English keeps a matched pair with “go well” and “go badly”. “Omnium”, “of all”, is given as “anyone”, each person whoever it is. The verbs are subjunctives used as mild commands. Other reading: Be glad at the advantages of all, be moved by their disadvantages
- *quae praestare debeas, quae cavere*: “Praestare” ranges over “provide, perform, make good, answer for”; “fulfil” is used as the plain word for a duty owed. “Cavere” is to beware of or guard against: it may mean what must not be done to others or what one must protect oneself from, and the Latin does not say which. Other reading: what you owe to others and what you must beware of from them

</details>

### 103.4

What would you gain by living this way? Not that they will not harm you, but that they will not deceive you. But withdraw into philosophy as far as you can: she will shelter you in her bosom, and in her shrine you will be either safe or safer. People do not ram into one another unless they are walking on the same road.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *quid consequaris*: A subjunctive of possibility, “what would you achieve”. It could also be read as a plain question about the result. Other reading: what do you get by living so?
- *non te ne noceant, sed ne fallant*: The Latin is compressed, and “te” sits oddly, since “noceant” would take a dative; it may belong by anticipation to “fallant”. The sense taken is: the gain is not that people will not harm you, but that they will not deceive you. “Fallant” can also mean “catch you unawares”. Other reading: Not that they will do you no harm, but that they will not take you by surprise
- *illa te sinu suo proteget*: “Sinus” is literally the fold of a garment over the chest, where something is held close and sheltered, and so “bosom” or “lap”. Philosophy is personified here (she has a bosom and a shrine), so the English has “she” and “her”, here and in the next section. Other reading: she will shelter you in the fold of her robe
- *in huiussacrario*: Read as “in huius sacrario”, “in the shrine of this one”, that is, philosophy’s. A “sacrarium” is a place where sacred things are kept. Other reading: in her sanctuary
- *aut tutus aut tutior*: The same adjective appears in the plain and then the comparative form: “safe, or (at least) safer”. The English keeps the bare pairing without adding “at least”. Other reading: either safe or at any rate safer
- *arietant*: “Arietare” comes from “aries”, a ram: to butt, to crash into. “Ram into” keeps the animal in the verb. The Latin names no subject; “people” is supplied. Other reading: People do not knock into one another

</details>

### 103.5

But philosophy herself you must not show off. To many she has been a cause of danger when handled arrogantly and defiantly. Let her strip vices from you, not reproach others with theirs. Let her not be at odds with the ways of people at large, nor make it her business to seem to condemn whatever she does not do. One may be wise without parade, without ill will. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *iactare*: “Iactare” is literally to toss about, and so to flaunt or boast of something. Other reading: you must not boast of philosophy
- *insolenter tractata et contumaciter*: “Insolenter” is “arrogantly, immoderately”; “contumaciter” is “stubbornly, defiantly”, the manner of someone who will not bend to others. The participle “tractata” has no “when” in the Latin; it is supplied. Other reading: handled with insolence and obstinacy
- *tibi vitia detrahat,non aliis exprobret*: “Tibi” is the person from whom the vices are pulled away; “exprobrare” is to cast something in someone’s teeth. “With theirs” is supplied: the Latin says only “not reproach others”, with “vices” understood. Other reading: let it take away your faults, not throw other people’s in their faces
- *Non abhorreat a publicis moribus*: “Abhorrere a” is literally to shrink back from, and so to be at odds with, out of keeping with. The second sense is taken, since the point is that philosophy should not stand apart from common ways. “Publici mores” are the ways of people in general. Other reading: Let her not shrink from the customs of the public
- *nec hoc agat ut quidquidnon facit damnare videatur*: Read as “quidquid non facit”. “Hoc agere ut” is “to make it one’s aim that”, and the English keeps the idea of aim. It could be weakened to a result, behaving so as to seem to condemn. Other reading: nor act in such a way that she seems to condemn everything she does not do
- *sine pompa*: “Pompa” is a public procession, and so show or display; “parade” keeps the picture. Other reading: without display
- *sine invidia*: “Invidia” can be the ill will one feels or the ill will one attracts. After the warning that philosophy has brought danger to many, ill will attracted is the likelier sense, but the Latin is bare and the English leaves it bare. Other reading: without malice

</details>

## Letter 104: On travel, health and the examples of great men

### 104.3

For one must indulge honourable feelings; and sometimes, even if there are pressing reasons, the breath must be called back, even with torment, out of regard for one’s own people, and held back in the very mouth, since the good man must live not as long as it pleases him but as long as he ought. The man who thinks neither a wife nor a friend worth so much that he would stay longer in life, who will persist in dying, is soft. This too the mind should command of itself, when the interest of its own people demands it: not only if it wants to die but if it has begun to, it should pause and oblige its own people.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *honestisadfectibus*: Two words run together in the copy (honestis adfectibus). The glossary gives “passion” for adfectus, with “feeling” where no fault is implied; here they are called honourable, so the English reads “feelings”. The feeling meant is Seneca’s care for his wife Paulina, from the section before. Other reading: honourable passions
- *etiam si premunt causae*: The Latin does not say reasons for what. From what follows they are reasons for dying, such as age or illness. The English leaves this as open as the Latin. Other reading: even if there are pressing reasons to die
- *spiritus*: Spiritus is breath, and so life. “Breath” is kept because the next words picture it as already in the mouth. Other reading: life
- *vel cum tormento*: Vel here is “even”. Tormentum is torture or severe pain; the pain is that of the one who goes on living. Other reading: even at the cost of suffering
- *in ipso ore retinendus est*: The image is of the last breath leaving through the mouth and being held back there. Os is “mouth”, and the English keeps it. Other reading: and held at the very lips
- *in honorem suorum*: Sui are a person’s own family and friends. The word comes three times in the section (suorum, suorum, suis) and “own people” is kept each time, even where the grammatical subject is the mind. Other reading: in honour of those who are his
- *delicatus est*: Delicatus is pampered, soft, fond of his own comfort. The point is that insisting on death can itself be a form of self-indulgence. “Soft” is also used for the same word at Ep. 107.2. Other reading: is self-indulgent
- *intermittat et suis commodet*: Neither verb has an object in the Latin. The dying is taken to be what is paused. Commodare with a dative is to oblige or do a service to someone; it could also be “lend”, with “itself” understood. Other reading: let it break off and lend itself to its own people

</details>

### 104.7

Place does not contribute much to this unless the mind puts itself at its own disposal; the mind will have seclusion in the midst of business if it wants it. But the man who picks out regions and chases after leisure will find everywhere something to pull him this way and that. For they say that Socrates, when someone complained that his travels had done him no good, answered: “This has happened to you, and not undeservedly, for you were travelling with yourself.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ad hoc*: “This” points back to the end of the section before, where Seneca says he is beginning to study with his whole mind after leaving the city. Other reading: to this purpose
- *locus confert*: Latin has no articles. The sentence is taken as a general remark about place, which the contrast with the man who picks out regions supports; it could also be said of the one place Seneca has just gone to. Other reading: The place does not contribute much to this
- *se sibi praestat*: Praestare se can mean to make oneself available, to show oneself, or to answer for oneself. The English takes it as the mind being present and available to itself. Other reading: unless the mind answers for itself to itself
- *secretum*: Secretum is a place or state apart: privacy, retreat, solitude. Other reading: a retreat
- *otium captat*: Captare is to snatch at or hunt for. Otium is leisure, time free of business. Other reading: hunts for free time
- *quo distringatur*: Distringere is to pull in different directions, and so to distract or keep busy. The English keeps the physical image without making it violent. Other reading: something to distract him
- *respondisse ferunt*: A reported saying of Socrates. Seneca tells much the same story in an earlier letter on travel (Ep. 28, from memory, unchecked). Other reading: is said to have replied
- *tecum enim peregrinabaris*: Literally “you were travelling abroad with you”. The imperfect covers the whole time of the travels. Other reading: for you took yourself along on your travels

</details>

### 104.26

But how much better I think of them! They too can do these things, but they do not want to. And after all, whom have those things ever failed when he tried them? To whom have they not appeared easier in the doing? It is not because things are difficult that we do not dare; it is because we do not dare that they are difficult.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *de illis melius existimo*: “They” are the people of the section before, who say that what they cannot do cannot be done. Seneca thinks better of them than they do of themselves. “Opinion” is avoided, since the glossary keeps it for opinio. Other reading: how much higher an opinion I have of them
- *sed nolunt*: Nolle is to be unwilling or to refuse. The English takes the milder sense. Other reading: but they refuse
- *quem umquam ista destituere temptantem*: Destituere is here the short perfect (destituerunt), “have let down”, not an infinitive. Ista, “those things”, are the things said to be beyond human nature, toil and death from the section before. Denique before it is taken as “and after all”. Other reading: whom did those things ever leave in the lurch when he attempted them?
- *in actu*: Literally “in the act”, that is, once one is actually doing them. Other reading: in practice
- *Non quia difficilia sunt non audemus*: The sentence turns the same words round (difficult, not dare; not dare, difficult). The Latin has no noun and the English supplies “things”. Other reading: We do not fail to dare because they are hard; they are hard because we do not dare.

</details>

### 104.27

If, however, you people want an example, take Socrates, a long-suffering old man, tossed through every hardship, yet unconquered both by poverty, which his household burdens made heavier for him, and by toils, which he bore in military service as well. By these he was harassed at home, whether [one thinks of] his wife, with her savage ways and abusive tongue, or [of] his children, unteachable and more like their mother than their father; [the text is damaged here] he lived either in war or under tyranny or in a freedom crueller than wars and tyrants.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *desideratis, accipite*: Both verbs are plural. Seneca is speaking to the objectors of the section before, not to Lucilius alone. “People” is added only to show the plural, which English “you” cannot. Other reading: If, however, you want an example, take Socrates
- *perpessicium*: A rare word, from perpeti, to endure to the end: one who has borne much, or one who is inured to bearing. Other reading: an old man hardened to endurance
- *quos militares quoque pertulit*: Literally “which he bore military ones too”: among his toils were those of a soldier. Other reading: including the toils of a soldier, which he also endured
- *Quibus ille domi exercitus*: Quibus can be a linking relative (“by these”, the toils) or an exclamation (“by what things!”); the English takes the first. Exercitus is a participle, “harassed, kept hard at it”, perhaps with a glance at the military word just before. Latin often leaves out “was” with such a participle, and the English supplies it. Other reading: What trials he had at home!
- *sive uxorem eius moribus feram, lingua petulantem*: “Wife” and “children” are accusatives with no verb in the Latin as given, so the sentence does not construe. The words in square brackets, “one thinks of” and “of”, are supplied to carry them. The wife is Xanthippe. Other reading: whether his wife, wild in character and aggressive in speech
- *+sivere+*: The edition marks this word as corrupt, and the English marks the place in square brackets instead of translating it. It may hide the verb that governed “wife” and “children”, or a phrase setting public life against “at home”, or both. From memory, unchecked: editors have proposed supplements of both kinds, and none is certain. Other reading: if you judge truly, he lived either in war …
- *in bellofuit*: Two words run together in the copy (in bello fuit). Literally “he was in war”, that is, his life fell in a time of war; the English writes “lived” so that it is not read as “he was fighting”. Other reading: he was either at war
- *in libertate bellis ac tyrannis saeviore*: The “freedom” is the restored democracy at Athens, which put Socrates to death, as the next section tells. Tyrannis here is “tyrants”, not “tyrannies”. Other reading: in a liberty more savage than wars and tyrants

</details>

### 104.28

There was fighting for twenty-seven years. After the war was ended the city was given up to be harmed by thirty tyrants, most of whom were his enemies. Last of all, his condemnation was carried through under the gravest charges: he was accused both of violating religion and of corrupting the young, whom he was said to be letting loose against the gods, against their fathers, against the state. After this, prison and poison. So far had these things been from moving the mind of Socrates that they did not move even his face. What a wonderful and unique distinction that is! To the very end no one saw Socrates more cheerful or more gloomy; he was unvarying amid such great variation of fortune.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Viginti et septem annis pugnatum est*: Impersonal: “it was fought”. This is the Peloponnesian War between Athens and Sparta. Other reading: The war lasted twenty-seven years.
- *noxae dedita est civitas*: Noxae dedere is a legal phrase: to surrender the one who did a wrong to the injured party. Noxa is also simply harm, and the English takes that plainer sense, since “for punishment” would imply that the city deserved it. Other reading: the city was surrendered to thirty tyrants for punishment
- *triginta tyrannis*: These are the oligarchs who ruled Athens after its defeat, usually called the Thirty Tyrants or the Thirty. The Latin says only “thirty tyrants”. Other reading: to the Thirty Tyrants
- *ex quibus plerique inimici erant*: The Latin does not say whose enemies. “His” is supplied, since inimicus is a personal enemy and the passage is about Socrates. Plerique is “most”, and can also be “very many”. Other reading: very many of whom were hostile
- *sub gravissimis nominibus impleta*: Nomina here are the heads or titles of a charge. Impleta is “filled up, completed”; it may mean that the condemnation crowned his troubles. Other reading: the measure was filled by his condemnation on the most serious counts
- *quam inmittere in deos*: By grammar quam could go with either noun, but by sense it is the youth that he is said to let loose against gods, fathers and state. Inmittere is to send against or let loose, as one sets a dog on someone. Other reading: a corruption which he was said to be letting loose on the gods
- *ne vultum quidem moverint*: The same verb, movere, is used of his mind and of his face. “Move” is kept for both. Other reading: they did not even change his expression
- *<O> illam mirabilem*: The “O” is an editor’s addition, shown by the angle brackets; with or without it the phrase is an exclamation. Laus is praise, or the merit that earns it; the English takes the second, since what follows describes the merit itself. Other reading: What wonderful and singular praise that is!
- *aequalis fuit in tanta inaequalitate fortunae*: A play on aequalis and inaequalitas, “even” and “unevenness”. “Unvarying” and “variation” keep the echo in natural English. Other reading: he was even amid such great unevenness of fortune

</details>

## Letter 105: On living safely among others

### 105.4

As for not being feared, both a middling fortune and a gentle disposition will secure that for you. Let people know that you are someone they can offend without danger; let reconciliation with you be both easy and sure. To be feared, on the other hand, is as troublesome at home as outside it, by slaves as much as by free men: there is no one who does not have strength enough to do harm. Add now that he who is feared fears: no one has been able to be terrifying and free from care.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Illud, ne timearis*: This is one item in a list. The section before deals with how to avoid being the object of others’ hopes, envy and hatred; this one turns to fear. Other reading: That other thing, that you not be feared
- *fortunae mediocritas*: Fortuna here is one’s means and standing. Mediocritas is a middle measure, with no slight intended. Other reading: a moderate station in life
- *quem offendere sine periculo*: Offendere is to offend, cross or wrong someone. The danger is to the offender: Lucilius is to be known as one who does not take revenge. Other reading: whom they can cross without risk
- *reconciliatio tua*: Literally “your reconciliation”. The English takes it as others winning back the good will of Lucilius after a quarrel; it could also be his own readiness to forgive. Other reading: let your forgiveness be easy and dependable
- *tam a servis quam a liberis*: Liberi can mean “free men” or “children”. Set against servis it is taken as “free men”, but with “at home” just before, “children” is possible. Other reading: by your slaves as much as by your children
- *nulli non*: A double negative, “to no one is there not”. The English keeps the negative form. Other reading: everyone has strength enough to do harm
- *secure*: Secure is “without care”, and can shade into “in safety”. The English follows the glossary’s “freedom from care” for securitas; the clause before, that he who is feared fears, points the same way. Other reading: no one has been able to inspire terror in safety

</details>

## Letter 107: On accepting fate willingly

### 107.2

None of these things is unusual, none is unexpected. To be jolted by such things is as ridiculous as to complain that you are splashed in public or dirtied in the mud. The terms of life are the same as those of a bathhouse, a crowd, a journey: some things will be thrown at you, some will fall on you. Living is not a soft business. You have set out on a long road: you are bound to slip, and knock into things, and fall, and grow tired, and cry out “O death!”, that is, tell a lie. In one place you will leave a companion behind, in another you will carry one out for burial, in another you will be afraid of one. Through jolts of this kind this rough journey has to be travelled to its end.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nihil horum*: “These things” are the troubles of the section before: the slaves of Lucilius ran away while he was busy. Other reading: Nothing of this kind
- *offendi*: Offendi is to be knocked against and so to be annoyed. The related noun offensas at the end of the section means knocks, stumbles or annoyances. “Jolted” and “jolts” are used so that the echo can be heard and so that the second fits the image of a rough road. Other reading: To be upset by such things … through upsets of this kind
- *spargaris in publico*: The next sentence names three settings (bathhouse, crowd, journey) while this one has only two complaints, so something may have dropped out. From memory, unchecked: later editions print a fuller text here, with splashing in the bath and jostling in public. The English translates what is given, with “in public” for in publico. Other reading: that you are splashed in the bath or jostled in public or dirtied in the mud
- *quaedam in te mittentur, quaedam incident*: The contrast is between things aimed at you and things that land on you by chance. Other reading: some things will be aimed at you, some will just happen to you
- *delicata res*: Delicatus is soft, dainty, for the pampered. “Soft” is used as in Ep. 104.3. Other reading: Living is no dainty affair.
- *labaris oportet*: Oportet with these verbs states what is bound to happen on such a road, not a duty. Other reading: you must slip
- *arietes*: A verb here, from arietare, to butt like a ram: to collide with things or people. Other reading: and stumble against things
- *id est mentiaris*: The cry “O death!” is a call for death to come. Seneca calls it a lie because the one who cries it does not really want to die. Other reading: and cry “Oh, for death!”, in other words, lie
- *efferes*: Efferre, “carry out”, is the usual Latin word for taking a body out to its funeral. The English keeps the carrying and adds “for burial” to make that plain. Other reading: in another you will bury one
- *alio timebis*: The verb has no object. “Companion” is carried through all three verbs (leave, carry out, fear). It could be a bare “you will be afraid”. Other reading: in another you will be afraid

</details>

### 107.4

There is no one who has not gone more bravely to meet what he had long braced himself for and stood up even to hard things if they had been thought over beforehand; the unprepared man, on the other hand, has taken fright at even the lightest things. We must see to it that nothing is unexpected for us; and because all things are heavier for being new, constant thought will secure this: that you meet no trouble as a new recruit.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nemo non*: A double negative, “no one has not”, which amounts to “everyone”. The English keeps the negative form, said once for both verbs. Other reading: Everyone has gone more bravely
- *cui se diu composuerat*: Se componere is to set oneself in position, as a fighter takes his stance. “Braced himself” keeps that; “prepared himself” would make an echo with “unprepared” that the Latin does not have. Other reading: what he had long prepared himself for
- *si praemeditata erant*: This is the Stoic practice of rehearsing troubles in thought before they come (praemeditatio). Other reading: if they had been rehearsed in advance
- *expavit*: The three perfects (accessit, obstitit, expavit) state what has always been found to happen. The English keeps them as “has” and does not turn them into a general present. Other reading: the unprepared man panics at even the slightest things
- *omnia novitate graviora*: Literally “all things are heavier by newness”. Other reading: everything is harder to bear when it is new
- *ut nulli sis malo tiro*: Tiro is a new recruit in the army, and the military image is kept. Malum is anything bad, an ill or a trouble, not only a moral evil; the Latin is literally “a recruit to no ill”, and the English supplies “meet”. The Latin moves from the first person plural to the second person singular here and the English keeps that. Other reading: that you are a raw recruit to no evil

</details>

### 107.6

Let us be surprised at none of the things we were born to; no one should complain of them, for this reason, that they are equal for all. I mean it: they are equal, for even what someone has escaped he could have suffered. And an even-handed law is not the one that all have come under but the one that has been passed for all. Let the mind be commanded to keep an even temper, and let us pay the taxes of mortality without complaint.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Nihil miremur*: Mirari is to wonder at or be surprised at. The English takes surprise, since the things in question are troubles. Other reading: Let us wonder at none of the things
- *Ita dico, paria sunt*: Ita dico insists on the word just used. Paria is “equal, the same”; the claim is that these things stand over everyone alike, not that everyone actually suffers the same. Other reading: Yes, I say, they are the same for all
- *Aequum autem ius est*: Aequum is both “equal” and “fair”, and aequitas in the next sentence picks it up. The English uses “even-handed” here and “even temper” there so that the link can be heard, and keeps “equal” for paria. Other reading: And a fair law is not the one
- *quo omnes usi sunt*: Literally “which all have used”, that is, which has actually been applied in everyone’s case. Other reading: the one that all have experienced
- *Imperetur aequitas animo*: Aequitas with animus is the usual Latin for an even, calm mind, but here it also echoes the even-handed law just before, so that fairness may be meant as well. The English takes the first sense and keeps the echo in the word “even”. Other reading: Let fairness be commanded of the mind
- *mortalitatis tributa*: Tributum is a tax or levy paid to the state. The image is of the troubles of mortal life as dues owed. Other reading: the tribute of our mortal condition

</details>

### 107.9

Our mind must be fitted to this law; let it follow this, let it obey this; and let it think that whatever happens ought to have happened, and not wish to scold nature. It is best to bear what you cannot correct, and, without grumbling, to go along with the god by whose doing all things come about: he is a bad soldier who follows his commander groaning.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Ad hanclegem*: Two words run together in the copy (hanc legem). “This law” is the one in the section before: nature governs the world by changes, and lasting things are made of opposites. Other reading: To this rule
- *debuisse fieri putet*: Debuisse can mean “ought to have happened” (rightness) or “was bound to happen” (necessity). The English takes rightness, because the next words forbid scolding nature, but both may be meant. Other reading: let it think that whatever happens had to happen
- *emendare non possis*: Emendare is to free from fault, to correct. The same verb returns at Ep. 107.12 and “correct” is used in both places. The “you” is general. Other reading: what you cannot mend
- *deum quo auctore cuncta proveniunt*: Auctor is an originator and also one on whose authority a thing is done. Proveniunt is “come forth, come about”. Other reading: the god on whose authority all things come forth
- *comitari*: To go as a companion. The verb returns in the verses at Ep. 107.11 and “go along” is used in both places. Other reading: to accompany
- *imperatorem gemens sequitur*: Imperator is the commanding general. “Groaning” returns in the verses at Ep. 107.11. Other reading: who follows his general with a groan

</details>

### 107.11

Lead me, O father and ruler of the high heaven, wherever you have decided: there is no delay in obeying; I am here, and eager. Suppose I am unwilling: I shall go along groaning, and as a bad man I shall be made to do what I was allowed to do as a good one. The fates lead the willing; the unwilling they drag.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Duc, o parens*: These are five lines of verse, Seneca’s own Latin version of lines by the Stoic Cleanthes addressed to Jupiter, as the section before says. The copy gives them as running text with no quotation marks, so the English sets them as prose without marks. The verb has no object in the Latin and the English supplies one. Other reading: Lead, O parent
- *celsique dominator poli*: Polus is the pole of the sky, and so the sky. Dominator is a master or ruler. Other reading: and master of the lofty sky
- *quocumque placuit*: Literally “wherever it has pleased”, with “you” understood. Other reading: wherever it has pleased you
- *nulla parendi mora est*: Parendi (“of obeying”) may echo parens (“father”) in the line before. The echo cannot be kept in English. Other reading: I obey without delay
- *adsum inpiger*: Inpiger is “not sluggish”: brisk, energetic, ready to act. “Eager” is used here and for the same word at Ep. 107.12; “unhesitating” would only repeat “no delay” from the clause before. Other reading: I am here, unhesitating
- *Fac nolle*: Fac with an infinitive is “suppose that”. The subject of “be unwilling” is not expressed and is taken to be the speaker. Other reading: Make me unwilling
- *malusque patiar facere quod licuit bono*: Patiar facere is hard: literally “shall suffer to do”, with suffering set against doing, and the English takes it as being made to do. From memory, unchecked: some editions read a second pati in place of facere, which would give “shall suffer as a bad man what could have been suffered as a good one”. The English translates the text as given. Other reading: and, as a bad man, I shall bear doing what I was allowed to do as a good one
- *Ducunt volentem fata, nolentem trahunt*: “Lead” repeats the first word of the verses, and “unwilling” repeats nolle from two lines before; both echoes are kept. From memory, unchecked: this last line has no counterpart in the Greek lines of Cleanthes preserved elsewhere and may be Seneca’s own. Other reading: Fate leads the one who is willing and drags the one who is not.

</details>

### 107.12

Let us live this way, let us speak this way; let fate find us ready and eager. This is a great spirit, the one that has handed itself over to fate; the small and ignoble one, on the other hand, is the one that struggles against it and thinks ill of the order of the world and would rather correct the gods than itself. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *sic loquamur*: “This way” points back to the verses of Cleanthes just quoted (Ep. 107.11): the speaking meant is speaking to the god as he does. Other reading: let us speak so
- *inpigros*: The same word as in the verses at Ep. 107.11, and “eager” is used in both places. Literally “not sluggish”. Other reading: ready and unhesitating
- *magnus animus*: The glossary prefers “mind” for animus and allows “spirit” where the sense is courage or greatness of spirit, as here, where it is set against the “small” one that follows. “A great mind” would suggest intellect. It is the same Latin word that is “mind” at Ep. 107.9. Other reading: This is a great mind
- *qui se ei tradidit*: Ei is “to it”, taken as fate, the last noun, and the English repeats the noun for clarity. The form could also be “to him”, the god. Other reading: that has surrendered itself to him
- *pusillus et degener*: Pusillus is very small, the opposite of magnus. Degener is “fallen away from its stock”, here from the mind’s high origin; “ignoble” gives that without the modern sense of “degenerate”. Other reading: small and unworthy of its birth
- *de ordine mundi male existimat*: Mundus is the world as an ordered whole, the universe. Ordo is its arrangement or sequence. Other reading: has a low view of the arrangement of the universe
- *emendare mavult deos*: The same verb as at Ep. 107.9 (“what you cannot correct”), and “correct” is kept. “Itself” follows the grammar, since the subject is the animus. Other reading: would rather reform the gods than himself

</details>

## Letter 108: On how to listen to philosophers

### 108.15

From that, some things have stayed with me, Lucilius; for I had come at everything with a great rush, and then, brought back to the life of a citizen, I kept few of the things I had begun well. From that, oysters and mushrooms were given up for life; for they are not foods but treats that force people who are full to eat (which is what the greedy, and those who stuff themselves beyond what they hold, like best), things that will go down easily, come back up easily.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Indemihi*: This is “Inde mihi” run together. “Inde” can mean “from that source” (the teaching described in the section before) or “from that time”. It opens five sentences across this section and the next, and the English keeps one rendering, “From that”, because what follows are things that came out of that teaching. Other reading: Since then, some things have stayed with me
- *magno enim in omnia impetu veneram*: “impetus” here is the ordinary word for a rush or burst of eagerness, not the Stoic technical term “impulse” of Ep. 113.18. Other reading: for I had come to it all with great eagerness
- *civitatis vitam reductus*: “civitas” is the body of citizens, the state, not the town as a place. The English reads “the life of a citizen”, set against the life of the philosopher’s school; whether a public career or simply the way citizens live is meant is left open. Other reading: brought back to public life
- *renuntiatum est*: The Latin is an impersonal passive with no “by me”. The English keeps the impersonal form, though Seneca clearly means himself. Other reading: I gave up oysters and mushrooms for life
- *in omnem vitam*: Literally “for all of life”. English “for life” already means for the whole of it, so no separate word for “all” is added. Other reading: for the whole of life
- *boletisque*: “boletus” is a particular prized mushroom, a luxury dish like the oysters, not mushrooms in general. English has no plain single word for it, so the English reads “mushrooms”. Other reading: oysters and choice mushrooms
- *oblectamenta*: The word means things that delight, set against “cibi”, real foods. The English reads “treats”. Other reading: they are not foods but amusements
- *quod gratissimum est edacibus*: “quod” can look back (being made to eat when already full is what the greedy like best) or forward to the last words (that these things go down and come back up easily). The English takes it as looking back, following the brackets of the edition. Other reading: and, what the greedy like best, they will go down easily, come back up easily
- *facile descensura, facile reditura*: Future participles: these things will slip down and will return, that is, be vomited up. The English keeps the plain image without naming vomiting, and keeps the abrupt pairing without “and”. Other reading: sure to go down easily and to come back easily

</details>

### 108.16

From that, we keep away from perfume for life, since the best smell on a body is none. From that, a stomach that goes without wine. From that, we avoid the bath for life; we came to believe that boiling the body down and emptying it out by sweating is both useless and self-indulgent. The other things I threw off have come back, but in such a way that, where I have broken off my abstinence, I keep to a limit, and one in fact nearer to abstinence, and perhaps harder, since for the mind some things are more easily cut off than kept within limits.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *abstinemus*: The verb is first person plural, used by Seneca of himself, as are two more verbs in the section before the singular returns. The English keeps the person and number of the Latin. Other reading: I keep away from perfume for life
- *Inde vino carens stomachus*: The Latin has no verb: literally “From that, a stomach lacking wine”. The English keeps it as a fragment. Other reading: That is why my stomach goes without wine.
- *balneum*: The next clause shows that the hot bath that makes one sweat is meant, not washing in general. Other reading: the hot bath
- *fugimus*: The form can be a present (“avoid”), in line with “abstinemus”, or a perfect (“have shunned”), in line with “credidimus”. The English reads the present; the sense hardly changes. Other reading: From that, the bath has been shunned for life
- *decoquere corpus*: “decoquere” is to boil down or cook away. The English keeps the cooking image. Other reading: to stew the body
- *delicatumque*: “delicatus” covers soft, pampered, effeminate. The English reads “self-indulgent”, as for the same word in Ep. 114.24. Other reading: both useless and soft
- *modumservem*: This is “modum servem” run together. “modus” is a measure or limit; the English reads “keep to a limit” and picks the word up again in “kept within limits” for “temperantur” at the end. Other reading: keep a measure, and one in fact nearer to abstinence
- *nescio an difficiliorem*: Literally a statement of not knowing whether it is harder. In classical usage the phrase leans towards yes, so the English reads “perhaps harder”. Other reading: and I do not know whether it is harder
- *absciduntur facilius animo quam temperantur*: “animo” can be “for the mind” (the one that finds it easier), “by the mind” (the one that cuts) or “from the mind” (what the things are cut away from). The English takes it as “for the mind”, since the comparison covers both cutting off and keeping within limits. Other reading: since some things are more easily cut off from the mind than kept within limits

</details>

### 108.21

“So hold back your judgment, but keep everything open for yourself. If those things are true, to have abstained from animals is freedom from guilt; if they are false, it is frugality. What does your credulity lose you in that case? I am taking from you the food of lions and vultures.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Itaque*: The speaker is Sotion, whose quoted speech began in the section before and ends here. The English opens with a quotation mark to show that the speech is continuing. “You” is the young Seneca and the speaker is Sotion. Other reading: (the same words with only the closing quotation mark, as in the Latin)
- *iudicium quidem tuum sustine*: “sustinere” here is to hold up or suspend. The hearer is asked not to decide yet. Other reading: So suspend your judgment
- *in integro serva*: A legal idiom: to keep a matter “whole”, that is, undecided and not prejudiced. The English reads “keep everything open”. Other reading: but keep the whole question untouched for yourself
- *innocentia*: Literally “doing no harm”, and so being clear of guilt. If souls do pass into animals, not eating them keeps one clear of bloodshed. The English reads “freedom from guilt” because “innocence” can suggest naivety. Other reading: to have abstained from animals is innocence
- *frugalitas*: The word covers thrift and sober living generally, not only saving money. Other reading: it is thrift
- *credulitatis tuae damnum*: Literally “the loss of your credulity”, with “istic”, “there, in that case”. The genitive is taken as the loss that believing causes, and the case meant is the one just named, that the doctrine is false. “credulitas” is given its plain sense, “credulity”. Other reading: What loss does your readiness to believe bring you there?
- *alimenta tibi leonum et vulturum eripio*: “The food of lions and vultures” is flesh. The present tense is kept. Other reading: All that is snatched from you is what lions and vultures feed on.

</details>

## Letter 109: On whether the wise can help the wise

### 109.4

A bad man harms a bad man and makes him worse, by stirring up his anger, by going along with his gloom, by praising his pleasures; and bad men suffer most when they have mixed their vices most and their wickedness has been brought together into one. So, conversely, a good man will benefit a good man. “How?” you say.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Malus malo nocet*: The Latin is masculine and general. The English reads “a bad man” to match “the wise man” elsewhere in the project. Other reading: A bad person harms a bad person
- *tristitiaeadsentiendo*: This is “tristitiae adsentiendo” run together. “tristitia” ranges from sadness to sourness, and “adsentiri” is to agree with or fall in with. Other reading: by agreeing with his sadness
- *laborant*: “laborare” is to be in distress, to be afflicted, often to be ill. The English reads “suffer”. Other reading: bad men are sickest
- *plurimumvitia miscuere*: This is “plurimum vitia” run together. “plurimum” is taken as an adverb of degree, “most”. It is not said with what the vices are mixed, presumably with each other’s. Other reading: when they have blended very many of their vices
- *in unum conlata nequitia est*: Literally “wickedness has been carried together into one”. The image is of pooling or contributing to a common stock. Other reading: and their wickedness has been pooled
- *proderit*: The same verb as in the question the section before asks (what good one wise man will do another). The English reads “benefit”. Other reading: a good man will be of use to a good man
- *inquis*: Seneca imagines Lucilius breaking in. The Latin has “you say”, not “you ask”. Other reading: “How?” you ask.

</details>

## Letter 110: On empty fears and true riches

### 110.1

From my place at Nomentum I greet you and tell you to have a good mind, that is, all the gods well disposed; and whoever has made himself well disposed to himself has them appeased and favouring him. Set aside for now what some hold, that each one of us is given a god as a minder, not one of the regular rank, to be sure, but this one of a lower grade, from the number of those whom Ovid calls “gods from the common people”. Yet I want you to set this aside in such a way that you remember that our ancestors, who believed it, were Stoics; for to individuals, one by one, they gave both Genius and Juno.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Ex Nomentano meo*: Literally “from my Nomentan”, that is, his estate at Nomentum, a town not far from Rome (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: From my Nomentan estate
- *iubeo habere mentem bonam*: This plays on the usual greeting, which tells the other to be well. “mens bona” is a good or sound mind, and was also worshipped as a goddess (from memory, unchecked), which leads into the talk of gods. The word is “mens”, not “animus”, but “mind” is the natural English for both. Other reading: and tell you to be of sound mind
- *sibi se propitiavit*: The verb repeats the adjective “propitios” used of the gods just before. The English reads “well disposed” for both so that the echo shows. Other reading: whoever has made his peace with himself
- *paedagogum*: A “paedagogus” was the slave who minded a boy and walked him to school, not a teacher. The English reads “minder”. Other reading: a god as a guardian
- *non quidem ordinarium*: “ordinarius” is “of the regular order”, as of a regular magistrate. The contrast is with “inferioris notae”, “of a lower mark”, a phrase used of grades of goods such as wine. Other reading: not indeed a god of the first rank, but one of an inferior brand
- *sed hunc inferioris notae*: “hunc” (“this one”) is slightly odd here. It is taken as pointing to the minder god under discussion, and the English reads “this one”. Other reading: but one of a lower grade
- *de plebe deos*: The phrase is from Ovid, Metamorphoses book 1, where Jupiter says he is no god from the common crowd (from memory, unchecked). “plebs” is the common people of Rome. Other reading: “plebeian gods”
- *maiores nostros qui crediderunt Stoicos fuisse*: The clause is taken as describing the ancestors as a whole: they believed it, and in that they were Stoics before the name. It could also be limited to those of the ancestors who believed it. “It” is supplied as the object of “believed”. Other reading: that those of our ancestors who believed this were Stoics
- *singulis enim et Genium et Iunonem dederunt*: In Roman belief each man had a Genius and each woman a Juno, a personal guardian spirit (from memory, unchecked). The Latin says that to individuals they gave “both Genius and Juno” without dividing them by sex, and the English keeps that, so that it can be heard as both for everyone or as one apiece. Other reading: they gave every man a Genius and every woman a Juno

</details>

### 110.2

We shall see later whether the gods have so much leisure that they manage the affairs of private persons. For the present know this: whether we have been assigned or neglected and given over to fortune, you can call down on no one anything worse than if you call down on him that he should have himself angry with him. But there is no reason for you to wish on anyone you think deserving of punishment that he should have the gods hostile: he has them so, I say, even if he seems to be carried forward by their favour.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *an tantum dis vacet*: Literally “whether there is so much free time for the gods”. Other reading: whether the gods are so unoccupied
- *privatorum negotia procurent*: “procurare” is to act as someone’s agent or steward. “privati” are individuals as opposed to the state or the world at large. Other reading: that they act as stewards of individuals’ business
- *adsignati sumus*: “Assigned” means assigned to a guardian god, as in the section before. The Latin does not say to whom, and nothing is added. Other reading: whether each of us has been allotted to a god
- *inprecari*: To pray something down on someone, usually a curse. The verb comes twice and the English reads “call down” both times. Other reading: you can curse no one with anything worse
- *ut se habeat iratum*: Literally “that he may have himself angry”. It is modelled on the phrase for having the gods angry with one, and answers “made himself well disposed to himself” in the section before. The English keeps “have” and one “himself”, and supplies “with him” so that the phrase is not read as “make himself angry”. Other reading: that he should be at enmity with himself
- *habet, inquam*: The object, “the gods hostile”, is understood from the clause before, and the English supplies “them so”. Other reading: he does have them hostile, let it be said
- *produci*: “produci” is to be led forward, and so to be advanced or promoted. Other reading: even if he seems to be advanced by their favour

</details>

### 110.4

But that falling itself has no evil in it, if you look to the end, beyond which nature has thrown no one down. The limit of all things is near; near, I say, is both that from which the fortunate man is thrown out and that from which the unfortunate man is let go: we stretch both out and make them long by hope and fear. But, if you are wise, measure all things by the human condition; draw in together both what you rejoice at and what you fear. And it is worth the price to rejoice at nothing for long, so that you may not fear anything for long.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ipsum illud cadere*: “That falling” picks up the fall from a height spoken of in the section before. Other reading: But the fall itself
- *exitum*: “exitus” is a way out, an end, an outcome, and often death. The next clause shows that death is meant, but the English keeps the wider word “end”. Other reading: if you look to death
- *et illud unde felix eicitur et illud unde infelix emittitur*: “illud unde” is left vague: the state, span or place the fortunate man is thrown out of and the one the unfortunate man is released from. The two verbs differ on purpose (thrown out against his will, let out as a release). “Fortunate” is used for “felix” to keep “happy” for “beatus”. Other reading: both the point at which the lucky man is cast out and the point at which the unlucky man is set free
- *longa spe ac metu facimus*: “longa” is taken as agreeing with “utraque” (“make both long”), with “spe ac metu” as the means. It could be read as “by long hope and fear”, but then “facimus” has nothing to complete it. Other reading: stretch both out, and do so by long hope and fear
- *si sapis*: An everyday phrase, “if you have sense”, but the verb is the one behind “sapiens”, the wise man, so the English reads “if you are wise”. Other reading: if you have any sense
- *omnia humana condicione metire*: This can be divided two ways: “measure all things by the human condition” or “measure all human things by their condition”, that is, by the terms on which they are held. The English takes the first, since “condicione” alone would not say whose condition is meant. Other reading: measure all human things by their condition
- *contrahe*: The opposite of “extendimus” (“stretch out”) just before, so the English reads “draw in” to keep the pair. “simul” with it is “together”, “at the same time”. Other reading: shorten at the same time both what you rejoice at and what you fear
- *Est autem tanti*: “tanti est” is an idiom of price: it is worth that much, it is worth it. Other reading: And it is worth while to rejoice at nothing for long

</details>

### 110.10

Whatever was going to be for our good, our god and parent placed close at hand; he did not wait for us to search and gave it of his own accord: the things that would harm us he pressed down very deep. We can complain of nothing but ourselves: the things we were to perish by we have brought out, though the nature of things was unwilling and was hiding them. We have bound the mind over to pleasure, and to indulge pleasure is the beginning of all evils; we have handed it over to ambition and reputation, and to the rest, things equally empty and hollow.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *bono futurum erat*: Literally “was going to be for a good”, that is, to do good to those it was given to. “Good” is kept because the passage sets it against the “evils” below. Other reading: Whatever was going to be of benefit to us
- *deus et parens noster*: “noster” can go with both nouns or only with “parens”. The English takes it with both, “our god and parent”. “parens” can also be “father”. Other reading: god, who is our father
- *ultro dedit*: “ultro” means unasked, of his own motion. Other reading: and gave it unasked
- *nocitura altissime pressit*: Literally “things going to harm he pressed down most deeply”. The section before speaks of digging in the earth, so buried metals are meant. The superlative can be “very deep” or “deepest”. Other reading: what would do harm he buried deepest
- *ea quibus periremus*: The subjunctive gives purpose or destiny: things meant to bring ruin on those who dug them up, not merely things that did. Other reading: the things that would destroy us
- *rerum natura*: The glossary gives “the nature of things” for the two-word phrase. Here it acts almost as a person, unwilling and hiding things, and plain “nature” would also be possible. Other reading: though nature was unwilling and was hiding them
- *Addiximus*: “addicere” is a legal word: to make a person or thing over to another by a ruling, as a debtor was handed to his creditor. The English reads “bound over” so that the bondage can be heard. Other reading: the mind has been made over to pleasure
- *ambitioni et famae*: “ambitio” is literally canvassing for office, and so the pursuit of standing. “fama” is what people say of one. Other reading: to the pursuit of office and to fame
- *vanis et inanibus*: The two words are near synonyms, both meaning empty. Two English words are used to keep the pair. Other reading: equally vain and empty

</details>

### 110.17

So this is what I say to myself whenever something of that kind has dazzled my eyes, whenever I come upon a splendid house, a well-groomed troop of slaves, a litter set on handsome bearers: “Why do you marvel? Why are you struck dumb? It is a procession. Those things are shown, not owned, and while they please they pass by.”

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ipse mihi dico*: From memory, unchecked: this section stands inside a long speech of the philosopher Attalus that Seneca is reporting, so the speaker here would be Attalus and not Seneca. Nothing in the Latin given settles it, and the English reads the same either way. Other reading: (no difference in the English)
- *praestrinxerit*: “praestringere oculos” is to dazzle or dull the eyes. The tense is “shall have dazzled”, which English gives as “has dazzled”. Other reading: whenever something of the kind blinds my eyes
- *cohors culta servorum*: “cohors” is a troop or retinue. “culta” is well cared for in dress and grooming. Other reading: a smartly dressed retinue of slaves
- *formonsis inposita calonibus*: “formonsis” is a spelling of “formosis”, handsome. “calones” are drudges or porters, here the men who carry the litter. Literally the litter is “placed upon” them. Other reading: a litter resting on the shoulders of good-looking porters
- *quid miraris? quid stupes?*: “mirari” is to wonder at or admire, and the English reads “marvel”, as in Ep. 115.9. “stupere” is to be stunned or to gape. Other reading: “Why do you admire? Why do you gape?”
- *pompa est*: “pompa” is a formal procession, the image carried over from the section before. It is not “pomp” in the English sense. Other reading: It is a parade.
- *istae res*: “istae” points away from the speaker, to the things the one addressed is staring at, so the English reads “Those things”. Other reading: These things are shown, not owned
- *dum placent transeunt*: “transeunt” is “pass by”, as a procession does, and also “pass away”. The English keeps the procession image. Other reading: and even while they please they are gone

</details>

## Letter 113: On whether the virtues are living beings

### 113.18

No rational living being does anything unless it has first been stirred by the impression of some thing, then has formed an impulse, and then assent has confirmed this impulse. I will say what assent is. I ought to walk: only then do I walk, when I have said this to myself and approved this opinion of mine; I ought to sit: only then do I sit. This assent is not found in virtue.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Omne rationale animal nihil agit nisi*: Literally “Every rational animal does nothing unless”. The English turns it as “No … does anything unless”, which says the same, and reads “living being” for “animal” because the argument counts gods as well as men under the word. Other reading: Every rational animal does nothing unless
- *specie alicuius rei*: “species” is the Stoic technical term for what appears to the mind, the Greek phantasia. The English reads “impression”. Other reading: by the appearance of some thing
- *impetum cepit*: “impetus” is here the Stoic technical term for the movement towards action, the Greek horme, and the English reads “impulse”. In Ep. 108.15 the same word is the ordinary “rush”. “cepit” is literally “has taken”. Other reading: then has felt an urge
- *adsensio confirmavit*: “adsensio” is the Stoic technical term for the mind’s agreeing to an impression, the Greek synkatathesis. The English reads “assent” throughout the section. Other reading: then agreement has ratified this impulse
- *Oportet me ambulare*: This is the thought to which assent is given. The Latin does not mark it as quoted, so the English uses a colon and no quotation marks. “oportet” is “it is right or fitting that”. Other reading: “It is fitting for me to walk”
- *adprobavi hanc opinionem meam*: “opinio” is “opinion” by the glossary. The approving of it is the assent being defined. Other reading: and have endorsed this belief of mine
- *Haec adsensio in virtute non est*: Literally “this assent is not in virtue”. The point is taken to be that a virtue does not itself give assent, so cannot act as a living being does. The Latin could also be heard as “this assent does not belong to virtue”. Other reading: This assent is no part of virtue.

</details>

### 113.31

Let him teach me how sacred a thing justice is, which looks to another’s good and asks nothing from itself except to be put to use. Let it have nothing to do with ambition and reputation: let it win its own approval. Let each person convince himself of this before everything: I ought to be just without payment. That is too little. Let him convince himself of this as well: let me be glad to go further and even spend on this most beautiful virtue; let all thought be turned as far as possible away from private advantages. There is no reason for you to look to what the reward of a just act is: there is a greater one in what is just.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Doceat me*: The subject of “let him teach” is not named in this section or in the one given before it; it must come from earlier in the letter. The English keeps an open “him”. Other reading: Let someone teach me
- *alienum bonum spectans*: “bonum” is “the good” by the glossary: justice looks to the good of someone else. “spectare” comes back in the last sentence, so the English reads “look to” in both places. Other reading: having regard to the good of others
- *nisi usum sui*: Literally “except the use of itself”: justice wants nothing from itself but to be exercised. The English reads “except to be put to use”. Other reading: except its own exercise
- *sibi placeat*: Literally “let it please itself”, that is, have its own approval and not look for other people’s. The English reads “let it win its own approval”, since “pleased with itself” would mean smug. Other reading: let it be pleased with itself
- *gratis*: “gratis” is without payment. The next sentences build on the money image (spending, reward), so the English reads “without payment” and keeps “reward” for “praemium”. Other reading: just for nothing
- *me in hanc pulcherrimam virtutem ultro etiam inpendere iuvet*: “me” can be the one who is glad to spend or the thing spent (“to spend myself”); the English takes the first, as in “me iustum esse” just before, which leaves “spend” without a stated object. “ultro etiam” is read as “going further, even”: not only unpaid but paying out. It can also be “of my own accord”. Other reading: let it be my delight even to spend myself, of my own accord, on this most beautiful virtue
- *iustae rei praemium*: Literally “the reward of a just thing”. The English reads “a just act”. Other reading: what the prize for a just deed is
- *maius in iusto est*: “maius” (“greater”) is neuter: the English understands “reward” with it, but it can also stand alone as “something greater”. “iusto” can be neuter (“in what is just”) or masculine (“in the just man”); the English takes it as neuter, following “iustae rei”. Other reading: there is something greater in the just man

</details>

### 113.32

Fix this in yourself as well, what I was saying a little earlier: that it is nothing to the point how many people know of your fairness. Anyone who wants his virtue made public is working not for virtue but for glory. Are you unwilling to be just without glory? But, by Hercules, you will often have to be just with a bad name, and then, if you are wise, let a bad reputation well won delight you. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *tibi adfige*: Literally “fix it onto yourself”, as one pins or nails something on so that it stays. Other reading: Fasten this to yourself as well
- *quod paulo ante dicebam*: This looks back to the remark in the section before that justice should have nothing to do with ambition and reputation. Other reading: the thing said just now
- *aequitatem tuam*: “aequitas” is fairness or even-handedness, a different word from “iustitia”. The English keeps them apart. Other reading: how many know of your justice
- *publicari*: “publicare” is to make public property. It can carry a harsher tone, of putting something up for common use, which the English does not bring out. Other reading: Anyone who wants his virtue advertised
- *Non vis esse iustus sine gloria?*: Literally “Do you not wish to be just without glory?”, that is, do you refuse to be just unless glory comes with it. Other reading: You do not want to be just without glory?
- *mehercules*: An everyday oath, “by Hercules”, used for emphasis. The name is kept. Other reading: But truly
- *cum infamia*: “infamia” is ill repute, the opposite of “gloria”. It answers “without glory”: not merely without it, but with its opposite, and the English keeps “with” for that reason. Other reading: you will often have to be just and be ill spoken of
- *mala opinio bene parta*: A play on “bad” and “well”: a bad opinion, well got. “opinio” here is what others think of one, so the English reads “reputation” and not the glossary’s “opinion”, which would be heard as one’s own. “Well won” means won by acting well, not deserved. Other reading: let a bad opinion, well got, delight you

</details>

## Letter 114: On style as a mirror of character

### 114.24

Since I have used this comparison, I will keep to it. Our mind is at one time a king, at another a tyrant: a king when it looks to what is honourable, cares for the welfare of the body entrusted to it and gives it no command that is shameful, none that is sordid; but when it is uncontrolled, greedy, self-indulgent, it passes over to a hateful and dreadful name and becomes a tyrant. Then uncontrolled passions take hold of it and press on it; and at first, indeed, it rejoices, as a people commonly does when it is filled to no purpose with a handout that will harm it and paws at what it cannot swallow;

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *hac similitudine*: The comparison is the one made in the section before, of the mind to a king. Other reading: this likeness
- *honesta intuetur*: “honesta” is the neuter plural, “honourable things”. The English reads the glossary’s “what is honourable”. Other reading: when it keeps its eyes on honourable things
- *salutem*: “salus” is both safety and health, what a king owes those in his charge. The English reads “welfare”, which covers both. Other reading: cares for the health of the body entrusted to it
- *nihil sordidum*: “sordidus” is dirty, and so low or base. The English keeps the Latin word in its English form, “sordid”. Other reading: none that is base
- *inpotens*: The word means lacking power over oneself, not “powerless”. It returns in the plural of the passions in the next sentence, and the English reads “uncontrolled” both times. Other reading: when it has no command of itself
- *delicatus*: Soft, pampered, given to luxury. The English reads “self-indulgent”, as in Ep. 108.16. Other reading: soft
- *transit in nomen detestabile ac dirum*: Literally “it crosses over into a name”. “dirus” is ill-omened, dreadful. Other reading: it takes on a name that is hated and accursed
- *excipiunt*: “excipere” is to catch, receive or take over next in turn. It may suggest the passions taking the mind over as attendants or captors. Other reading: Then uncontrolled passions receive it
- *largitione nocitura frustra plenus*: “largitio” is a public distribution of food or money, often a political bribe. “frustra” is taken with “plenus”, “full to no purpose”. Other reading: stuffed in vain with a largess that will do it harm
- *quae non potest haurire contrectans*: “haurire” is to drain or gulp down, and “contrectare” is to handle or finger. The section as given stops in mid-sentence at a semicolon: “quidem” (“at first, indeed”) expects an answering clause that comes only in the next section, so the English ends on a semicolon too. Other reading: and fingers what it cannot consume;

</details>

## Letter 115: On fussy style and the lure of wealth

### 115.9

We marvel at walls coated with thin marble, though we know what sort of thing it is that is being hidden. We cheat our own eyes, and when we have flooded ceilings with gold, what else are we rejoicing in but a lie? For we know that under that gold ugly timber is lurking. Nor is it only over walls or panelled ceilings that a thin ornament is stretched: the good fortune of all those you see striding loftily along is overlaid with gold leaf. Look inside, and you will know how much evil lies under that thin skin of rank.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Miramur*: “mirari” is to wonder at or admire. The English reads “marvel”, as in Ep. 110.17. Other reading: admire walls coated with thin marble
- *tenui marmore inductos*: Literally “overlaid with thin marble”, a veneer. “tenuis” comes three times in the section and the English keeps “thin” each time. Other reading: walls veneered with a thin layer of marble
- *Oculis nostris inponimus*: “imponere” with a dative is an idiom for deceiving or imposing on someone. Other reading: impose on our own eyes
- *auro tecta perfudimus*: “tecta” is taken as the noun, roofs or ceilings, the thing covered. “perfundere” is to drench or pour over. Other reading: with our roofs drenched in gold
- *lacunaribus*: “lacunaria” are ceilings divided into sunken panels. Other reading: over walls or coffered ceilings
- *quos incedere altos vides*: “incedere” is to walk with a stately step. “altos” is high or lofty, of their bearing or their position; the English reads “loftily”. Other reading: whom you see walking tall
- *bratteata felicitas*: “brattea” is metal beaten thin, gold leaf, and “bratteata” is “covered with gold leaf”: their good fortune is a surface layer only. The English reads “good fortune” for “felicitas” to keep “happy” for “beatus”. Other reading: all of them have a gilded happiness
- *membrana dignitatis*: “membrana” is a thin skin or film. “dignitas” is rank or standing. Other reading: that thin film of high position
- *quantum mali*: Literally “how much of bad”. The English reads “evil” for “malum” as elsewhere in these passages. Other reading: how much that is bad lies beneath

</details>

## Letter 116: On removing the passions altogether

### 116.1

It has often been asked whether it is better to have moderate passions or none. Our school drives them out; the Peripatetics temper them. For my part, I do not see how any moderate degree of a disease can be wholesome or useful. Do not be afraid: I am taking from you none of the things that you do not want denied to you. I will show myself easy and indulgent towards the things you are aiming at and that you think either necessary for life or useful or pleasant: I will take away the vice. For when I have forbidden you to crave, I will allow you to want, so that you may do those same things unafraid, so that you may do them with a surer purpose, so that you may feel the pleasures themselves more. Why should they not reach you more if you command them than if you are their slave?

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *adfectus*: The Stoic term for the passions, which the school treats as faults of the mind and not as neutral feelings. The glossary word “passions” is kept because the next sentences call them a disease. Other reading: moderate feelings or none
- *Nostri*: A possessive used as a noun, meaning the people of Seneca’s own side, the Stoics. The English says “school” so that the contrast with the Peripatetics, the followers of Aristotle, is seen. Other reading: The Stoics drive them out
- *temperant*: The verb can mean to moderate, to mix in the right proportion, or to keep within bounds. The plain “temper” is used. Other reading: the Peripatetics keep them within limits
- *mediocritas morbi*: The noun is the Latin word for the Peripatetic “mean” or middle state, and it answers “moderate” in the first sentence. “Moderate” is used in both places so that the echo shows, at the cost of the technical colour of “mean”. Other reading: any mean of a disease, or: any middling amount of illness
