- *detraham vitium*: Short and abrupt in the Latin: the things are granted and only what is wrong in the wanting is removed, as the next sentence explains. “Vice” is the glossary word and is the word used for the same noun at the end of this letter; “fault” is possible. Other reading: the fault is what will be taken away
- *cupere interdixero, velle permittam*: The argument rests on two verbs of wanting: the first names desire as a passion, the second reasoned wanting. English “desire” and “want” are too close to show this, so the pair is “crave” and “want”; “crave” is a little stronger than the Latin verb. Other reading: forbidden to desire, allowed to will
- *ut certiore consilio*: The clause has no verb of its own; “do them” is supplied from the clause before. The noun can mean purpose, plan or deliberation; “purpose” is taken. Other reading: so that you may do them with firmer deliberation
- *magis perventurae sint*: Literally “be going to come through to you more”. “More” may mean more fully or more surely; the English leaves it as open as the Latin. Other reading: why should they not come home to you more fully

</details>

### 116.8

Do you know why we cannot do those things? Because we do not believe that we can. Or rather, by Hercules, the fact of the matter is something else: we defend our vices because we love them, and we would rather excuse them than shake them off. Nature has given man strength enough, if we would use it, if we would gather our powers and rouse all of them on our own behalf, or at any rate not against ourselves. Not wanting to is the cause; not being able to is put forward as the pretext. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *ista*: “Those things” are the Stoic demands that the objector in the section before calls too great and too hard. The pronoun has a second-person colour, “those things you speak of”. Other reading: the things you mention
- *Immo mehercules aliud est in re*: Seneca corrects the answer just given: the first is the polite one, the second the true one. “By Hercules” is an everyday Roman oath, kept as it is; “the fact of the matter” expands two short words, literally “in the thing”. Other reading: No, in truth the real case is different
- *excusare illa quam excutere*: A play on two verbs that sound alike. The second means to shake out or shake off, and can also mean to search or examine closely; “shake off” is taken as the opposite of defending. Other reading: excuse them than examine them
- *certe non contra nos*: A fallback inside the sentence: use all the powers on one’s own behalf or, failing that, at least do not turn them against oneself. Other reading: certainly not against ourselves
- *Nolle in causa est, non posse praetenditur*: Two infinitives used as nouns: “to be unwilling” and “to be unable”. The second verb means to hold something out in front as a cover. “Not wanting” keeps the word used for the reasoned verb of wanting at the start of the letter. Other reading: Unwillingness is the reason; inability is the excuse held up in front of it

</details>

## Letter 118: On seeking nothing from fortune and defining good

### 118.7

But ignorance of the truth afflicts everyone. Deceived by common talk, they are carried towards things as if towards goods; then, when they have got them and have suffered much, they see that these are bad, or empty, or smaller than they had hoped. And most people admire things that deceive from a distance, and commonly goods count as great things.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *male habet*: An idiom, literally “has (someone) badly”: to trouble, distress or do harm to. “Afflicts” is used. Other reading: ignorance of the truth does harm to everyone
- *ignorantia ueri*: The genitive can be “of the truth” in general or “of what is true” in the particular case, that is, of what these things really are. The general sense is taken. Other reading: not knowing what is true
- *Tamquam ad bona feruntur*: The Latin gives the rush no object of its own: literally “they are carried as if towards goods”. “Things” is supplied so that what is got and then found bad has a name; “goods” is the glossary word and returns in the last clause. Other reading: they are carried along as if towards goods
- *rumoribus*: Not “rumours” in the narrow sense but what people commonly say, the general report about what is worth having. Other reading: deceived by rumours
- *adepti ac multa passi*: Two participles. The Latin does not say whether the suffering comes before or after the getting; the English keeps the Latin order and leaves it open. Other reading: having gained them at the cost of much suffering
- *miratur ex interuallo fallentia*: “From a distance” can go with “admire” or with “deceive”. It is attached to “deceive”, in line with the section before, where things look high to someone lying far from them. Other reading: most people admire from a distance things that deceive
- *uulgo*: The word can be the adverb “commonly” or a dative, “for the crowd”. The adverb is the usual use and is taken; the sense differs little. Other reading: and with the crowd goods count as great things
- *bona pro magnis sunt*: As given, the words say that goods count as great things, and the English follows them. The run of thought would lead one to expect the reverse, that great things are taken for goods; some editions may print the two words the other way round (from memory, unchecked). Other reading: and commonly great things count as goods

</details>

## Letter 120: On how we come to know the good

### 120.22

This above all is how an unwise mind is shown up: it comes forward now as one, now as another, and it does not match itself, and I judge nothing more shameful than that. Count it a great thing to play one man. But no one except the wise man plays one; the rest of us are many-shaped. At one moment we will seem to you thrifty and serious, at another spendthrift and frivolous; we change our mask again and again and put on the opposite of the one we have taken off. So demand this of yourself: keep yourself, right to the end, such as you set out to show yourself. Bring it about that you can be praised, or, failing that, that you can be recognised. Of someone you saw yesterday it can rightly be said, “Who is this?”: the change is so great. Farewell.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *animus inprudens*: The adjective means lacking practical good sense, and can also mean unaware or off guard. “Unwise” is used so that the contrast with the wise man two sentences later is heard, though the Latin word is not formed from the word for “wise”. Other reading: a mind without foresight, or: a mind off its guard
- *alius prodit atquealius*: The verb form can be from “to come forward”, as an actor does, or from “to betray, reveal”, which would need an object. “Comes forward” is taken, which suits the stage language that follows; the paired pronouns mean different at different times and agree with “mind”, so no noun is added. Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: it gives itself away as now one thing, now another
- *inpar sibi est*: Literally “unequal to itself”: not matching itself from one time to the next. The aside about shamefulness, which comes before this clause in the Latin, is moved to the end of the sentence to keep the English readable. Other reading: it is inconsistent with itself
- *unumhominem agere*: The verb is the stage word for playing a part. The point is to play a single character throughout, where most people play many. Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: to act the part of one man
- *frugi*: A word of broad praise: thrifty, sober, decent. Since it is set against the spendthrift, “thrifty” is used. Other reading: sober and serious
- *graues, modo prodigi et uani*: The first adjective is literally “heavy” and the last literally “empty”. Said of people they mean serious or steady and frivolous or unreliable, and the English gives those senses. Other reading: weighty, at another spendthrift and empty
- *personam*: The actor’s mask, and so a role or character. The image of the mask is kept because it is put on and taken off like clothing in the same sentence. Other reading: the role is changed again and again
- *usque ad exitum*: The noun means a way out and so an end: the end of life, and also the end of a play. English “end” covers both. Other reading: right up to your death
- *ut adgnosci*: The same verb is understood: “that you can be recognised”. It is taken as being known for the same person as before; it could also mean being acknowledged. Other reading: or, if not, at least acknowledged
- *hic qui est?*: A plain question of identity. The pronoun form could also ask what sort of man this is. The word for “yesterday” in the same sentence is an old spelling. Other reading: “What kind of man is this?”

</details>

## Letter 124: On the good belonging to reason alone

### 124.12

But what is this good? I will tell you: a free mind, standing tall, putting other things under itself and itself under none. Infancy is so far from being able to take in this good that boyhood does not hope for it and youth hopes for it presumptuously; old age is doing well if it has reached it by long and concentrated effort. If this is the good, it is also a thing grasped by the understanding.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *liber animus, erectus*: The second adjective means raised up, standing straight, not bowed. “Upright” would say this but is heard as “honest” when said of a mind, so the English has “standing tall”. Other reading: a free mind, unbowed
- *se nulli*: The form most often means “to no one”, but after “other things” it can as well mean “to no other thing”. “None” leaves the choice as open as the Latin. Other reading: and itself under no one, or: and itself under nothing
- *non recipit*: Literally “does not receive”: cannot take in, has no room for. The sentence climbs through the ages of life: infancy, boyhood, youth, old age. Other reading: infancy is so far from being capable of this good
- *inprobe speret*: The adverb means beyond what is proper: shamelessly, greedily, unreasonably. “Presumptuously” is taken, a hope beyond what one has a right to. Other reading: youth hopes for it shamelessly
- *bene agitur cum senectute*: An idiom, literally “it is dealt well with old age”: old age is fortunate, has no cause for complaint. Other reading: old age is fortunate if
- *peruenit*: The form can be present, “reaches”, or perfect, “has reached”. The perfect is taken: old age is doing well if by then it has arrived. Other reading: if it reaches it
- *longostudio intentoque*: The noun is application, zeal or study, and “effort” is used as the widest of these; the second adjective means stretched tight, intent. Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: by long and strenuous study
- *intellegibile*: A technical word: an object of the understanding as opposed to an object of the senses, and the surrounding letter argues over which of the two grasps the good. It is spelled out because “intelligible” in English today mostly means “clear”. Other reading: it is also intelligible
- *Si hoc est bonum*: Latin has no articles. “The good” is written because the section before speaks of man’s own good; “a good” is possible. Other reading: If this is a good

</details>

### 124.21

Do you ask what this discussion now leads to, and what use it will be to your mind? I say: it exercises the mind and sharpens it, and it keeps the mind, which will be doing something in any case, in an honourable occupation. And it is also of use in that it delays those who are hurrying towards what is wrong. But I say this: I can in no way be of more use than if I show you your own good, if I separate you from the mute animals, if I place you with god.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Quo nunc pertineat ista disputatio*: Literally “where this discussion now reaches to”: what it bears on, what its point is. The noun is a reasoned argument or debate. Other reading: what the point of this argument now is
- *profutura*: The verb “to benefit” comes three times in the section and is “be of use” each time. “Good” is kept for the different Latin word in “your own good”, the key term of the letter. Other reading: what good it will do your mind
- *utique aliquid acturumoccupatione honesta tenet*: The participle goes with the mind: it will be busy with something, so let it be this. The adverb is taken with the participle, “in any case”; it could go with the main verb instead. Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: and at the very least keeps it, which will be doing something, in an honourable occupation
- *etiam quo moratur*: The first word is taken as “also”, a further benefit, though it can be “even”. The second is read as “in that”; it could be “in so far as”, which would limit the claim. Other reading: it is of use even in so far as it delays
- *ad praua properantes*: The adjective is literally “crooked” and so wrong or bad; “wrong” is used because “crooked” today suggests dishonest dealing. The people are general, “those who hurry”, not Lucilius alone. Other reading: those hurrying towards what is crooked
- *mutis animalibus*: Animals without speech, which for the Stoics goes with being without reason. “Mute” is the nearest English word and avoids “dumb”, which is now heard as “stupid”. Other reading: from the animals that cannot speak
- *cum deo pono*: Singular, with no article in Latin: “with god” or “with a god”. “God” is written without a capital, as the glossary has it; the preposition says alongside, in the same class. Other reading: beside a god

</details>

### 124.22

Why, I say, do you feed and exercise the strength of your body? Nature has granted that strength in greater measure to farm animals and wild beasts. Why do you cultivate your beauty? When you have done everything, you will be beaten in looks by the mute animals. Why do you dress your hair with enormous care? When you have let it flow in the manner of the Parthians, or tied it up in the way of the Germans, or, as is the habit of the Scythians, spread it loose, a thicker mane will toss on any horse you like, and a more beautiful one will bristle on the neck of lions. When you have trained yourself for speed, you will not be the equal of a little hare.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *inquam*: A verb of saying in the first person, dropped into the question: Seneca picks up his own speech and presses the point of the section before. The first person is kept. Other reading: ask, where the English has “say”
- *alis et exerces*: The first verb is “you feed, nourish”, not the noun for wings. The pair is feeding up and training, as an athlete does. Other reading: do you build up and train
- *Pecudibus istas maiores*: The pronoun is the thing granted, “that strength”, with a second-person colour, and “greater” describes it: literally “has granted that, greater”. The first noun is herd or farm animals, set against wild ones. Other reading: Nature has given greater strength than yours to livestock and wild beasts
- *formam*: Outward shape, and so good looks or beauty. “Beauty” is used here and “more beautiful” for the related adjective said of the lion’s mane, which is in an old spelling; the different word in the next sentence is “looks”. Other reading: Why do you cultivate your looks?
- *decore uinceris*: The verb form could be present, “you are beaten”, or future, “you will be beaten”; the futures around it decide for the future. The noun, grace or handsomeness, is a different word from the one for beauty and is “looks”. Other reading: you are beaten in beauty
- *Germanorum modo uinxeris*: Literally “bound” it: taken as hair tied up or knotted, a style reported of some German peoples (from memory, unchecked). The Parthians are the eastern empire beyond the Euphrates, the Scythians the nomads north of the Black Sea. Other reading: or bound it as the Germans do
- *sparseris*: Literally “scattered”: hair left loose and unarranged. How this differs from the flowing Parthian style is not clear from the Latin alone; two different English verbs are kept. Other reading: left it dishevelled
- *horrebit*: The verb means to stand stiff, to bristle, and the image is kept. Other reading: a finer one will stand up on the necks of lions
- *lepusculo*: A diminutive, “little hare”, which makes the comparison more belittling. “Little” is kept. Other reading: a match for a hare

</details>

### 124.23

Will you not leave the things in which you are bound to be beaten while you strive for what is not your own, and go back to your own good? What is this? A mind, of course, freed from faults and pure, striving to equal god, lifting itself above human things, placing nothing of its own outside itself. You are a rational animal. What, then, is the good in you? Perfected reason. Summon it from here to its own goal, as far as it can possibly grow.

<details><summary>Notes</summary>

- *Vis tu*: Literally “do you want to”. With the pronoun this is a set way of urging someone, and English makes such an urging with a negative question, so “Will you not” is written although the Latin has no “not”. Other reading: Are you willing to leave the things
- *dum in alienaniteris*: “What is not your own” answers “your own good” and here means the goods of animals: strength, beauty, speed. The clause is taken as plain time, “while”; it could also give the reason for being beaten. Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: because you strain at things that belong to others
- *emendatusac purus*: The first word means freed of faults, as a text is corrected. “Corrected” said of a mind sounds like a marked exercise, so the sense is spelled out. Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: a mind corrected and clean
- *aemulator dei*: One who strives to equal: the word covers both “rival” and “imitator”. “Striving to equal” keeps both and avoids the hostility that “rival” can suggest in English. Other reading: a rival of god, or: an imitator of god
- *nihil extra se suiponens*: Literally “placing nothing of its own outside itself”: counting nothing external as its own. The literal image is kept. Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: reckoning nothing outside itself as its own
- *perfecta ratio*: The adjective is a participle, “brought to completion”. “Perfected” is written because the last sentence shows something finished by growth, not a fixed quality. Other reading: perfect reason
- *ad suum finem hinc euoca*: The noun is end in the sense of goal and also of limit; “goal” is written because “end” can be heard as ceasing. The adverb “from here” may mean from this point on or away from the bodily pursuits just listed, and the English leaves it as open as the Latin. Other reading: call it away from these things to its own limit
- *in quantum potest plurimumcrescere*: Construed as a clause of extent hanging on the command: as far as it can possibly grow. It reads a little abruptly, and some editors may supply a verb meaning “allow” before it (from memory, unchecked). Two words are run together in the copy. Other reading: and let it grow as great as it can

</details>
